They call it CHAPTER X, but in our house we never spoke of it as a number.

We named it the way my grandmother named her breads—by the sound it made in the kitchen when the weather changed.

That morning the estate did what it always did before a turning of the story: it woke in layers. First came the thin, shy creak of old rafters stretching, then the sparrows rehearsing their bright, needling scales under the eaves, then—last and most important—the steady, human noises that meant the day had not been stolen by illness or grief: the scuff of slippers on flagstone, the clink of iron on hearth, the breathy hush of flour being sifted as if it were snowfall.

In those domestic sounds lay a kind of law. A household that keeps its cadence keeps its mind. A household drowned beneath harsh, purchased racket—wheels, hammers, shouting men, the bragging brass of distant fairs—loses its memory the way a tongue loses words when nobody speaks them to children.

So I listened.

I listened as I tied my apron and set the bowl to warm by the range. I listened to the pepper-mill’s dry gravel, to the kettle’s rising note, to the yard beyond the window where the rooks argued in a dialect older than ours. I listened, too, for what was missing. There are absences that sound louder than anything.

And because this is a detective’s tale—though told through the steam of stew-pots and the scrape of knives—you will understand why I begin with the ear, not the eye.

The chapter begins, as it must, with labels.

FOURTH NARRATIVE, they wrote, as if you could pin a life to a ledger line the way you pin a sprig of thyme to a roast. FIFTH NARRATIVE. SIXTH NARRATIVE.

I have handled ledgers thick with accounts of wheat and butter, and I know that numbering makes people feel safe. But the truth is that stories do not march in order; they simmer. They bubble up again from the bottom when you thought they had settled. A taste returns from years ago—smoke on the tongue, salt behind the teeth—and suddenly you are in another kitchen entirely.

So let me set it properly, as one sets a table: the past at one end, the present at the other, and the same dish passing between them until everyone has taken their share.

I.

In the earliest telling—what they insist on calling the Fourth—an old man spoke in a rural cadence that the younger servants tried to smooth away when they repeated him, as if his vowels were dirt on a hem. He had the hands of an agrarian elder: knuckles like walnuts, nails permanently lined with honest earth, and a patience learned from watching weather.

He spoke while peeling apples, each strip of skin unbroken, falling in a long red ribbon into the bowl. The sound of the knife on fruit was steady and calming, and it kept the room from straying into the bright, brittle excitement that modern people mistake for intelligence.

He told of a stone—no, not yet named, for names are power and power hides itself in the dominant lexicon. He told of an object that brought trouble as surely as too much yeast brings a loaf that splits its own crust. He had no grand words for it. In his dialect it was simply “the cold light,” the thing that makes night look like day and day look like it’s lying.

While he spoke, the apples browned where they lay, and the cook’s boy—new, city-bred, ears always hungry for fashionable noises—kept humming a tune he’d bought at a fair, thin as tin. The old man stopped him with a look, not cruelly, but as one stops a fly from walking across a cooling pie.

“Hush,” he said. “You’ll frighten the house into forgetting itself.”

The boy fell silent. The room recovered its proper music: peel, slice, drop; peel, slice, drop.

And at the end of that telling the old man did not conclude with a moral. He only said, in words that don’t quite translate into standard speech, something like: What you cannot say in your own tongue will be said for you by another, and it won’t mean the same thing.

II.

The Fifth telling belonged to a woman who cooked like she loved—hotly, without apology. She kept a little notebook of recipes and origins, and I learned later that she was, in her way, a food historian: not the museum kind, but the kind who knows that a dish is a map of conquest and marriage and hunger, that a spice can be a scar.

She spoke while stirring a pot of broth so clear you could see the bones at the bottom like pale truths. She had roasted the onions first until their sugars turned to that deep brown that smells of comfort and loss at once. She crushed garlic with salt, not the other way round, because she believed—rightly—that salt is a memory-keeper, pulling the essential oils out as if coaxing a confession.

Her story braided back into the old man’s without warning. In one sentence she was describing the taste of saffron she’d once bartered for in a market where every stallholder spoke three languages and none of them agreed on the price. In the next, she was speaking of the same cold light the elder had named, now clothed in a more official term, a grander syllable borrowed from elsewhere.

“That’s what they call it,” she said, and her mouth tightened around the foreign word the way one tightens around a bitter rind. “But in the old talk—my mother’s talk—it’s different. In her mouth it was a warning you could feel in your teeth.”

She paused to skim the broth, lifting the grey foam away with such care that even her suspicion became delicate. Outside, a cart passed too fast on the lane; its wheels struck stones and made a hard, industrial clatter. She flinched, and for a moment her stirring became a little too quick, as if noise could hurry mistakes into existence.

“It comes,” she went on, quieter, “when people stop listening to the right sounds. When they buy music instead of making it. When they think a name is the thing itself.”

She served the broth in warmed bowls. Steam rose, carrying thyme and bay and the faint sweetness of roasted onion. We drank, and the taste stayed on the palate like a clue.

III.

The Sixth telling was sharper, more methodical. If the elder was soil and the cook was fire, this speaker was salt and steel: meticulous, measuring everything, insisting on sequence. It was the kind of narrative that makes investigators feel righteous.

He laid out facts the way I lay out ingredients for pastry: weighed, counted, set in their places so nothing can hide behind chance. He spoke of hands that moved through rooms, of doors that clicked, of objects placed and removed. He spoke, too, of the household itself—how servants’ routines create blind spots, how familiarity becomes camouflage.

While he spoke, I kneaded dough. The rhythm of it—push, fold, turn—kept my mind from being bullied by his certainty. Too much certainty is as dangerous as too much salt: it masks the subtle flavors where the truth lives.

He had his numbered points— I, II, III, IV, V—like a recipe written by someone who has never once cooked for people they love. Still, I could not deny the usefulness. There is a particular satisfaction in hearing a latch described accurately, in hearing the time of a footstep placed against the clock’s pulse.

But something troubled me: his ear did not attend to the house’s own voice. He did not ask what the floorboards said, whether the stair creaked for one person and not another, whether a door’s groan had changed since the last winter’s damp.

A detective who ignores acoustic ecology misses half the witnesses.

When he finished, the dough had become smooth beneath my palms, elastic with air. I set it to rise under a cloth. Even in a tale of theft and fear, bread must be made. The house expects it. The body expects it. Memory expects it.

SEVENTH NARRATIVE, they wrote next, as if the story had marched forward rather than circled back.

And it did circle.

The Seventh returned to the old languages—the ones that sit under our official speech like roots under a formal garden. A servant girl, half-ashamed of her rural accent, confessed she’d heard words spoken in a tongue she could not repeat without being mocked. She described the way the syllables had fallen—soft, urgent, like water over stones. She could not translate them, not truly. She could only say what they did to her: how they made her skin prickle, how they made her feel both threatened and strangely comforted, as if the speaker carried home in his mouth and weaponry in his pocket.

This mattered. Not because mysterious tongues are romantic—people who sell “exotic” sounds in the marketplace are no better than those who sell watered wine—but because meaning is not portable. A threat in one dialect may be a prayer in another; a name may be a chain; a silence may be a vow.

As she spoke, the house around us carried on with its ordinary tasks, which is how myth survives: not in temples, but in kitchens, in sculleries, in the steady repetition that makes room for the impossible. The kettle sang. A spoon tapped the rim of a pot. A knife hit the board with a clean, reassuring crack. The mythic occurrence—the cold light’s shadow over our daily bread—did not stop anyone from salting the beans.

EIGHTH NARRATIVE followed, and with it the sensation that everything you have heard before is being re-seasoned and served back to you in a new dish.

In that telling, the threads from the previous voices were pulled tighter. The elder’s warning, the cook’s fire, the methodical man’s numbered facts, the servant girl’s untranslatable sounds—all were laid side by side, like ingredients that might finally explain one another if combined in the right proportion.

The speaker of the Eighth was not loud. Loudness belongs to markets and machines. He spoke with the controlled firmness of someone who knows that truth, like stock, must be reduced patiently.

He noted, without sentiment, that the household’s soundscape had changed over the years. More metal, less wood. More purchased tunes, fewer sung. The servants were quicker now, anxious under the pressure of timepieces and expectations imported from cities. And under that new noise, old warnings were harder to hear.

He did not say the modern world was evil. He said it was careless. Careless with sound. Careless with words. Careless with the way names are taken from one tongue and forced into another until the thing named becomes something else entirely.

Then he did what the best investigators do: he stopped talking and let the room speak.

In the hush that followed, I heard the most telling thing of all: the low, almost inaudible settling of the house, as if it were shifting its weight away from a secret it could no longer hold comfortably.

EPILOGUE, they finally called it.

But an epilogue is only an ending if you believe time is a straight road. In a kitchen—and in a family—it is a round table. We come back to the same seat, the same dish, the same arguments and affections, carrying different ages in our faces and different losses in our hands.

So the epilogue did not conclude with fireworks or proclamations. It offered instead a quiet accounting, like the last tasting before you send food out to guests.

I. What had been said could not be unsaid. Words—especially those spoken in a tongue that is dying—leave a residue, like garlic on a wooden board. You can scrub, but it remains.

II. What had been heard could not be unheard. The house remembered its own noises, even if people tried to drown them in new clamor.

III. What had been done had consequences that would not respect the neat borders between narratives. The cold light—call it what you will, in whatever language you dare—had entered ordinary life and would not leave it untouched.

And when the day’s work resumed—as it must—the ordinary rituals took on the weight of the myth that had been laid among them. The loaf went into the oven. The broth was reheated. Plates were warmed. The knives were sharpened with a sound like a promise.

Outside, a distant engine groaned, a harsh intrusion on the air. Inside, the kettle answered with its own steady song, and for a moment the household’s true music held.

In that holding—between the bought noise and the lived sound, between the official name and the untranslatable feeling—you could sense the real mystery beginning, not ending: how a family, a house, a language, and an object of cold light might all be stolen, and how, perhaps, they might be recovered.

I write these lines in India, and I direct them to my relatives in England, because silence has begun to taste sour in my mouth.

There are things that, when they are left untold, are seasoned by other people with whatever spice best suits their own palates—pity, pride, convenience. My refusal to take my cousin John Herncastle by the hand has been served up at home as stubbornness, as hauteur, as some private whim. I cannot afford, with those whose good opinion keeps a roof over the name we share, to let that false dish be carried from table to table.

Read what follows before you judge me. I pledge, on my honour, that it is strictly and literally true.

The breach between Herncastle and me began not in some drawing-room quarrel, but in a public calamity we passed through together—the storming of Seringapatam under General Baird, on the 4th of May, 1799. If you would understand why I now turn away from my cousin’s offered friendship as if it were meat handled by unclean hands, you must allow me to go back a little—to the days before the assault, and to the talk that simmered in camp about the Sultan’s hoarded treasure of gold and jewels shut up in the palace.

Among all those camp-stories, one, the wildest, persisted like a strong smell that clings to cloth after the fire has died: a tale of a yellow diamond famous in the native annals—a stone that did not merely glitter, but belonged, by belief older than our own records, to a god.

They said it had once been set in the forehead of the four-handed deity who typifies the Moon. The stone’s colour—like clarified butter held up to lamplight, like saffron seeped into warm water—had given it its name. There was more: a superstition, repeated with solemn mouths by men who otherwise scoffed at everything unseen, that the gem’s lustre waxed and waned with the moon itself, as if the stone were a living thing answering to its master in the sky. In India, they call it to this day the Moonstone.

I have heard, too, that old Greece and Rome had their own lunar superstition—not of a diamond consecrated to a god, but of an inferior, semi-transparent stone that collectors still label by the moon’s name. The human mind, it seems, will always try to give a voice to what has none, and to translate light into feeling. But in India the story had teeth.

The first clear adventure of this Yellow Diamond begins, as the natives recount it, in the eleventh century. Mahmoud of Ghizni crossed India; seized Somnauth; stripped the famous temple—shrine of Hindoo pilgrimage and wonder of the Eastern world—until the very air must have rung with the clatter of torn metal and the cries of the dispossessed. Of all the deities worshipped there, the moon-god alone escaped the rapacity of the Mohammedans. Three Brahmins, keeping their wits when others lost their lives, preserved the god and the gem. By night they removed the idol, the yellow diamond still bright on its brow, and carried it in secret to Benares.

There, so the tradition runs, they set it up in a new shrine: a hall inlaid with precious stones, under a roof held by pillars of gold. On the night that shrine was completed, Vishnu the Preserver appeared to the three Brahmins in a dream. He breathed—so they said—the breath of his divinity upon the diamond in the forehead of the moon-god, and the priests knelt and hid their faces in their robes. Vishnu commanded that the Moonstone should be watched from that time forth by three priests in turn, night and day, to the end of the generations of men.

More dreadful than the command was the warning that came with it: disaster—certain disaster—to the mortal who laid hands on the sacred gem, and to all of his house and name who received it after him. The priests caused the prophecy to be written over the gates of the shrine, in letters of gold: a sentence set up like a brand, meant to burn the eye.

Ages passed, and the watch continued. Generation after generation, successors of the three Brahmins kept their vigil—by day under heat that makes the skin smart, by night when the insects’ chorus turns the air into a vibrating veil. Then came Aurungzebe, Mogul Emperor, in the early years of the eighteenth century. Again havoc and rapine were let loose among the temples of Brahmah’s worship. Sacred animals were slaughtered; images broken; the shrine polluted. The Moonstone was seized by an officer of rank in Aurungzebe’s army.

The three guardian priests were powerless to recover their treasure by open force; they followed it instead. They watched it in disguise, and so, through the turns of time, the stone passed—carrying its curse, if curse there was—from one lawless Mohammedan hand to another. The sacrilegious warrior perished miserably; the gem changed masters; and still the successors of the three priests kept their silent, patient watch, waiting for the will of Vishnu to restore what had been taken.

So time rolled on to our own century. At last the diamond fell into the possession of Tippoo, Sultan of Seringapatam. He caused it to be set as an ornament in the handle of a dagger, and kept it among the choicest treasures of his armoury. Even in Tippoo’s own palace, they said, the three priests remained near, hidden in plain sight. There were three officers of the Sultan’s household, strangers to the rest, who had won confidence by conforming—or appearing to conform—to the Mussulman faith. Camp gossip, eager for a pattern, pointed to these three as the priests in disguise.

Such was the tale, told and retold. It ran through our tents as naturally as talk of rations. Between coarse bread and salted meat, men spoke of a jewel that answered to the moon; between the scrape of bayonets on stone and the groan of wheels, they spoke of guardians who never slept. The camp itself had its own music then—the crackle of cooking fires, the slap of water in buckets, the little domestic clinks and murmurs that try to make a life out of a temporary place; and over it all, the steady background of war: distant drums, shouted orders, the metallic rasp of arms. It was a soundscape that left no mind untouched. Some grew calm under it, as if the noise filled the hollow places. Others—my cousin among them—seemed only more fevered, as if every clang of metal struck on a nerve already raw.

The story made no serious impression on any of us—except Herncastle. His love of the marvellous, always his weakness, induced him to believe it. On the night before the assault he grew absurdly angry with me, and with others, because we treated the whole thing as a fable. There was a wrangle—foolish in itself, and more foolish for the hour at which it took place. Herncastle’s temper, never a small thing, got the better of him.

In his boastful way, he declared that we should see the diamond on his finger if the English army took Seringapatam.

The sally was met with a roar of laughter. Men, busy sharpening steel and folding blankets and making what small arrangements they could against death, found relief in the joke. “Who’s got the Moonstone?” became a catchword before the night was out, tossed about with the same careless air as a soldier’s jest at his own hunger. And there, as we all supposed, the matter ended.

It did not.

The next day came—the day of the assault. Herncastle and I were separated at the outset. I did not see him when we forded the river; when we planted the English flag in the first breach; when we crossed the ditch beyond; when, fighting every inch, we entered the town. The world contracted to shouting men and smoke and stone. I remember sound as much as sight: the strange hollow boom when cannon speaks close by; the wet slap of boots in mud; the high, thin screaming of someone who could no longer keep terror inside his chest. Even now, when I try to recall faces, it is often a noise that returns first, and with it the feeling—the way the ear becomes a gate through which panic walks straight into the mind.

It was only at dusk, when the place was ours, after General Baird himself had found the dead body of Tippoo under a heap of the slain, that my cousin and I met again. We were each attached to a party sent out by the General’s orders to prevent the plunder and confusion that followed our conquest.

And plunder there was—deplorable excesses committed by camp-followers; and worse, the soldiers finding their way through an unguarded door into the palace treasury, loading themselves with gold and jewels as if the bright things could pay for what their eyes had seen that day. The air in the courtyards had a sickly mixture in it—smoke and blood and trampled fruit from broken stalls, and the stale sweetness of spilled spirits. On such a night, even a clean loaf would have tasted of horror.

It was in the court outside the treasury that Herncastle and I came together, both trying to enforce discipline upon our own men. His fiery temper, I could plainly see, had been exasperated to something like frenzy by the slaughter through which we had passed. He was, in my opinion, very unfit to perform the duty entrusted to him.

There was riot enough, but no violence that I saw in the treasury at first. The men disgraced themselves good-humouredly. They shouted rough jests, thrust jewels into their mouths to bite and test, stuck chains about their necks like children dressing in their mother’s finery. The catchword about the diamond sprang up again, wickedly apt to the hour: “Who’s got the Moonstone?” It became a rallying cry. The moment plundering was checked in one place, it broke out in another, drawn by that phrase as if by a bell.

While I was still vainly trying to establish order, a frightful yelling rose on the far side of the courtyard—different from the rowdy laughter, sharper, torn with fear. I ran at once toward it, dreading some new outbreak of pillage and violence. At an open door I stopped short.

Two Indian bodies lay across the entrance, dead—by their dress, officers of the palace. The sight was like a sudden silence in music: it cut through everything else.

Another cry from within hurried me into a room that appeared to serve as an armoury. There, a third Indian, mortally wounded, was sinking at the feet of a man whose back was toward me.

He turned at the instant I came in.

It was John Herncastle.

He held a torch in one hand and a dagger in the other—its blade wet, dripping. In the end of the dagger’s handle a stone was set like a pommel; and as he moved, it flashed in the torchlight like a little captive flame. Yellow—unmistakably yellow.

The dying Indian sank to his knees. He lifted an arm that could hardly keep its own weight, pointed to the dagger in Herncastle’s hand, and spoke in his native language words I understood well enough from their tone, even before sense caught up with sound:

“The Moonstone will have its vengeance yet on you and yours!”

He said those words, and fell dead upon the floor.

For a moment I stood as if struck deaf. Not by the prophecy—though the old camp-tale came back with a chill in it—but by the simplicity of the scene: three men dead or dying, and my cousin in the middle of it with that torch, that dagger, and the yellow fire at the hilt. Myth had stepped out of story and into a room any man could enter by an open door.

Before I could move—before I could speak—the men who had followed me across the courtyard crowded in.

Herncastle rushed at them like a madman. “Clear the room!” he shouted to me. “And set a guard on the door!”

The men fell back as he threw himself on them, brandishing torch and dagger. The torchlight flared, throwing hard shadows on the walls hung with arms. I did what immediate duty required. I put two sentinels of my own company, men I could rely on, to keep the door.

Through the remainder of the night I saw no more of my cousin.

Early in the morning, the plunder still going on, General Baird made proclamation by beat of drum that any thief detected in the fact—be he whom he might—should be hung. The provost-marshal attended, to show that the General was in earnest. The drum’s harsh, regular thud drove itself into the skull; it was an ugly sound, but a necessary one—an attempt, by measured noise, to impose law on chaos.

In the throng that followed the proclamation, Herncastle and I met again. He held out his hand to me, as usual, and said, “Good morning.”

I waited before I gave him mine.

“Tell me first,” I said, “how the Indian in the armoury met his death—and what those last words meant, when he pointed to the dagger in your hand.”

“The Indian met his death, as I suppose, by a mortal wound,” said Herncastle.

“What his last words meant I know no more than you do.”

I looked at him narrowly. The frenzy of the previous day had calmed. He stood collected, even indifferent, as if the night had been no more than a change of weather.

I determined to give him another chance.

“Is that all you have to tell me?” I asked.

“That is all,” he answered.

I turned my back on him; and we have not spoken since.

Let it be understood that what I write here about my cousin—unless necessity should arise for making it public—is for the family only. Herncastle has said nothing that can justify me in speaking to our commanding officer. He has been taunted more than once about the diamond by those who remember his angry outbreak on the night before the assault; but his own remembrance of the circumstances under which I surprised him in the armoury has been enough, I think, to keep him silent.

It is reported that he means to exchange into another regiment, avowedly to separate himself from me. Whether that is true or not, I cannot prevail upon myself to become his accuser; and I think with good reason.

If I made the matter public, I have no evidence but moral evidence. I have no proof he killed the two men at the door. I cannot even declare that he killed the third man inside—for I cannot say my own eyes saw the deed committed. It is true that I heard the dying Indian’s words; but if those words were pronounced the ravings of delirium, how could I contradict the assertion from my own knowledge? How does one translate the last taste of fear on another man’s tongue into a language a court will accept?

Let our relatives on either side form their own opinion on what I have written, and decide whether the aversion I now feel toward this man is well or ill founded.

Although I attach no sort of credit to the fantastic Indian legend of the gem, I must acknowledge before I conclude that I am influenced by a superstition of my own. It is my conviction—call it conviction or delusion as you please—that crime brings its own fatality with it. I am not only persuaded of Herncastle’s guilt; I am even fanciful enough to believe he will live to regret it if he keeps the diamond—and that others will live to regret taking it from him, if he gives the diamond away.

In the first part of Robinson Crusoe—page one hundred and twenty-nine, in my own stout copy, the one that opens as obediently as an oven-door to the place it knows by heart—you will find these words set down:

“Now I saw, though too late, the Folly of beginning a Work before we count the Cost, and before we judge rightly of our own Strength to go through with it.”

Only yesterday, by nothing more mysterious than a thumb that knows the grain of paper and the weight of habit, I fell on that passage. Only this morning—May the twenty-first, eighteen hundred and fifty—my lady’s nephew, Mr. Franklin Blake, came to me and spoke of a matter that has lain in our house like a taint you can’t scrub out of the boards, however often you scald and sand.

We were within doors; and the house had its usual honest sounds about it—the soft argument of the clock, the distant, steady breathing of servants at work, a kettle somewhere beginning its first thin singing (that good domestic note which promises warmth and steadiness), and outside, beyond the glass, the country making its own respectable music. There’s comfort in such a soundscape. It tells you where you are. It gives the mind its right measure. A man can think straight with a kettle in tune and a garden not yet invaded by the modern clatter that turns every ear into a sore place.

“Betteredge,” says Mr. Franklin, “I have been to the lawyer’s about some family matters; and, among other things, we have been talking of the loss of the Indian Diamond, in my aunt’s house in Yorkshire, two years since. Mr. Bruff thinks as I think, that the whole story ought, in the interests of truth, to be placed on record in writing—and the sooner the better.”

Not perceiving his drift yet—and being, for the sake of peace and quietness, always of opinion that it is generally wholesome to agree with the lawyer where you can—I said I thought so too.

Mr. Franklin went on, and there was that in his voice which had the ring of a spoon struck against a glass: not loud, but clean and resolute.

“In this matter of the Diamond,” he said, “the characters of innocent people have suffered under suspicion already—as you know. The memories of innocent people may suffer, hereafter, for want of a record of the facts to which those who come after us can appeal. There can be no doubt that this strange family story of ours ought to be told. And I think, Betteredge, Mr. Bruff and I together have hit on the right way of telling it.”

Very satisfactory to both of them, no doubt. But I failed to see what I myself had to do with it, so far—beyond listening, as a man listens in the pantry when the upper rooms begin discussing matters that drift down through the ceilings and settle, like flour-dust, on every household task.

“We have certain events to relate,” Mr. Franklin proceeded; “and we have certain persons concerned in those events who are capable of relating them. Starting from these plain facts, the idea is that we should all write the story of the Moonstone in turn—as far as our own personal experience extends, and no farther. We must begin by showing how the Diamond first fell into the hands of my uncle Herncastle, when he was serving in India fifty years since. This prefatory narrative I have already got by me in the form of an old family paper, which relates the necessary particulars on the authority of an eye-witness. The next thing to do is to tell how the Diamond found its way into my aunt’s house in Yorkshire, two years ago, and how it came to be lost in little more than twelve hours afterwards. Nobody knows as much as you do, Betteredge, about what went on in the house at that time. So you must take the pen in hand, and start the story.”

There it was, put into my keeping like a dish set down sharp and steaming before a man who has never been asked to carve: the responsibility of it warming your fingers whether you want it or not.

If you are curious to know what course I took under the circumstances, I beg to inform you that I did what you would probably have done in my place. I modestly declared myself to be quite unequal to the task imposed upon me—and I privately felt, all the time, that I was quite clever enough to perform it, if I only gave my own abilities a fair chance.

Mr. Franklin, I imagine, must have seen my private sentiments in my face. He declined to believe in my modesty; and he insisted on giving my abilities a fair chance.

Two hours have passed since Mr. Franklin left me. As soon as his back was turned, I went to my writing-desk to start the story. There I have sat helpless (in spite of my abilities) ever since; with the ink waiting like gravy that will skin over if you let it, with the paper staring white and blank as a plate brought back untouched, and with that sentence from Crusoe tasting in my mouth again—namely, the folly of beginning a work before we count the cost, and before we judge rightly of our own strength to go through with it.

Please to remember, I opened the book by accident, at that bit, only the day before I rashly undertook the business now in hand; and allow me to ask—if that isn’t prophecy, what is?

I am not superstitious. I have read a heap of books in my time. I am a scholar in my own way. Though turned seventy, I possess an active memory, and legs to correspond. And yet there are days when a house, a man, and a book seem to speak the same dialect together—one that isn’t written down anywhere, and can’t be translated into the fine phrases people use when they mean to sell you something. It is the old tongue of common experience, where the meaning lies as much in the tone as in the words: in the creak of a stair at the moment you don’t want it, in the hush before bad news, in the sudden wrongness of a familiar room.

You are not to take it, if you please, as the saying of an ignorant man, when I express my opinion that such a book as Robinson Crusoe never was written, and never will be written again. I have tried that book for years—generally in combination with a pipe of tobacco—and I have found it my friend in need in all the necessities of this mortal life. When my spirits are bad—Robinson Crusoe. When I want advice—Robinson Crusoe. In past times when my wife plagued me; in present times when I have had a drop too much—Robinson Crusoe.

I have worn out six stout Robinson Crusoes with hard work in my service. On my lady’s last birthday she gave me a seventh. I took a drop too much on the strength of it; and Robinson Crusoe put me right again. Price four shillings and sixpence, bound in blue, with a picture into the bargain. The very feel of it is familiar—like a well-seasoned pan that has done its duty and asks no praise. Open it, and there’s the smell of paper warmed by the hand and time, and the quiet, steady voice of print that doesn’t shout at you the way the new noises do. No grinding of wheels, no thin metallic racket forcing its way into your head; only the old, human rustle as a page turns, and the sense of company in black letters.

Still, this don’t look much like starting the story of the Diamond—does it? I seem to be wandering off in search of Lord knows what, Lord knows where.

We will take a new sheet of paper, if you please, and begin over again, with my best respects to you.

I mentioned my lady a little while back. And if the Moonstone—God save us from the troubles it brought—was ever to lie under our roof long enough to be missed there, it must first have been laid, like a tempting sweetmeat on a plate, in my lady’s daughter’s hands. And that daughter, of course, could never have drawn breath to receive any gift at all, if my lady had not first borne her into the world—painfully, as women do, and patiently, as only they can. So if you want a beginning that reaches far enough back to hold the whole mischief in it, you begin with my lady. It’s a comfort, when you’ve a knot to untie, to know you’ve taken hold of the right end of the string.

If you know the fashionable world—even by hearsay—you’ve heard of the three Miss Herncastles: Miss Adelaide, Miss Caroline, and Miss Julia; Miss Julia the youngest, and, to my mind, the best of the three. I had my opportunities of judging; you’ll see how, directly.

I went into the old lord’s service as page-boy—fifteen years old and as sharp-set as a farm pup—waiting on the three honourable young ladies. Thank God, in this business of the Diamond we needn’t trouble ourselves with the old lord. He had the longest tongue and the shortest temper of any man, gentle or simple, that ever made the air ring sour with his own voice. There are noises that soothe you—wind in elms, a kettle beginning to sing, a cart creaking honest on a country lane; and there are noises that set your teeth on edge and turn peace out of doors. The old lord’s talk was the second sort—forever clattering over other people, never leaving room for the quiet that keeps a household sweet.

I stayed in that service till Miss Julia married Sir John Verinder. An excellent man—only wanting somebody to manage him; and, between ourselves, he found somebody to do it. He throve on being managed, as a good joint thrives under a steady hand—no scorching, no hurry, just the right turning at the right time. He grew fat and comfortable, lived happy, and died easy; dating, as I say, from the day my lady took him to church to be married, to the day she took his last breath away from him as gently as she had taken his arm, and closed his eyes for ever.

I went with the bride down here to the husband’s house and lands.

“Sir John,” says my lady, “I can’t do without Gabriel Betteredge.”

“My lady,” says Sir John, “I can’t do without him, either.”

That was his way with her; and that was how I came into his service. It was all one to me where I went, so long as my mistress and I went together.

Now, my lady took an interest in the out-of-door work, the farms, and the people on the estate; and I took an interest too—partly because she set the tune and we all kept it, and partly because I was a small farmer’s seventh son myself, and the smell of turned earth and stored apples was in my blood. There’s a sound to good husbandry as well as a smell: the measured chop of a cleaver in a dairy yard, the soft rush of grain poured into a bin, the lowing at milking-time that comes like a hymn through the dusk. Those are the noises that tell you a place is alive and rightly ordered.

My lady got me put under the bailiff. I did my best, gave satisfaction, and got promotion, as you may say, by plain work. Some years later—on the Monday as it might be—my lady says: “Sir John, your bailiff is a stupid old man. Pension him liberally, and let Gabriel Betteredge have his place.”

On the Tuesday as it might be, Sir John says: “My lady, the bailiff is pensioned liberally; and Gabriel Betteredge has got his place.”

You hear enough and more than enough of married people living together miserably. Here’s an example the other way. Let it warn some of you and encourage others. In the meantime, I go on.

So there I was, you’ll say, in clover: a position of trust, a little cottage of my own, rounds on the estate in the morning, accounts in the afternoon; and in the evening my pipe and my _Robinson Crusoe_. What more could I want? Well—remember what Adam wanted when he was alone in Eden; and if you don’t blame it in Adam, don’t blame it in me.

The woman I fixed my eye on kept house for me at my cottage. Her name was Selina Goby.

I agree with the late William Cobbett about picking a wife: see that she chews her food well and sets her foot down firmly when she walks, and you’re safe enough. Selina was all right in both respects. You could hear, with comfort, the steady way she went about the pantry; not a rattling, fussy creature, slamming lids and banging pails as if noise was the same thing as work, but the sort that knows how to lay a knife down quiet and keep a fire up without making a show of it. She had a hand with broth—clear, strong, and honest, with the scum lifted as neatly as a lie taken off a story. She knew where things came from, too: which farmer’s butter would sweeten best, which orchard’s apples held their shape in a tart, which corner of the garden gave you thyme with a bite to it. A household lives by such knowledge; and when it goes, it goes like a dialect—first the old words are laughed at, then they’re forgotten, and then you find you’ve lost a way of tasting the world you can never get back.

But I had another reason for marrying Selina—entirely of my own discovering. Selina, being a single woman, made me pay her so much a week for board and services. Selina, being my wife, couldn’t charge for her board, and would have to give me her services for nothing. That was the point of view I looked at it from. Economy—with a dash of love.

I put it to my mistress, as in duty bound, just as I had put it to myself.

“I have been turning Selina Goby over in my mind,” I said, “and I think, my lady, it will be cheaper to marry her than to keep her.”

My lady burst out laughing, and said she didn’t know which to be most shocked at—my language or my principles. Some joke tickled her, I suppose, of the sort you can’t take unless you’re quality. I understood nothing, except that I was free to put it next to Selina; and I went and put it accordingly.

And what did Selina say? Lord! how little you must know of women, if you ask that. Of course she said yes.

As the time drew nearer—and there got to be talk of my having a new coat for the ceremony—my mind began to misgive me. I’ve compared notes with other men, and they all own that about a week beforehand they privately wish themselves out of it. I went a trifle further; I actually rose up, as it were, and tried to get out of it.

Not for nothing! I was too just a man to expect she’d let me off for nothing. Compensation to the woman when the man backs out is one of the laws of England. In obedience to the laws—and after turning it over careful in my mind—I offered Selina Goby a feather-bed and fifty shillings to be off the bargain.

You will hardly believe it, but it’s true: she was fool enough to refuse.

After that it was all over with me. I got the new coat as cheap as I could, and I went through the rest of it as cheap as I could.

We were not a happy couple, and not a miserable couple. We were six of one and half-a-dozen of the other. How it was, I don’t understand; but we always seemed, with the best motives, to be getting in each other’s way. When I wanted to go upstairs, there was my wife coming down; when my wife wanted to go down, there was I coming up. That is married life, according to my experience: two people trying to keep house in the same space, like two cooks reaching for the same salt at the same moment—no harm meant, but elbows forever meeting.

After five years of misunderstandings on the stairs, it pleased an all-wise Providence to relieve us of each other by taking my wife. I was left with my little girl Penelope, and with no other child.

Shortly afterwards Sir John died, and my lady was left with her little girl, Miss Rachel, and with no other child.

If I have written to any purpose of my lady, you won’t need to be told that Penelope was taken care of under my good mistress’s own eye. She was sent to school, taught, made sharp, and promoted, when old enough, to be Miss Rachel’s own maid. In those days the house had its own proper sounds: the swish of skirts on the stair, the soft clink of china at tea, the low voices that belonged to family life. Not like the new times, when people think a place must be lively if it is loud, and buy noise the way they buy pictures—because the shop tells them it is art. Give me the old quiet that lets you hear a spoon stir and know, by the very note of it, whether the sugar is fine or coarse.

As for me, I went on year after year as bailiff, up to Christmas 1847, when there came a change in my life.

On that day my lady invited herself to a cup of tea—alone with me—in my cottage. She remarked that, reckoning from the year when I started as page-boy in the old lord’s time, I had been more than fifty years in her service; and she put into my hands a beautiful waistcoat of wool that she had worked herself, to keep me warm in the bitter winter weather.

There are gifts that sit on you like a good supper—warming, steadying, making you ashamed of all the sharp words you ever spoke. I took that waistcoat, and for once in my life I was at a loss for the right thing to say.

But, to my great astonishment, it turned out the waistcoat was not an honour; it was a bribe.

My lady had discovered I was getting old before I had discovered it myself; and she had come to my cottage to wheedle me—if I may use such an expression—into giving up my hard out-of-door work as bailiff and taking my ease for the rest of my days as steward in the house.

I made as good a fight against the indignity of taking my ease as I could. But my mistress knew the weak side of me: she put it as a favour to herself. The dispute ended with me wiping my eyes, like an old fool, with my new woollen waistcoat, and saying I would think about it.

The perturbation in my mind, in regard to thinking about it, being truly dreadful after my lady had gone away, I applied the remedy which I have never yet found to fail in doubt and emergency. I smoked a pipe and took a turn at _Robinson Crusoe_.

Before I had been five minutes with that extraordinary book, I came on a comforting bit (page one hundred and fifty-eight), as follows: “Today we love, what tomorrow we hate.”

I saw my way clear directly. Today I was all for continuing to be farm-bailiff; tomorrow, on the authority of _Robinson Crusoe_, I should be all the other way. Take myself tomorrow, while in tomorrow’s humour, and the thing was done.

My mind relieved in this manner, I went to sleep that night in the character of Lady Verinder’s farm-bailiff, and I woke up the next morning in the character of Lady Verinder’s house-steward. All quite comfortable—and all through _Robinson Crusoe_!

My daughter Penelope has just looked over my shoulder to see what I have done so far. She remarks it is beautifully written, and every word of it true. But she points out one objection. She says what I have done so far isn’t in the least what I was wanted to do. I am asked to tell the story of the Diamond—and instead of that I have been telling the story of my own self.

Curious, and quite beyond me to account for. I wonder whether the gentlemen who make a business and a living out of writing books ever find their own selves getting in the way of their subjects, like me? If they do, I can feel for them.

In the meantime, here is another false start and more waste of good writing-paper. What’s to be done now? Nothing that I know of—except for you to keep your temper, and for me to begin it all over again for the third time.

How to begin a story decently is a question that can sour a man’s mouth like over-steeped tea. I tried first the old method—scratching my head until the skin warmed under my nails. Nothing came of it. Then I tried a better household remedy: I consulted my daughter Penelope, who keeps her thoughts as carefully as she keeps the linen—folded, aired, and locked away from meddling fingers.

Penelope’s plan was plain, orderly, and (I must admit) deliciously practical. “Set it down day by day,” says she, “beginning with the day we heard Mr. Franklin Blake was coming.” Dates, she added, would pin my wandering memory to the page the way a clove pins itself in a baked apple: once the spice is set, the fragrance rises of its own accord.

The trouble was fetching the dates. Penelope offered to find them in her diary—the little book she learnt to keep at school, and has continued ever since, as regular as salting the ham at the right season. I made an improvement of my own—why shouldn’t she tell the story straight out of that diary? At this she coloured up as if I had accused her of watering the broth, and looked fierce enough to curdle milk.

“My journal is for my own private eye,” says she; “and no living creature shall ever know what is in it but myself.”

“What does that mean?” says I.

“Fiddlesticks!” says Penelope.

“Sweethearts,” says I—because when a man has lived long with women, he learns that one soft word, properly served, will often cool what might otherwise boil over.

So: Penelope’s plan it shall be. And here is the first day, properly labelled, like a jar in the still-room.

On Wednesday morning, the twenty-fourth of May, eighteen hundred and forty-eight, I was specially called into my lady’s own sitting-room. The room had the settled quiet of a house that knows its own dignity—the curtains drawn to the right measure, the air faint with lavender, and (underneath) that clean, cool scent of beeswax that always makes me think of good order and good bread.

“Gabriel,” says my lady, “here is news that will surprise you. Franklin Blake has come back from abroad. He has been staying with his father in London, and he is coming to us tomorrow to stop till next month, and keep Rachel’s birthday.”

If I had had my hat in my hand, nothing but respect would have prevented me from tossing it up to the ceiling. I had not seen Mr. Franklin since he was a boy in this house; and, out of all sight, he was—so I remembered him—the nicest boy that ever spun a top or broke a window.

Miss Rachel was present, and when I said as much, she turned those steady eyes of hers on me and answered as cool as fresh cream:

She remembered him, she said, as the most atrocious tyrant that ever tortured a doll, and the hardest driver of an exhausted little girl in string harness that England could produce. “I burn with indignation, and I ache with fatigue,” was the way she summed it up, “when I think of Franklin Blake.”

You will naturally ask, hearing this, how it happened that Mr. Franklin passed all the years from boy to man out of his own country. The answer is as simple—and as sour—as a lawsuit that has sat too long. His father had the misfortune to be next heir to a Dukedom and not to be able to prove it.

My lady’s eldest sister married the celebrated Mr. Blake—famous alike for his great riches and his great suit at law. How long he worried the tribunals to turn out the Duke in possession and put himself in the Duke’s place; how many lawyers’ purses he filled till they burst; how many harmless people he set by the ears disputing whether he was right or wrong—these are more years and more quarrels than I can count. His wife died, and two of his three children died, before the tribunals could make up their minds to show him the door and take no more of his money. When it was all over, and the Duke in possession was left in possession, Mr. Blake discovered the only way of being even with his country was not to let his country have the honour of educating his son.

“How can I trust my native institutions,” says he, “after the way in which my native institutions have behaved to me?”

Add to this that Mr. Blake disliked all boys, his own included, and you will admit it could only end one way. Master Franklin was taken from us and sent to institutions his father could trust in that superior country, Germany—Mr. Blake himself staying snug in England to improve his fellow-countrymen in Parliament, and to publish a statement on the subject of the Duke in possession, which has remained unfinished from that day to this.

There. Thank God, that’s told. Neither you nor I need trouble our heads any more about Mr. Blake senior. Leave him to his Dukedom; let us stick to the Diamond.

For the Diamond takes us back to Mr. Franklin, who was the innocent means of bringing that unlucky jewel into the house.

Our nice boy did not forget us when he went abroad. He wrote now and then—sometimes to my lady, sometimes to Miss Rachel, and sometimes to me. We had had a little transaction together before he left: he borrowed from me a ball of string, a four-bladed knife, and seven-and-sixpence in money—the colour of which last I have not seen, and never expect to see again. His letters to me chiefly related to borrowing more.

From my lady I heard how he got on as he grew in years and stature. After Germany had taught him what Germany could, he gave the French a turn, and the Italians after that. They made him among them—so far as I could understand—a sort of universal genius. He wrote a little; painted a little; sang and played and composed a little—borrowing, I suspect, in all these cases just as he had borrowed from me. His mother’s fortune—seven hundred a year—fell to him when he came of age, and ran through him as if through a sieve. The more he had, the more he wanted; there was a hole in Mr. Franklin’s pocket that nothing would sew up.

Wherever he went, his lively, easy way made him welcome. He lived here, there, and everywhere; his address (as he used to put it) being “Post Office, Europe—to be left till called for.” Twice he made up his mind to come back to England and see us; twice (saving your presence) some unmentionable woman stood in the way and stopped him. The third attempt succeeded; and on Thursday, the twenty-fifth of May, we were to see what our nice boy had grown to be as a man—good blood, high courage, and five-and-twenty by our reckoning.

Now you know as much of Mr. Franklin Blake as I did—before Mr. Franklin Blake came down to our house.

Thursday was as fine a summer’s day as ever you saw: sunlight like warmed honey on the stone, and the air full of those small countryside sounds that keep a house from feeling lonely—the far-off bleat of sheep, the soft scissor of leaves, a cart somewhere singing on its axles as it went. My lady and Miss Rachel, not expecting Mr. Franklin till dinner-time, drove out to lunch with friends in the neighbourhood.

When they were gone, I went and had a look at the bedroom prepared for our guest, and saw that all was straight—the sheets turned down like a clean page, the water laid ready, the fire-place blacked to a proper sober shine. Then, being butler as well as steward in my lady’s establishment (at my own particular request, mind, because it vexed me to see anybody but myself in possession of the key of the late Sir John’s cellar), I fetched up some of our famous Latour claret and set it in the warm summer air to take off the chill before dinner.

There is an art to wine, as there is to soup: treat it harshly, serve it cold from pride or ignorance, and you kill what time and care have put into it. So I set the bottle where the sun could coax it, not scorch it. Concluding to set myself in the warm summer air next—seeing what is good for old claret is equally good for old age—I took up my beehive chair to go out into the back court.

I had barely got it settled when I heard a sound like the soft beating of a drum from the terrace in front of the house. It was not the friendly country percussion—the thud of a mallet, the rhythm of a pail—no; it was a deliberate tap-tap, as if somebody were trying to write a message on the air.

I went round to the terrace and found three mahogany-coloured Indians in white linen frocks and trousers looking up at the house. Each had a small hand-drum slung in front of him. Behind them stood a little delicate-looking light-haired English boy with a bag in his hand.

I judged them strolling conjurors; the boy with the bag carried the tools. One of the three—who spoke English, and had manners so smooth they might have been polished like silver—presently told me my judgment was right. He requested permission to show his tricks in the presence of the lady of the house.

Now I am not a sour old man. I am generally for amusement, and I am the last person in the world to distrust another because he is a few shades darker than myself. But the best of us have our weaknesses—and my weakness, when I know the family plate-basket is out on a pantry-table, is to be instantly reminded of that basket by the sight of a strolling stranger whose manners are superior to my own. Besides, there was something in their drumming that sat wrong in the day’s clean country quiet; it was like a foreign tongue spoken too loudly in a small room, drowning the familiar speech of the house.

So I told the Indian the lady of the house was out, and I warned him and his party off the premises. He made me a beautiful bow and went off with his companions.

I returned to my beehive chair, chose the sunny side of the court, and fell—if truth must be owned—not exactly into sleep, but into the next best thing to it, when the mind goes soft and the world’s noises arrange themselves into something like music.

I was roused by my daughter Penelope running out at me as if the house was on fire.

What do you think she wanted? She wanted the three Indian jugglers instantly taken up; because they knew who was coming from London to visit us, and meant mischief to Mr. Franklin Blake.

Mr. Franklin’s name woke me to the bone. I opened my eyes and made Penelope explain herself.

It appeared she had just come from our lodge, where she had been gossiping with the lodge-keeper’s daughter. The two girls saw the Indians pass out after I had warned them off, followed by their little boy. The girls took it into their heads the boy was ill-used—no reason that I could discover except that he was pretty and delicate-looking. So they stole along the inner side of the hedge between us and the road and watched the foreigners on the outer side.

And what they saw—if you take my word—was stranger than any kitchen tale ever told over a winter fire.

The Indians first looked up the road and down the road to make sure they were alone. Then they faced about and stared hard in the direction of our house. Then they jabbered and disputed in their own language, casting looks at one another like men at a crossroad uncertain which way leads home. Then they all turned to their little English boy as if they expected him to help them.

Then the chief Indian—the one with the English and the elegant manners—said to the boy, “Hold out your hand.”

At hearing those words, Penelope declared she did not know what prevented her heart from flying straight out of her. I thought privately it might have been her stays. What I said was, “You make my flesh creep.” (Women like these little compliments.)

The boy shrank back, shook his head, and said he didn’t like it. The Indian asked him—not unkindly—whether he would like to be sent back to London and left where they had found him: sleeping in an empty basket in a market, hungry, ragged, and forsaken. That ended it. The little chap unwillingly held out his hand.

The Indian took a bottle from his bosom and poured out some black stuff, like ink, into the hollow of the boy’s palm. He touched the boy’s head, made signs over it in the air, and said, “Look.”

The boy became quite stiff, like a statue, staring into the ink. So far it seemed to me juggling—and a foolish waste of ink, at that. I was beginning to feel the sun tug me back towards drowsiness, when Penelope’s next words stirred me up.

The Indians looked up the road and down the road once more. Then the chief said to the boy, “See the English gentleman from foreign parts.”

The boy answered, “I see him.”

The Indian asked, “Is it on the road to this house, and on no other, that the English gentleman will travel today?”

The boy said, “It is on the road to this house, and on no other, that the English gentleman will travel today.”

The Indian waited a little, as if listening for something beyond human hearing—some change in the air, some shift in the day’s soundscape that would tell him when to strike the next note. Then he asked, “Has the English gentleman got It about him?”

The boy waited too, and then said, “Yes.”

The Indian put a third and last question: “Will the English gentleman come here, as he has promised to come, at the close of day?”

The boy said, “I can’t tell.”

The Indian asked why.

“I am tired,” the boy answered. “The mist rises in my head, and puzzles me. I can see no more today.”

With that the catechism ended. The chief Indian said something in his own language to the other two, pointing to the boy and pointing towards the town where, as we afterwards discovered, they were lodged. Then, after making more signs on the boy’s head, he blew on the boy’s forehead and woke him with a start. After that they all went on their way towards the town, and the girls saw them no more.

Most things have a moral, if you look for it—like a stew that tells you, by its taste, what hand has been in the pot. I found mine at once.

First: the chief juggler had heard Mr. Franklin’s arrival talked of among the servants out-of-doors, and saw his way to making a little money by it.

Second: he and his men and boy meant to hang about till they saw my lady drive home, then come back and foretell Mr. Franklin’s arrival by magic.

Third: Penelope had heard them rehearsing their hocus-pocus, like actors rehearsing a play.

Fourth: I should do well to keep an eye that evening on the plate-basket.

Fifth: Penelope would do well to cool down and leave her father to doze again in the sun.

That was the sensible view.

If you know anything of young women, you won’t be surprised to hear Penelope wouldn’t take it. The moral, according to my daughter, was serious. She fastened particularly on the Indian’s third question.

“Oh, father!” says Penelope, clasping her hands, “don’t joke about this. What does ‘It’ mean?”

“We’ll ask Mr. Franklin, my dear,” says I, “if you can wait till Mr. Franklin comes.” I winked to show I meant it in joke.

Penelope took it as solemn as a prayer. Her earnestness tickled me.

“What on earth should Mr. Franklin know about it?” I asked.

“Ask him,” says Penelope. “And see whether he thinks it a laughing matter, too.”

With that parting shot she left me.

When she was gone, I settled with myself that I really would ask Mr. Franklin—mainly to set Penelope’s mind at rest. What passed between us when I did ask him later that same day will be found set out fully in its proper place. But I warn you here, before we go further: you will not find the ghost of a joke in our conversation about the jugglers.

To my great surprise, Mr. Franklin—like Penelope—took the thing seriously. How seriously you will understand when I tell you that, in his opinion, “It” meant the Moonstone.

I am truly sorry to keep you hanging about with me and my old beehive chair, in the sun at the back of the house. A man of my years, half-asleep, is not much to look at—least of all to anyone impatient for Mr. Franklin Blake. But if you want the truth fit to stand up in court, you must take it as it came: in its own order, with its own interruptions, as obstinate as a fly in the pantry when the sugar’s out.

Penelope had scarcely left me, after her message, when the house gave the signal that sets every servant’s stomach to marching. From the servants’ hall came the bright racket of plates, the clack of knives against trenchers, the little shriek of a teapot lid—music to those who dine at the proper hour, and torture to those kept waiting. I took my meals in my own room, as was my right, and had nothing to do with the servants’ dinner beyond wishing them a good appetite—then easing myself back to my chair, thinking to doze again.

I had just got my legs stretched out, with my stick handy, when out bounced another woman on me.

Not Penelope this time—Nancy, the kitchen-maid. A nice plump young lass, and generally as hearty as the gravy that runs clear when the joint’s properly rested. But now she had a face on her like sour ale; and a sulky face, in a well-ordered household, is like a bad smell in the larder—it must be traced and stopped at once.

“Why are you turning your back on your dinner?” I asked. “What’s wrong now, Nancy?”

She tried to squeeze by without answering. I got up—slowly, as you will when you’ve lived as long as I have—and took her by the ear. It’s my way of showing I bear no malice and mean to set things right. “What’s wrong now?” I said again.

“Rosanna’s late again for dinner,” says Nancy. “And I’m sent to fetch her in. All the hard work falls on my shoulders in this house. Let me alone, Mr. Betteredge!”

Rosanna Spearman was our second housemaid. I had my reasons—good reasons—for not liking that girl to be hauled in by a kitchen-maid with a temper sharpened on hunger. I had nothing particular to do; and a man who knows his servants knows when a word spoken kindly will go farther than a reprimand flung like dishwater. So I said:

“Where is Rosanna?”

“At the sands, of course!” Nancy tosses her head, as if Rosanna had taken a holiday in the moon. “She had another of her fainting fits this morning, and she asked to go out for fresh air. I have no patience with her!”

“Go back to your dinner,” I told her. “I have patience with her, and I’ll fetch her in.”

Nancy, who has a fine appetite and no shame about it, looked pleased at being excused from trudging. When she looks pleased, she looks nice; and when she looks nice, I chuck her under the chin. It’s not immorality—it’s habit, like tasting the broth before you serve it.

I took my stick and was for setting off; and then found myself obliged—again—to stop you. Because the sands are not like other sands; and Rosanna is not like other housemaids; and, worse luck for all of us, the matter of the Diamond touches both them and her so near that you can’t understand what follows unless you’ve smelt the place for yourself and taken the measure of the girl.

Rosanna was the only new servant in the house. About four months before the time I’m writing of, my lady had been in London and had visited a Reformatory—one of those places meant to keep a poor forlorn creature from slipping back into the old mire after prison has let her go. The matron—knowing a compassionate heart when she saw it—told my lady a miserable story about a girl named Rosanna Spearman. I won’t repeat it. There are sorrows that do no good by being served up again; and besides, I have always held that the kitchen is no place for needless waste.

It came to this: Rosanna had been a thief. Not one of the grand rogues who dress their plunder in ledgers and call it “business,” but a small one, caught with her hand where it shouldn’t be. So the law did what the law does—seized her, locked her up, labelled her, and handed her on, like a joint passed from cook to scullery, to reformatory.

The matron’s opinion—spite of everything—was that Rosanna was one in a thousand, and wanted only a fair chance. My lady, being a Christian woman if ever there was one, said at once: “Rosanna Spearman shall have her chance, in my service.”

In a week she entered our establishment as second housemaid. Not a soul was told her story except Miss Rachel and me. My lady consulted me—she honoured me that way—and, having fallen into Sir John’s old habit of agreeing with her in most things, I agreed heartily. The chance was as fair as clean linen: wages, privileges, a roof, and now and then a friendly word from my lady in private, given quietly as you give a child an extra spoonful without making the others jealous.

And Rosanna, I will say for her, worked well. She wasn’t strong—she had those fainting fits—and yet she went about her duty modest and uncomplaining, careful as a woman who knows she has only one good gown and must keep it for Sunday. But she never took among the other maids. Penelope was kind to her, always; never intimate, but never cruel. The rest kept her out.

Why? Not beauty; she had none to make them envious. She was the plainest in the house, and had the misfortune of one shoulder bigger than the other—a thing she tried to hide with a grey cloak when she went out. I think what the women resented most was her silence and her solitary ways. In leisure hours when they sat chattering, she read or worked. When her turn came to go out, nine times out of ten she went alone, bonnet on, hands folded, stepping away as if the very air of company scalded her. She never quarrelled; never snapped back; never took offence—only kept a distance, civilly and obstinately, as if there was an invisible line drawn around her.

And there was another thing—harder to name. A dash of something that wasn’t like a housemaid and was like a lady. In the voice, perhaps; or in the look of the face when she forgot herself. The women pounced on it the first day and said she gave herself airs. Unjust, as most quick judgments are.

Now, having put the Person before the Thing, I must come to the Thing: the sands.

Our house stands high on the Yorkshire coast, close by the sea. Walks there are in every direction but one. That one—if you value your spirits—is a walk you avoid. It takes you for a quarter of a mile through a plantation of firs where the wind makes a sound like a steady hiss over a kettle that will never boil, and where the branches rub and whisper, as if rehearsing some old tale in a dialect no living tongue can translate. Then you come out between low cliffs upon the loneliest, ugliest little bay on our coast.

Here the sandhills slope down to the sea and end in two spits of rock running out opposite each other until the water swallows them: the North Spit and the South. Between them lies—shifting with the seasons—the most horrible quicksand on all the Yorkshire shores.

At the turn of the tide, something stirs in the unknown deeps below. The whole face of the sand begins to tremble—shiver and quiver in a way that turns your stomach. The country folk call it the Shivering Sand; and a fitter name was never given. Half a mile out, a bank breaks the ocean’s force. So when the tide comes in over the quicksand, the waves seem to be left behind out there, and the water rolls in smooth, with a heavy heave—and covers the sand in silence.

Silence, mind you. Not the wholesome quiet of a field at dusk, where you can hear a bird settling and a cow shifting its weight. This is a deadened hush, like a mouth shut tight over a scream. No boat ever ventures into that bay. No children from Cobb’s Hole—the fishing village—come there to play. Even the birds, to my thinking, give it a wide berth, as if the place offends their very hearing.

And yet Rosanna, with dozens of good walks to choose from and company enough if she had only said “Come,” took this miserable place for her favourite. She would sit there, alone, working or reading when it was her turn out. Only now and then she went to Cobb’s Hole to see the one friend she had in our neighbourhood, of whom you shall hear later.

So this was where I now set off, stick in hand, to fetch her in to dinner.

I saw no sign of her in the fir plantation. The firs stood close, dark and sour-smelling, their fallen needles making a carpet that muffled my steps. The air had that resinous tang like pinewood thrown on a fire before the flame has taken—sharp in the nose, and not pleasant long. And above it all, the sea’s far breath—one steady undertone—kept time with my own.

When I got through the sandhills and out upon the beach, there she was at once: little straw bonnet, plain grey cloak, standing alone and looking out upon the quicksand and the sea.

She started when I came up. Then she turned her head away.

Not looking me in the face is another proceeding I never allow, on principle, to pass without inquiry. I took her gently by the arm, turned her my way, and saw she had been crying.

One of my bandanna handkerchiefs—one of six beauties my lady gave me—was in my pocket. I drew it out and said: “Come and sit down, my dear, on the slope of the beach along with me. I’ll dry your eyes first, and then I’ll make so bold as to ask what you have been crying about.”

Sitting down on a slope of sand is a longer business than you think, until your joints begin to argue with you at every bend. By the time I was settled, Rosanna had dried her own eyes with a little cheap cambric thing, poor and thin beside mine. She looked quiet—too quiet—and wretched; but she sat down by me when I told her, like a good girl.

“Now, tell me,” I said, “what are you crying about?”

“About the years that are gone, Mr. Betteredge,” she answered. “My past life still comes back to me sometimes.”

“Come, come,” I said. “Your past life is all sponged out. Why can’t you forget it?”

She took hold of the lappet of my coat. I am a slovenly old man, and a good deal of my meat and drink gets splashed on me. One woman servant or another is always scraping or rubbing at my spots. Only yesterday Rosanna had cleaned a greasy mark from that very lappet with a new composition, warranted to remove anything. The grease was gone; but the cloth showed dull where the nap had been hurt.

She pointed to the place and shook her head. “The stain is taken off,” she said. “But the place shows, Mr. Betteredge—the place shows!”

Now there’s a remark that takes a man unawares by means of his own coat. I didn’t find an answer ready. And there was something in her that made me sorrier for her than I liked to be. She had nice brown eyes, plain though she was; and she looked at me with a respect—as if my comfortable age and my good character were things as far beyond her as a foreign tongue she could never speak, no matter how she listened.

Not knowing how to comfort her, I took the only sensible step: I meant to get her in to dinner.

“Help me up,” I said. “You’re late for dinner, Rosanna—and I have come to fetch you in.”

“You, Mr. Betteredge!” she cried.

“They told Nancy to fetch you,” I said. “But I thought you might like your scolding better from me.”

Instead of helping me up, she slid her hand into mine and gave it a little squeeze. She tried hard not to cry again—and managed it, which I respected.

“You’re very kind, Mr. Betteredge,” she said. “I don’t want any dinner today—let me bide a little longer here.”

“What makes you like to be here?” I asked. “What is it that brings you everlastingly to this miserable place?”

“Something draws me to it,” she said, tracing little shapes with her finger in the sand as if she were writing a language only she could read. “I try to keep away, and I can’t. Sometimes”—she lowered her voice, as if frightened by her own thought—“sometimes, Mr. Betteredge, I think my grave is waiting for me here.”

“There’s roast mutton and suet pudding waiting for you,” I said. “Go in to dinner directly. This is what comes of thinking on an empty stomach!”

I spoke sharp, for her sake. At my time of life, to hear a young woman of five-and-twenty talk of her end sets one against it like vinegar against milk.

She scarcely seemed to hear. She put her hand on my shoulder and kept me where I sat.

“I think the place has laid a spell on me,” she said. “I dream of it night after night; I think of it when I sit stitching at my work. You know I am grateful, Mr. Betteredge—you know I try to deserve your kindness, and my lady’s confidence. But I wonder sometimes whether the life here is too quiet and too good for such a woman as I am, after all I have gone through. It’s more lonely to be among the other servants, knowing I am not what they are, than it is to be here. My lady doesn’t know—the matron doesn’t know—what a dreadful reproach honest people are, just by being honest, to a woman like me.”

She stopped, swallowed, and went on with a quick little pleading that cut me. “Don’t scold me—there’s a dear good man. I do my work, don’t I? Please not to tell my lady I am discontented—I am not. My mind’s unquiet sometimes, that’s all.”

Then, as if the sand itself had spoken into her ear, she snatched her hand away and pointed down.

“Look!” she said. “Isn’t it wonderful? isn’t it terrible? I have seen it dozens of times, and it’s always as new to me as if I had never seen it before!”

The tide was on the turn. The broad brown face of the quicksand began to shiver. It heaved slowly, then dimpled and quivered all over—like a great living thing breathing under a blanket. There was no crash of surf here, no cheerful slap of water on pebbles—only that muffled, dreadful stirring, and the long far sound of the sea beyond the bank, as steady as a drum heard from a distance.

“Do you know what it looks like to me?” Rosanna said, catching my shoulder again, her fingers tight. “It looks as if it had hundreds of suffocating people under it—all struggling to get up, and all sinking lower and lower in the dreadful deeps! Throw a stone in, Mr. Betteredge! Throw a stone in—and let’s see the sand suck it down!”

Unwholesome talk. A hungry body and an unquiet mind making a meal of horrors. I was just about to answer her—as sharp as need be—when my words were snapped short by a shout from among the sandhills.

“Betteredge!” cried a voice. “Where are you?”

“Here!” I shouted back, without knowing who it was.

Rosanna sprang to her feet and stared toward the sound. I was thinking to get on my own legs next, when the girl’s face changed so suddenly it startled me more than the quicksand. A beautiful red rushed into her cheeks—colour I had never seen in them before. She brightened all over with a speechless, breathless surprise.

“Who is it?” I asked.

She gave my own question back to me, softly, more to herself than to me. “Oh—who is it?”

I twisted round on the sand and looked.

Coming out upon us from among the hills was a bright-eyed young gentleman in a beautiful fawn-coloured suit, gloves and hat to match, a rose in his button-hole, and a smile on his face that might have set the Shivering Sand itself smiling if anything could. Before I could right myself, he dropped down by me on the sand, flung an arm round my neck in the foreign fashion, and hugged me till the breath went out of my body.

“Dear old Betteredge!” he cried. “I owe you seven-and-sixpence. Now do you know who I am?”

Lord bless us and save us—four good hours before we expected him, there was Mr. Franklin Blake.

Before I could get a word out, I saw him—surprised a little, as it seemed—lift his eyes from me to Rosanna. I followed his look.

She was blushing deeper than ever, as if his glance had touched a secret place in her; and then, in confusion quite beyond my understanding, she turned and went off without a curtsey to the gentleman and without a word to me—away among the sandhills, her little grey cloak fluttering like a scrap of cloud.

Very unlike her. A civiller, better-behaved servant you never met with.

“That’s an odd girl,” says Mr. Franklin. “I wonder what she sees in me to surprise her?”

“I suppose, sir,” I answered—drolling on his Continental education—“it’s the varnish from foreign parts.”

I set down his careless question and my foolish answer for the comfort of all stupid people. Neither Mr. Franklin, with his wonderful foreign training, nor I, with my age and experience and mother-wit, had the ghost of an idea what Rosanna Spearman’s strange behaviour meant. She was out of our minds before we had seen the last flutter of her cloak.

And what of that? you will ask.

Read on, as patiently as you can—and perhaps you may be as sorry for Rosanna Spearman as I was, when the truth came out.

The first thing I tried, when we found ourselves alone at last, was to get up from my place on the sand—for the third time. The Shivering Sand gives you no honest footing. It sucks at a man’s boots like bad dough at the heel of the hand, and the sea keeps breathing in and out over it with that soft, sly sound—no louder than a cook’s spoon scraping the bottom of a copper pan, and just as steady.

Mr. Franklin stopped me.

“There is one advantage about this horrid place,” he said. “We have got it all to ourselves. Stay where you are, Betteredge; I have something to say to you.”

While he spoke, I took him in—trying, as any man would, to find the boy I remembered inside the gentleman he had turned into. The man put me out completely. Not a bit of the rosy cheeks remained, nor the trim look of him in his little jacket, as if he had stepped out of a picture-frame. His skin had paled; and, what was worse to my old-fashioned taste, the lower part of his face was grown over with curly brown beard and moustachios, like a foreigner’s. His manner had a pleasant, quick touch-and-go to it—light enough to make you feel a room go airy when he entered—but it wasn’t the free-and-easy way of other days. And he had promised, when he went away, to be tall; but he had not kept his promise. Neat he was, and slim, and well made; but by an inch or two he fell short of that middle height which looks right in an English gentleman.
