In short, he baffled me—till I came to his eyes. There, in the bright straightforward look, was our nice boy again; and there I stopped my investigation, as I might shut the lid on a spice-box when I have found the true scent.

“Welcome back to the old place, Mr. Franklin,” I said. “All the more welcome, sir, that you have come some hours before we expected you.”

“I have a reason for coming before you expected me,” he answered. “I suspect, Betteredge, that I have been followed and watched in London, for the last three or four days; and I have travelled by the morning instead of the afternoon train, because I wanted to give a certain dark-looking stranger the slip.”

Those words set something stirring in my memory, as quick as a knife-blade under a whetstone. The three jugglers. Penelope’s notion that they meant mischief.

“Who’s watching you, sir—and why?” I asked.

“Tell me about the three Indians you have had at the house today,” says Mr. Franklin, without so much as taking up my question. “It’s just possible, Betteredge, that my stranger and your three jugglers may turn out to be pieces of the same puzzle.”

“How do you come to know about the jugglers, sir?” I said—putting one question on the top of another, which is bad manners, I own; but when the mind is once heated, it pops and hisses like a kettle on a fast fire.

“I saw Penelope at the house,” says he; “and Penelope told me. Your daughter promised to be a pretty girl, Betteredge, and she has kept her promise. Penelope has got a small ear and a small foot. Did the late Mrs. Betteredge possess those inestimable advantages?”

“The late Mrs. Betteredge possessed a good many defects, sir,” says I. “One of them—if you will pardon my mentioning it—was never keeping to the matter in hand. She was more like a fly than a woman: she couldn’t settle on anything.”

“She would just have suited me,” says Mr. Franklin. “I never settle on anything either. Betteredge, your edge is better than ever. Your daughter said as much when I asked for particulars about the jugglers. ‘Father will tell you, sir. He’s a wonderful man for his age; and he expresses himself beautifully.’ Penelope’s own words—blushing divinely. Not even my respect for you prevented me from—never mind; I knew her when she was a child, and she’s none the worse for it. Let’s be serious. What did the jugglers do?”

I was dissatisfied with my daughter—not for letting Mr. Franklin kiss her; he was welcome to that—but for making me tell the story at second hand, like warmed-over broth when the joint is in the house. Still, there was no help for it; so I went through the circumstances from beginning to end.

As I spoke, his merriment dried up, as if a draught had gone through him. He sat knitting his eyebrows and twisting at his beard, listening the way a good carver listens for the bone under the knife. When I had done, he repeated—just as I had given them—two of the questions the chief juggler had put to the boy; repeating them, as it were, to taste them and be sure of the flavour.

“‘Is it on the road to this house, and on no other, that the English gentleman will travel today?’ ‘Has the English gentleman got It about him?’”

He paused; and I heard, underneath our voices, the small noises of the place—the faint lap and draw of the tide, the far cry of a gull that sounded thin in that wide air, and, now and then, the soft shift of sand as it settled. It is an unwholesome music. Give me, for comfort, the honest clatter of plates in a kitchen, and the steady chopping of a cook’s knife, and the friendly talk that rises and falls with the fire. This whispering, empty shore makes a man feel watched—even when he is alone.

“I suspect,” says Mr. Franklin—pulling a little sealed paper parcel from his pocket—“that ‘It’ means this. And ‘this,’ Betteredge, means my uncle Herncastle’s famous Diamond.”

“Good Lord, sir!” I cried. “How do you come to be in charge of the wicked Colonel’s Diamond?”

“The wicked Colonel’s will has left his Diamond as a birthday present to my cousin Rachel,” says Mr. Franklin. “And my father, as the wicked Colonel’s executor, has given it in charge to me to bring down here.”

If the sea, then oozing in smoothly over the Shivering Sand, had turned to dry land before my eyes, I doubt I could have stared harder.

“The Colonel’s Diamond left to Miss Rachel!” says I. “And your father, sir—the Colonel’s executor! Why, I would have laid any bet you like, Mr. Franklin, that your father wouldn’t have touched the Colonel with a pair of tongs!”

“Strong language, Betteredge! What was there against the Colonel? He belonged to your time, not to mine. Tell me what you know about him, and I’ll tell you how my father came to be his executor—and more besides. I have made some discoveries in London about my uncle Herncastle and his Diamond which have rather an ugly look to my eyes; and I want you to confirm them. You called him the ‘wicked Colonel’ just now. Search your memory, my old friend, and tell me why.”

I saw then he was in earnest; and I told him. What follows is the substance of it, written out straight, because if you miss the savour of these particulars now, you will find yourself at sea later—as lost as a man who has swallowed a mouthful of wind and calls it nourishment.

My lady’s father—old lord with the short temper and the long tongue—had five children. Two sons first; then, after a long interval, his lady broke out breeding again, and the three young ladies came briskly one after the other, as fast as nature would permit—my mistress being the youngest and best of them.

Of the sons, the eldest, Arthur, inherited title and estate. The second was the Honourable John; he had a fine fortune left him by a relative, and he went into the army.

It’s an ill bird that fouls its own nest. The Herncastles are my nest; and I take it as a favour not to be pressed for the nastier particulars. But this much I will say plain: the Honourable John was, in my honest belief, one of the greatest blackguards that ever lived. That is the taste he left on all tongues, and no sauce will sweeten it.

He began in the Guards. He had to leave the Guards before he was two-and-twenty—never mind why. The army is strict, and it was too strict for him. He went out to India to see whether they were equally strict there, and to try active service. For courage—if you must give the devil his due—he was a mixture of bull-dog and game-cock, with a dash of savage. He was at the taking of Seringapatam. Afterwards he shifted from one regiment to another, and in the third got his last step as lieutenant-colonel; and, getting that, got also a sunstroke, and came home to England.

He came back with a character that shut the doors of all his family against him—my lady, then just married, taking the lead, and declaring (with Sir John’s approval, of course) that her brother should never enter any house of hers.

There were slurs enough on the Colonel to make decent people shy; but the blot of the Diamond is all I need name here. It was said he had got possession of his Indian jewel by means which, bold as he was, he did not dare acknowledge. He never tried to sell it—not for want of money, for he was not poor, and not, in that way, greedy. He never gave it away. He never even showed it to a living soul. Some said he feared trouble with the military authorities; others—ignorant of the man—said he feared that if he showed it, it would cost him his life. There may have been a grain of truth in that last rumour, only not as they meant it. It was false to say he was afraid; but it was true his life had been twice threatened in India, and it was firmly believed the Moonstone lay at the bottom of it.

When he returned to England and found himself avoided by everybody, the Moonstone was thought to be at the bottom of that too. The mystery that clung to him got in his way and outlawed him among his own. Men would not have him at their clubs. Women—more than one—whom he tried to marry refused him. Friends and relations went suddenly short-sighted in the street.

Another man might have tried to set himself right with the world. But to give in, even when wrong, even with society all against him, was not the Colonel’s way. He had kept the Diamond, in flat defiance of assassination, in India; he kept it, in flat defiance of public opinion, in England. There you have him: a character that braved everything; and a face, handsome as it was, that looked possessed by the devil.

From time to time we heard rumours. Sometimes he was said to be given up to opium-smoking and collecting old books; sometimes to strange chemistry; sometimes seen carousing in the lowest slums of London with the lowest people. Anyhow, a solitary, vicious, underground life—that was his.

Once, and once only, I saw him face to face after his return. It was about two years before the time I now write of, and about a year and a half before his death. The Colonel came unexpectedly to my lady’s house in London. It was the night of Miss Rachel’s birthday—the twenty-first of June—and there was a party in honour of it, as usual.

I received word from the footman that a gentleman wanted me. I went into the hall and found the Colonel there—wasted, worn, old, shabby; as wild and as wicked-looking as ever. The hall was bright with candles, and the sound of company came faintly through the doors—laughter, music, the rustle and stir of a great house in full swing. But he stood in the middle of it like a dark stain on white linen.

“Go up to my sister,” says he, “and say that I have called to wish my niece many happy returns of the day.”

He had tried more than once by letter to be reconciled with my lady—for no other purpose, I am firmly persuaded, than to annoy her. But this was the first time he had come himself. I had it on the tip of my tongue to say there was a party. But the look of him daunted me; it had a kind of listening cruelty in it, as if he heard the smallest weakness in you the way a rat hears bread crumbling.

I went up with his message and left him, by his own desire, waiting in the hall. The servants stared at him from a distance, as if he were a walking engine of destruction, loaded with powder and shot, and likely to go off among them at a moment’s notice.

My lady had a dash—no more—of the family temper.

“Tell Colonel Herncastle,” she said, when I delivered his message, “that Miss Verinder is engaged, and that I decline to see him.”

I tried to plead for a civiller answer, knowing his superiority to restraints that govern gentlemen in general. It was useless. The family temper flashed out at me directly.

“When I want your advice,” says my lady, “you know that I always ask for it. I don’t ask for it now.”

So I went down again with the message; but I took the liberty of putting a better dressing on it, to make it go down if it could.

“My lady and Miss Rachel regret that they are engaged, Colonel,” I said, “and beg to be excused having the honour of seeing you.”

I expected an outbreak, even at that. To my surprise, he took it with an unnatural quiet. His eyes—glittering bright grey—settled on me for a moment; and he laughed, not out of himself like other people, but into himself, softly, chuckling, horridly mischievous.

“Thank you, Betteredge,” he said. “I shall remember my niece’s birthday.”

With that, he turned on his heel and walked out.

The next birthday came round, and we heard he was ill in bed. Six months after that—six months before the time I now write of—a letter came to my lady from a highly respectable clergyman. It told two wonderful things: first, that the Colonel had forgiven his sister on his death-bed; second, that he had forgiven everybody else and made a most edifying end.

I have an unfeigned respect for the Church; but I am firmly persuaded that the devil remained in undisturbed possession of the Honourable John, and that the last abominable act in the life of that abominable man was—saving your presence—to take the clergyman in.

That was all I had to tell Mr. Franklin. And I noticed he listened more and more eagerly as I went on, and that the story of the Colonel being turned away from his sister’s door on Miss Rachel’s birthday struck him like a shot that hits the mark. He did not say so; but his face showed me plainly enough that I had made him uneasy.

“You have said your say, Betteredge,” he remarked. “It’s my turn now. Before, however, I tell you what discoveries I have made in London and how I came to be mixed up in this matter of the Diamond, I want to know one thing. You look, my old friend, as if you didn’t quite understand the object to be answered by this consultation of ours. Do your looks belie you?”

“No, sir,” I said. “My looks, on this occasion at any rate, tell the truth.”

“In that case,” says Mr. Franklin, “suppose I put you up to my point of view before we go any further. I see three very serious questions involved in the Colonel’s birthday-gift to my cousin Rachel. Follow me carefully, Betteredge; and count me off on your fingers, if it will help you.”

He had a certain pleasure in showing how clear-headed he could be, which brought the boy back to me again—like hearing an old tune played on a new instrument.

“Question the first: Was the Colonel’s Diamond the object of a conspiracy in India? Question the second: Has the conspiracy followed the Colonel’s Diamond to England? Question the third: Did the Colonel know the conspiracy followed the Diamond; and has he purposely left a legacy of trouble and danger to his sister, through the innocent medium of his sister’s child? That is what I am driving at, Betteredge. Don’t let me frighten you.”

It was all very well to say it. He had frightened me.

If he was right, our quiet English house—our ordinary life of dinners, and dressing bells, and household keys—was to be invaded by a devilish Indian Diamond, and by a conspiracy of living rogues trailing after it, set loose on us by the vengeance of a dead man. It was as if a strange spice had been shaken into the pot without warning—not a wholesome one that warms, but a hot, bitter dust that stings the nose and will not be named in our plain kitchen language. And what made it worse was the sound of the thing: the thought of stealthy feet, and foreign whispers, and the delicate household noises—our good, known noises—listened to and measured by strangers.

Who ever heard the like of it—in the nineteenth century; in an age of progress; in a country rejoicing in the blessings of the British constitution? Nobody ever heard the like of it; and nobody, therefore, can be expected to believe it.

I shall go on with my story, however, in spite of that.

When you get a sudden alarm of the sort I had got now, nine times out of ten you feel it in your stomach first. And when it catches you there, your attention wanders, and you begin to fidget. I fidgeted silently on the sand. Mr. Franklin noticed me—whether it was a perturbed stomach or a perturbed mind (they mean the same thing in practice)—and, checking himself just as he was starting with his part of the story, said sharply:

“What do you want?”

What did I want? I didn’t tell him; but I will tell you, in confidence. I wanted a whiff of my pipe, and a turn at Robinson Crusoe.

I kept my own feelings locked up, and only said, as civil as might be, that I was ready to hear him out. Mr. Franklin stopped me with one of his quick looks.

“Don’t fidget, Betteredge,” he said; and began at the beginning, where the clue had first come into his hands—at another man’s table, after another man’s dinner, with the clink of plates still in the air and the smell of wine and roasted meat still hanging about the lawyer’s room at Hampstead.

He told me his discoveries about the Colonel and the Diamond had started from a visit, paid before ever he came down to us, to Mr. Bruff the family lawyer. A chance word, dropped when they were alone after dinner, let out that Mr. Franklin had been charged by his father to carry Miss Rachel a birthday gift. One question drew another, as gravy draws bread; and at last Mr. Bruff, between the walnuts and the decanter, named what the gift really was, and how the late Colonel Herncastle and Mr. Blake’s father had first struck up their ill-assorted friendship.

The thing was so out of all common experience that I mistrusted my own plain way of speaking it. Mr. Franklin’s telling had its own sharp relish; I set it down as nearly as possible in his words.

“You remember the time, Betteredge,” he said, “when my father was trying to prove his title to that unlucky dukedom. Well—at that same time my uncle Herncastle came back from India. My father discovered that he held certain papers likely to help him in the suit. He called on the Colonel, pretending good-will.

“The Colonel saw through him at once. ‘You want something,’ he said, ‘or you would never have compromised your reputation by calling on me.’ My father—finding there was no use in seasoning the truth—owned he wanted the papers.

“The Colonel asked a day to consider. And his answer came in a letter—an extraordinary letter—which Bruff showed me. He began by saying he wanted something of my father, and begged to propose an exchange of friendly services. The fortune of war—that was his phrase—had put him in possession of one of the largest diamonds in the world; and he had reason to believe that neither he nor his jewel was safe in any house, in any quarter of the globe, which they occupied together.

“Under those circumstances, he had determined to place the Diamond in some other person’s keeping. The person chosen was not expected to run any risk. He might lock it away anywhere especially guarded—like a banker’s or a jeweller’s strongroom. His chief responsibility was passive: he was to undertake, either himself or by a trustworthy representative, to receive at a prearranged address, on certain prearranged days in every year, a note from the Colonel, simply stating that he was living at that date. If the date passed and no note came, the silence was to be taken as certain proof that the Colonel had been murdered. Then, and then only, certain sealed instructions, deposited with the Diamond, were to be opened and followed to the letter. If my father accepted this strange charge, the Colonel’s papers were to be his in return. That was the letter.”

“What did your father do, sir?” I asked.

“Do?” says Mr. Franklin. “He did what a man does when he has that useful faculty called common sense. He declared the whole thing absurd. Somewhere in India the Colonel had picked up a wretched crystal and mistaken it for a diamond. As for danger of murder and all that—this was the nineteenth century, and any man in his senses had only to apply to the police.

“The Colonel was a notorious opium-eater; and, if the only way to get at the valuable papers was to treat opium as if it were proof, my father was willing to take the ridiculous responsibility—especially as it involved no trouble to himself. The Diamond and sealed instructions went into his banker’s strongroom; and the Colonel’s yearly letters, reporting him alive, were received and opened by Mr. Bruff as my father’s representative. No sensible man could have seen it differently. Nothing is probable unless it appeals to our own trumpery experience; and we only believe a romance when we see it in a newspaper.”

That told me plain enough that Mr. Franklin had his doubts of his father’s judgment. I tried him.

“And what is your own opinion, sir?”

“Let’s finish the story first,” he said. “There’s a curious want of system in the English mind, Betteredge; your question proves it. When we are not making machinery, we are, mentally speaking, the most slovenly people alive.”

“So much,” thought I, “for foreign schooling. He has picked up that way of girding at us abroad.”

He took up the thread again.

“My father got his papers,” he went on, “and never saw his brother-in-law again. Year after year, on the prearranged days, the prearranged letter came, and Mr. Bruff opened it. I have seen them in a heap—every one in the same curt business form:

“‘Sir,—This is to certify that I am still a living man. Let the Diamond be. John Herncastle.’

“That, and nothing more; regular to the day. Until six or eight months since, when the form altered for the first time. It ran:

“‘Sir,—They tell me I am dying. Come to me, and help me to make my will.’

“Mr. Bruff went, and found him in a little suburban villa, with its own grounds, where he had lived alone since leaving India. Dogs, cats, birds—creatures to be fed and soothed by familiar sounds and footsteps—but no human company, except the daily woman and the doctor at his bed.

“The will was simple. He had wasted most of his fortune on chemical experiments. He dictated three clauses, perfectly clear-headed. First, provision for the support and safe keeping of his animals. Second, a professorship of experimental chemistry at a northern university. Third, the Moonstone as a birthday present to his niece—on condition that my father acted as executor.

“My father refused at first; then gave way—partly because he was assured it would be no trouble; partly because Bruff suggested, in Rachel’s interest, the Diamond might after all be worth something.”

“Did the Colonel give any reason, sir,” I asked, “for leaving it to Miss Rachel?”

“He not only gave it—he wrote it into the will,” said Mr. Franklin. “You shall see it presently. One thing at a time. You have heard the will; now hear what followed his death.

“Before the will could be proved, the Diamond had to be valued. Every jeweller consulted confirmed the Colonel’s assertion: it was among the largest in the world. But valuing it exactly was difficult. Its size made it a phenomenon; its colour put it in a class by itself; and, worst of all, there was a flaw in the very heart of the stone. Even so, the lowest estimate was twenty thousand pounds. Conceive my father’s astonishment! He had been within a hair’s-breadth of refusing the executorship—and of letting such a jewel slip out of the family.

“That interest induced him to open the sealed instructions deposited with the Diamond. Bruff showed me the document with the other papers; and to my mind it suggests a clue to the conspiracy which threatened the Colonel’s life.”

“Then you do believe, sir,” I said, “that there was a conspiracy?”

“Not possessing my father’s excellent common sense,” Mr. Franklin answered, “I believe the Colonel’s life was threatened exactly as he said. The sealed instructions, I think, explain why he died quietly in his bed. If he died by violence—that is, if the yearly letter failed—my father was directed to send the Moonstone secretly to Amsterdam. There it was to be deposited with a famous diamond-cutter, and cut into four to six separate stones. Those stones were to be sold, and the money applied to the professorship of chemistry which the Colonel afterwards endowed by his will.

“Now, Betteredge—exert those sharp wits of yours, and observe what this points to.”

I exerted them. They were of the plain English pattern; and they muddled the matter till he took them in hand and set them straight.

“Remark,” he said, “how the integrity of the Diamond as one stone is made dependent on the Colonel’s life being preserved. He does not say to the enemies he fears: ‘Kill me—and you gain nothing; the jewel lies where you cannot reach it, in a bank.’ He says instead: ‘Kill me—and the Diamond will be the Diamond no longer; its identity will be destroyed.’ What does that mean?”

I had, as I fancied, a flash of foreign brightness.

“It means lowering the value, sir—cheating the rogues!”

“Nothing of the sort,” he said. “I have inquired. The flawed Diamond, cut up, would fetch more than the large imperfect stone; because four to six perfect brilliants could be cut from it, and those, collectively, would be worth more. If robbery for gain was the object, the instructions make it better worth stealing, and easier to dispose of—once it passed through Amsterdam.”

“Lord bless us!” I cried. “What was the plot then?”

“A plot among the Indians who originally owned it,” said Mr. Franklin, “with some old Hindoo superstition at the bottom. That is my opinion—and I have a family paper in my pocket that supports it.”

So there was the connection, now, between his thoughts and the three Indian jugglers that had shown themselves at our house.

“I don’t force my opinion on you,” he went on. “But the notion of chosen servants of an old superstition devoting themselves, through years and dangers, to watching for the chance of recovering a sacred gem, is perfectly consistent with what we know of Oriental patience and religious influence. I am an imaginative man; and the butcher, the baker, and the tax-gatherer are not the only realities to my mind.

“Let that guess go for what it’s worth. The practical question is: does the conspiracy survive the Colonel’s death? And did he know it when he left the birthday gift to his niece?”

I began to see my lady and Miss Rachel at the end of this chain, as one sees a candle at the end of a long dark passage. I listened hard.

“I was not very willing,” said Mr. Franklin, “when I discovered the story of the Moonstone, to be the means of bringing it here. But Bruff reminded me somebody must put my cousin’s legacy into her hands—and I might as well do it as another.

“After taking the Diamond out of the bank, I fancied I was followed by a shabby dark-complexioned man. I went to my father’s house for my luggage, and found a letter which unexpectedly detained me in London. I went back to the bank with the Diamond, and thought I saw the shabby man again. Taking it out once more this morning, I saw him for the third time—gave him the slip—and started by the morning train instead of the afternoon.

“Here I am, with the Diamond safe; and what is the first news I hear? Three strolling Indians have been at the house; and my arrival from London, and something I am expected to have about me, are two special objects of inquiry to them, when they think themselves alone. I don’t waste time on their pouring ink into the boy’s hand and telling him to look for a man at a distance and for something in that man’s pocket. I have seen that Eastern trick often enough. Hocus-pocus, as you and I think.

“The question is—am I attaching meaning to accident? or have we proof that the Indians are on the track of the Moonstone the moment it is removed from the bank?”

Neither of us liked the feel of that part of the business. We looked at each other; then away, at the tide creeping in, smoothing the Shivering Sand and covering it inch by inch, as if some soft-footed thing kept coming nearer without ever showing its face. The faint wash and draw of the water made its own talk, steady and secret, under ours.

“What are you thinking of?” Mr. Franklin asked suddenly.

“I was thinking, sir,” I said, “I should like to shy the Diamond into the quicksand, and settle the question that way.”

“If you’ve got twenty thousand pounds in your pocket,” he answered, “say so, Betteredge—and in it goes!”

It is odd, when you are anxious, what relief there is in the smallest joke. We found a sort of mirth at the idea of making away with Miss Rachel’s lawful property and getting Mr. Blake, as executor, into dreadful trouble—though, looking back, I can’t find where the merriment lay.

Mr. Franklin was the first to bring us back. He drew an envelope from his pocket, took out a paper, and gave it to me.

“We must face the Colonel’s motive,” he said, “for my aunt’s sake. Remember how Lady Verinder treated her brother from the time he returned to England to the time he told you he should remember his niece’s birthday. And read that.”

It was an extract from the Colonel’s will. I copy it here word for word:

“Thirdly, and lastly, I give and bequeath to my niece, Rachel Verinder, daughter and only child of my sister, Julia Verinder, widow—if her mother, the said Julia Verinder, shall be living on the said Rachel Verinder’s next Birthday after my death—the yellow Diamond belonging to me, and known in the East by the name of The Moonstone: subject to this condition, that her mother, the said Julia Verinder, shall be living at the time. And I hereby desire my executor to give my Diamond, either by his own hands or by the hands of some trustworthy representative whom he shall appoint, into the personal possession of my said niece Rachel, on her next birthday after my death, and in the presence, if possible, of my sister, the said Julia Verinder. And I desire that my said sister may be informed, by means of a true copy of this, the third and last clause of my Will, that I give the Diamond to her daughter Rachel, in token of my free forgiveness of the injury which her conduct towards me has been the means of inflicting on my reputation in my lifetime; and especially in proof that I pardon, as becomes a dying man, the insult offered to me as an officer and a gentleman, when her servant, by her orders, closed the door of her house against me, on the occasion of her daughter’s birthday.”

More followed: if my lady was dead, or if Miss Rachel was dead at the testator’s decease, the Diamond was to be sent to Holland according to the sealed instructions; and the proceeds were then to be added to the money left for the professorship.

I gave the paper back, troubled to the roots of me. Up to then I had believed the Colonel died as wicked as he lived. I can’t say the will turned me—only that it shook me.

“Well?” says Mr. Franklin. “Now you have the Colonel’s own statement, what do you say? In bringing the Moonstone here, am I serving his vengeance blindfold—or vindicating him as a penitent and Christian man?”

“It seems hard to say, sir,” I answered, “that he died with a horrid revenge in his heart and a horrid lie on his lips. God alone knows. Don’t ask me.”

He sat twisting the paper in his fingers, as though the truth might be squeezed out like juice from a lemon. His manner altered strangely. The brisk, bright young gentleman sank into a slow, solemn, pondering one.

“This question has two sides,” he said. “An Objective side and a Subjective side. Which are we to take?”

He had German learning as well as French; and now it seemed the German was stepping in. One of my rules is to take no notice of what I don’t understand. So I stared hard and held my tongue.

“Let’s extract the inner meaning,” he said. “Why did my uncle leave the Diamond to Rachel? Why not to my aunt?”

“That’s easy guessing, sir,” I said. “Colonel Herncastle knew my lady well enough to know she would have refused any legacy from him.”

“How did he know Rachel might not refuse too?”

“Is there a young lady alive, sir,” I answered, “who could resist such a birthday present as the Moonstone?”

“That’s the Subjective view,” says Mr. Franklin. “It does you credit, Betteredge, to take it. But there is another mystery. Why give it conditionally—only if her mother is alive?”

“I don’t want to slander a dead man,” I said. “But if he meant to leave a legacy of trouble and danger to his sister, by the means of her child, it must be conditional on his sister living to feel the vexation.”

“Oh! that’s your interpretation? Subjective again! Have you ever been in Germany, Betteredge?”

“No, sir. What’s yours?”

“I can see,” he said, “that his object may have been—not to benefit a niece he never saw—but to prove to his sister that he died forgiving her, and to prove it prettily by a gift to her child. A totally different explanation from yours, arising from a Subjective-Objective point of view. As far as I can see, one is as likely as the other.”

Having landed us in that comforting bog, he seemed to consider his duty done. He laid himself flat on his back on the sand and asked what was to be done next.

He had led so clearly up to this point, that I was unprepared for his sudden helplessness—his throwing his own burden on me. It was not until later that I learned, from Miss Rachel first of all, that these odd shifts in him came of foreign training; sent abroad at the age when character takes colour, he had been passed from nation to nation before any one tint could settle. The result was a man made up of sides that jarred together—busy and idle, cloudy and clear, determined and helpless by turns; French, German, Italian, with the English foundation showing through now and then like the bottom crust of a pie. Miss Rachel used to say the Italian side was uppermost when he unexpectedly gave in and asked you, sweet-tempered, to take his responsibility. That side was uppermost now.

“Isn’t it your business, sir,” I said, “to know what to do next? It can’t be mine.”

He stared up at the sky as if it held the answer.

“I don’t want to alarm my aunt without reason,” he said. “And I don’t want to leave her without warning if warning is needed. If you were in my place, Betteredge—one word—what would you do?”

In one word, I told him: “Wait.”

“With all my heart,” he said. “How long?”

Then I put it in plain order.

“As I understand it, sir, somebody is bound to put this plaguy Diamond into Miss Rachel’s hands on her birthday—and you may as well do it as another. This is the twenty-fifth of May; her birthday is the twenty-first of June. We have close on four weeks. Let’s wait and see what happens in that time; and warn my lady—or not—as circumstances direct.”

“Perfect, Betteredge—as far as it goes!” Mr. Franklin said. “But between this and the birthday, what is to be done with the Diamond?”

I was in no humour to be crossed, and yet the house has a way of placing one’s own flesh and blood exactly on the stair where a man most wants a clear passage. Penelope met me—soft-footed, steady-eyed, with that quiet obstinacy of her late mother’s that could trip a man more surely than any loose board—and claimed, in the mild voice that never sounded like a claim, to know all that had passed between Mr. Franklin Blake and me.

There are moments when a steward’s dignity must be defended with a lie as carefully set as a soufflé—light on the surface, firm enough underneath to stand. I clapped the lid down on her curiosity at once.

“Mr. Franklin and I,” I said, “talked foreign politics till neither of us could talk any longer; and then we fell asleep in the sun.”

Try that answer, if you ever wish to stop a question without starting a quarrel. A woman will seem to swallow it—because sweetness is part of her breeding—and she will remember it—because memory is part of her power. Penelope let me go, as if my nonsense had satisfied her; and the afternoon ran on, the household noises settling into their accustomed pattern: the faint clink of china in the pantry, the brushed whisper of skirts in passages, the far-off calling of a man in the stable-yard, all tempered by the thick, warm air that made even the clocks sound drowsy.

My lady and Miss Rachel came back at last. Of course they were astonished to hear that Mr. Franklin had arrived by the early train and vanished again on horseback. Of course they asked their awkward questions straight out, like people born to have the truth served to them hot and ready, without the trouble of watching it simmer.

“Foreign politics” and “falling asleep in the sun” were a dish I couldn’t send up twice.

At the end of my invention, I fell back on what the gentry always find easiest to believe of each other.

“It’s one of Mr. Franklin’s freaks, my lady,” I said.

“And his galloping off again?” my lady asked, with that dry sharpness that cuts through a man’s excuses as a carving-knife goes through fat.

“Yes,” I answered. “Another freak.”

I slipped out of it in that way—cleverly enough to keep my footing for the moment, though any man who has lived long with women knows that a clever evasion is only a debt postponed.

When I got back to my own room, my difficulties were waiting there as surely as the kettle waits for the fire. In came Penelope, as if to prove my own little maxim: with the natural sweetness of women, to kiss and make it up again; and with the natural curiosity of women, to begin afresh at the very point you had hoped was buried.

This time she wanted to talk of Rosanna Spearman.

After leaving Mr. Franklin and me at the Shivering Sand, Rosanna had returned to the house in a state that Penelope could not make head nor tail of. Penelope described it with the sort of exactness she might have given to a recipe remembered by heart—this pinch of feeling, that sudden turn, the whole mixture rising and collapsing without warning.

Rosanna, it seemed, had gone all colours in her face as if she had been held over a fire and then plunged into cold water. She had been merry without reason, and sad without reason. One minute she was pouring out questions about Mr. Franklin Blake—hundreds of them, tumbling one after another like peas from a split sack—and the next she was offended with Penelope for so much as hinting that a strange gentleman could be of any interest to her. Penelope had found her smiling, scribbling Mr. Franklin’s name inside her workbox as if the very letters could be kneaded into her life and make it new. Then, as suddenly, she had been crying before the glass, staring at her deformed shoulder with a bitterness that made Penelope’s own voice falter as she repeated it.

Had Rosanna and Mr. Franklin known one another before that day? Impossible. Had they even heard of each other? Impossible again. I could answer for Mr. Franklin’s genuine surprise when the girl stared at him; Penelope could answer for Rosanna’s genuine eagerness when she pressed for every scrap of knowledge about him. The talk between my daughter and me went on in that weary circle—facts that didn’t fit, guesses that wouldn’t hold—until Penelope suddenly flung out what I believed to be the most monstrous notion ever to come out of a sensible girl’s mouth.

“Father,” she said, grave as a judge, “there’s only one explanation. Rosanna has fallen in love with Mr. Franklin Blake at first sight.”

You may have heard of handsome young ladies falling in love at first sight, and called it natural enough. But a housemaid out of a reformatory, with a plain face and a twisted shoulder, falling in love at first sight with a gentleman newly come to visit his cousin—match me that absurdity out of any story-book in Christendom, if you can.

I laughed till the tears ran. And at my laughter—at my coarse, comfortable laughter—Penelope turned white, as if I had struck her.

“I never knew you cruel before, father,” she said—very gently, which was the worst of it—and she went out.

The words hit me like cold water dashed in the face. I was savage with myself for feeling—uneasy, of all things—at being called cruel; and yet I did feel it. I had not meant harm. But the harm, once done, does not ask what you meant.

We will leave that subject. I am sorry I drifted into it at all; and not without reason, as you will see when we have gone on a little farther.

The evening came. The dressing-bell rang for dinner before Mr. Franklin returned from Frizinghall. I took his hot water up to his room myself, expecting, after such delay, some report of accident or adventure—some stir in the story, after all the heat and flurry about the Diamond earlier in the day.

To my disappointment—flat enough, no doubt, to yours as well—nothing had happened.

He had not met the Indians, going or coming. He had put the Moonstone away in the bank, calling it only a valuable of great price, and the receipt was safe in his pocket. That was all.

I went downstairs with the sensation of a pot watched to the boil, only to find it lukewarm when the lid is lifted.

What passed between Mr. Franklin and his aunt and cousin I cannot tell. I would have given something to wait at table that night and see it with my own eyes. But in my position, waiting at dinner—except on high family festivals—was considered a letting down of my dignity in the servants’ hall; and my lady had long held that I was prone enough to forget my place already, without seeking temptations.

The news that filtered down to me from the upper regions came, as such news does, flavoured by the mouths it travelled through: Penelope and the footman.

Penelope said she had never known Miss Rachel so particular about her hair; never seen her look so bright and pretty as she did when she went down to meet Mr. Franklin in the drawing-room. There is a kind of careful impatience in a young lady’s dressing when her heart is in it: pins set and reset; a curl coaxed into place as if it were a stubborn dough that must be worked till it yields; the faint scent of pomatum warmed by the skin—small domestic acts that carry, for those with ears to hear, the loudest meaning.

The footman’s report was different in taste, but not in substance. He said that keeping a respectful composure in the presence of one’s betters and waiting on Mr. Franklin Blake at dinner were two of the hardest things to reconcile that had ever tried his training in service. I understood him. There are gentlemen whose very cheerfulness makes a servant’s face ache with the effort of not smiling back.

Later in the evening we heard them singing and playing duets—Mr. Franklin piping high, Miss Rachel piping higher, and my lady on the piano following them as if over hedge and ditch, bringing them safe through in a manner wonderful and pleasant to hear through the open windows on the terrace at night.

I stood outside a while, letting the music wash over the gravel and the shrubs and the dark side of the house. The air held sound the way a good broth holds spice—each note carried, each pause tasted. Beyond the garden walls the world was quiet, as country should be at that hour. No clatter of carts, no shriek of machinery, none of that harsh modern uproar that makes art into something shouted and sold, instead of something listened to. Only the human voices, the struck keys, and beneath them, far away and faint, the steady breath of the sea.

Later still I went to Mr. Franklin in the smoking-room with the soda-water and brandy, and found that Miss Rachel had put the Diamond clean out of his head.

“She’s the most charming girl I have seen since I came back to England!” was all I could get from him, when I tried to lead him—gently, as one tests a sauce before adding salt—back to more serious matters.

Towards midnight I went my round to lock up, as was my duty, with my second in command—Samuel the footman—at my heels. When all the doors were made fast, except the side door that opened on the terrace, I sent Samuel to bed and stepped out myself for a breath of air before turning in.

The night was still and close. The moon was full. It was so silent out of doors that, from time to time, I caught—very faint and low—the fall of the sea as the ground-swell heaved it in on the sand-bank near the mouth of our little bay. That sound, when the rest of the world holds its tongue, is like an old language spoken under the breath—unfashionable, perhaps, and ignored by those who live among louder things, but carrying a meaning you cannot translate into any other words.

As the house stood, the terrace side lay in darkness; but the broad moonlight showed fair on the gravel walk that ran along the next side. Looking that way, after looking up at the sky, I saw the shadow of a person in the moonlight, thrown forward from behind the corner of the house.

Being old and sly, I did not call out. Being old and heavy, my feet betrayed me on the gravel with a crunch that sounded, in such stillness, like breaking bones.

Before I could steal round the corner as I meant, I heard lighter feet than mine—and more than one pair, as it seemed—retreating in a hurry. By the time I reached the corner, the trespassers had run into the shrubbery on the far side of the walk and were hidden among the thick trees and bushes. From there they could easily make their way over our fence into the road.

If I had been forty years younger I might have had a chance of catching them before they got clear. As it was, I went back to set younger legs moving. Without disturbing anybody, Samuel and I got a couple of guns and went all round the house and through the shrubbery, listening—listening harder than we looked—for the slightest answering sound: a snapped twig, a hissed breath, the restless settling of leaves. But the night had swallowed them. We made sure no one was lurking in the grounds, and turned back.

Passing again over the walk where I had seen the shadow, I noticed, for the first time, a little bright object lying on the clean gravel under the moon. I stooped and picked it up.

It was a small bottle, and in it a thick liquor, sweet-smelling, black as ink. The scent rose at once—cloying and strange, like syrup warmed too long, like sugar turned dark at the edge of burning. I said nothing to Samuel. But Penelope’s earlier account came back to me, clear as if spoken into my ear: the jugglers; the little pool of black poured into the boy’s palm.

In that moment I suspected I had disturbed the three Indians, lurking about the house, bent—after their heathen fashion—on finding out where the Diamond lay that night.

Here, for one moment, I must put down my pen, as you might set a lid on a pot to keep it from boiling over too fast.

When I call up my own memory—and when I get Penelope to fetch hers, by the plain, steady help of her journal—I find there is little profit in stirring the days between Mr. Franklin Blake’s coming among us and Miss Rachel’s birthday. They simmered along without any fresh flavour worth naming. So, with your leave, I shall mark only the dates that mattered; and I shall keep the close, minute, day-by-day telling for the time when the Moonstone—like a strong spice dropped into common broth—made everybody’s business in the house taste of nothing else.

Now then. Back we go, as we must, to that bottle of sweet-smelling ink I picked up on the gravel walk at night.

On the next morning—the twenty-sixth—I showed it to Mr. Franklin, and told him what I have told you. He laughed, in his quick, clever way, and said it proved two things: first, that the Indians had been lurking about after the Diamond; and second, that they were simple enough to believe in their own trickery—meaning the signs on the boy’s head and the ink poured into his hand, and then expecting him to see beyond the reach of honest eyes.

“In England,” he said, “there are people who play at the same thing—without the ink; and they give it a French name, something like brightness of sight. Depend on it, Betteredge, the Indians counted on our keeping the Diamond here, and they brought their seeing-boy to guide them to it, if they got into the house last night.”

“Will they try again, sir?” I asked.

“That depends,” says he, “on what the boy can do. If he can see the Diamond through the iron safe at the bank in Frizinghall, we shall have no more visits just yet. If he can’t, we shall likely catch them in the shrubbery before many nights are over our heads.”

I waited—confident as a cook waiting for a second pan to hiss when the first has begun—but, strange as it sounds, the second pan never sang. No Indian shadow crossed our grounds again through all the weeks before Miss Rachel’s birthday.

Whether the jugglers heard in the town that Mr. Franklin had been seen at the bank and took the hint; whether the boy truly “saw” what I, for one, will never believe ink can show him; or whether it was mere chance—that is more than I can season to certainty. The plain truth is all I can serve you: not the ghost of an Indian came near the house again. They stayed in and about the town, practising on fair-day crowds; and Mr. Franklin and I stayed quiet, watching, and resolute not to frighten the rogues off by showing too soon that we had our suspicions. So ends, for the present, what I have to say of them.

On the twenty-ninth, Miss Rachel and Mr. Franklin found a new way of spending the time that would otherwise have sat on their hands like stale bread—filling, but tasteless. There are reasons for noting it. It will have its bearing, later, on what is still to come.

Gentry, as I have observed in my station, have an awkward rock ahead in life: the rock of their own idleness. A man who must earn his dinner knows what the day is for; but a man who has dinner provided often wanders about, hunting for “pursuits,” and ends by making a mess in the house under pretence of improving his mind. I have seen them turn curiosity into cruelty and call it knowledge; or turn cleanliness into filth and call it art. And if you ask what it means, the answer is always the same—time must be got through, time must be got through. A child makes mud-pies; the grown gentlefolk make finer messes, with worse smells.

As for Mr. Franklin and Miss Rachel, they tortured nothing, I am glad to say. They only spoiled a door.

Mr. Franklin’s universal genius—which is a kind of cook’s fire that will heat anything, but is apt to scorch—took to what he named “decorative painting.” He had invented a new mixture for moistening paint, which he called a “vehicle.” What it was made of, I cannot tell you. What it did, I can tell you to the letter: it stank. Not a wholesome kitchen smell—no yeast rising, no peel of orange in hot sugar, no clean scent of herbs bruised between fingers—but a sharp, head-turning reek that got into the curtains and seemed to argue with the very air of the room. When he mixed it, even the dogs sneezed and backed out, affronted in their honest noses.

Miss Rachel, being wild to try her hand, had the materials sent for from London. Mr. Franklin put an apron and bib over her gown as careful as if he were tying up a child for jam-making, and set her to work in her own little sitting-room—called, for want of an English word ready to hand, her “boudoir.” They began with the inside of the door. Mr. Franklin scraped off the decent varnish with pumice-stone—grinding and rasping till the wood looked raw and thirsty—and called it “a surface to work on.” Then, under his directions, Miss Rachel laid her patterns: griffins, birds, flowers, cupids, and such-like, copied from designs by a famous Italian painter—one of the Virgin-Mary makers, and (as I have heard it said) a man with a sweetheart at a baker’s.

The work was slow, and it was dirty—paint and vehicle, grit and rag, the smell hanging about like a bad tune you cannot forget. But they never tired of it. When they were not riding, or seeing company, or taking their meals, or piping their songs, there they were, heads bent close together, busy as bees, spoiling the door with the happiest faces in England. If Satan does indeed find mischief for idle hands, he might have sat down in that little room and taken notes—Miss Rachel with her brush, and Mr. Franklin with his vile-smelling “vehicle.”

The next date worth setting down is Sunday, the fourth of June.

That evening, in the servants’ hall, we debated a domestic question for the first time—one that, like the decoration of the door, points forward to something yet.

Seeing the pleasure Mr. Franklin and Miss Rachel took in one another’s company, and noting what a pretty match they made to the eye, we naturally speculated on whether their heads might be put together for something besides patterns and devices. Some said there would be a wedding before the summer was out. Others—led by me—allowed it was likely enough Miss Rachel might marry, but doubted, for reasons I shall soon show, whether her bridegroom would be Mr. Franklin Blake.

That Mr. Franklin was in love, any person with ears could tell—his voice altered when he spoke to her, and his footsteps altered when he followed her into a room, as if the very boards listened. The difficulty was Miss Rachel. Let me make you acquainted with her, and then you may try your hand at fathoming—if you can.

Her eighteenth birthday was coming, on the twenty-first of June. If you like dark women—and fashion, I am told, has been fickle on that point—Miss Rachel was, for her size, one of the prettiest creatures a man could look on without losing his wits. She was small and slim, but finely made all over. To see her sit down, to see her rise, and above all to see her walk, proved that what people call grace was in her flesh and not hung on her by dressmakers. Her hair was black as night; her eyes matched it. Her nose, I admit, was not quite large enough. Her mouth and chin, Mr. Franklin declared, were morsels for the gods; and her complexion—on the same authority—was warm as the sun, with this advantage over the sun, that it was always in nice order to look at. She carried her head upright as a dart, in a thoroughbred way; her voice rang true; and when she smiled, it began in her eyes before it reached her lips—like a light kindled behind a shutter before the room shows it.

And her disposition? Had she no faults? Just as many as you have, ma’am—no more and no less.

Seriously, with all her charms, she had one defect which impartiality obliges me to confess. She had ideas of her own, and spirit enough to defy the fashions if the fashions displeased her. In trifles, that independence was well enough; but in matters of consequence, it carried her further than my lady thought right—and further than I thought right too. She judged for herself as few women of twice her age judge; she never asked advice; never told you beforehand what she meant to do; never came with confidences to anybody, from her mother down. With those she loved, and those she hated—and she did both heartily—she went her own way, sufficient to herself in her joys and sorrows. Many a time I have heard my lady say, “Rachel’s best friend and Rachel’s worst enemy are—Rachel herself.”

One thing more, and I have done with the portrait.

With all her secrecy and self-will, there was not so much as a shadow of falsehood in her. I never knew her break her word. I never knew her say No and mean Yes. In her childhood, more than once, she took the blame and bore the punishment for a fault done by some playfellow she loved. When it came out, and she was charged after, she never confessed; but she never lied about it, either. She looked you straight in the face, shook her saucy little head, and said plain, “I won’t tell you.” Punished again, she might be sorry for saying “won’t”—but, bread-and-water notwithstanding, she never told. Self-willed—devilish self-willed sometimes, I grant; but the finest creature, for all that, that ever walked this lower world. If you spy a contradiction there, I whisper you a word: study your wife for four-and-twenty hours. If she shows no contradiction in that time, Heaven help you—you have married a monster.

And now, being acquainted with Miss Rachel, we come face to face with her matrimonial views.

On the twelfth of June, my mistress sent an invitation to a gentleman in London to come and help keep Miss Rachel’s birthday. This was the fortunate person on whom I believed her heart to be privately set. Like Mr. Franklin, he was a cousin. His name was Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite.

My lady’s second sister—no fear, I won’t drag you deep into family affairs—had been disappointed in love, and then, taking a husband on the neck-or-nothing principle, made what the world calls a misalliance. There had been terrible work when the Honourable Caroline insisted on marrying plain Mr. Ablewhite, the banker at Frizinghall. He was rich and respectable, and he begot a prodigious large family—much in his favour. But he had presumed to rise from a low station—that was against him. Time, however, and modern enlightenment put things right, and the misalliance passed muster. We are all liberal now; and (provided you can scratch me if I scratch you) what do I care, in Parliament or out, whether you are Dustman or Duke? That is the modern way; and I keep up with it. The Ablewhites lived in a fine house with grounds a little out of Frizinghall; worthy people, respected in the neighbourhood. We shall not be much troubled with them—excepting Mr. Godfrey, who must take his place here, for Miss Rachel’s sake.

With all Mr. Franklin’s brightness and general good qualities, his chance, in my opinion, of topping Mr. Godfrey in Miss Rachel’s estimation was a poor chance indeed.

In the first place, Mr. Godfrey was, for size, the finest man of the two. Over six feet; beautiful red-and-white colour; smooth round face shaved as bare as your hand; and lovely long flaxen hair, falling negligent over the back of his neck. But why do I waste words describing him? If you ever subscribed to a Ladies’ Charity in London, you know Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite as well as I do. He was a barrister by profession, a ladies’ man by temperament, and a good Samaritan by choice. Female benevolence and female destitution could do nothing without him. Maternal societies, Magdalen societies, strong-minded societies—he was vice-president, manager, referee to them all. Wherever there was a table with a committee of ladies sitting round it, there was Mr. Godfrey at the bottom, hat in hand, keeping the temper of the meeting and leading the dear creatures through the thorny ways of business. I do suppose he was the most accomplished philanthropist (on a small independence) England ever produced. As a speaker, he could draw your tears and your money with a handkerchief and a glass of water. The last time I was in London, my mistress gave me two treats: the theatre, to see a dancing woman all the rage; and Exeter Hall, to hear Mr. Godfrey. Crowds for the legs, crowds for the tongue. And with all this—sweet-tempered, simple, pleasant, easy to please. He loved everybody, and everybody loved him. What chance had Mr. Franklin—what chance had any man of average reputation—against such a favourite of the female world?

On the fourteenth came Mr. Godfrey’s answer.

He accepted the invitation from Wednesday, the birthday, to Friday evening, when his charitable duties would call him back to town. He enclosed, besides, a copy of verses on what he elegantly termed his cousin’s “natal day.” Miss Rachel, I heard, joined Mr. Franklin in making fun of the verses at dinner; and Penelope—who was all on Mr. Franklin’s side—asked me in triumph what I thought of that.

“Miss Rachel has led you off on a false scent, my dear,” I said. “But my nose is not so easily mystified. Wait till Mr. Ablewhite’s verses are followed by Mr. Ablewhite himself.”

Penelope replied that Mr. Franklin might strike in and try his luck before the poet arrived. And to do him justice, he left no chance untried.

Though one of the most inveterate smokers I ever met, he gave up his cigar because Miss Rachel said, one day, she hated the stale smell of it in his clothes. He slept badly after the sacrifice—missing, as some men miss their night-cup, the composing comfort he had made a habit of—and came down morning after morning haggard and worn. Miss Rachel herself begged him to take to his cigars again. No: he would take to nothing that could annoy her; he would fight it out, and win back sleep by patience and sheer will. Some downstairs said devotion like that must make its way into any girl’s heart—backed by the daily work, heads together over the door. All very well. But Miss Rachel had a photograph of Mr. Godfrey in her bedroom—taken at a public meeting, hair blown out by the breath of his own eloquence, eyes (most lovely eyes) charming the money out of your pockets. Every morning, as Penelope owned to me, there he was, looking on in effigy while Miss Rachel had her hair combed. Before long, he would be looking on in flesh and blood—that was my belief.

June the sixteenth brought an incident that made Mr. Franklin’s chance look worse to me than ever.

A strange gentleman came to the house that morning, speaking English with a foreign accent, and asked to see Mr. Franklin on business. The business could not have concerned the Diamond—for two reasons: first, Mr. Franklin told me nothing; second, he communicated it, when the gentleman had gone, to my lady. She, in her turn, must have hinted something to her daughter, for Miss Rachel was reported to have said severe things to Mr. Franklin at the piano that evening—sharp little strokes about the people he had lived among abroad, and the principles he had picked up in foreign parts. The next day, for the first time, nothing was done toward decorating the door. I suspected some Continental imprudence of his—with a woman, or a debt, at the bottom; but that is only guesswork. In this matter, not only Mr. Franklin, but my lady too, left me—strange to say—completely in the dark.

On the seventeenth, the cloud seemed to pass. They went back to their work on the door, and were as good friends as ever. If Penelope was to be trusted, Mr. Franklin seized the chance of reconciliation to speak out to Miss Rachel, and had neither Yes nor No in answer. My girl was sure, by signs and tokens she did not trouble to set forth, that her mistress had parried him by saying she could not believe he was in earnest—and had then, in secret, regretted it. But I knew Miss Rachel’s reserve too well to think she would show her mind so freely to anybody. What Penelope told me was—so I suspected—more what she wished than what she knew.

On the nineteenth another matter occurred. We had the doctor in the house, professionally, for a person I have already had occasion to name—our second housemaid, Rosanna Spearman.

That poor girl—who had puzzled me at the Shivering Sand—puzzled me again and again through this interval. Penelope’s notion that Rosanna was in love with Mr. Franklin (a notion I made my daughter keep strictly secret) seemed, at first, as absurd as ever. But what I saw, and what Penelope saw too, began to look mysterious—mysterious, at the very least.

For instance, Rosanna was forever putting herself in Mr. Franklin’s way—quietly, slyly, like a hand stealing spice from the pantry when it thinks no eye observes. Mr. Franklin took no more notice of her than he took of the cat; it never entered his head to waste so much as a glance on her plain face. Her appetite—never hearty—fell away to nothing; and her eyes in the morning told their own tale of wakefulness and crying at night. One day Penelope made an awkward discovery, and we hushed it up on the spot. She caught Rosanna at Mr. Franklin’s dressing-table, secretly taking away a rose Miss Rachel had given him for his button-hole, and putting another rose like it—one Rosanna had picked herself—in its place. After that, she was once or twice impudent to me when I gave her a general hint to be careful; and, worse still, she showed less respect than was decent on the few occasions when Miss Rachel spoke to her by chance.

My lady noticed the change and asked what I thought. I tried to screen the girl, and said I believed she was out of health; and that ended in the doctor being sent for, as I have said, on the nineteenth. He called it her nerves, and doubted if she was fit for service. My lady offered to send her for change of air to one of our farms inland. Rosanna begged and prayed, with tears in her eyes, to be allowed to stay; and in an evil hour I advised my lady to try her a little longer.

As events proved—as you will soon see—this was the worst advice I could have given. If I could only have looked a little way forward, I would have taken Rosanna Spearman out of the house that day with my own hand.

June the twenty-first—birthday day—broke up in a fret of cloud and wind, like a kettle that won’t come to the boil. But by noon the sky cleared its throat and steadied, and the house set itself to festivity as naturally as a kitchen sets itself to dinner.

Down in the servants’ hall we began, as always, with our little offerings to Miss Rachel. I delivered my annual speech as chief of the household below-stairs—a speech made on the same plan as great people use in opening Parliaments: you say near enough the same thing every year, and everybody listens as if they had never heard a word of it before. They grumble afterwards that it wasn’t new; then they grow hopeful for next year’s novelty. A simple governance, whether you rule Ministers or maids.

After breakfast Mr. Franklin Blake and I had our private conference—quiet talk, but not quiet minds—upon the Moonstone. The day had come when the jewel must be taken out of the bank at Frizinghall and put, by right and letter of the Colonel’s will, into Miss Rachel’s own hands.

Mr. Franklin was not at his best. Whether his sleep had been broken again, or whether he had been trying to press his suit with his cousin and had met discouragement, I can’t say. I only know that he turned the question of the Diamond as a cook turns a pancake—first this side, then the other—never satisfied it was browned as it should be. Twenty doubts in twenty minutes.

I held to the plain facts, like a man who knows his own pantry inventory. Nothing had happened to justify frightening my lady about the jewel; and nothing could alter Mr. Franklin’s legal obligation to deliver it. Twist and turn as he might, he ended where I began. We settled that he should ride to Frizinghall after luncheon, bring the Diamond back, and likely return in company with Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite and the two Miss Ablewhites, who might ride home with him.

With that arranged, he went back to Miss Rachel. The morning and a slice of afternoon were spent in their everlasting business of decorating the door—Penelope mixing colours on command, and my lady drifting in and out, handkerchief to her nose, trying to drive them from their labour. They used a deal of vehicle that day—strong stuff, sharp as vinegar, with a bite that climbed into the head and sat there, and a smell that overrode even honest kitchen odours. You could hear it, too, in a manner of speaking: the house’s usual hush had a thin, irritated edge to it, as if every footstep scraped.

It was three o’clock before they shed their aprons, loosed Penelope from service (the girl looking pale and queerly shiny about the eyes), and scrubbed themselves fit to be seen. They had done what they wanted: the door was finished on the birthday. Griffins, cupids, flowers, devices—beautiful, yes, but so crowded and so madly tangled that your brain felt as if it had been stirred with a spoon after you’d admired it. Penelope ended her part by being sick in the back-kitchen. I mention it not against Art, nor against the vehicle either. The stench leaves off when it dries. If Art asks a sacrifice, let it have it—even if the sufferer is my own daughter.

Mr. Franklin snatched a morsel at luncheon and rode off “to escort his cousins,” as he told my lady. To fetch the Moonstone, as was privately known to himself and me.

This being one of the high festivals when I take my post at the sideboard and command the waiting, my head was full of sober business while he was away. Wine to see to, men and women to review, the table-service to marshal—each thing in its place, like ingredients laid out before the fire is lit. When my arrangements were made, I retired a little to collect myself. A whiff of—you know what—and a turn at that certain book I’ve spoken of before, set my mind and body steady.

I must have slipped into something between thought and doze, for I was roused by the clatter of hoofs outside—a sudden burst of sound in the courtyard, the kind that makes a house’s very beams listen. The rhythm of it told you more than sight could: four riders, one with a groom’s seat behind, the whole company coming in brisk and not over-cautious. I went to the door and received them—Mr. Franklin with his three cousins, and one of old Mr. Ablewhite’s grooms attending.

Mr. Godfrey struck me as being out of his usual trim. He shook my hand kindly, admired me for “wearing so well,” and spoke as politely as ever; but there was a cloud over him. When I asked after his father’s health, he answered shortly, “Much as usual,” and looked away, as if the words had a taste he didn’t relish.

The two Miss Ablewhites, however, were cheerfulness enough for twenty. Great rosy creatures, yellow-haired and overflowing with health—so full of blood and spirits that the poor horses trembled under them. They jumped down without waiting to be helped, and bounced on the gravel as if they were made of india-rubber. Everything they said began with a large “O,” and everything they did ended with a bang. Their laughter was not mere noise; it was a kind of percussion, loud enough to drown the smaller sounds of the house—doors, whispers, the little breathing creaks of old wood—and for that reason I did not thank it. A house has its own proper music, and these young ladies played over it like brass over strings.

Under cover of their uproar I got a word with Mr. Franklin in the hall.

“Have you got the Diamond safe, sir?”

He nodded and tapped the breast-pocket of his coat.

“Have you seen anything of the Indians?”

“Not a glimpse.”

He asked for my lady, heard she was in the small drawing-room, and went there straight. In less than a minute the bell rang; Penelope was sent to tell Miss Rachel that Mr. Franklin wanted to speak to her.

About half an hour later, crossing the hall, I was stopped short by a burst of screams from the small drawing-room. I knew the tone of those screams—Miss Ablewhite’s favourite large “O”—so I was not alarmed; still, I went in on pretence of asking instructions about the dinner, to make sure no real mischief had happened.

There stood Miss Rachel at the table, like a person spell-bound, holding the Colonel’s Diamond in her hand. On either side of her the two “Bouncers” knelt, devouring it with their eyes and screaming in ecstasy whenever it answered the light with a new flash. At the opposite side of the table Mr. Godfrey stood clapping his hands like a child and singing out softly, “Exquisite! exquisite!” Mr. Franklin sat by the bookcase tugging at his beard, his gaze travelling anxiously towards the window. And at the window stood my lady herself, back turned upon the whole company, the extract from the Colonel’s will in her hand.

When I spoke, asking for my instructions, she faced me. I saw the family frown settling over her eyes and the family temper twitching at her mouth—those hereditary signs that come down like an old recipe, never altered, always reliable.

“Come to my room in half an hour,” she said. “I shall have something to say to you then.”

And she went out.

It was plain enough to me what worked in her mind. The same doubt that had troubled Mr. Franklin and me before—was the legacy of the Moonstone proof that she had wronged her brother cruelly, or proof that he was worse than she had thought? Serious questions, with her daughter standing there innocent as milk, holding the Colonel’s “birthday gift” in her hand.

Before I could leave, Miss Rachel stopped me, kindly, as she always was with an old servant who had been in the house since her birth.

“Look, Gabriel!” she said.

She held the jewel into a ray of sunlight pouring through the window, and the room changed as if a lamp had been uncovered in a dark pantry. Lord bless us—it was a Diamond indeed. As large, or near it, as a plover’s egg; and the light that streamed from it was like the harvest moon’s—yellow, deep, and drawing the eyes inward. When you looked down into it, you looked into a depth of colour that seemed to swallow seeing itself. The thing was small enough to hold between finger and thumb, and yet it had the air of being unfathomable, like sky.

We set it in the sun; then shut the light out of the room; and there it was again—shining of its own accord with a moony gleam, cold and dreadful in the dark. No wonder Miss Rachel was fascinated; no wonder her cousins screamed. The Diamond took hold on me too; I burst out with as large an “O” as any of the Bouncers.

The only one who kept his senses was Mr. Godfrey. He put an arm round each sister’s waist and, looking compassionately from the jewel to me, said, as if he were laying down kitchen-science,

“Carbon, Betteredge! Mere carbon, my good friend, after all!”

His object, I suppose, was instruction. The effect was to remind me of dinner. I hobbled off to my army of waiters downstairs. As I went, Mr. Godfrey—embracing his sisters and ogling Miss Rachel while he spoke—called after me:

“Dear old Betteredge, I have the truest regard for him!”

A convenient stock of love to draw on, that man had. Mr. Franklin was a savage by comparison.

At the end of the half-hour I presented myself in my lady’s room as ordered. What passed between us was, in the main, the same as what had passed between Mr. Franklin and me before—questions of the Colonel, of his will, of motives black or not black, and what was to be done with the gift he had sent into the house. I kept my own counsel about certain jugglers, for nothing had happened to justify alarming her on that head. When she dismissed me, I could see she took the darkest view possible of the Colonel’s intentions, and that she was bent on getting the Moonstone out of her daughter’s possession at the first opportunity.

Going back to my own quarters I was met by Mr. Franklin. He asked if I had seen Miss Rachel. I had not. Could I tell him where Mr. Godfrey was? I didn’t know; but I began to suspect, by the look in Mr. Franklin’s face, that cousin Godfrey might not be far from cousin Rachel. Mr. Franklin’s suspicions ran the same track. He tugged hard at his beard and shut himself into the library with a bang that had a world of meaning in it—an honest sound, at least, not like the polite noises people make when they mean something else.

I was not disturbed again in preparing for the birthday dinner until it was time to smarten myself for receiving the company. Just as I had got my white waistcoat on, Penelope came to my toilet, pretending to brush the little hair I had left and improve the tie of my white cravat. The girl was in high spirits and could hardly keep her news from spilling out, like sauce in an overfilled spoon.

She kissed the top of my bald head and whispered, “News for you, father! Miss Rachel has refused him.”

“Who’s ‘him’?” I asked.

“The ladies’ committee-man, father,” says Penelope. “A nasty sly fellow! I hate him for trying to supplant Mr. Franklin!”

I should certainly have reproved her for speaking so of an eminent philanthropic character—if she had not been at that moment tightening my cravat with all the force of her feelings in her fingers. I was never nearer to being strangled by my own child.

“I saw him take her away alone into the rose-garden,” she went on. “And I waited behind the holly to see how they came back. They went out arm-in-arm, both laughing. They came back separate, grave as grave could be, looking straight away from each other—no mistaking it. I never was more delighted in my life! There’s one woman in the world who can resist Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite, at any rate; and if I was a lady, I should be another!”

Here I should have protested again. But she had taken up the hair-brush, and all her triumph travelled into that. If you are bald you will know what I suffered; if you are not, thank God for your natural defence.

“Just on the other side of the holly,” Penelope said, “Mr. Godfrey came to a standstill. ‘You prefer,’ says he, ‘that I should stop here as if nothing had happened?’ Miss Rachel turned on him like lightning. ‘You have accepted my mother’s invitation,’ she said, ‘and you are here to meet her guests. Unless you wish to make a scandal in the house, you will remain, of course!’ She went on a few steps, then seemed to relent a little. ‘Let us forget what has passed, Godfrey,’ she said, ‘and let us remain cousins still.’ She gave him her hand. He kissed it—which I should call taking a liberty—and she left him. He stood a little by himself, head down, heel grinding a hole in the gravel; I never saw a man look more put out. ‘Awkward!’ he said between his teeth, when he looked up and went on to the house—‘very awkward!’ And the end of it is what I told you, father,” she cried, finishing me off with the hottest scarification of all. “Mr. Franklin’s the man!”

I got the hair-brush out of her hands and opened my lips to administer the reproof her language and conduct deserved. Before I could speak, the crash of carriage-wheels outside cut in and stopped me—the first of the dinner-company arriving, the sound rolling through the hall like a drum-beat. Penelope ran off at once.

I put on my coat and looked in the glass. My head was as red as a lobster; but in other respects I was as properly dressed for the evening’s ceremonies as any man need be. I got into the hall just in time to announce the first guests.

You needn’t feel particularly interested. Only the philanthropist’s father and mother—Mr. and Mrs. Ablewhite.

One after another the Ablewhites arrived and were folded into place until the dining-room held them all—four-and-twenty souls, family and followers together—like a harvest sheaf bound tight with manners. It was, to do it justice, a handsome muster when they had settled round the long table, glass catching candle, linen shining like new-milled flour; and the Rector of Frizinghall rose with his beautiful elocution and laid grace over us as neatly as sifted sugar.

I will not trouble you with a catalogue of the guests. The greater part of them vanish from my sight after this night, and (in my share of these matters at least) never come back again—except for two persons placed, by chance or fate, on either side of Miss Rachel.

She sat there as queen of the day, pale and proud in her white dress; and the birthday gift—more than any cake, more than any toast, more than any compliment—drew eyes to her bosom as naturally as scent draws bees. The Diamond had come to her without setting; but Mr. Franklin, being that universal genius who will make a tool out of anything and an instrument out of nothing, had contrived with neat fingers and a twist of silver wire to fix the Moonstone as a brooch. It lay against the white stuff like a bit of captured moonlight pinned to snow.

Everybody, of course, admired the prodigious size and the pure fire of it. But only the two who sat nearest said anything that went beyond the ordinary gasp of envy. On Miss Rachel’s left was Mr. Candy, our doctor from Frizinghall; on her right sat the celebrated Indian traveller, Mr. Murthwaite.

Mr. Candy was a pleasant little man to have by you—companionable, quick to laugh, quick to make you laugh too, though (if you ask me) overfond of his joke in season and out, and forever plunging into talk with strangers without testing the depth first. In company, that habit of his set people by the ears without his intending it; in practice, oddly enough, he was a safer hand—picking up discretion by instinct, so his enemies said, and proving right where grave doctors, full of carefulness, proved wrong.

His remark on the Diamond came out in his usual way—half profession, half mystification. He leaned toward Miss Rachel with a grave face that could never quite hide the twinkle at the edge of it, and entreated her, in the interests of science, to let him take the jewel home and burn it.

“We will first heat it, Miss Rachel,” says the doctor, “to such and such a degree; then we will expose it to a current of air; and, little by little—puff!—we evaporate the Diamond, and spare you a world of anxiety about the safe keeping of a valuable precious stone!”

My lady, who had been eating little and watching much, listened with a careworn look, as if she could have wished the doctor’s chemistry were no jest and her daughter’s courage zealous enough to sacrifice the gift for peace of mind.
