Mr. Murthwaite, on the other side, made a stranger contrast—long, lean, wiry, brown as if sun-dried by other climates, silent as a man whose ears have been taught by deserts and jungles to hoard sound. His eye, steady and attentive, seemed always to be listening as much as looking; and I have often thought there is a kind of hearing in the gaze of such men—an attention that takes the measure of a room as a farmer takes the measure of weather by the trees.

It was rumoured he was sick to death of our humdrum English talk and longing to be off again where the world speaks in unfamiliar tongues. I doubt if he uttered six words to any one all dinner, or drank even a single glass of wine. The Moonstone was the one thing that roused him. The fame of it, it seemed, had travelled even to those perilous Indian places where his own footsteps had been laid.

He looked at the Diamond so long in silence that Miss Rachel began to colour under his regard; then he spoke to her in his cool, immovable way, as if he were stating a fact about weather.

“If you ever go to India, Miss Verinder, don’t take your uncle’s birthday gift with you. A Hindoo diamond is sometimes part of a Hindoo religion. I know a certain city, and a certain temple in that city, where, dressed as you are now, your life would not be worth five minutes’ purchase.”

Miss Rachel, safe in England, was delighted—delighted!—to hear of her danger in India. The two Miss Ablewhites (whom I call the Bouncers, as I have called them before, for their way of bursting out) were more delighted still; down went their knives and forks with a crash, and out came together, like a chorus in a cheap song, “O! how interesting!” My lady fidgeted, and changed the subject as if she had been stung.

As the dinner went on, I began to feel, little by little, that our festival did not prosper as other festivals have prospered. Looking back now—by the light of all that followed—I am half inclined to believe the cursed Diamond cast a blight on the company, the way a bad smell will spoil a whole kitchen, though the roast be sound and the sauce made to perfection.

And mind you, our table was no poor one. There were dishes sent up that had cost good money and good thought; there were wines that might have coaxed a parson into a second glass and a miser into a smile. I plied them well, I can tell you. Being privileged as old Betteredge is privileged, I followed the unpopular dishes round with a confidential whisper—“Please to change your mind and try it; for I know it will do you good.” Nine times out of ten they changed their minds, out of regard for me they were pleased to say. It did no good. The talk ran into gaps of silence that made me feel personally responsible, as if I had under-seasoned the very air. When tongues did start again, they started innocently and fell into the worst possible mischief—like a cook who means well and drops vinegar where sugar should go.

Mr. Candy, for one, was more unlucky than I ever knew him.

Among the ladies sat worthy Mrs. Threadgall, widow of the late Professor. She talked of her husband perpetually, and never mentioned to strangers that he was dead. She seemed to think every able-bodied adult in England ought to know it of course. In one of the silences, somebody touched on the dry and rather nasty subject of human anatomy; and Mrs. Threadgall at once brought in the Professor, as usual, without saying he was deceased. Anatomy, she declared, had been his favourite recreation in his leisure hours.

Unluckily Mr. Candy, opposite, knew nothing of the Professor’s ten years in the grave. Being politeness itself, he seized the chance of being useful on the spot.

“They have got some remarkably fine skeletons lately at the College of Surgeons,” says Mr. Candy, across the table in a loud cheerful voice. “I strongly recommend the Professor, ma’am, when he next has an hour to spare, to pay them a visit.”

You might have heard a pin fall into the gravy. Out of respect to the Professor’s memory the company sat speechless. I was behind Mrs. Threadgall at the time, offering her, in my private way, a glass of hock. She dropped her head and said, very low:

“My beloved husband is no more.”

Mr. Candy heard nothing, and suspected less. On he went, louder and politer than ever.

“The Professor may not be aware,” says he, “that the card of a member of the College will admit him, on any day but Sunday, between the hours of ten and four.”

Mrs. Threadgall dropped her head right into her tucker and, lower still, repeated:

“My beloved husband is no more.”

I winked hard at Mr. Candy. Miss Rachel touched his arm. My lady looked unutterable things. All useless. The doctor’s good breeding drove him on like a horse with blinkers.

“I shall be delighted,” says he, “to send the Professor my card, if you will oblige me by mentioning his present address.”

“His present address, sir, is the grave,” cries Mrs. Threadgall, temper bursting out at last, emphasis and fury making the glasses ring again. “The Professor has been dead these ten years.”

“Oh, good Heavens!” says Mr. Candy.

Excepting the Bouncers, who burst into laughter as if a funeral were a fair, such a blank fell then on the company that you might have thought we were all going the same way as the Professor, and hailing him from the direction of the earth.

So much for Mr. Candy; and the rest were nearly as provoking, each in their own manner. When they ought to have spoken, they did not; when they did speak, they managed to speak at cross purposes, like a pair of fiddlers playing different tunes in the same room. Mr. Godfrey, who can be eloquent enough in public to make a collection-box tremble, declined to exert himself in private. Whether he was sulky or bashful after his discomfiture in the rose-garden, I cannot say. He kept his talk for the private ear of the lady of our family next to him—one of his committee-women; spiritually minded, with a fine show of collar-bone and a pretty taste in champagne. Dry, you understand, and plenty of it.

Being stationed close behind them at the sideboard, drawing corks and carving mutton, I picked up scraps of what the company lost. What they said about Charities I did not catch. When I had time to listen, they were beyond women to be confined and women to be rescued, and disputing on serious subjects, with the clink of glass and the soft, greedy sound of wine into crystal marking their pauses.

Religion, I understand Mr. Godfrey to say, meant love; and love meant religion; and earth was heaven a little the worse for wear; and heaven was earth done up again to look like new. Earth had some very objectionable people in it; but, to make amends, all the women in heaven would be members of a prodigious committee that never quarrelled, with all the men in attendance as ministering angels. Beautiful! beautiful! But why the mischief did he keep it all to his lady and himself, and let the rest of us sit chewing our words as well as our food?

You will tell me Mr. Franklin at least must have stirred them to better spirits.

Nothing of the sort.

He had quite recovered himself and was in wonderful force and animation—Penelope having, I suspect, informed him of Mr. Godfrey’s reception in the rose-garden. But talk as he might, nine times out of ten he pitched on the wrong subject or addressed the wrong person; so that he offended some and puzzled all. That foreign training of his—the French and German and Italian sides I have spoken of—came out at my lady’s hospitable board in a manner bewildering enough to curdle the best cream.

Imagine him discussing the lengths to which a married woman might let admiration go for a man who was not her husband—and putting it, too, in his clear-headed, witty French way—to the maiden aunt of the Vicar of Frizinghall. Imagine him shifting to the German side and telling the lord of the manor, while that great authority on cattle quoted his experience in breeding bulls, that experience, properly understood, counted for nothing; that the proper way to breed bulls was to look deep into your own mind, evolve the idea of a perfect bull, and produce him. Imagine our county member, heated at cheese and salad time about the spread of democracy, bursting out, “If we once lose our ancient safeguards, Mr. Blake, I beg to ask you, what have we got left?”—and Mr. Franklin answering from the Italian point of view, “We have got three things left, sir—Love, Music, and Salad.”

He terrified them with outbreaks like these; and when the English side of him turned up in its turn, he lost his foreign smoothness and spoke so downright on the subject of doctors that he actually put good-humoured little Mr. Candy into a rage.

The dispute began in Mr. Franklin being led—how, I forget—to admit he had slept badly of late. Mr. Candy told him his nerves were out of order and he ought to go through a course of medicine immediately. Mr. Franklin replied that a course of medicine and a course of groping in the dark were, in his estimation, one and the same thing. Mr. Candy hit back smartly that Mr. Franklin himself, constitutionally speaking, was groping in the dark after sleep and nothing but medicine could help him to find it. Mr. Franklin kept the ball up: he had often heard of the blind leading the blind, and now for the first time he knew what it meant.

So they went on—cut and thrust—till they were both hot, and the doctor, especially, lost self-control in defence of his profession. My lady was obliged to interfere and forbid the dispute. That act of authority put the last extinguisher on the company. The talk spurted up here and there for a minute or two, like damp wood trying to catch; then sank again into miserable lifelessness. The Devil—or the Diamond—possessed that dinner-party; and it was a relief to everybody when my mistress rose and gave the ladies the signal to withdraw, leaving the gentlemen over their wine.

I had just ranged the decanters in a row before old Mr. Ablewhite (who represented the master of the house), when a sound from the terrace startled me clean out of my company manners. Mr. Franklin and I looked at each other.

It was the Indian drum.

As I live by bread, here were the jugglers returning—returning with the return of the Moonstone to the house!

As they rounded the terrace corner into sight, I hobbled out to warn them off. But ill-luck had its own feet that night. The two Bouncers were beforehand with me—whizzing out like a pair of rockets, wild to see the Indians exhibit their tricks. Other ladies followed; the gentlemen came out on their side. Before you could say “Lord bless us,” the rogues were making their salaams and the Bouncers were kissing the pretty little boy.

Mr. Franklin got on one side of Miss Rachel and I took my place behind her. If our suspicions were right, there she stood, innocent as milk, showing the Indians the Diamond pinned in her dress—putting, as it were, the sacred thing under their very noses.

I cannot tell you what tricks they performed or how. Between vexation at the dinner and provocation at the rogues coming back at the very nick of time, I lost my head. The first thing I clearly remember is the sudden appearance of Mr. Murthwaite.

Skirting the half-circle of gentlefolks, he came quietly behind the jugglers and spoke on a sudden in their own language.

If he had pricked them with a bayonet, I doubt they could have started and turned on him with a more tigerish quickness than they did at the first words. The next moment they were bowing and salaaming in their politest, snakiest way. A few sentences passed in that unknown tongue; then Mr. Murthwaite withdrew as quietly as he had approached.

The chief Indian—acting interpreter—wheeled again toward the company. I noticed his coffee-coloured face had turned grey since Mr. Murthwaite spoke to him. He bowed to my lady and said the exhibition was over. The Bouncers, indescribably disappointed, burst out with a loud “O!” aimed at Mr. Murthwaite for stopping the performance. The chief Indian laid his hand humbly on his breast and repeated that the juggling was over. The little boy went round with the hat. The ladies withdrew to the drawing-room; the gentlemen (excepting Mr. Franklin and Mr. Murthwaite) returned to their wine. I and the footman saw the Indians safe off the premises.

Going back by way of the shrubbery, I smelt tobacco and found Mr. Franklin and Mr. Murthwaite walking slowly among the trees—the traveller smoking a cheroot. There is a particular sound among shrubs at night—the leaves rubbing like paper, the distant clink from the dining-room, a footfall softened by grass—that makes a man feel he is eavesdropping on the house itself. It did not comfort me.

Mr. Franklin beckoned me.

“This,” says he, presenting me, “is Gabriel Betteredge, the old servant and friend of our family of whom I spoke to you just now. Tell him, if you please, what you have just told me.”

Mr. Murthwaite took the cheroot from his mouth and leaned, weary as ever, against a tree trunk.

“Mr. Betteredge,” he said, “those three Indians are no more jugglers than you and I are.”

It struck me dumb. I asked if he had met them before.

“Never,” says he. “But I know what Indian juggling really is. All you have seen tonight is a very bad and clumsy imitation of it. Unless, after long experience, I am utterly mistaken, those men are high-caste Brahmins. I charged them with being disguised, and you saw how it told on them—clever as the Hindoo people are in concealing their feelings. There is a mystery about their conduct that I can’t explain. They have doubly sacrificed their caste—first, in crossing the sea; secondly, in disguising themselves as jugglers. In the land they live in, that is a tremendous sacrifice. There must be some very serious motive at the bottom of it, and some justification of no ordinary kind to plead for them, in recovery of their caste, when they return.”

He went on smoking, as if caste and sacrifice were no more than weather signs. Mr. Franklin, who seemed to me to veer inwardly between the different sides of himself before he spoke, broke the silence.

“I hesitate, Mr. Murthwaite, to trouble you with family matters in which you can have no interest, and which I am not very willing to speak of out of our own circle. But, after what you have said, I feel bound, in the interests of Lady Verinder and her daughter, to tell you something that may possibly put the clue into your hands. I speak in confidence. You will oblige me by not forgetting that.”

With that he told him all he had told me at the Shivering Sand. Even Mr. Murthwaite, immovable as he was, grew so interested that he let his cheroot go out.

When Mr. Franklin had done, he asked, “What does your experience say?”

“My experience,” answered the traveller, “says that you have had more narrow escapes in your life, Mr. Franklin Blake, than I have had in mine—and that is saying a great deal.”

Mr. Franklin stared. “Is it really as serious as that?”

“In my opinion it is,” said Mr. Murthwaite. “I can’t doubt, after what you have told me, that the restoration of the Moonstone to its place on the forehead of the Indian idol is the motive and the justification of that sacrifice of caste I spoke of. Those men will wait their opportunity with the patience of cats, and use it with the ferocity of tigers. How you have escaped them I can’t imagine.” He lit his cheroot again and stared hard at Mr. Franklin. “You have been carrying the Diamond backwards and forwards in London and here, and you are still alive. Let us account for it. It was daylight, both times, when you took the jewel out of the bank in London?”

“Broad daylight,” says Mr. Franklin.

“And plenty of people in the streets?”

“Plenty.”

“You arranged to arrive here at a certain time? It’s lonely country between this and the station. Did you keep your appointment?”

“No. I arrived four hours earlier.”

“I beg to congratulate you on that proceeding! When did you take the Diamond to the bank at the town here?”

“An hour after I brought it to this house—and three hours before anybody was prepared for seeing me.”

“I beg to congratulate you again! Did you bring it back here alone?”

“No. I happened to ride back with my cousins and the groom.”

“I beg to congratulate you for the third time! If you ever feel inclined to travel beyond the civilised limits, Mr. Blake, let me know, and I will go with you. You are a lucky man.”

I could not let such talk pass without speaking, for it did not square with my English ideas.

“You don’t really mean to say, sir,” I asked, “that they would have taken Mr. Franklin’s life, to get their Diamond, if he had given them the chance?”

“Do you smoke, Mr. Betteredge?” says the traveller.

“Yes, sir.”

“Do you care much for the ashes left in your pipe when you empty it?”

“No, sir.”

“In the country those men came from, they care just as much about killing a man as you care about emptying the ashes out of your pipe. If a thousand lives stood between them and the getting back of their Diamond—and if they thought they could destroy those lives without discovery—they would take them all. The sacrifice of caste is serious in India, if you like. The sacrifice of life is nothing.”

I gave it as my opinion they were a set of murdering thieves. Mr. Murthwaite gave it as his opinion they were a wonderful people. Mr. Franklin, offering no opinion at all, brought us back to what mattered.

“They have seen the Moonstone on Miss Verinder’s dress,” he said. “What is to be done?”

“What your uncle threatened to do,” answered Mr. Murthwaite. “Colonel Herncastle understood the people he had to deal with. Send the Diamond tomorrow—under guard of more than one man—to be cut up at Amsterdam. Make half a dozen diamonds of it instead of one. There is an end of its sacred identity as the Moonstone—and there is an end of the conspiracy.”

Mr. Franklin turned to me. “There is no help for it. We must speak to Lady Verinder tomorrow.”

“What about tonight, sir?” I asked. “Suppose the Indians come back?”

“The Indians won’t risk coming back tonight,” said Mr. Murthwaite. “The direct way is hardly ever the way they take to anything—least of all to a matter like this, where the slightest mistake might be fatal to their reaching their end.”

“But suppose the rogues are bolder than you think?” I persisted.

“In that case,” says Mr. Murthwaite, “let the dogs loose. Have you got any big dogs in the yard?”

“Two, sir. A mastiff and a bloodhound.”

“They will do. In the present emergency, Mr. Betteredge, the mastiff and the bloodhound have one great merit—they are not likely to be troubled with your scruples about the sanctity of human life.”

As he fired that shot at me, the strumming of the piano reached us from the drawing-room—thin and tinkling through the closed windows, as if the house were trying to play at calm while danger walked under the trees. Mr. Murthwaite threw away his cheroot and took Mr. Franklin’s arm to go back to the ladies. I followed, and noticed the sky clouding over fast, the air thickening with the smell of wet earth to come.

Mr. Murthwaite noticed it too. He looked round at me in his dry, droning way and said:

“The Indians will want their umbrellas, Mr. Betteredge, tonight!”

It was all very well for him to joke. He was an eminent traveller; his life had led him into playing ducks and drakes with his own skin among outlandish risks. Mine had not. I went into my own little room and sat down in my chair in a perspiration, wondering helplessly what was to be done next.

Other men, in such a state, might have worked themselves up into a fever. I ended differently. I lit my pipe, and took a turn at Robinson Crusoe.

Before five minutes were over, I came upon this amazing bit—page one hundred and sixty-one:

“Fear of Danger is ten thousand times more terrifying than Danger itself, when apparent to the Eyes; and we find the Burthen of Anxiety greater, by much, than the Evil which we are anxious about.”

When the last wheels had hissed away from the gravel and the house drew in its breath again, I went back to the inner hall. Samuel had already planted himself at the side-table as if the decanters were a parish to be governed, and he, the rector: brandy in one hand, soda in the other, both bright as new coin under the lamps.

My lady and Miss Rachel came out of the drawing-room, with the two gentlemen behind them. Mr. Godfrey accepted the brandy-and-soda, the bubbles pricking up his spirits as briskly as a charitable subscription-list. Mr. Franklin took nothing; he dropped into a chair with the look of a man who had been talked at till his thoughts were worn thin. Some people are sustained by company; some are emptied by it. He was the emptied sort.

My lady, turning to bid them good-night, fixed her eyes—hard, steady, and not altogether loving—on the Colonel’s legacy blazing in her daughter’s dress, that cold lunar shine which, even by lamplight, had a way of making other jewels look like common glass.

“Rachel,” she said, “where are you going to put your Diamond tonight?”

Miss Rachel was in those high spirits that run away with a young lady’s sense and leave her nonsense to do duty for it. She began with declaring she didn’t know; then that she would put it, of course, on the dressing-table with her other things; then—remembering how it seemed to carry its own pale radiance—she protested it might shine of itself in the dark and turn her blood to water. At last she remembered the Indian cabinet in her sitting-room, and instantly decided that an Indian diamond should sleep in an Indian cabinet, so that the two native wonders might admire each other in private.

My lady cut the froth off her talk, as you skim the scum from a pot before it boils over.

“My dear, your Indian cabinet has no lock.”

“Good Heavens, mamma!” Miss Rachel cried. “Is this an hotel? Are there thieves in the house?”

My lady took no more notice of that than she would of a child insisting the moon is following it down the road. She wished the gentlemen good-night, kissed her daughter, and then said—quietly, with that quiet that does not invite refusal:

“Why not let me keep the Diamond for you tonight?”

Miss Rachel drew back, as if you had asked her to hand over a new doll when she was ten years old. My lady saw, as I did, that there was no reasoning with her in that temper.

“Come into my room, Rachel, the first thing tomorrow morning,” she said. “I shall have something to say to you.”

Then she left us, slowly, with her thoughts marching on before her like troops—none of us welcome among them.

Miss Rachel was next to take leave. She shook hands with Mr. Godfrey, who had drifted to the other end of the hall and was standing before a picture, looking at it with the solemn attention he usually reserved for good works. Then she turned back to Mr. Franklin, still sitting weary and silent in his corner.

What passed in words I can’t say. But I happened to be near the old oak frame that holds our great looking-glass, and I saw her, reflected in it, slyly slip out the locket Mr. Franklin had given her from the bosom of her dress, show it to him for one moment, and smile—a smile that had meaning in it, and more than common meaning too—before she went off light-footed to bed. That little glimpse shook my confidence in my own judgment. It set me thinking Penelope might be right, after all, about the state of her young lady’s affections.

As soon as Miss Rachel had left him eyes to see with, Mr. Franklin noticed me. His humour, which shifted like wind in a tree-top, had already veered round upon the matter of the Indians.

“Betteredge,” he said, “I’m half inclined to think I took Mr. Murthwaite too seriously in the shrubbery. Perhaps he has been trying his traveller’s tales on us. Do you really mean to let the dogs loose?”

“I’ll take off their collars, sir,” I answered, “and leave them free to take a turn in the night—if they smell a reason for it.”

“All right. We’ll see what is to be done tomorrow,” he said. “I am not disposed to alarm my aunt without a very pressing reason. Good-night.”

He looked so pale and worn as he took his candle that I ventured to advise a drop of brandy-and-water as a night-cap. Mr. Godfrey, coming up at that moment, backed me with the pleasant firmness he could always manage when there was no real resistance to meet. He pressed Mr. Franklin, in the friendliest manner, to take something before going upstairs.

I note these trifles because it pleased me, after all I had seen and heard that day, to observe the two gentlemen were still on good terms. Whatever words had been exchanged in the drawing-room, whatever rivalry had been at work about Miss Rachel, it had made no lasting breach. Men of their station have this merit: they keep their quarrels as clean as their linen, and hang them out of sight.

Mr. Franklin declined and went up with Mr. Godfrey, their rooms being next door. On the landing, either his cousin persuaded him, or he changed again—as he so often did.

“Perhaps I may want it in the night,” he called down. “Send up some brandy-and-water.”

I sent Samuel with it, and went out to the kennels. The dogs, unbuckled at that hour, lost their heads with astonishment and sprang on me like puppies. The rain soon cooled their rejoicing. They lapped a little water and crawled back to their straw. The night smelt of soaked earth, bruised leaves, and wet stone; the sound of it was all drip and rush and the long sighing in the trees as the wind tried its strength. For the moment it still poured; the lawn was a sponge underfoot. But I saw, in a lifting of the cloud and a thinning of the black, signs of a break.

Samuel and I went over the house shutting up as usual. This time I trusted nothing to my deputy. I tried every bolt myself, listened for each click, and set my hand on the wood to feel it firm. All was safe and fast when I got my old bones to bed, between midnight and one.

The worries of the day had been a little too much for me. At any rate, I had a touch of Mr. Franklin’s malady. It was sunrise before sleep came at last. All the time I lay awake, the house was as quiet as the grave—nothing stirring but the rain’s steady splash and the wind’s breath through the trees. Sound is a kind of guard in a house; when it keeps to its proper duties—the clock, the settling timber, the distant sea of leaves—you feel safe. But when it is all one monotone, like water on water, it deadens the ear, and a man begins to distrust even silence, because it can hide a footfall in its folds as easily as it hides a whisper.

At half-past seven I woke. The weather had turned as if a curtain had been drawn back: fine sunshine, washed air, and birds taking possession of the morning in a hundred bright notes. The clock struck eight. I was just going out to chain the dogs again when I heard a sudden whisking of petticoats behind me on the stairs—quick, frightened, and out of place in that clear morning music.

I turned, and there was Penelope flying down after me like one distraught.

“Father!” she screamed. “Come upstairs, for God’s sake! The Diamond is gone!”

“Are you out of your mind?” I asked.

“Gone!” says Penelope. “Gone, nobody knows how. Come up and see.”

She dragged me into Miss Rachel’s sitting-room, which opened into her bedroom. There, on the threshold of the bedroom door, stood Miss Rachel—white as the dressing-gown she wore, her face looking as if the colour had been rinsed out of it in the night’s rain. The two doors of the Indian cabinet stood wide open. One drawer was pulled out as far as it would go, like a tongue thrust out in mockery.

“Look!” Penelope cried. “I saw Miss Rachel put the Diamond in that drawer last night.”

I went to it. The drawer was empty.

“Is this true, miss?” I asked.

With a look not like herself, and a voice not like her own—flat, frightened, and yet stubborn—Miss Rachel answered as Penelope had done:

“The Diamond is gone!”

And with those words she withdrew into her bedroom and shut and locked the door.

Before we knew which way to turn, my lady came in, hearing my voice, and asked what had happened. When she learnt it, she seemed turned to stone. She went straight to Miss Rachel’s door and insisted on being admitted. Miss Rachel let her in.

The alarm ran through the house like fire in dry straw. Mr. Godfrey was the first of the gentlemen to appear. He held up his hands in bewilderment, which didn’t speak much for his natural strength of mind. Mr. Franklin, from whom I had counted on clear counsel, was for once as helpless as his cousin. For a wonder he had slept well—and the unaccustomed luxury had, as he said himself, stupefied him. But when he had swallowed his coffee—always taken, foreign fashion, hours before breakfast—his wits began to come back to him, and he took hold of the matter, resolute and quick.

He sent for the servants, ordered that all the lower doors and windows (except the front door, which I had opened) were to remain exactly as they had been since we locked up. He then proposed to my lady and to us to make sure the Diamond hadn’t slipped somewhere out of sight—behind the cabinet, or down behind the table. We searched there and found nothing. He questioned Penelope and got no more from her than she had already told me. Then he suggested we must try Miss Rachel, and sent Penelope to knock.

My lady answered, closing the door behind her. The next moment we heard it locked on the inside by Miss Rachel. My mistress came out among us, sorely puzzled and distressed.

“The loss of the Diamond seems to have quite overwhelmed Rachel,” she said. “She shrinks, in the strangest manner, from speaking of it even to me. It is impossible you can see her for the present.”

Having added that to our perplexities, my lady recovered herself, as she always did, and spoke with decision.

“I suppose there is no help for it,” she said quietly. “I suppose I have no alternative but to send for the police.”

“And the first thing for the police to do,” Mr. Franklin added, catching at the idea as if it were a rope, “is to lay hands on the Indian jugglers who performed here last night.”

My lady and Mr. Godfrey started, surprised, knowing nothing of what Mr. Franklin and I knew.

“I can’t stop to explain now,” Mr. Franklin went on. “I can only tell you the Indians have stolen the Diamond. Give me a letter of introduction to one of the magistrates at Frizinghall—merely saying I represent your interests—and let me ride off with it instantly. Our chance may depend on not wasting one minute.”

He put pen, ink, and paper before my lady. She wrote what he wanted—somewhat unwillingly, as it seemed to me. If it had been possible to overlook the loss of twenty thousand pounds, I do believe—with my lady’s opinion of her late brother and her distrust of his gift—it would have been privately a relief to her to let the Moonstone go.

I went out with Mr. Franklin to the stables, and took the chance to ask how the Indians could have got into the house.

“One of them might have slipped into the hall in the confusion when the dinner company were going away,” he said. “He may have been under the sofa while my aunt and Rachel were talking. He only had to wait till the house was quiet—and there it would be in the cabinet, to be had for the taking.”

With that, he called to the groom to open the gate and galloped off.

It sounded rational enough—until you set it against the facts. How had the thief got out again? I had found the front door locked and bolted as I left it. The other doors and windows were still fast. The dogs—if he dropped from an upper window, how did he escape them? Drugged meat? As that crossed my mind, the dogs themselves came tearing round a corner, rolling each other on the wet grass in such roaring health that I could hardly bring them to reason and chain them up. The more I turned it over, the less Mr. Franklin’s notion satisfied me.

We had our breakfasts. Whatever happens in a house—robbery or murder—it doesn’t matter, you must have your breakfast. There are domestic rites that go on as steadily as kneading and baking: they bind the living to the living, and keep the mind from running wild. The clink of crockery, the smell of tea, the scrape of a knife on toast—poor things, perhaps, against a calamity; and yet, without them, the day would have no spine.

Afterward my lady sent for me. I was compelled to tell her what I had concealed about the Indians and their plot. Being a woman of high courage, she soon got over the first start of it. Her mind was more perturbed about her daughter than about any heathen conspiracy.

“You know how odd Rachel is,” she said, “and how differently she behaves sometimes from other girls. But I have never seen her so strange and so reserved as she is now. The loss of her jewel seems almost to have turned her brain. Who would have thought that horrible Diamond could have laid such a hold on her in so short a time?”

It was strange. Miss Rachel was not commonly mad after trinkets as many girls are. Yet there she was, locked up, inconsolable. And it wasn’t only she who was thrown out of the regular groove. Mr. Godfrey—though professionally a kind of consoler-general—seemed at a loss for his own resources. With no company to amuse him and no chance of trying his experience of women in distress on Miss Rachel, he wandered about house and gardens in an uneasy, aimless way, debating whether to relieve us of him as a guest or to stay on the chance his humble services might be of use. He decided it was perhaps most customary to remain. Circumstances try the metal a man is made of; Mr. Godfrey, tried by circumstances, showed himself weaker metal than I had thought.

The women-servants, except Rosanna Spearman—who kept by herself—took to whispering in corners and staring suspiciously at nothing. I myself was fidgety and ill-tempered. The cursed Moonstone had turned us all upside down—like a pot set to boil with no lid, so that every small bubble shows itself and makes a fuss.

A little before eleven Mr. Franklin came back. He had left at a gallop; he returned at a walk. He had gone away like iron; he came back stuffed with cotton.

“Well,” says my lady, “are the police coming?”

“Yes,” says Mr. Franklin. “They said they would follow me in a fly—Superintendent Seegrave, of the local force, and two of his men. A mere form. The case is hopeless.”

“What! have the Indians escaped, sir?” I asked.

“The poor ill-used Indians have been most unjustly put in prison,” says Mr. Franklin. “They are as innocent as the babe unborn. My idea of one being hidden in the house has ended, like my ideas generally, in smoke. It’s been proved—simply impossible.”

He sat down and explained. He had laid the case before the magistrate at Frizinghall, who had sent for the police. Inquiry showed the Indians had not attempted to leave town. They had been seen returning with their boy between ten and eleven, which proved they had walked straight back after performing on our terrace. Later, at midnight, the police had searched the common lodging-house where they lived and had seen all three there again, with the boy. Soon after midnight I had shut up our house safe. It was evidence enough to break any suspicion. The magistrate said there was not even a case against them so far—yet, as it was possible something might still turn up, he would contrive, by committing them as rogues and vagabonds, to keep them under lock and key for a week. They had ignorantly done something in town—what it was I forget—that just brought them within the law. Every human institution, justice included, will stretch if you pull it the right way. The magistrate was an old friend of my lady’s, and the Indians were “committed” as soon as court opened.

So the Indian clue broke in our hands. If the jugglers were innocent, who, in the name of wonder, had taken the Moonstone out of Miss Rachel’s drawer?

Ten minutes later, to our relief, Superintendent Seegrave arrived. He reported passing Mr. Franklin on the terrace sitting in the sun, warning the police as they went by that the investigation was hopeless before it had begun.

For a family in our situation, Mr. Seegrave was the most comforting officer you could wish to see. Tall and portly, military in manner, commanding in voice, resolute in eye, and buttoned into a grand frock-coat up to a leather stock—he looked like a man with a warrant in his pocket for mischief itself. He ordered his two inferior men about with such severity that the servants began to believe, in sheer self-defence, that a discovery must be made.

He went round the premises, out and in, and satisfied himself no thieves had broken in from outside. The robbery, therefore, must have been committed by some person in the house. I leave you to imagine what that did among the servants. He decided to begin with the boudoir, then examine the servants. At the same time he posted one of his men on the staircase leading to the servants’ bedrooms, with orders to let nobody pass.

At that, the women went distracted. They bounced from corners, whisked upstairs in a body to Miss Rachel’s room—Rosanna Spearman swept along among them—and burst in upon the Superintendent, all looking equally guilty, demanding to know which of them he suspected at once.

Mr. Seegrave proved equal to them. He faced them with his resolute eye, and cowed them with his military voice.

“Now, then, you women—downstairs again, every one of you. I won’t have you here. Look!”

He pointed suddenly to a little smear of decorative paint on Miss Rachel’s door, at the outer edge just under the lock.

“See what mischief the petticoats of some of you have done already. Clear out!”

Rosanna Spearman, nearest to him and nearest the smear, set the example and slipped off at once to her work. The rest followed. The Superintendent finished his examination and, making nothing of it, asked who had first discovered the robbery. Penelope had. Penelope was sent for.

He was a little too sharp with her at starting.

“Now, young woman, attend to me, and mind you speak the truth.”

Penelope flamed up. “I’ve never been taught to tell lies, Mr. Policeman! And if father can stand there and hear me accused of falsehood and thieving, and my own bedroom shut against me, and my character taken away—which is all a poor girl has left—he’s not the good father I take him for!”

A timely word from me set justice and Penelope on a pleasanter footing. The questions and answers went smoothly and ended in nothing: she had seen Miss Rachel put the Diamond in the drawer last thing at night; she went in with the tea at eight and found the drawer open and empty; she alarmed the house.

The Superintendent next asked to see Miss Rachel. Penelope carried the request through the door. The answer came back the same way.

“I have nothing to tell the policeman—I can’t see anybody.”

Mr. Seegrave looked surprised and offended. I told him my young lady was ill and begged him to wait. We went downstairs and met Mr. Godfrey and Mr. Franklin crossing the hall.

As inmates, the two gentlemen were examined. They knew nothing. Had they heard any suspicious noise? Nothing but the patter of rain. Had I, lying awake longer, heard anything? Nothing. Released, Mr. Franklin whispered to me, still clinging to his helpless view:

“That man will be of no earthly use. Superintendent Seegrave is an ass.”

Released in his turn, Mr. Godfrey whispered:

“Evidently a most competent person. Betteredge, I have the greatest faith in him!”

Many men, many opinions.

Mr. Seegrave’s next step took him back to the boudoir, with Penelope and me following. He wanted to discover whether any furniture had been moved in the night—his former search not satisfying him on that point. We were still poking among chairs and tables when the bedroom door suddenly opened.

After denying herself to everybody, Miss Rachel, to our astonishment, walked straight into the midst of us of her own accord. She took up her garden hat from a chair; then she went directly to Penelope and put a question to her.

Thursday night went by like a pot left too long at the edge of the range: the heat was there, the expectation was there, and yet nothing came to a boil. Then Friday morning arrived and, with it, two morsels of news dropped on the table together—one stale, one scalding.

First: the baker’s man swore he had met Rosanna Spearman the afternoon before—thick veil down, walking towards Frizinghall by the moor-footpath. Rosanna, with her poor twisted shoulder, was as easy to know as a burnt loaf is to smell. And yet the man must have mistaken her; for Rosanna, as I knew it, had been upstairs all Thursday afternoon, ill in her room.

Second: the postman brought word of Mr. Candy. He had joked on the birthday night, in the wet, that a doctor’s skin was waterproof. The rain had answered him. He had taken a chill and was now down with fever—light-headed, talking delirious nonsense as fluently, poor little man, as ever he talked it in his right mind. We pitied him honestly. Mr. Franklin pitied him too—but with Miss Rachel in his thoughts, as if the girl’s spirit, kept too long on edge by the Moonstone’s loss, might soon need a physician’s calm hand more than any of us did.

Breakfast had scarcely settled when another message arrived—this one sharp as a knife on a board. A telegram from Mr. Blake the elder: he had, with the Commissioner’s help, secured the right man. Sergeant Cuff. He would come down from London by the morning train.

At the name, Mr. Franklin started as if he had heard a pot crack.

“I begin to hope we are seeing the end of our anxieties,” he said. “If half the stories I’ve heard are true, there isn’t his equal in England for unravelling a mystery.”

Excitement ran through the house like the first good scent of broth through a cold kitchen. Superintendent Seegrave, hearing Cuff was expected, shut himself away with pen, ink, and paper—making notes for the report he knew would be demanded of him. For my own part, I should have liked to go to the station; but my lady’s carriage was not to be put into harness for any man alive—not even a celebrated detective—and the pony-chaise was needed later for Mr. Godfrey, who regretted leaving at such a time but had a Ladies’ Charity in difficulties waiting his counsel in town. He agreed to stay till the last train, to hear what London’s clever man would say—then off he must go.

When the hour came, I went down to the gate.

A fly from the railway rolled up as I reached the lodge; out stepped a grizzled elderly man, miserably lean, as if life had pared him down to the bone. He wore decent black and a white cravat; his face was sharp as a hatchet, his skin yellow and dry as a leaf left too long in an autumn hedge. His eyes—steely light grey—had a trick of meeting yours as if they expected you to produce something you hadn’t even admitted to yourself. He walked softly; his voice fell melancholy; his long fingers hooked like claws. Parson, undertaker, or any other gloomy trade might have suited his look—anything, in short, except what he was.

“Is this Lady Verinder’s?” he asked.

“Yes, sir.”

“I am Sergeant Cuff.”

“This way, sir, if you please.”

On the way I gave him my name and place in the household, so he might speak freely to me of the robbery. But he said nothing of it. He admired the grounds, said the sea air was brisk and refreshing. I wondered—privately—how such a man had got such a name. We walked together with the constrained caution of two strange dogs coupled to one chain.

Lady Verinder, I learned, was in one of the conservatories. We went round to the back gardens and sent a servant for her. While we waited, Sergeant Cuff looked through the evergreen arch on the left, spied the rosery, and walked into it with the first sign of interest he had shown.

To the gardener’s astonishment and my own disgust, the celebrated policeman proved a mine of learning on what I had always thought a trumpery subject.

“Ah, you’ve got the right exposure here to the south and sou’-west,” he said, wagging his grizzled head; and the melancholy voice took a thin streak of pleasure, as if the scent of living things softened it. “This is the shape for a rosery—nothing like a circle set in a square. Yes, yes; with walks between all the beds. But they oughtn’t to be gravel walks like these. Grass, Mr. Gardener—grass walks between your roses; gravel’s too hard for them. That’s a sweet pretty bed of white roses and blush roses. They always mix well together, don’t they? Here’s the white musk rose, Mr. Betteredge—our old English rose holding up its head along with the best and newest of them. Pretty dear!”

He fondled the musk rose with those lank fingers, talking to it as if it were a child whose name mattered. I could not help thinking: This is the man to recover Miss Rachel’s Diamond and find out the thief?

“You seem fond of roses, Sergeant?” I said.

“I haven’t much time to be fond of anything,” he answered. “But when I have a moment’s fondness to bestow, most times, Mr. Betteredge, the roses get it. I began my life among them in my father’s nursery garden, and I shall end my life among them, if I can. One of these days—please God—I shall retire from catching thieves and try my hand at growing roses. There will be grass walks, Mr. Gardener, between my beds.” The gravel seemed to offend him like grit between the teeth.

“It seems an odd taste, sir,” I ventured, “for a man in your line.”

“If you will look about you (which most people won’t do), you will see that the nature of a man’s tastes is most times as opposite as possible to the nature of a man’s business. Show me two things more opposite than a rose and a thief; and I’ll correct my tastes accordingly—if it isn’t too late at my time of life.”

He lifted his head. “Here’s a lady coming. Is it Lady Verinder?”

He had seen her before either I or the gardener—though we knew which way to watch, and he did not. That, more than all his rose-talk, told me there was metal under the withered skin.

Lady Verinder appeared—embarrassed, for the first time in all my experience of her, at an interview with a stranger. Something in the man’s appearance, or in his errand, put her a little at a loss. He put her at ease directly by turning to business as if business were a familiar domestic routine, like carving or pouring tea.

Had anyone else been employed about the robbery before him? Yes—another person had been called in and was now in the house. He begged leave to speak to that person before anything else.

My lady led the way back. Before following, the Sergeant could not resist one last sour look at the rosery paths.

“Get her ladyship to try grass,” he said to the gardener. “No gravel! no gravel!”

Why Superintendent Seegrave should have seemed to shrink several sizes when presented to Sergeant Cuff, I cannot explain. The fact is plain enough. They retired together, shut up for a weary long time. When they came out, Mr. Superintendent was excited; Mr. Sergeant was yawning.

“The Sergeant wishes to see Miss Verinder’s sitting-room,” said Seegrave, with pomp and eagerness. “The Sergeant may have questions to ask. Attend the Sergeant, if you please!”

While I was being ordered about, I looked at Cuff. He looked at Seegrave in that quiet, expecting way—like a man waiting to see whether another will serve him sense or nonsense. I will not say he watched for his brother officer’s appearance as an Ass; I will say I suspected it.

I led them upstairs. Sergeant Cuff went softly all over the Indian cabinet and all round the boudoir, asking questions now and then of Mr. Superintendent, and continually of me—questions whose drift I believe was equally unintelligible to both of us. In due course he came to the door and faced the decorative painting you know of. He laid one lean finger on the small smear just under the lock—the one Superintendent Seegrave had noticed while driving the women-servants out the previous morning.

“That’s a pity,” said Cuff. “How did it happen?”

I explained about the women crowding in, and their petticoats doing the mischief.

“Superintendent Seegrave ordered them out, sir,” I added, “before they did any more harm.”

“Right!” said Seegrave in his military way. “I ordered them out. The petticoats did it, Sergeant—the petticoats did it.”

“Did you notice which petticoat did it?” asked Cuff—still addressing me.

“No, sir.”

He turned on Seegrave. “You noticed, I suppose?”

Mr. Superintendent was a little taken aback. “I can’t charge my memory, Sergeant—a mere trifle.”

Sergeant Cuff looked at him as he had looked at the gravel walks—displeased, and certain.

“I made a private inquiry last week, Mr. Superintendent. At one end of the inquiry there was a murder, and at the other end there was a spot of ink on a tablecloth that nobody could account for. In all my experience along the dirtiest ways of this dirty little world, I have never met with such a thing as a trifle yet. Before we go a step further we must see the petticoat that made the smear, and we must know for certain when that paint was wet.”

Seegrave, sulky under the set-down, asked if he should summon the women. Cuff considered, sighed, and shook his head.

“No. We’ll take the matter of the paint first. It’s a question of Yes or No with the paint—which is short. It’s a question of petticoats with the women—which is long. What o’clock was it when the servants were in this room yesterday morning? Eleven—eh? Is there anybody in the house who knows whether that paint was wet or dry at eleven yesterday morning?”

“Her ladyship’s nephew, Mr. Franklin Blake, knows,” I said.

“Is the gentleman in the house?”

He was close at hand—waiting, as eager as a man longing to be called to table, for his first chance of being introduced to the great Cuff. In half a minute he was in the room, giving his evidence.

“That door, Sergeant,” he said, “has been painted by Miss Verinder, under my inspection, with my help, and in a vehicle of my own composition. The vehicle dries whatever colours may be used with it, in twelve hours.”

“Do you remember when the smeared bit was done?” asked Cuff.

“Perfectly. That was the last morsel of the door to be finished. We wanted to get it done on Wednesday last—and I myself completed it by three in the afternoon, or soon after.”

“Today is Friday,” said Cuff to Seegrave. “Let us reckon back. At three on Wednesday afternoon, that bit was completed. The vehicle dried it in twelve hours—that is to say, by three on Thursday morning. At eleven on Thursday morning you held your inquiry here. Take three from eleven, and eight remains. That paint had been eight hours dry, Mr. Superintendent, when you supposed the women-servants’ petticoats smeared it.”

That was the first knock-down blow. If Seegrave had not suspected poor Penelope, I might have pitied him.

Having disposed of the paint, Sergeant Cuff abandoned his brother officer as a bad job and addressed himself to Mr. Franklin as the more promising assistant.

“It’s quite on the cards, sir,” he said, “that you have put the clue into our hands.”

As he spoke, the bedroom door opened, and Miss Rachel came out among us suddenly.

She went straight to Cuff, not seeming to notice—or care—that he was a stranger.

“Did you say,” she asked, pointing to Mr. Franklin, “that he had put the clue into your hands?”

“This is Miss Verinder,” I whispered behind the Sergeant.

“That gentleman, miss,” said Cuff, his steely eyes studying her face as carefully as if her expression were a handwriting sample, “has possibly put the clue into our hands.”

For one moment she tried to look at Mr. Franklin—I say tried, for she looked away again before their eyes met. Something was disturbed in her. Colour rose, then fled; and with the pallor came a new look, which startled me—like a door in the mind swinging open on a room you did not know existed.

“Having answered your question,” said Cuff, “I beg leave to make an inquiry in my turn. There is a smear on the painting of your door. Do you happen to know when it was done? or who did it?”

Instead of replying, she went on with her own questions, as if she had not heard.

“Are you another police-officer?” she asked.

“I am Sergeant Cuff, miss, of the Detective Police.”

“Do you think a young lady’s advice worth having?”

“I shall be glad to hear it, miss.”

“Do your duty by yourself—and don’t allow Mr. Franklin Blake to help you!”

She spoke with such spite, such savage ill-will towards Mr. Franklin, that—though I had known her from a baby, though I loved and honoured her next to my lady—I was ashamed of Miss Rachel for the first time in my life.

Cuff’s eyes never moved from her face. “Thank you, miss. Do you happen to know anything about the smear? Might you have done it by accident yourself?”

“I know nothing about the smear.”

With that she turned away and shut herself into her bedroom again. This time I heard what Penelope had heard before—her burst of crying, the private storm behind the closed door.

I could not bring myself to look at the Sergeant; I looked at Mr. Franklin. He seemed more distressed than I was.

“I told you I was uneasy about her,” he said. “And now you see why.”

“Miss Verinder appears to be a little out of temper about the loss of her Diamond,” remarked Cuff. “It’s a valuable jewel. Natural enough! natural enough!”

A cold shudder ran through me that I could not then name. The excuse I had made for her—made again by a stranger, without any personal interest in softening her behaviour—left me with the sense that Cuff had seen something in Miss Rachel, and heard something from her, that lit the case in a new and horrid way.

“A young lady’s tongue is a privileged member, sir,” said Cuff to Mr. Franklin. “Let us forget what has passed, and go straight on. Thanks to you, we know when the paint was dry. The next thing to discover is when the paint was last seen without that smear. You have got a head on your shoulders—and you understand what I mean.”

“I think I do,” said Mr. Franklin, steadying himself. “The more we narrow the question of time, the more we narrow the field of inquiry.”

“That’s it, sir. Did you notice your work here on Wednesday afternoon, after you had done it?”

Mr. Franklin shook his head. “I can’t say I did.”

“Did you?” Cuff asked, turning to me.

“I can’t say I did either, sir.”

“Who was the last person in the room—the last thing—on Wednesday night?”

“Miss Rachel, I suppose, sir.”

Mr. Franklin put in, “Or possibly your daughter, Betteredge.” He explained to the Sergeant that my daughter was Miss Verinder’s maid.

“Mr. Betteredge, ask your daughter to step up. Stop!” Cuff said, drawing me to the window out of earshot. “Your Superintendent has made a pretty full report to me of the manner in which he has managed this case. Among other things, he has, by his own confession, set the servants’ backs up. It’s very important to smooth them down again. Tell your daughter, and tell the rest of them, these two things, with my compliments: First, that I have no evidence before me, yet, that the Diamond has been stolen; I only know that the Diamond has been lost. Second, that my business here with the servants is simply to ask them to lay their heads together and help me to find it.”

My experience of the women-servants—when Seegrave had laid his embargo on their rooms—helped me here.

“May I make so bold, Sergeant, as to tell the women a third thing?” I asked. “Are they free, with your compliments, to fidget up and downstairs, and whisk in and out of their bedrooms, if the fit takes them?”

“Perfectly free,” said Cuff.

“That will smooth them down, sir,” I said, “from the cook to the scullion.”

“Go, and do it at once, Mr. Betteredge.”

I did it in under five minutes. Only one difficulty: when I came to the part about the bedrooms, it took a stiff exertion of my authority to prevent the entire female household from following me and Penelope upstairs—volunteer witnesses in a fever of helpfulness.

Sergeant Cuff seemed to approve of Penelope. He grew a trifle less dreary; his face took on something like what I had seen in the rosery when he found the white musk rose. Here is my daughter’s evidence, as drawn out by the Sergeant. She gave it prettily, I think—though there: she is my child all over; nothing of her mother in her, Lord bless you, nothing of her mother in her.

Penelope examined: She had taken a lively interest in the painting on the door and had helped to mix the colours. She noticed the bit under the lock because it was the last bit done. She had seen it some hours afterwards without a smear. She had left it, as late as twelve at night, without a smear. At that hour she had wished her young lady good-night in the bedroom; she had heard the clock strike in the boudoir; her hand at the time was on the handle of the painted door; she knew the paint was wet (having helped to mix the colours); she took particular pains not to touch it; could swear she held up the skirts of her dress, and that there was no smear then; could not swear her dress mightn’t have touched it accidentally in going out. She remembered the dress she wore because it was new—a present from Miss Rachel; her father remembered and could speak to it too; she fetched it; I recognised it as the dress she wore that night. The skirts were examined—long job, from the size of them—and not the ghost of a paint-stain was discovered anywhere. End of Penelope’s evidence. Signed, Gabriel Betteredge.

Next, the Sergeant questioned me about any large dogs in the house—whether one might have got in and done the mischief with a whisk of its tail. That being impossible, he sent for a magnifying-glass and examined the smear. No skin-mark of a human hand printed off on the paint. All visible signs told that some loose article of dress had touched the wet colour in passing. That somebody—putting Penelope’s evidence and Mr. Franklin’s evidence together—must have been in the room, and smeared the paint, between midnight and three in the morning.

Having brought it thus far, Sergeant Cuff seemed to remember that Superintendent Seegrave still remained in the room. He summed up for his brother-officer’s benefit, in a tone as quiet as a knife laid back on its plate.

“This trifle of yours, Mr. Superintendent,” he said, pointing to the door, “has grown a little in importance since you noticed it last. At the present stage there are, as I take it, three discoveries to make, starting from that smear. Find out (first) whether there is any article of dress in this house with the smear of the paint on it. Find out (second) who that dress belongs to. Find out (third) how the person can account for having been in this room, and smeared the paint, between midnight and three in the morning. If the person can’t satisfy you, you haven’t far to look for the hand that has got the Diamond. I’ll work this by myself, if you please, and detain you no longer from your regular business in the town. You have got one of your men here, I see. Leave him here at my disposal, in case I want him—and allow me to wish you good morning.”

Seegrave’s respect for the Sergeant was great; his respect for himself greater still. Hit hard, he hit back as smartly as he could manage on the way out.

“I have abstained from expressing any opinion so far,” he said, in his military voice. “I have now only one remark to offer on leaving this case in your hands. There is such a thing, Sergeant, as making a mountain out of a molehill. Good morning.”

“There is also such a thing,” Cuff replied, “as making nothing out of a molehill, in consequence of your head being too high to see it.”

With that he wheeled about and walked away to the window by himself, leaving the rest of us to taste the silence he had made—thin, sharp, and full of a meaning that had not yet found words.

I found my lady seated in her own sitting-room, the air about her close with the heat of late summer and the faint, comforting ghost of lavender-water that never left her cushions. Outside, the house had its usual breath and pulse—distant footfalls on boards that had been scrubbed till they shone, the soft draw of curtains, a sparrow’s chatter in the ivy. It was an ordinary domestic music; and yet, when I told her that Sergeant Cuff wished to speak with her, she started as if some harsh instrument had been struck too near her ear.

“Must I see him?” she said. “Can’t you represent me, Gabriel?”

I was plain enough in my face, I dare say, for she explained at once—being as honest with me as she was proud with the world.

“I am afraid my nerves are a little shaken,” she said. “There is something in that police-officer from London which I recoil from—I don’t know why. I have a presentiment that he is bringing trouble and misery with him into the house. Very foolish, and very unlike me—but so it is.”

It astonished me. To my mind, the Sergeant had come in like a man who listens—who hears, not only what is spoken, but what the house itself whispers in its timbers and its silence. I had taken to him the more I saw of him. Still, my lady, after letting her fear show, gathered herself again, as she always did—high courage, and no vanity about it.

“If I must see him, I must,” she said. “But I can’t prevail on myself to see him alone. Bring him in, Gabriel, and stay here as long as he stays.”

It was the first such attack of megrims I remembered in her since she had been a young girl. I went back to the boudoir. Mr. Franklin strolled out into the garden, and joined Mr. Godfrey, whose time for departure was now drawing near. Sergeant Cuff and I went straight to my mistress’s room.

I do declare my lady turned a shade paler at the sight of him. She mastered herself directly, however, and asked him whether he objected to my being present. She added, with that just pride which never forgot the place of her people, that I was her trusted adviser as well as her old servant, and that, in what touched the household, I was the likeliest person to consult.

The Sergeant answered, with civility, that he took my presence as a favour—he had something to say about the servants in general, and had already found my experience of use to him. My lady pointed to two chairs, and we sat down at once, as if it were a business of accounts and not a trouble that could sour the very bread we broke under this roof.

“I have already formed an opinion on this case,” says Sergeant Cuff, “which I beg your ladyship’s permission to keep to myself for the present. My business now is to mention what I have discovered upstairs in Miss Verinder’s sitting-room, and what I have decided—if your ladyship leaves it to me—on doing next.”

Then he spoke of the smear on the paint, and what it meant, with the same clearness as he had used downstairs, only in language that showed respect where Seegrave showed only suspicion. He had the manner of a man who could name a thing precisely, as a cook names the instant when a sauce turns—too soon and it is thin, too late and it is ruined.

“One thing,” he said, winding up, “is certain. The Diamond is missing out of the drawer in the cabinet. Another thing is next to certain. The marks from the smear on the door must be on some article of dress belonging to somebody in this house. We must discover that article of dress before we go a step further.”

“And that discovery,” my mistress said, “implies, I presume, the discovery of the thief?”

“I beg your ladyship’s pardon—I don’t say the Diamond is stolen. I only say, at present, that the Diamond is missing. The discovery of the stained dress may lead the way to finding it.”

Her ladyship looked at me, as if to make sure she had not misheard a word.

“Do you understand this?” she asked.

“Sergeant Cuff understands it, my lady,” I answered. “How do you propose to discover the stained dress?” she asked him.

Now my lady’s temper, where her people were concerned, came out in its best strength. “My good servants, who have been with me for years, have had their boxes and rooms searched already by the other officer. I can’t and won’t permit them to be insulted in that way a second time!”

There was a mistress to serve. There was a woman in ten thousand, if you like.

“That is the very point I was about to put to your ladyship,” said the Sergeant. “The other officer has done a world of harm to this inquiry, by letting the servants see that he suspected them. If I give them cause to think themselves suspected a second time, there’s no knowing what obstacles they may not throw in my way—the women especially. At the same time, their boxes must be searched again—for this plain reason, that the first investigation only looked for the Diamond, and that the second investigation must look for the stained dress. I quite agree with you, my lady, that the servants’ feelings ought to be consulted. But I am equally clear that the servants’ wardrobes ought to be searched.”

It sounded a dead-lock, and my lady said as much, though in finer words than mine.

“I have got a plan to meet the difficulty,” said Sergeant Cuff, “if your ladyship will consent to it. I propose explaining the case to the servants.”

“The women will think themselves suspected directly,” I said, breaking in—knowing the kitchen as well as I knew my own hands: how a whisper curdles there, and how quickly a look becomes a verdict.

“The women won’t, Mr. Betteredge,” answered the Sergeant, “if I can tell them I am going to examine the wardrobes of everybody—from her ladyship downwards—who slept in the house on Wednesday night. It’s a mere formality,” he added, with a side-look at my mistress; “but the servants will accept it as even dealing between them and their betters; and, instead of hindering the investigation, they will make a point of honour of assisting it.”

I saw the truth of that, and so did my lady when her first surprise passed. A house is like a well-ordered table: if the carving is done in view of all, and the portions are plainly fair, envy is quieter and tongues are less busy.

“You are certain the investigation is necessary?” she asked.

“It’s the shortest way that I can see, my lady, to the end we have in view.”

My mistress rose to ring the bell for her maid.

“You shall speak to the servants,” she said, “with the keys of my wardrobe in your hand.”

He checked her with a question I did not expect.

“Hadn’t we better make sure first,” he asked, “that the other ladies and gentlemen in the house will consent, too?”

“The only other lady in the house is Miss Verinder,” my mistress answered, surprised. “The only gentlemen are my nephews, Mr. Blake and Mr. Ablewhite. There is not the least fear of a refusal from any of the three.”

I reminded her then that Mr. Godfrey was going away. And as I spoke, Mr. Godfrey himself knocked to say good-bye, and Mr. Franklin followed him in, meaning to go with him to the station.

My lady explained the difficulty. Mr. Godfrey settled it on the spot—one of those men who are quickest with a gesture when it makes him look good. He called to Samuel through the window to take his portmanteau upstairs again, and then he put the key into Sergeant Cuff’s hand.

“My luggage can follow me to London,” he said, “when the inquiry is over.”

The Sergeant took the key, with a proper apology. “I am sorry to put you to any inconvenience, sir, for a mere formality; but the example of their betters will do wonders in reconciling the servants to this inquiry.”

Mr. Godfrey, after taking leave of my lady in his most sympathising manner, left a message for Miss Rachel which made it plain enough to me that he had not taken No for an answer, and meant to put the marriage question again when next he could. Mr. Franklin, on following his cousin out, told the Sergeant that all his clothes were open to examination, and that nothing he owned was kept under lock and key. Sergeant Cuff acknowledged it as he should.

So far, you see, his way had been met with readiness by my lady, by Mr. Godfrey, and by Mr. Franklin. There remained only Miss Rachel to follow their lead, before we called the servants together and began the search for the stained dress.

But my lady’s strange shrinking from the Sergeant came back on her the moment we were alone. She wanted him out of her room, out of her hearing—out of the sound of his voice, as if it jarred the air itself.

“If I send you down Miss Verinder’s keys,” she said, “I presume I shall have done all you want of me for the present?”

“I beg your ladyship’s pardon,” he answered. “Before we begin, I should like, if convenient, to have the washing-book. The stained article of dress may be an article of linen. If the search leads to nothing, I want to be able to account next for all the linen in the house, and for all the linen sent to the wash. If there is an article missing, there will be at least a presumption that it has got the paint-stain on it, and that it has been purposely made away with, yesterday or today, by the person owning it. Superintendent Seegrave,” he added, turning to me, “pointed the attention of the women-servants to the smear, when they all crowded into the room on Thursday morning. That may turn out, Mr. Betteredge, to have been one more of Superintendent Seegrave’s many mistakes.”

My lady told me to ring the bell and order the washing-book. She stayed until it came, in case the Sergeant had anything further to ask of her.

The washing-book was brought in by Rosanna Spearman. The girl had come down to breakfast that morning miserably pale and haggard, but was recovered enough from the illness of yesterday to do her work again. When she entered, the paper in her hand looked almost too heavy for her; when she went out, her crooked shoulder seemed to drag a little more than usual, as if the very air of the house pressed there.

Sergeant Cuff watched her closely—her face coming in; her shoulder going out—his eyes quiet, but missing nothing.

“Have you anything more to say to me?” my lady asked, eager as ever to escape him.

The great Cuff opened the washing-book, took it in as if it were a recipe he had known from boyhood, and shut it again in half a minute.

“I venture to trouble your ladyship with one last question,” he said. “Has the young woman who brought us this book been in your employment as long as the other servants?”

“Why do you ask?” said my lady.

“The last time I saw her,” said the Sergeant, “she was in prison for theft.”

After that, there was no help but to tell him the truth. My mistress spoke strongly of Rosanna’s good conduct in service, and of the high opinion the matron at the Reformatory had of her.

“You don’t suspect her, I hope?” my lady said at the end—earnest, almost fierce, as if to ward off a stain upon the girl’s name as you would ward off smoke from bread fresh out of the oven.

“I have already told your ladyship,” he answered, “that I don’t suspect any person in the house of thieving—up to the present time.”

That done, my lady rose and went upstairs herself to ask Miss Rachel for the keys. The Sergeant opened the door for her, bowing low. My lady shuddered as she passed him, as if the sound of his coat-sleeve brushing the air was something she could not bear.

We waited. And waited. The house made its small, familiar noises again—somebody shifting a chair; a distant door softly closing; the faint tick of a clock that always seemed louder when people held their breath. Sergeant Cuff said nothing to me. He turned his melancholy face to the window, thrust his lank hands into his pockets, and whistled “The Last Rose of Summer” under his breath—so softly that it seemed less a tune than a kind of listening.

At last Samuel came in—not with the keys, but with a morsel of paper for me.

I got my spectacles out, fumbling more than usual under the Sergeant’s fixed, dismal eyes. The paper held two or three lines in pencil, written by my lady. Miss Rachel flatly refused to have her wardrobe examined. Asked for her reasons, she had burst out crying. Asked again, she had said: “I won’t, because I won’t. I must yield to force if you use it, but I will yield to nothing else.”

I understood my lady’s unwillingness to face Sergeant Cuff with such an answer from her daughter. If I had not been too old for the amiable weaknesses of youth, I believe I should have blushed to face him myself.

“Any news of Miss Verinder’s keys?” asked the Sergeant.

“My young lady refuses to have her wardrobe examined.”

“Ah!” said the Sergeant.

His face kept its discipline better than his voice did. That “Ah!” came like a note he had expected to hear, and yet it half angered and half frightened me without my knowing why.

“Must the search be given up?” I asked.

“Yes,” he said. “The search must be given up, because your young lady refuses to submit to it like the rest. We must examine all the wardrobes in the house or none. Send Mr. Ablewhite’s portmanteau to London by the next train, and return the washing-book, with my compliments and thanks, to the young woman who brought it in.”

He laid the washing-book on the table, took out his penknife, and began trimming his nails—cool as if we had been talking of garden-work and not of a jewel that carried with it, as old stories carry with them, a sort of fate that slips through families and years and will not be scrubbed away like dirt from a doorstep.

“You don’t seem to be much disappointed,” I said.

“No,” said Sergeant Cuff. “I am not much disappointed.”

I tried to make him speak plainer.

“Why should Miss Rachel put an obstacle in your way?” I asked. “Isn’t it her interest to help you?”

“Wait a little, Mr. Betteredge—wait a little.”

Cleverer heads than mine might have seen what he was driving at. Or a person less fond of Miss Rachel might have seen it. My lady’s horror of him might—so I have thought since—have meant that she saw it too, dimly, as the Scripture says, “in a glass darkly.” I didn’t see it yet—that’s all I know.

“What’s to be done next?” I asked.

He finished the nail he was at, looked at it a moment with melancholy interest, put up the penknife, and got quietly to his feet.

“Come out into the garden,” he said, “and let’s have a look at the roses.”

The quickest way out of my lady’s sitting-room, if you wanted the garden air on your face, was by the shrubbery path. I knew every turn of it—the boxed scent of laurel, the soft scuff of sand underfoot, the sparrows quarrelling in the leaves as if they owned the place. It was, besides, Mr. Franklin Blake’s favourite walk. When the house had mislaid him—as we mislaid keys, letters, tempers—we found him there, pacing where the green muffled the sound of the world.

I am an obstinate old servant; the longer a man keeps house with other people’s secrets, the more he fancies he has a right to taste them. Sergeant Cuff had kept his thoughts corked up as tight as a bottle of good port, and that only sharpened my appetite to get at them.

As we turned into the shrubbery path, I tried him again, by a side-entrance.

“As things are now,” I said, “if I was in your place, I should be at my wits’ end.”

“If you were in my place,” says he, “you would have formed an opinion—and, as things are now, any doubt you might previously have felt about your own conclusions would be completely set at rest. Never mind for the present what those conclusions are, Mr. Betteredge. I haven’t brought you out here to draw me like a badger; I have brought you out here to ask for some information. You might have given it to me, no doubt, in the house, instead of out of it. But doors and listeners have a knack of getting together; and, in my line of life, we cultivate a healthy taste for the open air.”

The open air had its own sound that day: bees worrying the rosemary by the wall, the distant clink of a spade, and—under it all—the hush that sits on a house when suspicion has been served up with the meals like a bitter sauce no one ordered and no one can send back.

“We won’t enter into your young lady’s motives,” the Sergeant went on, “we will only say it’s a pity she declines to assist me, because, by so doing, she makes this investigation more difficult than it might otherwise have been. We must now try to solve the mystery of the smear on the door—which, you may take my word for it, means the mystery of the Diamond also—in some other way. I have decided to see the servants, and to search their thoughts and actions, Mr. Betteredge, instead of searching their wardrobes. Before I begin, however, I want to ask you a question or two. You are an observant man—did you notice anything strange in any of the servants (making due allowance, of course, for fright and fluster), after the loss of the Diamond was found out? Any particular quarrel among them? Anyone of them not in his or her usual spirits? Unexpectedly out of temper, for instance? or unexpectedly taken ill?”

Rosanna Spearman’s sudden illness at yesterday’s dinner flashed on me—her face the colour of skimmed milk, her spoon pausing halfway as if the very steam from the dish turned her sick. I had barely time to shape the first word when Sergeant Cuff’s eyes slid aside into the shrubbery, sharp as a knife’s point, and I heard him say under his breath, “Hullo!”

“What’s the matter?” I asked.

“A touch of the rheumatics in my back,” said the Sergeant—loud, easy, for anybody within earshot. “We shall have a change in the weather before long.”

A few steps took us to the corner of the house. He turned short to the right and brought me out on the terrace. Below, the garden opened—lawn like a green cloth, beds stitched with flowers, the fountain’s thin talk going on and on. He stopped where the air was wide and there was nowhere to hide but in plain sight.

“About that young person, Rosanna Spearman?” he said. “It isn’t very likely, with her personal appearance, that she has got a lover. But, for the girl’s own sake, I must ask you at once whether she has provided herself with a sweetheart, poor wretch, like the rest of them?”

Of all questions, at such a time! I could only stare.

“I saw Rosanna Spearman hiding in the shrubbery as we went by,” he added.

“When you said ‘Hullo’?”

“Yes—when I said ‘Hullo!’ If there’s a sweetheart in the case, the hiding doesn’t much matter. If there isn’t—as things are in this house—the hiding is a highly suspicious circumstance, and it will be my painful duty to act on it accordingly.”

Now came my pinch. I knew the shrubbery was Mr. Franklin’s walk; I knew he’d likely choose it on his return from the station; and I knew, too, what Penelope had said, time after time, with that sharp young sense of hers—Rosanna hanging about there, waiting like a moth for the candle, foolishly trying to catch Mr. Franklin’s eye. If I spoke, I must repeat Penelope’s fancy as if it was my own; if I held my tongue, I must leave a poor, wild-hearted girl to the mercy of a man whose suspicions were as quiet as they were deadly.

Out of sheer pity—on my honour, out of sheer pity—I told him. Rosanna, I said, had been mad enough to set her heart on Mr. Franklin Blake.

Sergeant Cuff never laughed. If anything amused him, the corners of his mouth only curled the least little way, as if the thought warmed him without lighting him. They curled now.

“Hadn’t you better say she’s mad enough to be an ugly girl and only a servant?” he asked. “The falling in love with a gentleman of Mr. Franklin Blake’s manners and appearance doesn’t seem to me to be the maddest part of her conduct by any means. However, I’m glad the thing is cleared up: it relieves one’s mind to have things cleared up. Yes, I’ll keep it a secret, Mr. Betteredge. I like to be tender to human infirmity—though I don’t get many chances of exercising that virtue in my line of life. You think Mr. Franklin Blake hasn’t got a suspicion of the girl’s fancy for him?
