Ah! he would have found it out fast enough if she had been nice-looking. The ugly women have a bad time of it in this world; let’s hope it will be made up to them in another.

“You have got a nice garden here, and a well-kept lawn. See for yourself how much better the flowers look with grass about them instead of gravel. No, thank you. I won’t take a rose. It goes to my heart to break them off the stem. Just as it goes to your heart, you know, when there’s something wrong in the servants’ hall. Did you notice anything you couldn’t account for in any of the servants when the loss of the Diamond was first found out?”

He slipped that question in, as a cook slips a pinch of salt into a sweet dish to see who notices. I had got on tolerably with him; but I did not relish helping him creep among my people like a draught under the door. So I shut my mouth on the little I knew.

“I noticed nothing,” I said, “except that we all lost our heads together, myself included.”

“Oh,” says he, “that’s all you have to tell me, is it?”

“That is all,” I answered, steady as I could keep it.

His dismal eyes fixed on me. “Mr. Betteredge,” he says, “have you any objection to oblige me by shaking hands? I have taken an extraordinary liking to you.”

Why he chose the exact moment when I was deceiving him to offer me that compliment, I cannot say. I felt, for an instant, as absurdly proud as a boy who has palmed a stolen tart and not been found out.

We went back to the house. He asked for a room to himself, and then for the servants—indoor servants only—to be sent in one by one, in order of rank, first to last.

I took him into my own room. Then I called the servants together in the hall, where the air always carried a faint mingling of soap, boots, and yesterday’s cooking—the homely smell of work that never gets talked about upstairs.

Rosanna Spearman stood among them much as usual: a quick, close-held look in her, like a cat that never quite sleeps. I daresay she had heard enough to know what was coming. There she was, at any rate, with a face that might have sworn the shrubbery had never existed.

I sent them in, one by one.

The cook first—our cook, who could turn simple country produce into comfort, who knew by scent alone when the bread had taken colour enough and when a sauce had gone one degree too far. She came out quickly.

“Sergeant Cuff is depressed in his spirits,” she reported, “but Sergeant Cuff is a perfect gentleman.”

My lady’s maid went next, and stayed much longer. She came out stiff as starch.

“If Sergeant Cuff doesn’t believe a respectable woman,” says she, “he might keep his opinion to himself, at any rate!”

Penelope followed. In and out in a moment or two, like a sparrow that will not stay where the air is foul.

“Sergeant Cuff is much to be pitied,” says she. “He must have been crossed in love, father, when he was a young man.”

The first housemaid went after Penelope, and stayed as long as my lady’s maid. Out she came with her cheeks burning.

“I didn’t enter her ladyship’s service, Mr. Betteredge, to be doubted to my face by a low police-officer!”

Rosanna Spearman went next. She stayed longer than any of them. When she came out, there was no report—no bounce, no complaint, no small vanity wounded into noise. Dead silence. Her lips were pale as ashes.

Samuel, the footman, followed. He barely stayed.

“Whoever blacks Sergeant Cuff’s boots,” says Samuel, “ought to be ashamed of himself.”

Nancy, the kitchen-maid, went last. She stayed no longer than Samuel.

“Sergeant Cuff has a heart,” says she. “He doesn’t cut jokes, Mr. Betteredge, with a poor hard-working girl.”

When it was done, I went into my room to hear if there were further commands. There was the Sergeant at his old trick—standing at the window, looking out as if the glass showed him more than the garden, whistling “The Last Rose of Summer” very softly to himself. It was an odd sound in a house where every voice now seemed to listen for some other voice behind it.

“Any discoveries, sir?” I asked.

“If Rosanna Spearman asks leave to go out,” he said, “let the poor thing go; but let me know first.”

I might as well have held my tongue about Rosanna and Mr. Franklin. Plain enough—she was under his eye, in spite of me.

“I hope you don’t think Rosanna is concerned in the loss of the Diamond?” I ventured.

Again, the little curl at the corners of his mouth; again, the hard look.

“I think I had better not tell you, Mr. Betteredge,” he said. “You might lose your head, you know, for the second time.”

I began to doubt whether I had been one too many for Sergeant Cuff, after all.

A knock came, and the cook’s message followed it: Rosanna Spearman had asked to go out—her head was bad, she said; she wanted a breath of fresh air.

At the Sergeant’s sign, I said yes.

“Which is the servants’ way out?” he asked, when the messenger had gone.

I showed him.

“Lock the door of your room,” says he; “and if anybody asks for me, say I’m in there, composing my mind.”

The faint curl showed again—and he was gone.

Left alone with my own thoughts, curiosity ate at me like hunger at an empty table. The Sergeant’s suspicion of Rosanna had not come from nothing; he had found some taste of guilt somewhere in my room while he questioned them. Now, the only two who had been under his hand for any length of time—besides Rosanna—were my lady’s maid and the first housemaid, the very pair who had led the persecution of the poor creature from the first.

So I went where women’s truths are most likely to rise—over tea. In the servants’ hall I found the tray set, the pot steaming, and the first cups already pouring out that strong brown comfort which loosens tongues as surely as it warms fingers. I invited myself without ceremony. A drop of tea, mind you, is to a woman’s tongue what a drop of oil is to a lamp that’s burning low.

My reliance on the tea-pot did not fail me. Within half an hour I knew as much as the Sergeant.

Neither my lady’s maid nor the housemaid had believed Rosanna’s illness the day before. Those two—devils, if you like; what other word fits women who feed on another woman’s misery?—had stolen upstairs again and again through Thursday afternoon. They had tried Rosanna’s door and found it locked; they had knocked and got no answer; they had listened and heard no sound. When Rosanna came down to tea and was sent up again, still out of sorts, they tried once more. Locked again. They looked at the keyhole and found it stopped. At midnight they saw a light under the door. At four in the morning they heard the crackling of a fire—a fire in a servant’s bedroom in the month of June.

All this they had told Sergeant Cuff. And he, in return for their eager spite, had eyed them sourly and suspiciously and showed them plainly he did not believe either one or the other. Hence the offended virtue in their reports when they came out. Hence, too, how freely they served the story to me now, delighted to have their injuries heard, never dreaming how their malice had made a ladder for the Sergeant to climb.

Knowing Cuff’s roundabout ways, and having last seen him bent on slipping after Rosanna when she went out, I could see why he had not let the two women know how much they had helped him. If he had treated their evidence as gold, they would have strutted with it, and something in their talk or triumph would have put Rosanna on her guard.

I went out into the fine summer afternoon with a weight on me, sorry for the girl and uneasy at the turn events had taken. The estate had its own steady household music—the rustle of leaves, the far-off sheep, the softened footfalls on turf—and yet beneath it the house’s new noise persisted: the invisible clatter of suspicion, harsher than any kitchen racket.

Later, drifting as my feet always drifted when my mind had no rest, I found myself near the shrubbery. There I met Mr. Franklin, just returned from seeing his cousin off at the station. He had been with my lady, talking long; she had told him of Miss Rachel’s refusal to let her wardrobe be examined. Whatever she had said had worked on him: he looked as if the air had turned heavy in his lungs. The family temper showed in his face that evening for the first time since I had known him—a look that belongs to old blood and old pride, like an inherited scar.

“Well, Betteredge,” he said, “how does the atmosphere of mystery and suspicion in which we are all living now, agree with you? Do you remember that morning when I first came here with the Moonstone? I wish to God we had thrown it into the quicksand!”

After that outburst he held his tongue, mastering himself. We walked side by side in silence for a minute or two—only the birds speaking above us, and the faint wash of wind through firs like the sea practising at a distance. Then he asked where Sergeant Cuff was.

I could not put him off with any tale of “composing his mind.” So I told him exactly what had happened, and I mentioned, in particular, what the maid and the housemaid had said about Rosanna.

Mr. Franklin’s clear head took the turn of it in an instant.

“Didn’t you tell me this morning,” he said, “that one of the tradespeople declared he had met Rosanna yesterday, on the footway to Frizinghall, when we supposed her to be ill in her room?”

“Yes, sir.”

“If my aunt’s maid and the other woman have spoken the truth, you may depend upon it the tradesman did meet her. The girl’s attack of illness was a blind to deceive us. She had some guilty reason for going to the town secretly. The paint-stained dress is a dress of hers; and the fire heard crackling in her room at four in the morning was a fire lit to destroy it. Rosanna Spearman has stolen the Diamond. I’ll go in directly, and tell my aunt the turn things have taken.”

“Not just yet, if you please, sir,” said a melancholy voice behind us.

We turned, and there stood Sergeant Cuff.

“Why not just yet?” asked Mr. Franklin.

“Because, sir, if you tell her ladyship, her ladyship will tell Miss Verinder.”

“Suppose she does. What then?”

There was heat in Mr. Franklin’s words, sudden and sharp, as if the Sergeant’s caution had struck some hidden sore. The garden seemed to listen; even the breeze fell quiet for a second, and the fountain’s thin voice went on alone.

“Do you think it’s wise, sir,” said Sergeant Cuff—quiet as ever—“to put such a question as that to me, at such a time as this?”

A moment passed. Mr. Franklin stepped close. The two looked straight at each other, as men do when they have both hit the same truth from opposite sides and neither wants to name it first.

Mr. Franklin spoke, dropping his voice.

“I suppose you know, Mr. Cuff, that you are treading on delicate ground?”

“It isn’t the first time, by a good many hundreds, that I find myself treading on delicate ground,” the Sergeant answered, immovable.

“I am to understand that you forbid me to tell my aunt what has happened?”

“You are to understand, if you please, sir, that I throw up the case, if you tell Lady Verinder, or tell anybody, what has happened, until I give you leave.”

That settled it. Mr. Franklin had no choice but to swallow his anger. He turned away—white with it—and left us.

I stood trembling, not knowing whom to suspect next or what to think. Still, two things were plain as salt on the tongue. First: my young lady, in some unaccountable manner, lay at the bottom of those sharp speeches. Second: they understood each other perfectly, without having exchanged a word of explanation that I had heard.

“Mr. Betteredge,” says the Sergeant, “you have done a very foolish thing in my absence. You have done a little detective business on your own account. For the future, perhaps you will be so obliging as to do your detective business along with me.”

He took me by the arm and walked me away along the road he had come by. I dare say I deserved the rebuke. But I was not going to help him set traps for Rosanna Spearman for all that. Thief or no thief, legal or not legal, I pitied her—pitied her as you pity any creature who has grown up starved of kindness and then been blamed for snatching at it when it passes.

“What do you want of me?” I asked, stopping short and freeing my arm.

“Only a little information about the country round here,” he said.

That much I could give without betraying anybody.

“Is there any path, in that direction, leading to the sea-beach from this house?” he asked, pointing towards the fir-plantation—the dark straight ranks that led, as everybody hereabouts knew, to the Shivering Sand.

“Yes,” I said. “There is a path.”

“Show it to me.”

So, side by side, in the grey of the summer evening—when the birds quieted and the world’s sounds sank as if the day’s bustle were being covered like a dish to keep it warm—Sergeant Cuff and I set forth for the Shivering Sand.

Sergeant Cuff said nothing more while we kept to the shelter of the fir-plantation. The needles underfoot muffled our tread with a dry, brushed whisper, and the wind moved high in the dark boughs like a hand passing over harp-strings. It was the only gentle sound in a countryside that, nearer the sea, always seemed to hold its breath.

When the last trees thinned and the sand-hummocks began, he roused, as if a decision had finished baking in him and was now ready to be served.

“Mr. Betteredge,” he said, “you’ve done me the honour of taking an oar in my boat; and you may do me another service before the evening is over. So we’ll have done with mystifying. You mean to tell me nothing that might hurt Rosanna Spearman. She has been a good girl to you; you pity her; and you credit your tenderness for humanity. Admirable—only wasted. Rosanna Spearman is in no danger of trouble. No—though I fixed her with a hand in the Diamond’s disappearance, on evidence as plain as the nose on your face.”

“Then my lady won’t prosecute?” I asked.

“Your lady can’t prosecute,” the Sergeant said. “Rosanna Spearman is an instrument in another person’s hands; and Rosanna Spearman will be held harmless—for that other person’s sake.”

He meant it. His voice had the settled weight of a man who has sifted a thing down to its last grain. And yet something in me—some loyalty that had been salted into my bones by long service—scraped uneasily against him.

“Can you give that other person a name?” I said.

“Can you, Mr. Betteredge?”

“No.”

He stopped dead and looked me over with a melancholy patience, as if my mind were a cupboard he had opened and found ill-stocked.

“I’m always tender towards human infirmity,” he said. “I’m particularly tender, at this moment, towards you. And you—out of the same excellent motive—are tender towards Rosanna Spearman. Has she had a new outfit of linen lately?”

The question came on me like a spoonful of cold water dashed into a hot pan. I couldn’t imagine what he was seasoning his talk with; but seeing no harm in the truth, I owned that Rosanna had come sparely provided, and that my lady—because of her good conduct (I laid stress on that as if it were yeast in bread)—had given her new linen not a fortnight before.

“This is a miserable world,” said the Sergeant. “Human life is a sort of target—misfortune fires at it and hits. But for that outfit, we should have found a new nightgown or petticoat among Rosanna’s things, and nailed her that way. You follow? You examined the servants. You know what two of them found outside her door. You know what she was about yesterday, after she was taken ill.”

I hesitated.

“Oh dear me,” he went on, as if the whole matter were a recipe and I the slowest kitchen-boy in England, “it’s as plain as that strip of light at the end of the trees. Thursday morning, at eleven, Superintendent Seegrave—who is a mass of human infirmity—shows the women-servants the smear on the door. Rosanna has her reasons for suspecting her own things. She gets to her room, finds the paint-stain on her nightgown or petticoat or what not, shams ill, slips away to the town, buys stuff to make a new petticoat or nightgown, makes it alone in her room on Thursday night—lights a fire, not to burn the old thing (two servants are prying at her door; she knows better than to make a smell of burning), but to dry and iron the substitute after wringing it out. Keeps the stained thing hidden—probably on her. And at this moment she is making away with it, in some convenient place, on that lonely beach ahead. I traced her this evening to your fishing village and to one cottage, which we may have to visit before we go back. She stayed there some time and came out with—so I believe—something under her cloak. A cloak on a woman’s back is an emblem of charity; it covers a multitude of sins. I saw her set off northward along the coast. Is your sea-shore considered a fine specimen of marine landscape, Mr. Betteredge?”

“Yes,” I said, as short as could be.

“Tastes differ,” he answered. “From my point of view, I never admired a marine landscape less. If you are following a person along this shore and that person looks round, there isn’t a scrap of cover to hide you. I had to choose: take Rosanna in custody on suspicion, or leave her, for the present, with her little game in her own hands. For reasons I won’t trouble you with, I’d make any sacrifice rather than give the alarm tonight to a certain person who shall be nameless between us. So I came back to the house to ask you to bring me to the north end of the beach by another way. Sand, in the matter of printing footsteps, is one of the best detective officers I know. If we don’t meet Rosanna by coming round on her, the sand may tell us what she has been at—if the light holds. Here is the sand. And if you’ll excuse my suggesting it—hold your tongue, and let me go first.”

If doctors keep a name for catching the detective-fever, then I was seized and burning. He went on among the hillocks, down to the open beach. I followed with my heart up in my throat, keeping a little behind, as if the quiet itself might crack under too near a foot.

I found myself almost on the very spot where Rosanna and I had spoken together when Mr. Franklin first came briskly upon us, arriving from London. My eyes watched the Sergeant; my mind, like a pot left too long unattended, boiled over into old remembrance. I could almost feel Rosanna’s hand again—cold and grateful—squeezing mine because I had spoken kindly. I could almost hear her voice telling me of the Shivering Sand and how it drew her against her will, the way some people are drawn by a tune they can’t stop hearing. I saw her face brighten, as it brightened when she first caught sight of Mr. Franklin stepping lively between the hummocks.

My spirits sank lower with every recollection. And the place itself—when I made myself look at it—did the rest.

The last of the evening light was failing. An awful calm lay over the little bay like a lid put down. The long heave of the ocean on the far sandbank made no sound; the inner water lay dim and breathless. Yellow-white patches of ooze floated on the dead surface, and scum and slime shone faintly where the last light struck the two great spits of rock that jutted out north and south. It was the turn of the tide; and even as I watched, the broad brown face of the quicksand began to dimple and quiver—the only moving thing in that horrid stillness.

The Sergeant started when he saw it. After watching a minute, he turned and came back to me.

“A treacherous place, Mr. Betteredge,” he said. “And no signs of Rosanna Spearman anywhere on the beach.”

He led me lower, and I saw it with my own eyes: his footsteps and mine were the only marks on the sand.

“How does the fishing village bear from here?” he asked.

“Cobb’s Hole,” I said. “Due south, as near as may be.”

“I saw the girl walking northward from Cobb’s Hole,” he replied. “So she must have been coming towards this place. Is Cobb’s Hole on the other side of that point? And can we get to it now, at low water, by the beach?”

“Yes,” I said, to both.

“If you’ll excuse my suggesting it, we’ll step out briskly,” he answered. “I want to find where she left the shore before it’s dark.”

We had gone, I should say, two hundred yards, when Sergeant Cuff dropped to his knees on the sand as if seized with a sudden piety.

“There’s something to be said for your marine landscape after all,” he remarked. “Here are a woman’s footsteps, Mr. Betteredge. Let’s call them Rosanna’s until we can’t. Confused footsteps—purposely confused, I should say. Ah, poor soul, she knows the detective virtues of sand as well as I do! But hasn’t she been in too great a hurry to tread them out thoroughly? Here’s one step going from Cobb’s Hole; and here’s another going back. See that toe pointing to the water’s edge? And don’t you see two heel-marks further down, close to the water too? I don’t want to hurt your feelings, but I’m afraid Rosanna is sly. It looks as if she meant to get to that place you and I have just come from, without leaving marks. Shall we say she walked through the water from here to that ledge of rocks behind us, and came back the same way, and then took to the beach again where those two heel-marks are? Yes, we’ll say that. It fits with my notion of something under her cloak. Not something to destroy—if she meant destruction, why all these precautions? Something to hide is the better guess. Perhaps, if we go on to the cottage, we may find out what that something is.”

At the word cottage my fever cooled as suddenly as if cold sea-mist had blown through my mind.

“You don’t want me,” I said. “What good can I do?”

“The longer I know you, Mr. Betteredge,” he returned, “the more virtues I find. Modesty—oh dear me, how rare; and how much of that rarity you possess! If I go alone, tongues will be tied at my first question. If I go with you, I go introduced by a respected neighbour, and conversation flows of necessity. That is how it strikes me. How does it strike you?”

I tried to gain time by asking what cottage he meant.

When he described it, I knew it directly: the fisherman Yolland’s, with his wife and their grown son and daughter. Rosanna, I had said before, sometimes varied her walks to the Shivering Sand by calling on friends at Cobb’s Hole—the Yollands. She had made their acquaintance through the daughter, a poor afflicted girl with a misshapen foot, known among us as Limping Lucy. The two deformed girls, I suppose, had a fellow-feeling, as people do who share the same silent hunger. In any case, Rosanna and the Yollands always got on in a friendly, homely way.

The Sergeant tracing Rosanna to their cottage changed the flavour of the matter. If she had been with those worthy people, it looked like innocence, so far. I told myself—eagerly—that in helping the Sergeant I might be serving Rosanna instead of harming her. So I allowed myself to be convinced and went on with him.

We kept to the beach towards Cobb’s Hole, reading the sand as long as the light would let us.

At the cottage we found the fisherman and his son out in the boat; and Limping Lucy, always weak and weary, was resting upstairs. Mrs. Yolland received us alone in her kitchen. The place smelt of sea-salt dried into clothing, of old smoke in the rafters, and of spirits kept for company rather than cheer. When she heard Sergeant Cuff was a celebrated London man, she clapped a bottle of Dutch gin and two clean pipes on the table, and stared at him as if he were a rare fish hauled up alive.

I sat quiet in a corner, waiting for the Sergeant to come to Rosanna. His roundabout way was roundabout beyond belief. How he steered from one thing to the next was a mystery then and is a mystery now; but I know where he began and where he landed. He began with the Royal Family, the Primitive Methodists, and the price of fish; and he got—by his dismal underground burrowing—to the loss of the Moonstone, the spitefulness of our first house-maid, and the hard usage of the women-servants towards Rosanna Spearman. Having reached that, he represented himself as inquiring partly to find the Diamond and partly to clear Rosanna from the unjust suspicions of her enemies. In a quarter of an hour, good Mrs. Yolland was persuaded she was speaking to Rosanna’s best friend, and she pressed the Sergeant to comfort his stomach and revive his spirits out of the Dutch bottle.

Thinking he was wasting breath on her, I sat and listened much as I have listened to a play—amused in spite of myself by his patience and his dreary little shots fired this way and that on the chance of striking the mark. But it ended always the same: everything to Rosanna’s credit, nothing to her prejudice. Mrs. Yolland talked nineteen to the dozen and trusted him completely. His last shot came when we looked at our watches and got up to go.

“I shall wish you good-night, ma’am,” said the Sergeant. “And I shall only add that Rosanna Spearman has a sincere well-wisher in me. But, oh dear me, she will never get on in her present place; and my advice is—leave it.”

“Bless your heart alive! she is going to leave it!” cried Mrs. Yolland.

Rosanna going to leave us—without a word first to my lady or to me? The statement pricked me like a pin through cloth. A doubt rose—whether the Sergeant’s random shot had hit home at last. I began to suspect my part in this business was not so harmless as I had comforted myself it was. I didn’t like lies in any shape; and though the Sergeant might think it all fair in his trade to net an honest woman with falsehoods, my Protestant conscience remembered that the father of lies is the Devil, and that mischief and the Devil seldom eat at separate tables.

Smelling mischief, I tried to draw the Sergeant out. He sat down again at once and asked for a little drop of comfort. Mrs. Yolland poured it. I went to the door, thoroughly uncomfortable, said I must bid them good-night—and yet stood there, unable to go.

“So she means to leave?” said the Sergeant. “What is she to do when she does? Sad, sad! The poor creature has no friends in the world except you and me.”

“Ah, but she has though!” said Mrs. Yolland. “She came in this evening, as I told you; and after talking a bit with my girl Lucy and me, she asked to go upstairs by herself into Lucy’s room. It’s the only room with pen and ink. ‘I want to write to a friend,’ she says, ‘and I can’t do it for the prying and peeping of the servants up at the house.’ Who the letter was to I can’t tell you. It must have been mortal long, by the time she stayed. I offered her a postage-stamp when she came down. She hadn’t the letter in her hand, and she wouldn’t take the stamp. Close, poor soul, about herself and her doings. But a friend she’s got somewhere, I’ll answer for that; and to that friend she’ll go.”

“Soon?” asked the Sergeant.

“As soon as she can,” said Mrs. Yolland.

That was enough. As chief of my lady’s household I couldn’t sit quiet and hear a servant’s going and coming treated as gossip.

“You must be mistaken,” I said. “If Rosanna meant to leave, she would have mentioned it first to me.”

“Mistaken?” cried Mrs. Yolland. “Why, only an hour ago she bought things for travelling—of my own self, Mr. Betteredge, in this very room. And that reminds me,” she added, fumbling in her pocket, “of something I’ve got on my mind about Rosanna and her money. Are you either likely to see her when you go back?”

“I’ll take a message with the greatest pleasure,” said Sergeant Cuff, before I could stop him.

Mrs. Yolland drew out a little heap of shillings and sixpences and counted them in her palm with a carefulness that would have suited a miser weighing flour by the grain. She offered the money to the Sergeant, looking sorry to part with it and yet determined.

“Might I ask you to give this back to Rosanna, with my love and respects?” she said. “She would pay me for what she took a fancy to—money’s welcome enough, I don’t deny. Still, I’m not easy taking the poor thing’s little savings. And to tell you truth, I don’t think my man would like to hear I took Rosanna’s money when he comes in tomorrow. Say she’s welcome to the things—as a gift. And don’t leave the money on the table,” she added, putting it down as if it burnt her fingers. “Times are hard, flesh is weak, and I might be tempted to put it back in my pocket.”

“Come along,” I said. “I can’t wait. I must go back.”

“I’ll follow you directly,” said the Sergeant.

For the second time I went to the door; for the second time I couldn’t bring myself to cross it.

“It’s a delicate matter, ma’am,” I heard the Sergeant say, “giving money back. You charged her cheap, I’m sure?”

“Cheap!” cried Mrs. Yolland. “Come and judge.”

She took up the candle and led him to a corner. Against my will I followed—drawn like a man drawn by the smell of something he shouldn’t taste. In the corner lay a heap of odds and ends—old metal mostly—salvage from wrecks that the fisherman had gathered but not yet sold to his liking. Mrs. Yolland plunged her hands into it and brought up an old japanned tin case with a lid and a hasp—the sort sailors use to keep maps and charts from wet.

“There!” she said. “Rosanna bought the fellow to that. ‘It will just do,’ she says, ‘to put my cuffs and collars in, and keep them from being crumpled in my box.’ One and ninepence, Mr. Cuff. As I live by bread, not a halfpenny more.”

“Dirt cheap!” said the Sergeant, with one of his heavy sighs.

He weighed the case in his hand. I thought I caught, faint and odd, a note or two of “The Last Rose of Summer” in him—as if the tune were a private way he had of tasting a discovery.

Then I knew. In the one place I had thought Rosanna safest, he had found something against her—and through me. My shame and anger were bitter; they lay in my mouth like burnt crust.

“That will do,” I said. “We really must go.”

Mrs. Yolland ignored me and plunged into the rubbish again. This time she came up with a dog-chain.

“Weigh it in your hand, sir,” she said to the Sergeant. “We had three of these, and Rosanna took two. ‘What can you want with a couple of dog’s chains?’ says I. ‘If I join them they’ll go round my box nicely,’ says she. ‘Rope’s cheapest,’ says I. ‘Chain’s surest,’ says she. ‘Who ever heard of a box corded with chain?’ says I. ‘Oh, Mrs. Yolland, don’t make objections!’ says she; ‘let me have my chains!’ A strange girl, Mr. Cuff—good as gold, and kinder than a sister to my Lucy—but always a little strange. There! I humoured her. Three and sixpence. On the word of an honest woman, three and sixpence.”

“Each?” asked the Sergeant.

“Both together,” said Mrs. Yolland. “Three and sixpence for the two.”

“Given away, ma’am,” said the Sergeant, shaking his head. “Clean given away.”

“There’s the money,” Mrs. Yolland said, edging back towards the silver on the table as if it had a pull on her like the tide. “The tin case and the dog-chains were all. One and ninepence and three and sixpence—five and three. With my love and respects—and I can’t find it in my conscience to take a poor girl’s savings.”

“I can’t find it in my conscience to give it back,” said Sergeant Cuff. “You’ve as good as made her a present—you have indeed.”

“Is that your sincere opinion?” Mrs. Yolland brightened at once.

“There can’t be a doubt,” he said. “Ask Mr. Betteredge.”

It was no use asking me. All they got out of me was, “Good-night.”

“Bother the money!” cried Mrs. Yolland; and with that she snatched the heap up and pocketed it in one motion. “It upsets one’s temper to see it lying there and nobody taking it!” She sat down with a thump and looked at the Sergeant as much as to say, It’s in my pocket now—get it out if you can.

This time I went out—fairly out—into the road back. Explain it as you may, I felt as if one or both had offended me past bearing. The cottage behind me held the clink of coins and the thick scent of gin; ahead lay the open air, salted and clean, with the far, steady breathing of the sea. Yet even that natural music seemed troubled in my ears—because the shore, with its treacherous hush and its whispering sand, had begun to feel like a place where human speech itself could be twisted and lost.

I had not taken three steps down the village when I heard the Sergeant behind me.

“Thank you for your introduction, Mr. Betteredge,” he said. “I am indebted to the fisherman’s wife for an entirely new sensation. Mrs. Yolland has puzzled me.”

We found my lady in a room that looked, at first glance, as if it had been set for quiet study and nothing more: one reading-lamp burning low, the rest of the candles refused; the shade screwed down so tight that the light fell in a miserly circle—bright on the page, stingy on the face above it. The house beyond her door had its own uneasy music: a distant clink of closing shutters, a floorboard’s complaining creak, the faint, restless shift of wind in the chimneys like a hand rubbing at a sore place.

My lady sat close to the table, not lifting her eyes in her usual frank fashion, but pinning them stubbornly to an open book as if the printed words were a shield she could hold between herself and the rest of us.

“Officer,” she said, without looking up, “is it important to the inquiry you are conducting, to know beforehand if any person now in this house wishes to leave it?”

“Most important, my lady,” answered Sergeant Cuff.

“I have to tell you, then, that Miss Verinder proposes going to stay with her aunt, Mrs. Ablewhite, of Frizinghall. She has arranged to leave us the first thing tomorrow morning.”

The Sergeant’s eyes moved—one quiet glance, and it found me at once. I made a step forward to speak to my mistress; and, in the same instant, felt my courage go like heat out of bread when the oven-door is thrown open too soon. I took a step back again, and said nothing.

“May I ask your ladyship when Miss Verinder informed you that she was going to her aunt’s?” inquired the Sergeant.

“About an hour since,” my mistress answered.

The Sergeant looked at me again. They say old hearts are hard to stir. Mine beat like a kitchen clock that has been knocked out of its case.

“I have no claim, my lady,” he resumed, “to control Miss Verinder’s actions. All I can ask you to do is to put off her departure, if possible, till later in the day. I must go to Frizinghall myself tomorrow morning—and I shall be back by two o’clock, if not before. If Miss Verinder can be kept here till that time, I should wish to say two words to her—unexpectedly—before she goes.”

My lady, still with her eyes on the book, told me to give the coachman her orders: the carriage was not to come for Miss Rachel until two.

When I returned and said it had been done, my mistress asked the Sergeant, “Have you more to say?”

“Only one thing, your ladyship. If Miss Verinder is surprised at this change in the arrangements, please not to mention me as being the cause of putting off her journey.”

At that, my lady lifted her head suddenly, as if something sharp had pricked her under the ribs. Her lips parted. She checked herself by a force that seemed to pinch the breath back down her throat, let her eyes fall again to the open page, and dismissed us with a motion of her hand.

In the hall, where the sounds of the house came clearer—servants moving soft-footed, a latch whispered to, a clock far off counting like a steady knife—Sergeant Cuff spoke low.

“That’s a wonderful woman. But for her self-control, the mystery that puzzles you, Mr. Betteredge, would have been at an end tonight.”

It was as if those words had carried a hidden spice—something bitter and unmistakable—straight to my tongue. The truth rushed at last into my slow old head.

For a moment I believe I went clean out of my senses. I seized the Sergeant by the collar and pinned him against the wall.

“Damn you!” I cried. “There’s something wrong about Miss Rachel—and you have been hiding it from me all this time!”

He lay there flat against the paneling, not raising a hand, not shifting a muscle in that melancholy face of his; and looked up at me as if I had merely spilled a glass of ale.

“Ah,” he said, “you’ve guessed it at last.”

My hand fell away. My head sank. Remember, if you please, I had served the family fifty years. Miss Rachel had climbed upon my knees and pulled my whiskers, many and many a time, when she was a child. Whatever her faults, she was, in my mind, the dearest and prettiest and best young mistress an old servant ever waited on—and loved.

I begged his pardon; but I fear I did it with watery eyes, and in no very becoming way.

“Don’t distress yourself, Mr. Betteredge,” he said, kinder than I had any right to expect. “In my line of life, if we were quick at taking offence, we shouldn’t be worth salt to our porridge. If it’s any comfort to you, collar me again. You don’t in the least know how to do it; but I’ll overlook your awkwardness in consideration of your feelings.”

He curled up the corners of his lips, and in his dreary way seemed pleased with his own joke.

I led him into my little sitting-room and shut the door; and there, though the table was laid for supper and the smell of it rose warm and homely—bread cut, meat waiting, the comfort of a covered dish holding its heat like a living thing—I could not bring myself to taste any of it. Food, that is commonly a mercy, turned to mere matter in my mouth.

“Tell me the truth, Sergeant,” I said. “What do you suspect? It’s no kindness to hide it from me now.”

“I don’t suspect,” he answered. “I know.”

My temper rose again like a pot beginning to boil.

“Do you mean to tell me, in plain English, that Miss Rachel has stolen her own Diamond?”

“Yes,” said the Sergeant. “That is what I mean to tell you, in so many words. Miss Verinder has been in secret possession of the Moonstone from first to last; and she has taken Rosanna Spearman into her confidence, because she has calculated on our suspecting Rosanna Spearman of the theft. There is the whole case in a nutshell. Collar me again, Mr. Betteredge. If it’s any vent to your feelings, collar me again.”

God help me, my feelings were not to be relieved by laying hands on him.

“Give me your reasons!” was all I could manage.

“You shall hear my reasons tomorrow,” he said. “If Miss Verinder refuses to put off her visit to her aunt (which you will find Miss Verinder will do), I shall be obliged to lay the whole case before your mistress tomorrow. And as I don’t know what may come of it, I shall request you to be present, and to hear what passes on both sides. Let the matter rest for tonight. No, Mr. Betteredge—you don’t get a word more on the subject of the Moonstone out of me. There is your table spread for supper. That’s one of the many human infirmities which I always treat tenderly. If you will ring the bell, I’ll say grace. ‘For what we are going to receive—’”

“I wish you a good appetite to it, Sergeant,” I said. “My appetite is gone. I’ll wait and see you served, and then I’ll ask you to excuse me, if I go away and try to get the better of this by myself.”

I saw him served with the best of everything—and I shouldn’t have been sorry if the best of everything had choked him. The clatter of plate and the pour of drink sounded too loud in that little room, as if the house itself had lost the trick of quiet. A bottle of Scotch whisky appeared, and with it the head gardener, Mr. Begbie, bringing in his weekly account; and the Sergeant, as if he had not just turned my world upside down, slipped into talk of roses, grass-walks, and gravel-walks, with the ease of a man tasting a familiar broth.

I left them and went out with a heart that felt heavy as a wet sack. This was the first trouble I remember, for many a year, that wasn’t to be blown off by a whiff of tobacco and that was beyond the reach even of Robinson Crusoe. Restless and miserable, with no room in the house that would hold me comfortably, I took a turn on the terrace.

The night had its own kitchen-work going on: wind kneading the trees, clouds dragged and folded over the moon, the shrubs hissing as if a pot were set too near the fire. In quieter times, those sounds were part of our place’s old language—each corner had its note, each season its tune. Now even the familiar noises seemed to speak in a dialect I could not follow, the same words but altered meaning; and that, to my mind, is how misfortune enters a household first—by changing what the ear has trusted all its life.

It doesn’t much matter what I thought. I felt wretchedly old, worn out, and unfit for my place; and wondered for the first time when it would please God to take me. Yet I held firm to my belief in Miss Rachel. If Sergeant Cuff had been Solomon in all his glory and had told me my young lady had mixed herself up in a mean and guilty plot, I should have answered Solomon, wise as he was, “You don’t know her; and I do.”

Samuel interrupted me. He brought a written message from my mistress. When I went into the house to get a light, he remarked that there seemed a change coming in the weather. My troubled mind had kept me from noticing it; but now, with my attention pricked, I heard the dogs uneasy in their sleep, and the wind moaning low along the eaves like a creature shut out. Looking up, I saw the rack of clouds blackening and hurrying over a watery moon. Wild weather coming—Samuel was right—wild weather coming.

My lady’s message informed me that the magistrate at Frizinghall had written to remind her about the three Indians. Early in the coming week, those men must be released and left free to follow their own devices. If we had more questions to ask, there was no time to lose. She had forgotten to mention this when last she saw Sergeant Cuff and now desired me to supply the omission.

The Indians had gone clean out of my head—as, no doubt, they had gone clean out of yours. I saw little use in stirring that pot again; but I obeyed, as a matter of course.

I found Sergeant Cuff and the gardener with the whisky between them, deep in argument on the growing of roses. The Sergeant was so intent that he held up his hand to silence me as I came in, signing not to interrupt. The question, as far as I could make out, was whether the white moss rose did or did not require budding on the dog-rose to make it grow well. Mr. Begbie said Yes; Sergeant Cuff said No. They turned on me and appealed as hotly as boys over a stolen apple.

Knowing nothing whatever of roses, I steered a middle course—much as her Majesty’s judges do when the scales trouble them by hanging even to a hair.

“Gentlemen,” I said, “there is much to be said on both sides.”

In the lull my impartial sentence produced, I laid my lady’s message on the table under the Sergeant’s eyes.

By this time I had got, as nearly as might be, to hate him; but truth compels me to own he was wonderful in readiness. In half a minute he had read the note, rummaged in his memory for Superintendent Seegrave’s report, picked out the part concerning the Indians, and had his answer ready.

A certain great traveller, who understood the Indians and their language, had been named in the report, hadn’t he? Very well. Did I know the gentleman’s name and address? Very well again. Would I write them on the back of my lady’s message? Much obliged. Sergeant Cuff would look the gentleman up when he went to Frizinghall in the morning.

“Do you expect anything to come of it?” I asked. “Superintendent Seegrave found the Indians as innocent as the babe unborn.”

“Superintendent Seegrave has been proved wrong, up to this time, in all his conclusions,” the Sergeant answered. “It may be worth while to find out tomorrow whether Superintendent Seegrave was wrong about the Indians as well.”

And with that, as if the matter of human guilt and innocence were no more than a change in the weather, he turned straight back to Mr. Begbie, taking up the dispute exactly where it had left off.

“This question between us is a question of soils and seasons, and patience and pains, Mr. Gardener. Now let me put it to you from another point of view. You take your white moss rose—”

I shut the door on them and went out. In the passage I met Penelope hanging about, and asked what she waited for. She was waiting for her young lady’s bell, whenever her young lady chose to call her back to go on with the packing for the next day’s journey.

Further inquiry revealed that Miss Rachel had given, as her reason for wanting to go to her aunt at Frizinghall, that the house was unendurable to her and that she could bear the odious presence of a policeman under the same roof with herself no longer. When she had been told, half an hour since, that her departure would be delayed till two in the afternoon, she had flown into a violent passion. My lady, present at the time, had severely rebuked her, and then—having apparently something to say for her daughter’s private ear—had sent Penelope out.

My girl was in miserably low spirits.

“Nothing goes right, father; nothing is like what it used to be. I feel as if some dreadful misfortune was hanging over us all.”

It was my feeling too; but I put a good face on it before her. Miss Rachel’s bell rang while we spoke. Penelope ran up the back stairs to go on with the packing, and I went the other way towards the hall to see what the glass said about the weather.

As I approached the swing-door leading from the servants’ offices, it was flung open violently from the other side, and Rosanna Spearman ran by me with pain stamped on her face and one hand pressed hard over her heart, as if the pang lived there.

“What’s the matter, my girl?” I called, stopping her. “Are you ill?”

“For God’s sake, don’t speak to me,” she answered; and she twisted out of my hands and fled towards the servants’ staircase.

I called to the cook, within hearing, to look after the poor girl. Two other persons proved to be within hearing as well. Sergeant Cuff darted softly out of my room and asked what was the matter. I answered, “Nothing.”

Mr. Franklin, on the other side, pulled open the swing-door and beckoned me into the hall. His voice had that brittle edge it takes when a man is trying not to show he is frightened.

“Have you seen anything of Rosanna Spearman?” he asked.

“She has just passed me, sir, with a very disturbed face, and in a very odd manner.”

“I am afraid I am innocently the cause of that disturbance, Betteredge.”

“You, sir!”

“I can’t explain it,” said Mr. Franklin; “but if the girl is concerned in the loss of the Diamond, I do really believe she was on the point of confessing everything—to me, of all the people in the world—not two minutes since.”

As he looked towards the swing-door, I fancied I saw it open a little from the inner side. Was there somebody listening? Before I could get to it, the door fell to. When I looked through the moment after, I thought I caught the tails of Sergeant Cuff’s respectable black coat disappearing round the corner of the passage.

He knew, as well as I did, that he could expect no more help from me now that I had found out the direction of his suspicions. Under those circumstances, it was like him to help himself—and to do it by an underground way. Still, not being sure I had really seen him, and not wishing to make needless mischief where Heaven knows there was mischief enough already, I told Mr. Franklin I thought one of the dogs had got into the house; and then begged him to describe what had passed.

“Were you passing through the hall, sir?” I asked. “Did you meet her accidentally, when she spoke to you?”

He pointed to the billiard-table.

“I was knocking the balls about,” he said, “and trying to get this miserable business of the Diamond out of my mind. I happened to look up—and there stood Rosanna Spearman at my side, like a ghost! Her stealing on me in that way was so strange, I hardly knew what to do at first. Seeing a very anxious expression in her face, I asked her if she wished to speak to me. She answered, ‘Yes, if I dare.’

“Knowing what suspicion attaches to her, I could only put one construction on such language. I confess it made me uncomfortable. I had no wish to invite the girl’s confidence. At the same time, in the difficulties that now beset us, I could hardly feel justified in refusing to listen to her, if she was really bent on speaking. It was an awkward position; and I dare say I got out of it awkwardly enough.

“I said, ‘I don’t quite understand you. Is there anything you want me to do?’ Mind, Betteredge, I didn’t speak unkindly. The poor girl can’t help being ugly—I felt that, at the time. The cue was still in my hand, and I went on knocking the balls about, to take off the awkwardness. As it turned out, I only made matters worse. I’m afraid I mortified her without meaning it. She suddenly turned away. ‘He looks at the billiard balls,’ I heard her say. ‘Anything rather than look at me!’ Before I could stop her, she had left the hall.

“I am not quite easy about it, Betteredge. Would you mind telling Rosanna I meant no unkindness? I have been a little hard on her, perhaps, in my own thoughts—I have almost hoped the loss of the Diamond might be traced to her. Not from any ill-will to the poor girl: but—”

He stopped, and returned to the billiard-table, knocking the balls about again—click, click—little sharp sounds that seemed to speak of impatience, not play.

After what had passed between the Sergeant and me, I knew as well as Mr. Franklin what he had left unspoken. Nothing but tracing the Moonstone to our second housemaid could now raise Miss Rachel above the infamous suspicion resting on her in Sergeant Cuff’s mind. It was no longer a question of quieting my young lady’s nerves; it was a question of proving her innocence.

If Rosanna had done nothing to compromise herself, Mr. Franklin’s confessed hope would have been hard enough on her. But she had compromised herself. She had pretended illness and gone secretly to Frizinghall. She had been up all night making something—or destroying something—in private. And she had been at the Shivering Sand that evening under circumstances suspicious, to say the least. For all these reasons, sorry as I was for Rosanna, I could not think Mr. Franklin’s view unnatural in his position.

I said so.

“Yes, yes!” he returned. “But there is just a chance—a very poor one, certainly—that Rosanna’s conduct may admit of some explanation we don’t see yet. I hate hurting a woman’s feelings, Betteredge. Tell the poor creature what I told you to tell her. And if she wants to speak to me—I don’t care whether I get into a scrape or not—send her to me in the library.”

With those words he laid down the cue and left me.

Inquiry at the servants’ offices informed me Rosanna had retired to her room. She declined all offers of assistance with thanks, and asked only to be left to rest in quiet. So there was an end of any confession—if confession there was—for that night. I reported as much to Mr. Franklin; he left the library and went up to bed.

I was putting the lights out and making the windows fast when Samuel came in with news of the two guests I had left in my room. The argument about the white moss rose had apparently come to an end at last. The gardener had gone home. Sergeant Cuff was nowhere to be found below stairs.

I looked into my room. True enough—nothing there but a couple of empty tumblers and a strong smell of hot grog hanging in the air, sweet and harsh at once, like a comfort turned sour.

Had the Sergeant gone to the bedchamber prepared for him? I went upstairs to see.

After reaching the second landing, I thought I heard the sound of quiet, regular breathing on my left-hand side. That way led to the corridor that communicated with Miss Rachel’s room.

I looked in; and there, coiled up on three chairs placed right across the passage—there, with a red handkerchief tied round his grizzled head, his respectable black coat rolled for a pillow—lay Sergeant Cuff, asleep.

He woke instantly and quietly, like a dog the moment you step near.

“Good-night, Mr. Betteredge,” he said. “And mind—if you ever take to growing roses, the white moss rose is all the better for not being budded on the dog-rose, whatever the gardener may say to the contrary!”

“What are you doing here?” I asked. “Why are you not in your proper bed?”

“I am not in my proper bed,” he answered, “because I am one of the many people in this miserable world who can’t earn their money honestly and easily at the same time. There was a coincidence, this evening, between the period of Rosanna Spearman’s return from the Sands and the period when Miss Verinder stated her resolution to leave the house. Whatever Rosanna may have hidden, it’s clear to my mind that your young lady couldn’t go away until she knew that it was hidden. The two must have communicated privately once already tonight. If they try to communicate again when the house is quiet, I want to be in the way and stop it. Don’t blame me for upsetting your sleeping arrangements, Mr. Betteredge—blame the Diamond.”

From somewhere outside, the wind found a keyhole and began to whistle thinly through it—an ugly little tune, like a modern contrivance imitating nature and failing.

“I wish to God the Diamond had never found its way into this house!” I broke out.

Sergeant Cuff looked with a rueful face at the three chairs that were to be his bed.

“So do I,” he said, gravely.

Nothing stirred in the house through the night—not so much as a whispered errand along the passages; and, what was better still, no secret message passed between Miss Rachel and Rosanna to reward the Sergeant’s watchfulness. The servants rose, the grates were coaxed into life, the kettles set singing their thin, bright notes; and yet the hush that lay on us all was heavier than the steam on the window-panes.

I had expected Sergeant Cuff to be off at daybreak for Frizinghall. Instead, he lingered about the place as if he were tasting the air first, like a man who can tell from one breath of it whether rain will come or whether truth will. The morning had a sound of its own—wind worrying the laurels, distant surf labouring, and, nearer, the domestic percussion of breakfast being made ready: knives laid, lids lifted, china clinking as if the house tried to persuade itself it was an ordinary day.

Leaving the Sergeant to whatever quiet mischief he was brewing, I went out into the grounds. The shrubbery walk—Mr. Franklin’s favourite—was all wet leaf and resinous scent. The box and the bay gave off a sharp green perfume where the night’s damp still clung. I had hardly set eyes on Mr. Franklin before Sergeant Cuff appeared, as if he had stepped from the shadow of the bushes on purpose.

The Sergeant went straight to him. Mr. Franklin received the “good morning” with coldness enough to sour milk.

“Have you anything to say to me?” says Mr. Franklin.

“I have something to say to you, sir,” answers the Sergeant, “on the subject of the inquiry I am conducting here. You detected the turn that inquiry was really taking, yesterday. Naturally enough, in your position, you are shocked and distressed. Naturally enough, also, you visit your own angry sense of your own family scandal upon me.”

“What do you want?” Mr. Franklin broke in.

“I want to remind you, sir, that I have, at any rate, thus far, not been proved to be wrong. Bearing that in mind, be pleased to remember, at the same time, that I am an officer of the law acting here under the sanction of the mistress of the house. Under these circumstances, is it, or is it not, your duty as a good citizen, to assist me with any special information which you may happen to possess?”

“I possess no special information,” says Mr. Franklin.

Sergeant Cuff set that answer aside as if it were an empty plate.

“You may save my time, sir, from being wasted on an inquiry at a distance,” he went on, “if you choose to understand me and speak out.”

“I don’t understand you,” says Mr. Franklin; “and I have nothing to say.”

“One of the female servants (I won’t mention names) spoke to you privately, sir, last night.”

Again Mr. Franklin cut him short, with the same stiff denial: “I have nothing to say.”

Standing silent, I remembered a small thing—the swing-door’s faint stir the night before, and the glimpse of coat-tails vanishing down the passage. The Sergeant had heard enough, no doubt, before I came upon him, to suspect that Rosanna had eased her mind by confessing something to Mr. Franklin. The thought was scarcely formed when the subject of it walked into our very path.

Rosanna Spearman appeared at the far end of the shrubbery walk—Rosanna herself, in her plain print dress, following her own desperate will. Penelope came after, plainly trying to turn her back. Rosanna stopped when she saw Mr. Franklin was not alone; her face—what I could make of it at the distance—was perplexed, as if she had been brought up suddenly against a locked door. Penelope hovered behind her, anxious and helpless.

Mr. Franklin saw them as I did. The Sergeant—devilish clever in his way—took no notice at all. In less time than it takes a cook to pinch salt between finger and thumb, he struck in as if the talk had never paused. He raised his voice, too, so that the words travelled down the wet green tunnel of the walk and could not help but reach Rosanna’s ears.

“You needn’t be afraid of harming the girl, sir,” he said, loud and smooth. “On the contrary, I recommend you to honour me with your confidence, if you feel any interest in Rosanna Spearman.”

Mr. Franklin, catching the trap as a practised man catches a false note in music, instantly affected not to have seen them either. He answered, loudly enough to carry:

“I take no interest whatever in Rosanna Spearman.”

I looked down the walk. Rosanna turned round the instant those words reached her. The struggle went out of her. Where a moment before she had resisted Penelope’s coaxing, she now let my daughter take her by the arm and lead her away—quiet as a child being brought back from the edge of a pond.

The breakfast-bell rang just then—its bright metallic insistence cutting through the wind like a spoon struck on a copper pot. Even Sergeant Cuff had to own himself beaten for the moment.

He came close to me. “I shall go to Frizinghall, Mr. Betteredge,” he said quietly, “and I shall be back before two.”

And off he went, without another word. For a few hours, at least, the house could breathe without feeling his eyes in every crack and corner.

When we were alone, Mr. Franklin broke out to me, his temper working under his politeness as yeast works under dough.

“You must make it right with Rosanna,” he said. “I seem to be fated to say or do something awkward, before that unlucky girl. You must have seen yourself that Sergeant Cuff laid a trap for both of us. If he could confuse me, or irritate her into breaking out, either she or I might have said something which would answer his purpose. On the spur of the moment, I saw no better way out of it than the way I took. It stopped the girl from saying anything, and it showed the Sergeant that I saw through him. He was evidently listening, Betteredge, when I was speaking to you last night.”

Listening, yes—and remembering too, I thought; remembering what I had told him of Rosanna’s miserable love, and using it as neatly as a cook uses a sharp knife. But I kept that to myself.

“As to listening, sir,” I said, “we shall all be rowing in the same boat if this sort of thing goes on much longer. Prying, and peeping, and listening are the natural occupations of people situated as we are. In another day or two, Mr. Franklin, we shall all be struck dumb together—for this reason, that we shall all be listening to surprise each other’s secrets, and all know it. Excuse my breaking out, sir. The horrid mystery hanging over us in this house gets into my head like liquor, and makes me wild. I won’t forget what you have told me. I’ll take the first opportunity of making it right with Rosanna Spearman.”

“You haven’t said anything to her yet about last night, have you?” he asked.

“No, sir.”

“Then say nothing now. I had better not invite the girl’s confidence, with the Sergeant on the look-out to surprise us together. My conduct is not very consistent, Betteredge—is it? I see no way out of this business, which isn’t dreadful to think of, unless the Diamond is traced to Rosanna. And yet I can’t, and won’t, help Sergeant Cuff to find the girl out.”

Unreasonable enough—yet I understood it. A man’s heart does not always keep time with his fears; and, in a house like ours, where suspicion blew in with the sea-wind and rattled the very window-sashes, there was no keeping one’s feelings in a tidy pantry, labelled and shelved.

While Sergeant Cuff rode to Frizinghall, the day’s life went on in its strange mixture of routine and dread—like a family meal eaten under a thundercloud.

Miss Rachel waited for the carriage that was to take her to her aunt’s, still shut up obstinately in her room. My lady and Mr. Franklin breakfasted together. I did not see the table close, but I heard the small domestic sounds through the door—cups set down, a chair moved, the clink of a spoon against china; and under it all the wind, pushing at the house with gusty shoulders. After breakfast, Mr. Franklin took one of his sudden resolutions and went out at once, as if walking could blow the thoughts out of his head. I alone saw him go; he told me he would return before the Sergeant.

The weather had changed, as it had threatened in the night. Heavy rain had come before dawn, then given way to high wind. Though the clouds gathered and broke apart again, the rain held off for the present. The air smelled of soaked earth and bruised leaves, and, now and then, of the salt from the sea when the gusts opened a path for it. It was no bad day for a long walk—if you were young and strong, and could set your body against the wind’s steady bullying.

After breakfast I attended my lady and helped her with the household accounts. Figures, ink, and ruled paper—our little ceremony of control, as if sums could keep calamity from spilling over. She only once spoke of the Moonstone, and then only to forbid speaking.

“Wait till that man comes back,” she said, meaning Sergeant Cuff. “We must speak of it then: we are not obliged to speak of it now.”

When I left her, Penelope was waiting for me in my room, her face anxious in that quiet way she has—like a cook listening for a boil-over she can’t yet see.

“I wish, father, you would come and speak to Rosanna,” she said. “I am very uneasy about her.”

I suspected readily enough. Still, it is a maxim of mine—men being superior creatures—that when a woman wants me to do anything, I make her tell me why. The more you force them to rummage their minds for reasons, the more manageable they are. (It is not their fault, poor souls, that they act first and think afterwards; it is the fault of those who humour them.)

Penelope’s reason was plain enough, and she gave it me straight.

“I am afraid, father,” she said, “Mr. Franklin has hurt Rosanna cruelly, without intending it.”

“What took Rosanna into the shrubbery walk?” I asked.

“Her own madness,” says Penelope. “I can call it nothing else. She was bent on speaking to Mr. Franklin, this morning, come what might of it. I did my best to stop her; you saw that. If I could only have got her away before she heard those dreadful words—”

“There! there!” I said. “Don’t lose your head. I can’t call to mind that anything happened to alarm Rosanna.”

“Nothing to alarm her, father. But Mr. Franklin said he took no interest whatever in her—and, oh, he said it in such a cruel voice!”

“He said it to stop the Sergeant’s mouth,” I answered.

“I told her that,” says Penelope. “But you see, father (though Mr. Franklin isn’t to blame), he’s been mortifying and disappointing her for weeks and weeks past; and now this comes on the top of it all! She has no right, of course, to expect him to take any interest in her. It’s quite monstrous that she should forget herself and her station in that way. But she seems to have lost pride, and proper feeling, and everything. She frightened me, father, when Mr. Franklin said those words. They seemed to turn her into stone. A sudden quiet came over her, and she has gone about her work, ever since, like a woman in a dream.”

Something in the way she spoke—more than the words themselves—made me uneasy. I remembered Rosanna the night before, her face cut to the heart; and now, by ill-luck and cunning, she had been stung again in the tenderest place. Sad—sad, and worse for the pitying, because she had no right that could be owned aloud. Still, pain does not ask permission.

I had promised Mr. Franklin to put matters right, and this seemed the fittest time. We found Rosanna sweeping the corridor outside the bedrooms—neat as ever, pale, composed, her broom moving back and forth with the steady patience of habit. The corridor held its usual smells: beeswax, clean linen, a faint trace of yesterday’s coal-smoke. Underneath them all, the wind made a low, continuous moan in the house’s seams, as if the building itself were trying to speak and could not find the words.

I noticed something in Rosanna’s eyes—a dimness, a dulness—not the look of crying, but of staring too long at one thought until it blurred everything else. There was nothing new for her to see there; she had passed that corridor hundreds upon hundreds of times. What she was looking at was inside.

“Cheer up, Rosanna!” I said. “You mustn’t fret over your own fancies. I have got something to say to you from Mr. Franklin.”

And I put the matter before her in the right light, as friendly and comforting as I knew how. My principles about women are severe; my practice, when a woman stands before me with misery on her face, is softer than I like to confess.

“Mr. Franklin is very kind and considerate. Please to thank him.”

That was all she said. No tremble, no flush, no tears—nothing. Penelope had said she worked like a woman in a dream. Now I saw she listened and spoke like one too—like a thing wound up and set moving.

“Are you quite sure, Rosanna, that you understand me?” I asked.

“Quite sure.”

She echoed me, not like a living woman, but like a creature worked by machinery, and kept sweeping all the while. I took the broom from her as gently as I could, the bristles whispering against my sleeve.

“Come, come, my girl!” I said. “This is not like yourself. You have got something on your mind. I’m your friend—and I’ll stand your friend, even if you have done wrong. Make a clean breast of it, Rosanna—make a clean breast of it!”

There had been a time when such words would have filled her eyes. Now they did not alter her face by the breadth of a hair.

“Yes,” she said, “I’ll make a clean breast of it.”

“To my lady?” I asked.

“No.”

“To Mr. Franklin?”

“Yes; to Mr. Franklin.”

I hardly knew what to say. She was not fit to take in cautions, and Mr. Franklin had expressly told me not to encourage her confidence while the Sergeant lay in wait to catch us together. Feeling my way, I told her only that Mr. Franklin had gone out for a walk.

“It doesn’t matter,” she answered. “I shan’t trouble Mr. Franklin, today.”

“Why not speak to my lady?” I said. “The way to relieve your mind is to speak to the merciful and Christian mistress who has always been kind to you.”

She looked at me then—grave, steady, as if she were storing the words away where they could do no good and no harm. Then she took the broom back from my hands and moved slowly down the corridor, setting herself again to the same back-and-forth labour, as if the scrape of bristle on board could scour something out of her thoughts.

“No,” she said, speaking more to herself than to us; “I know a better way of relieving my mind than that.”

“What is it?”

“Please to let me go on with my work.”

Penelope followed, offering to help. Rosanna answered quietly, “No. I want to do my work. Thank you, Penelope.” Then she looked round at me. “Thank you, Mr. Betteredge.”

There was no moving her—no reaching her. Penelope and I came away as we had come, leaving her sweeping the corridor, like a woman in a dream.

“This is a matter for the doctor to look into,” I said. “It’s beyond me.”

Penelope reminded me that Mr. Candy was ill still, laid up by the chill he had caught the night of the dinner-party. His assistant—Mr. Ezra Jennings—was at hand, certainly; but in our parts nobody knew much of him, and, right or wrong, we did not like him or trust him. There were other doctors at Frizinghall, but they were strangers to our house; and Penelope feared strangers might do Rosanna more harm than good in her state.

I thought of speaking to my lady. Then I remembered the weight already on her, and I hesitated—like a man who holds back a fresh bad dish from a table already bitter. Still, something had to be done. The girl’s condition was downright alarming, and my mistress ought to know.

Unwilling enough, I went to my lady’s sitting-room. Nobody was there. My lady was shut up with Miss Rachel, and I could not see her until she chose to come out.

I waited—listening to the house’s sounds as if they might tell me what to do: the wind’s fretful drone, the distant clatter from the kitchen, and now and then a door-latch clicking like a tongue checking itself. I waited until the clock on the front staircase struck the quarter to two.

Five minutes afterwards, I heard my name called from the drive outside. I knew the voice at once.

Sergeant Cuff had returned from Frizinghall.

I went down to the front door, and found Sergeant Cuff on the steps, as quietly planted there as if the house had grown him like an iron gate-post.

After what had passed between us, it went sorely against my grain to seem eager in his affairs; but curiosity is like the smell of onions in a warm kitchen—once it gets into you, it clings. My dignity slipped on the wet flags, and out came my question before I could salt it with reserve.

“What news from Frizinghall?”

“I have seen the Indians,” he said. “And I have found out what Rosanna bought privately in the town, on Thursday last. The Indians will be set free on Wednesday next week. There isn’t a doubt on my mind, and there isn’t a doubt on Mr. Murthwaite’s mind, that they came here to steal the Moonstone. Their calculations were all thrown out by what happened in the house on Wednesday night; and they have no more to do with the actual loss of the jewel than you have. But I can tell you one thing, Mr. Betteredge—if we don’t find the Moonstone, they will. You have not heard the last of the three jugglers yet.”

As he said those startling words, Mr. Franklin came back from his walk. He managed his curiosity better than I had managed mine; passed us without a syllable; and went straight in, as if he had left his mind somewhere out on the gravel and meant to fetch it later.

I had already made a sacrifice of my pride; I resolved to dine on the whole dish.

“So much for the Indians,” I said. “What about Rosanna next?”

He shook his head, like a man smelling a pot that wouldn’t declare itself. “The mystery in that quarter is thicker than ever,” he answered. “I have traced her to a shop at Frizinghall, kept by a linen-draper named Maltby. She bought nothing at any other draper’s, or at any milliner’s, or tailor’s; and she bought nothing at Maltby’s but a piece of long cloth. She was very particular in choosing a certain quality. As to quantity, she bought enough to make a nightgown.”

“Whose nightgown?” I asked.

“Her own, to be sure. Between twelve and three on the Thursday morning, she must have slipped down to your young lady’s room, to settle the hiding of the Moonstone while all the rest of you were in bed. In going back to her own room, her nightgown must have brushed the wet paint on the door. She couldn’t wash out the stain; and she couldn’t safely destroy the nightgown without first providing another like it, to make the inventory of her linen complete.”

“What proves it was Rosanna’s nightgown?” I objected. (A man may be cornered by facts, and still feel stubborn, as a cook does when told the fault is in the seasoning and not in the fire.)

“The material she bought for making the substitute,” he said. “If it had been Miss Verinder’s nightgown, she would have had to buy lace, and frilling, and Lord knows what besides; and she wouldn’t have had time to make it in one night. Plain long cloth means a plain servant’s nightgown. No, no, Mr. Betteredge—all that is clear enough. The pinch of the question is—why, after having provided the substitute, does she hide the smeared nightgown instead of destroying it? If the girl won’t speak out, there is only one way of settling the difficulty. The hiding-place at the Shivering Sand must be searched—and the true state of the case will be discovered there.”

“How are you to find the place?” I asked.

“I am sorry to disappoint you,” he said, in that mild, dreary way of his which gives a chill, “but that’s a secret which I mean to keep to myself.”

“Now, Mr. Betteredge,” he went on, “suppose we drop speculation, and get to business. I told Joyce to have an eye on Rosanna. Where is Joyce?”

Joyce was the Frizinghall policeman left at his disposal. The clock struck two; and, punctual as the kitchen clock when the joint is ready, the carriage came round to take Miss Rachel to her aunt’s.

“One thing at a time,” said the Sergeant, stopping me as I was about to send to look for Joyce. “I must attend to Miss Verinder first.”

The rain was still threatening; so it was the close carriage that had been appointed. Sergeant Cuff beckoned Samuel down from the rumble.

“You will see a friend of mine waiting among the trees, on this side of the lodge-gate,” he said. “My friend, without stopping the carriage, will get up into the rumble with you. You have nothing to do but to hold your tongue, and shut your eyes. Otherwise, you will get into trouble.”

He sent Samuel back, and there was no more to be said. But in my own mind the kettle began to boil over. A watch set on my young lady! A spy behind her in her mother’s carriage! The very notion jarred in my ears as if somebody had brought a factory hammer into the garden—smashing the proper quiet of the place where you ought to hear birds, rain, and the soft talk of leaves. I could have bitten my own tongue clean off for speaking to Sergeant Cuff at all.

My lady was the first to come out. She stood aside on the top step, not saying a word either to him or to me. Lips closed; arms folded under her light garden cloak; still as a statue, waiting.

Then Miss Rachel came down—very nicely dressed in soft yellow stuff that set off her dark complexion, clipped tight at the waist like a little jacket. A smart straw hat; a white veil twisted round it; primrose gloves fitting her hands like a second skin. Her black hair lay smooth as satin; her ears were rosy shells with a pearl dangling from each. She moved swiftly—straight as a lily on its stem, lithe as a young cat. Nothing altered in her pretty face that I could see, but her eyes and her lips. Her eyes were brighter and fiercer than I liked; her lips had lost their colour and their smile so completely that I hardly knew them.

She kissed her mother, sudden and hasty, on the cheek.

“Try to forgive me, mamma,” she said—then pulled down her veil so vehemently she tore it. Next moment she ran down the steps and rushed into the carriage as if it was a hiding-place.

Sergeant Cuff was as quick. He put Samuel back, and stood before Miss Rachel with the carriage-door open in his hand, just as she had settled herself.

“What do you want?” says Miss Rachel, from behind her veil.

“I want to say one word to you, miss,” he answered. “I can’t presume to stop your paying a visit to your aunt. I can only venture to say that your leaving us, as things are now, puts an obstacle in the way of my recovering your Diamond. Please to understand that; and now decide for yourself whether you go or stay.”

Miss Rachel never answered. “Drive on, James!” she called out.

Without another word, the Sergeant shut the door.

Just as it closed, Mr. Franklin came running down the steps.

“Good-bye, Rachel,” he said, holding out his hand.

“Drive on!” cried Miss Rachel, louder than ever, and took no more notice of him than she had taken of Sergeant Cuff.

Mr. Franklin stepped back thunderstruck. The coachman looked towards my lady. My lady—anger, sorrow, and shame all struggling together in her face—made him a sign to start the horses; then turned and went back hastily into the house.

Mr. Franklin found his voice again and called after her, as the carriage drove off, “Aunt! you were quite right. Accept my thanks for all your kindness—and let me go.”

My lady turned as though to speak; then, as if distrusting herself, waved her hand kindly.

“Let me see you, before you leave us, Franklin,” she said, in a broken voice—and went to her room.

“Do me a last favour, Betteredge,” Mr. Franklin said to me, tears in his eyes. “Get me away to the train as soon as you can!”

And he too went in. Miss Rachel had unmanned him utterly; and if that doesn’t tell you how fond he was of her, nothing will.

Sergeant Cuff and I were left face to face at the foot of the steps. He stood with his face set towards a gap in the trees, commanding a view of one of the windings of the drive; hands in pockets; softly whistling “The Last Rose of Summer” to himself, as if the tune were the only thing in the world that still had its right notes.

“There’s a time for everything,” I said, savage enough. “This isn’t a time for whistling.”

At that moment the carriage showed through the gap, winding towards the lodge-gate. Another man besides Samuel was plainly visible in the rumble behind.

“All right!” said the Sergeant to himself. He turned to me. “It’s no time for whistling, Mr. Betteredge, as you say. It’s time to take this business in hand, now, without sparing anybody. We’ll begin with Rosanna Spearman. Where is Joyce?”

We both called; no answer. I sent one of the stable-boys to look for him.

“You heard what I said to Miss Verinder?” the Sergeant remarked, while we waited. “And you saw how she received it? I tell her plainly that her leaving us will be an obstacle in the way of my recovering her Diamond—and she leaves, in the face of that statement! Your young lady has got a travelling companion in her mother’s carriage, Mr. Betteredge—and the name of it is, the Moonstone.”

I said nothing. I held on to my belief in Miss Rachel as a man holds on to the taste of home bread when he’s forced to swallow strange fare: whatever else is taken, that must remain.

The stable-boy came back, with Joyce behind him—very unwillingly, as it seemed to me.

“Where is Rosanna Spearman?” asked Sergeant Cuff.

“I can’t account for it, sir,” Joyce began; “and I am very sorry. But somehow or other—”

“Before I went to Frizinghall,” said the Sergeant, cutting him short, “I told you to keep your eyes on Rosanna Spearman, without allowing her to discover that she was being watched. Do you mean to tell me you have let her give you the slip?”

“I am afraid, sir,” says Joyce, beginning to tremble, “that I was perhaps a little too careful not to let her discover me. There are such a many passages in the lower parts of this house—”

“How long is it since you missed her?”

“Nigh on an hour since, sir.”

“You can go back to your regular business at Frizinghall,” said the Sergeant, composed as ever, quiet and dreary as a winter drizzle. “I don’t think your talents are at all in our line, Mr. Joyce. Your present form of employment is a trifle beyond you. Good morning.”

The man slunk off.

How I was affected by finding Rosanna missing is beyond me to put neatly. I was in fifty different minds at once. I stood staring at Sergeant Cuff, and language went out of me like a fire going down for want of air.
