“No, Mr. Betteredge,” he said, as if he could pick my thoughts like currants from a cake. “Your young friend Rosanna won’t slip through my fingers so easy as you think. As long as I know where Miss Verinder is, I have the means at my disposal of tracing Miss Verinder’s accomplice. I prevented them from communicating last night. Very good. They will get together at Frizinghall, instead of getting together here. The present inquiry must be simply shifted (rather sooner than I had anticipated) from this house to the house at which Miss Verinder is visiting. In the meantime, I’m afraid I must trouble you to call the servants together again.”

It is disgraceful, but true: those words gave me another attack of the detective-fever. I forgot I hated him. I seized him confidentially by the arm.

“For goodness’ sake, tell us what you are going to do with the servants now?”

Sergeant Cuff stood stock-still, and addressed himself in a kind of melancholy rapture to the empty air.

“If this man,” said the Sergeant (meaning me, as plain as day), “only understood the growing of roses he would be the most completely perfect character on the face of creation!”

After that strong outburst, he sighed, and put his arm through mine as if we were old friends walking out to inspect a bed of blooms.

“This is how it stands,” he said, dropping down again to business. “Rosanna has done one of two things. She has either gone direct to Frizinghall (before I can get there), or she has gone first to visit her hiding-place at the Shivering Sand. The first thing to find out is, which of the servants saw the last of her before she left the house.”

We instituted the inquiry. It turned out Nancy, the kitchenmaid, had seen the last of Rosanna. Nancy had seen her slip out with a letter in her hand and stop the butcher’s man, who had just been delivering some meat at the back door—raw joints in their brown paper, smelling of rain and blood and the road; the sort of smell that clings under the nails even after scrubbing.

Nancy had heard Rosanna ask him to post the letter when he got back to Frizinghall. The man had looked at the address and said it was a roundabout way of delivering a letter directed to Cobb’s Hole, to post it at Frizinghall—and that, moreover, on a Saturday, which would prevent the letter getting to its destination until Monday morning. Rosanna had answered that the delay till Monday was of no importance. The only thing she wished to be sure of was that he would do what she told him. The man had promised, and driven away. Nancy had been called back to her work in the kitchen; and no other person had seen anything afterwards of Rosanna Spearman.

“Well?” I asked, when we were alone again.

“Well,” says the Sergeant, “I must go to Frizinghall.”

“About the letter, sir?”

“Yes. The memorandum of the hiding-place is in that letter. I must see the address at the post-office. If it is the address I suspect, I shall pay our friend Mrs. Yolland another visit on Monday next.”

I went with him to order the pony-chaise. In the stable-yard we got a new light thrown on the missing girl.

News of Rosanna Spearman’s vanishing didn’t stay politely indoors. It ran, as rumor always runs, along the back passages and out into the yard, leaping from mouth to mouth with the clatter of pails and the thud of boots on stones. The out-of-door servants had been questioning each other in their own sharp dialect—words shortened by wind and salted by the sea—and they had at last caught hold of a quick little imp of a boy, nicknamed “Duffy,” who weeded in the garden when he felt like earning pennies.

Duffy swore he had seen Rosanna no more than half an hour ago—seen her in the fir-plantation, not walking as a tired girl might, but running, with her skirts snatched up and her breath likely tearing at her throat, straight in the direction of the shore.

“Does this boy know the coast hereabouts?” Sergeant Cuff asked.

“He’s been born and bred on it,” I said. You could hear it in the boy’s voice—sea-worn, bird-quick, tuned to distance and weather. There are ears in such children that notice what older people drown out with talk.

“Duffy,” says the Sergeant, “do you want to earn a shilling? If you do, come along with me. Keep the pony-chaise ready, Mr. Betteredge, till I come back.”

He set off for the Shivering Sand as if the wind pushed him from behind; and Duffy—like all our young savages when excitement gets in them—gave a wild howl and scampered after, his feet making that light staccato patter that’s almost more alarming than heavy running, because it sounds like a creature chasing rather than being led.

After they left, my mind went into a strange state—restless and stupefied together, like a kitchen fire that smokes instead of drawing. I did a dozen needless things, turning from room to room and door to door, and afterwards could not have named one of them. I only remember the house noises: the hollow complaint of the wind in the passages, the rain beginning to tick at the panes, the servants’ voices muffled, as if the very air was thickening with what we feared.

How long it was before Duffy came back I can’t truly say. Time, when trouble is brewing, turns queer—now dragging like a heavy cart-wheel in mud, now skipping whole yards like a hare. The boy came running, hair plastered already with damp, and thrust a leaf of paper at me. Sergeant Cuff had torn it from his pocket-book and scribbled in pencil:

“Send me one of Rosanna Spearman’s boots, and be quick about it.”

I sent the first woman-servant I could lay hands on up to Rosanna’s room, and I sent Duffy back with word that I would follow myself with the boot.

This wasn’t the quickest obedience—no, I know that as well as any man. But something in me would not let Rosanna’s boot go out of my sight like a mere object passed along a chain. My old foolish resolve to screen the poor girl—if there was still anything left to screen—came back upon me at the eleventh hour, and with it that detective-fever which makes a man fancy he can smell out truth as one might smell a stew that’s beginning to scorch.

When I had the boot in my hand—heavy, damp-limbed leather, with the faint sourness of worn days in it, and a whisper of blacking long gone stale—I started off at the nearest approach to a run that a man turned seventy can reasonably attempt. I could hear my own breath rasping, and the blood knocking at my ears with a dull drumlike pulse, as if my body itself had become part of the storm’s music.

As I drew nearer the shore, the sky closed in like a lid. Clouds gathered black; rain came down in great white sheets, driven so hard before the wind that it looked less like falling than like flying sideways. The sea made its own terrible sound—no polite lap of waves, but the deep thunder of water striking the sand-bank at the mouth of the bay, booming and withdrawing and booming again, as if the coastline were a great instrument played by a ruthless hand.

A little further on, I passed Duffy crouching for shelter under the lee of the sandhills. Even over the gale I caught a thin, frightened whimper in his breathing, the way a child tries not to cry.

And then I saw it all: the raging sea; the rollers tumbling in on the sand-bank; the driven rain sweeping over the waters like a garment torn loose; and the yellow wilderness of beach with one solitary black figure standing on it. Sergeant Cuff.

He waved his hand towards the north as soon as he saw me. “Keep on that side!” he shouted. “And come on down here to me!”

I went down, choking for breath, heart leaping as if it meant to jump clean out of my chest. I had a hundred questions, and not one of them would pass my lips. His face stopped them. There was something in his eyes—something fixed and dreadful—that chilled me more than the rain.

He snatched the boot from my hand and set it in a footmark on the sand, bearing south from where we stood and pointing straight towards the rocky ledge called the South Spit. The mark was not yet blurred away by the rain, and the boot fitted it to a hair—as if the ground itself remembered her weight and would not be denied.

He pointed at the boot in the footprint without speaking. The sea roared behind him, and the wind hissed through the rain, and in that monstrous noise his silence was like a knife.

I caught at his arm and tried to speak. Nothing. My throat felt sealed.

He moved on, following the footsteps down and down to where sand met rock. The South Spit lay just awash with the flowing tide; the waters heaved over the hidden face of the Shivering Sand. Now this way, now that, with an obstinate patience dreadful to watch, Sergeant Cuff tried the boot in the prints, and always found it pointing the same way—straight to the rocks. Hunt as he might, no sign could he find anywhere of footsteps walking from them.

At last he gave it up. Still silent, he looked at me again; then he looked out over the water, rising deeper and deeper over the quicksand.

I looked where he looked—and saw his thought written in his face.

A dumb trembling crawled over me. My knees gave way. I sank down on the beach, the wet sand sucking at my clothes as if it too wanted to keep what fell into it.

“She has been back at the hiding-place,” I heard him say to himself. “Some fatal accident has happened to her on those rocks.”

But as he spoke, Rosanna’s altered looks and words and actions rose before me—the numbed way she had listened and answered when I found her sweeping the corridor only hours before, as if her mind was elsewhere and her body only doing what habit required. The Sergeant’s guess struck wide of the dreadful truth; I knew it—knew it in a way that didn’t come by reasoning but by the sort of knowledge you can’t translate into neat words, because it’s made of pity, and memory, and a man’s fear for his own.

I tried to tell him. I tried to say, “The death she has died, Sergeant, was a death of her own seeking.” But my tongue would not serve me. The trembling held me. I could not feel the rain; I could not see the tide rising. Everything turned unreal, like a dream where you watch your own hands and don’t know how to move them.

The poor lost creature came back to me as she had been in earlier days—on that morning when I went to fetch her into the house, when she spoke of the Shivering Sand as if it called to her against her will, and wondered aloud whether her grave was waiting for her there. There are old country sayings for such things, in words half-forgotten now, because the young learn polished speech and lose the rough, true names that fitted the land. And yet no fine language in the world can carry what a place like that means to those who live beside it: the slow swallowing, the silence after the cry, the cruel patience of the sand.

The horror struck at me through my own child. My girl was just Rosanna’s age. If my girl had been tried as Rosanna was tried, she too might have lived that miserable life and died this dreadful death.

Sergeant Cuff, kindly enough, lifted me up and turned me away from the sight of the place where she had perished. With that small mercy, I began to fetch breath again and to notice what was before me.

Looking towards the sandhills, I saw the men-servants from out-of-doors and the fisherman named Yolland running down together, calling out through the storm to know if the girl had been found. Their voices came broken on the wind; the sea kept its own harsh chant underneath, never pausing, as if it would drown human speech and human grief alike.

In the fewest words, the Sergeant showed them the evidence of the footmarks and told them a fatal accident must have happened. Then he singled out the fisherman and asked, turning again to the sea:

“Tell me. Could a boat have taken her off, in such weather as this, from those rocks where her footmarks stop?”

Yolland pointed to the rollers tumbling in on the sand-bank, and to the great waves leaping in clouds of foam against the headlands.

“No boat that ever was built,” he said, “could have got to her through that.”

Sergeant Cuff looked one last time at the footmarks, already fast blurring under the rain.

“There,” he said, “is the evidence she can’t have left this place by land. And here is the evidence she can’t have got away by sea.”

He paused, thinking, the rain running off the brim of his hat in steady streams. “She was seen running towards this place, half an hour before I got here from the house. Some time has passed since then—call it an hour ago. How high would the water be, at that time, on this side of the rocks?” He pointed to the south side—the side not filled up by the quicksand.

“As the tide makes today,” Yolland answered, “there wouldn’t have been water enough to drown a kitten on that side of the Spit an hour since.”

The Sergeant turned northward, towards the quicksand. “How much on this side?”

“Less still,” said Yolland. “The Shivering Sand would have been just awash, and no more.”

The Sergeant turned to me and said the accident must have happened on the quicksand side.

That loosened my tongue at last, like a knot giving way.

“No accident!” I cried. “When she came to this place, she came weary of her life, to end it here.”

He started back. “How do you know?”

The others crowded close, hungry for meaning in the wind-whipped confusion, like people pressing around a hearth when the kettle begins to sing.

The Sergeant recovered instantly. He put them back with a hand and a look. “He’s an old man,” he said. “The discovery has shaken him. Let him alone a little.”

Then, to Yolland: “Is there any chance of finding her, when the tide ebbs again?”

“None,” Yolland answered. “What the Sand gets, the Sand keeps for ever.”

After saying this, the fisherman stepped nearer to me. His voice lowered, as if even now he respected the dead, and the storm’s roar made a kind of harsh privacy around us.

“Mr. Betteredge,” he said, “I have a word to say to you about the young woman’s death. Four foot out, broadwise, along the side of the Spit, there’s a shelf of rock, about half fathom down under the sand. My question is—why didn’t she strike that? If she slipped, by accident, from off the Spit, she fell in where there’s foothold at the bottom, at a depth that would barely cover her to the waist. She must have waded out, or jumped out, into the Deeps beyond—or she wouldn’t be missing now. No accident, sir! The Deeps of the Quicksand have got her. And they have got her by her own act.”

After that—spoken by a man whose knowledge came not from books but from tides, seabirds, and the sound the sand makes when it shifts—Sergeant Cuff said nothing. None of us did. The storm filled our mouths for us.

With one accord we turned and went back up the slope of the beach. At the sandhillocks we met the under-groom running from the house. He was a good lad, with an honest respect for me, and he held a little note as carefully as if it were something breakable, his face decent with sorrow.

“Penelope sent me with this, Mr. Betteredge,” he said. “She found it in Rosanna’s room.”

It was Rosanna’s last word to the old man who had tried—thank God, had always tried—to befriend her.

“You have often forgiven me, Mr. Betteredge, in past times. When you next see the Shivering Sand, try to forgive me once more. I have found my grave where my grave was waiting for me. I have lived, and died, sir, grateful for your kindness.”

No more than that. Yet it was enough to break me. Tears come easy when you’re young and beginning the world; they come easy when you’re old and leaving it. I burst out crying there in the rain, the note trembling in my hand like a living thing.

Sergeant Cuff stepped nearer—kindly meant, I don’t doubt. I shrank back.

“Don’t touch me,” I said. “It’s the dread of you that has driven her to it.”

“You are wrong, Mr. Betteredge,” he answered, quietly. “But there will be time enough to speak of it when we are indoors again.”

I followed the rest of them, leaning on the groom’s arm. Through the driving rain we went back—to meet the trouble and the terror waiting for us at the house.

The alarm had outrun us down the passages, as a smell outruns the cook who carries it—sharp, raw fear, like a pan forgotten on a sudden flame. We came on the servants gathered and scattering again in little eddies; a whisper flew, a sob answered it; skirts brushed and shoes scuffed the boards so that the house sounded altered, as if the very timber had taken to trembling. In such moments a great place has its own weather: you hear it before you see it, a draught of voices under doors, a choking hush where there ought to be the ordinary music of work.

We were passing my lady’s door when it burst open from within, flung wide as if the room itself could no longer bear what had been said in it. Her ladyship came out among us—Mr. Franklin at her shoulder, speaking to her in low urgent tones that could not mend her—she being, for once, wholly mastered by horror.

“You are answerable for this!” she cried, and her hand went out at Sergeant Cuff as though she would strike him with the accusation. “Gabriel! give that wretch his money—and release me from the sight of him!”

It was dreadful to see her so; and yet, in the very violence, I could hear the strain of the house’s discipline snapping: the old quiet order, like a well-tended stock-pot kept at a patient simmer, broken into boil-over. The women behind me made small sounds—breaths caught, a faint moan—such thin noises as show how near a mind is to spilling.

The Sergeant, alone among us, held himself as steady as a knife laid flat. He did not lift his voice; he did not shrink. He answered her with that grave restraint of his, respectful and hard as a properly set joint.

“I am no more answerable for this distressing calamity, my lady, than you are. If, in half an hour from this, you still insist on my leaving the house, I will accept your ladyship’s dismissal, but not your ladyship’s money.”

The firmness of it—quiet, not insolent—took hold of her. Mr. Franklin caught at the advantage; he drew her back by degrees, as you might coax a frightened creature from the edge of a drop. The door shut on them.

In the momentary lull, Sergeant Cuff’s eyes travelled over the servants with the same exactness with which a good judge of provisions will glance at a market-stall and know what is fresh and what is tainted. All the women were frightened; one alone was crying. He marked Penelope’s tears as if they were a stain that must have a cause.

“When your father has changed his wet clothes,” he said to her, “come and speak to us—in your father’s room.”

Before the half-hour was out, I had got myself into dry things—the damp having sunk through me to the bones, like cold brine—and had found such change of dress as the Sergeant required. Penelope came in then, obedient to the message; and if my tongue goes plain in many matters, it fails me at the sight of my own child in trouble. There are comforts that do not belong to grand folks alone; poor people have them too, though we are forced to take them quickly and in silence, between one duty and the next.

I drew her to me and set her on my knee. I kissed her, and I prayed God bless her. She hid her face against my chest and put her arms about my neck; and we waited so, without speech, while the house beyond us kept up its uneasy murmur. It seemed to me then that the sound of the great place had changed its key, as if some note had died in it and left a hole where the daily noises used to be—pots set down, knives on boards, the comforting clink of plates that tells you life is going on. Now there was only the restless tread and the half-stifled whispering, and every creak in the passage made you start.

The poor dead girl—God rest her—must have been at the bottom of it, I thought, for my daughter and for me. Sergeant Cuff went to the window and stood looking out, as if the air beyond might speak plainer than we could. I felt it right to thank him for giving us this breathing space, and I did. Fine people have, among their many luxuries, the luxury of indulging their feelings. We have no such privilege. Necessity—who is a harsh master to those under stairs—spares our betters and has no pity on us. We learn to press our feelings down and go on with the work, as you press down a stubborn paste and knead it smooth. I don’t complain. I only note it.

When he turned from the glass, Penelope and I were ready.

He asked her, first, if she knew what had led her fellow-servant to destroy herself.

My daughter answered—what you will foresee—that it was for love of Mr. Franklin Blake.

He asked next if she had spoken this notion of hers to any other person.

“I have not mentioned it,” Penelope said, “for Rosanna’s sake.”

I added, for I could not keep it back, “And for Mr. Franklin’s sake too, my dear. If Rosanna has died for love of him, it is not with his knowledge nor by his fault. Let him leave the house to-day, if he does leave it, without the useless pain of knowing the truth.”

“Quite right,” said Sergeant Cuff.

Then he fell silent again, with that look of his which seems always to be measuring one story against another—comparing what Penelope had said with some private thought of his own, kept close as a receipt written in a language you are not meant to read.

At the end of the half-hour, my mistress’s bell rang.

On my way to answer it, I met Mr. Franklin coming out of her ladyship’s sitting-room. He told me her ladyship was ready to see Sergeant Cuff—again in my presence—and he added that he himself wished to say two words to the Sergeant first.

As we went back towards my room, he stopped in the hall and looked at the railway time-table, as if the paper could tell him how to bear what was waiting. The house was quieter there, but not kindly quiet. You could hear, through the stillness, the far-off stir of servants at loose ends: a door closing too sharply; a footstep checked, then hurried; the whispering that runs like mice behind wainscot when disaster has happened.

“Are you really going to leave us, sir?” I asked. “Miss Rachel will surely come right again, if you only give her time?”

“She will come right again,” Mr. Franklin answered, “when she hears that I have gone away, and that she will see me no more.”

I took it at first for resentment, in a young man naturally wounded. But it was not so. My mistress had noticed, from the first arrival of the police, that the bare mention of him was enough to set Miss Rachel’s temper on fire. He had been too fond of his cousin to confess such a thing to himself until the truth had been forced upon him—cruelly, when she drove off to her aunt’s. Then his eyes were opened; and being what he is, he took the one resolution a man of spirit could take—to go.

What he had to say to Sergeant Cuff was said before me.

He told the Sergeant that Lady Verinder was willing to acknowledge she had spoken over-hastily. And he asked whether, in that case, Sergeant Cuff would consent to accept his fee and to leave the matter of the Diamond where it stood.

“No, sir,” said the Sergeant. “My fee is paid me for doing my duty. I decline to take it, until my duty is done.”

“I don’t understand you,” Mr. Franklin said.

“I’ll explain myself, sir,” returned Sergeant Cuff. “When I came here, I undertook to throw the necessary light on the matter of the missing Diamond. I am now ready, and waiting, to redeem my pledge. When I have stated the case to Lady Verinder as the case now stands, and when I have told her plainly what course of action to take for the recovery of the Moonstone, the responsibility will be off my shoulders. Let her ladyship decide, after that, whether she does, or does not, allow me to go on. I shall then have done what I undertook to do—and I’ll take my fee.”

There was nothing more to be said. Even in the Detective Police, a man has a reputation to lose; and the Sergeant spoke like one who will not have his name cheapened by being dismissed before he has delivered what he came to deliver.

I rose to conduct him to my lady’s room. He paused at the door and asked Mr. Franklin, civilly enough, whether he wished to be present.

“Not unless Lady Verinder desires it,” Mr. Franklin answered.

Then he bent to me and whispered, “I know what that man is going to say about Rachel; and I am too fond of her to hear it, and keep my temper. Leave me by myself.”

So I left him—miserable enough to see—leaning on the sill of my window, his face hidden in his hands. Penelope was peeping through the door, longing to comfort him. In Mr. Franklin’s place I should have called her in. When one woman uses you ill, there is great ease in telling it to another—because, nine times out of ten, the other takes your side. Perhaps, when my back was turned, he did call her in. If so, it is only justice to my daughter to say she would stick at nothing, in the way of comforting Mr. Franklin Blake.

In the meantime, Sergeant Cuff and I went on to my lady’s room.

At our last conference, her ladyship had sat with her eyes on the book before her, as if the print might keep the world out. Now there was a change for the better. She met the Sergeant’s look with a steadiness as resolute as his own. The family spirit showed itself in every line of her face; and I knew, as I stood behind, listening to the house hold its breath, that Sergeant Cuff would find his match in a woman strung up to hear the worst he could say.

We had barely settled ourselves when my lady spoke first—no clatter, no preface, only that controlled gentleness which, in a great house, can still a room more surely than a handbell.

“Sergeant Cuff,” she said, “there may have been excuse for my manner to you half an hour ago. I do not plead it. If I wronged you, I regret it—sincerely.”

The man’s hard features loosened, as if warmth had reached him for a moment through his official coat. He asked leave to justify himself—not for his own pride, he said, but out of respect to her. He could not be answerable for the calamity that had struck us, because his success depended on keeping Rosanna Spearman from being frightened. Had he, or had he not, held to that object? He appealed to me.

I could—and did—declare that he had.

I thought then we might have let the matter end, like a pot taken off the fire at the right moment. But Sergeant Cuff, with that cold patience of his, moved the lid again—slowly, as if to make the steam scald.

“I have heard a motive named for the young woman’s suicide,” he said, “and it may be true. It is not connected with the case I am conducting. But my own opinion points the other way. I believe some unbearable anxiety, connected with the missing Diamond, drove her to her death. I don’t pretend to know what that anxiety was. But, with your ladyship’s permission, I can lay my hand on a person capable of deciding whether I am right or wrong.”

“Is the person in the house?” my mistress asked, after a pause that seemed to lengthen with every beat of the rain on the pane.

“The person has left the house, my lady.”

That answer ran straight as a skewer to its meat. Miss Rachel’s name was not spoken, but it was served up between us all the same. The silence that followed had a sound of its own—the wind worrying the chimneys, the rain striking the window as if it wanted admission, and underneath it the hush of a household whose common music—pots in the kitchen, steps on the stairs, a distant door—had been drowned by grief. There are noises that comfort and noises that corrode; and on that morning nothing in our soundscape belonged to peace.

“Be so good as to speak plainly,” said my lady at last. “Do you refer to my daughter?”

“I do,” said Sergeant Cuff.

Her cheque-book lay on the table—no doubt to end matters like a lady: fee paid, stranger dismissed, scandal shut out with the door. She put the book back into the drawer; and the movement was slight, but her hand trembled as if the drawer were heavy iron. It went through me. That hand had portioned kindness to half the estate as naturally as it portioned bread at table; and I—old Betteredge—could only sit and feel my loyalty burn like a brand.

“I had hoped,” she said, very quietly, “to recompense your services, and to part with you, without my daughter’s name being openly mentioned between us as it has been mentioned now. My nephew has probably said something of this before you came in?”

“Mr. Blake gave his message, my lady. And I gave him a reason—”

“It is needless. After what you have just said, you know as well as I do that you have gone too far to go back. I owe it to myself, and to my child, to insist on your remaining—and on your speaking out.”

He glanced at his watch, as if even this misery must keep time.

“If there had been time, my lady, I should have preferred putting my report in writing. But if the inquiry is to go on, time is too precious to waste. I am ready. It is painful for me to speak of, and for you to hear.”

She stopped him again—steady as a cook who will not let a rash hand throw spice into a dish before tasting.

“I may make it less painful to you—and to my good servant and friend here—if I set the example of speaking boldly. You suspect Miss Verinder of deceiving us all by secreting the Diamond for some purpose of her own. Is that true?”

“Quite true, my lady.”

“Very well. Before you begin, I tell you, as her mother, she is absolutely incapable of what you suppose. Your knowledge of her is a matter of days. Mine dates from the beginning of her life. State your suspicion as strongly as you please—you cannot offend me. I am sure, beforehand, that circumstances have misled you. Mind: I possess no private information. I am as shut out from my daughter’s confidence as you are. My reason is the one you have heard already. I know my child.”

She turned and gave me her hand. I kissed it, for I had no words that would not have sounded thin beside that simple certainty.

“You may go on,” she said, facing him.

His sharp face softened for a moment, not in conviction but in pity—like a knife laid down briefly on a cloth. Then it hardened again. She had not moved him a hair’s breadth.

“I must ask your ladyship,” he began, “to look at this from my point of view as well as from yours. Suppose yourself coming down here in my place, with my experience. Will you allow me to mention what that experience has been?”

My mistress signed that she would.

“For the last twenty years,” he said, “I have been employed largely in cases of family scandal—as a confidential man. The result of that domestic practice bearing on this matter is soon told. It is within my experience that young ladies of rank do occasionally have private debts, which they dare not acknowledge even to their nearest relations. Sometimes milliner and jeweller are at the bottom of it. Sometimes the money is wanted for purposes I don’t suspect here—and I won’t shock your ladyship by naming. Bear that in mind—and now let us see how events in this house forced me back on my experience, whether I liked it or not.”

He paused, as if arranging his facts the way a careful man arranges a serviceable meal: nothing wasted, nothing out of place, everything meant to satisfy.

“My first information came from Superintendent Seegrave. He proved to my satisfaction that he was incapable of managing the case. One thing he said was worth listening to: Miss Verinder declined to be questioned and spoke to him with incomprehensible rudeness and contempt. I thought it curious—but I put it down to clumsiness on the Superintendent’s part.

“Then I applied myself to the case. It ended—as you know—in the discovery of the smear on the door, and in Mr. Franklin Blake’s evidence satisfying me that the smear and the loss of the Diamond were pieces of the same puzzle. So far, if I suspected anything, I suspected the Moonstone had been stolen—and that one of the servants might prove to be the thief.

“In that state of things, Miss Verinder suddenly comes out and speaks to me. I observe three suspicious appearances. She is still violently agitated, though more than four-and-twenty hours have passed. She treats me as she treated Superintendent Seegrave. And she is mortally offended with Mr. Franklin Blake.

“Here is a young lady who has lost a valuable jewel—a young lady, as my own eyes and ears inform me, of an impetuous temperament. What does she do? She betrays resentment against Mr. Blake, the Superintendent, and myself—the very three people trying to help her recover it. Having brought my inquiry to that point, then—and not till then—I look back to my experience. It explains her conduct. It associates her with those other young ladies. It tells me she has debts she dares not acknowledge, that must be paid. And it sets me asking whether the loss of the Diamond may not mean—the Diamond is secretly pledged to pay them. That is my conclusion from plain facts. What does your ladyship’s experience say against it?”

“What I have said already,” answered my mistress. “The circumstances have misled you.”

I said nothing. My head, muddled with outrage and weather, fetched up Robinson Crusoe—God knows why. If Sergeant Cuff could have been marooned on his own desert island, with nobody to answer back and no boat to fetch him off, he would have been placed exactly where I wished him.

Sergeant Cuff went on.

“Right or wrong, having drawn my conclusion, I put it to the test. I suggested examining all the wardrobes. It was a means of finding the article of dress that had, in all probability, made the smear; and it was a means of testing my conclusion. How did it turn out? Your ladyship consented; Mr. Blake consented; Mr. Ablewhite consented. Miss Verinder alone stopped the proceeding by refusing point-blank. That satisfied me my view was right.

“And if your ladyship and Mr. Betteredge persist in not agreeing with me, you must be blind to what happened today. In your hearing I told the young lady that leaving the house would put an obstacle in the way of recovering her jewel. You saw her drive off in the face of that. You saw her—so far from forgiving Mr. Blake, who had done more than any of you to put the clue into my hands—publicly insult him on the steps of her mother’s house. What do these things mean? If Miss Verinder is not privy to the suppression of the Diamond, what do these things mean?”

He looked at me. It was frightful, hearing him stack proof on proof, and feeling the wretched truth of every particular—while my whole heart strained to defend Miss Rachel and found no handle for its grip. Yet I am, thank God, constitutionally superior to reason; and it was that blessed defect which kept me loyal to my lady’s faith when logic came clanking in with its chains.

Finding neither of us spoke, he proceeded—steady as the dripping of the eaves.

“There is the case, as it stands against Miss Verinder alone. Next I will put the case as it stands against Miss Verinder and the deceased Rosanna Spearman taken together.

“Go back to your daughter’s refusal to let her wardrobe be examined. My mind being made up, I had two questions. First: the right method of conducting my inquiry. Second: whether Miss Verinder had an accomplice among the female servants. After thinking it over, I determined to conduct the inquiry in what we call at our office a highly irregular manner—for this reason: I had a family scandal to deal with, which it was my business to keep within family limits. The less noise, and the fewer strangers employed, the better. As to taking people in custody, going before a magistrate, and so forth—nothing of that sort was to be thought of when your ladyship’s daughter was, as I believed, at the bottom of it.

“In this case, I felt that a person of Mr. Betteredge’s character and position—knowing the servants, and having the honour of the family at heart—would be safer as an assistant than any other person I could lay my hand on. I should have tried Mr. Blake too—but he saw the drift of my proceedings early; and with his interest in Miss Verinder, mutual understanding was impossible. I mention this to show your ladyship I have kept the family secret within the family circle. I am the only outsider who knows it—and my professional existence depends on holding my tongue.”

At that, my own professional existence—such as it is—depended on not holding mine. To be made, in my old age, into a sort of deputy-police before my mistress was more than my Christianity could swallow whole.

“I beg to inform your ladyship,” I burst out, “that I never, to my knowledge, helped this abominable detective business in any way from first to last; and I summon Sergeant Cuff to contradict me, if he dares!”

Having said it, I felt as if I had flung open a window in a close room. My lady rewarded me with a light pat on the shoulder. I fixed the Sergeant with righteous indignation. He looked back like a lamb, and seemed—worse luck—to like me better than ever.

My lady told him to proceed.

“I understand,” she said, “that you have honestly done your best in what you believe to be my interest. I am ready to hear what comes next.”

“What comes next,” he answered, “relates to Rosanna Spearman. I recognised the young woman—as your ladyship may remember—when she brought the washing-book into this room. Up to that time I doubted whether Miss Verinder had trusted her secret to anyone. When I saw Rosanna, I altered my mind. I suspected her at once of being privy to the suppression of the Diamond.

“The poor creature has met her death by a dreadful end, and I don’t want your ladyship to think, now she’s gone, that I was unduly hard on her. If this had been a common case of thieving, I should have given her the benefit of the doubt as freely as any servant. Our experience of Reformatory women is that, when tried in service and kindly treated, they prove in the majority of cases honestly penitent and honestly worthy of the pains taken with them. But this was not a common case. It was, in my mind, a deeply planned fraud—with the owner of the Diamond at the bottom of it. Holding that view, the first consideration with Rosanna was this: Would Miss Verinder be satisfied with leading us all to think the Moonstone was merely lost? Or would she go further and delude us into believing it was stolen? In the latter event there was Rosanna Spearman—with the character of a thief—ready to her hand; the person of all others to lead your ladyship and me on a false scent.”

I thought he had struck the lowest note of cruelty. He found a deeper.

“I had another reason for suspecting the deceased woman,” he said, “stronger still. Who would be the very person to help Miss Verinder raise money privately on the Diamond? Rosanna Spearman. No young lady in Miss Verinder’s position could manage so risky a matter alone. A go-between she must have. And who so fit as Rosanna? Your ladyship’s deceased housemaid was at the top of her profession when she was a thief. She had relations—certainly to my knowledge—with one of the few men in London who would advance a large sum on such a notable jewel without asking awkward questions or insisting on awkward conditions. Bear that in mind—and now let me show you how my suspicions have been justified by Rosanna’s own acts, and by the plain inferences from them.”

Then he went over Rosanna’s doings from first to last, as if he were reading them out of a book whose pages had been wetted and dried and still held their ink. I knew the story already—every step of it—and yet, in his mouth, the chain seemed tightened, link by link, around the poor dead girl’s memory. Even my mistress was daunted. When he stopped, she made him no answer.

It did not appear to matter to him whether he was answered or not.

“Having stated the whole case as I understand it,” he said, “I have only to tell your ladyship what I propose to do next. I see two ways of bringing the inquiry successfully to an end. One I look upon as a certainty. The other is a bold experiment, and nothing more. Your ladyship shall decide. Shall we take the certainty first?”

My mistress made him a sign to choose.

“Thank you,” he said. “We’ll begin with the certainty. Whether Miss Verinder remains at Frizinghall, or whether she returns here, I propose, in either case, to keep a careful watch on all her proceedings—on the people she sees, on the rides and walks she may take, and on the letters she may write and receive.”

“What next?” asked my lady, her voice tightened now, like twine pulled hard.

“I shall next request your ladyship’s leave to introduce into the house, as a servant in the place of Rosanna Spearman, a woman accustomed to private inquiries of this sort, for whose discretion I can answer.”

“What next?” she repeated.

“Next—and last—I propose to send one of my brother-officers to make an arrangement with that money-lender in London whom I mentioned just now as formerly acquainted with Rosanna Spearman—and whose name and address, your ladyship may rely on it, have been communicated by Rosanna to Miss Verinder. I don’t deny the course will cost money and consume time. But the result is certain. We run a line round the Moonstone, and we draw that line closer and closer till we find it in Miss Verinder’s possession—supposing she decides to keep it. If her debts press and she decides on sending it away, then we have our man ready, and we meet the Moonstone on its arrival in London.”

To hear Miss Rachel spoken of as prey to be ringed and driven stung my mistress into anger at last.

“Consider your proposal declined,” she said, “in every particular. Go on to your other way.”

“My other way,” said the Sergeant, as calmly as if he were changing trains, “is to try that bold experiment. I think I have formed a pretty correct estimate of Miss Verinder’s temperament. She is quite capable—according to my belief—of committing a daring fraud. But she is too hot and impetuous, and too little accustomed to deceit as a habit, to play the hypocrite in small things and restrain herself under all provocations. Her feelings have repeatedly got beyond her control at the very time when it was plainly her interest to conceal them. It is on this peculiarity that I propose to act.

“I want to give her a great shock suddenly, under circumstances that will touch her to the quick. In plain English, I want to tell Miss Verinder, without warning, of Rosanna’s death—on the chance that her better feelings will hurry her into making a clean breast of it. Does your ladyship accept that alternative?”

My mistress astonished me. She did not waver a moment.

“Yes,” she said. “I do.”

“The pony-chaise is ready,” said the Sergeant. “I wish your ladyship good morning.”

She stopped him at the door with a lifted hand.

“My daughter’s better feelings shall be appealed to,” she said. “But I claim the right, as her mother, of putting her to the test myself. You will remain here; and I will go to Frizinghall.”

For once, the great Cuff was struck dumb—like any common man.

My lady rang for her waterproof things. The rain was still falling as if it meant to wear the very earth away; and the close carriage had gone already with Miss Rachel. I tried to dissuade her from facing such weather. Useless. I begged to go with her and hold the umbrella. She refused—utterly.

The pony-chaise came round, the groom in charge. The yard outside had that soaked, sharp smell of wet leather, steaming horse, and beaten gravel; the wind made a harsh, fretful music in the bare branches—no birds, no kindly rural hush, only weather and worry and the far-off industrial murmur that seems, these days, to creep even into country places and sour their air.

In the hall she spoke to Sergeant Cuff.

“You may rely on two things. I will try the experiment on Miss Verinder as boldly as you could try it yourself. And I will inform you of the result, either personally or by letter, before the last train leaves for London tonight.”

Then she stepped into the chaise, took the reins in her own hands, and drove off through the rain to Frizinghall.

When my mistress had gone, the house fell into that uneasy hush which comes after a storm—when the drip from the eaves keeps time in the silence, and the servants go softly as if sound itself might offend. I had leisure then to look for Sergeant Cuff.

I found him tucked into a snug corner of the hall, his long body folded close, his memorandum-book open on his knee, and the corners of his lips drawn up in that sharp, offended curl of his—as if the case had put a bad taste into his mouth and he meant to spit it out rather than swallow.

“Making notes of the case?” I asked.

“No,” says he. “Looking to see what my next professional engagement is.”

“Oh!” says I. “Then you think it’s all over here?”

“I think,” he answered, shutting the book with a snap that sounded like a lid on a coffin, “that Lady Verinder is one of the cleverest women in England. I also think a rose much better worth looking at than a diamond. Where is the gardener, Mr. Betteredge?”

There was no getting a word more out of him on the Moonstone. The man who had sniffed out suspicion like a hound at dawn had suddenly turned gardener himself—nose to the flower-bed, eyes narrowed, all his fine detective appetite transferred from jewel to blossom. He would talk of nothing but roses; he would hunt for nothing but Mr. Begbie.

An hour later, I heard them in the conservatory, their voices rising like quarrelling rooks among the glass and greenery. The wet scent of mould and leaf came wafting through the doorway; and under it, sharp as vinegar, the dispute over the dog-rose flared again. To any one else it might have sounded like trifling. To me—listening to that domestic uproar, and the steady patter outside where the rain was easing—it felt like the house trying to go on with its ordinary life, cooking its dinners and pruning its plants, while a theft and a death sat at table with us all the same.

In the meantime, it was my business to find out whether Mr. Franklin still meant to leave by the afternoon train. The conference in my lady’s room had altered his plan at once; he decided to wait for news from Frizinghall. Quite natural. With most people, it would have ended there.

With Mr. Franklin, it left him stranded with a long stretch of idle time—idle time being to him what a warm kitchen is to flies. Out came, one after another, all the foreign sides of him, slipping loose like rats from a bag. Italian-Englishman for a quarter of an hour; then German-Englishman; then French-Englishman—each with his own way of making a meal of misery, turning Miss Rachel’s treatment of him over and over as if it were a bitter olive he couldn’t stop tasting. And as luck would have it, there was nobody convenient for him to serve it to but me.

I found him, for example, in the library, planted under the map of Modern Italy, as if he meant to take refuge in continents.

“I have several worthy aspirations, Betteredge,” he says, “but what am I to do with them now? I am full of dormant good qualities, if Rachel would only have helped me to bring them out!”

He was so fluent in praising his own neglected merits, and so moving in lamenting over them when he had done, that I was driven to my last resource—the one medicine I have always found wholesome for minds that run away with themselves.

I hobbled off to my room and fetched my faithful _Robinson Crusoe_. There’s moral nutriment in that book, like good broth long simmered—plain, strengthening, and fit to put heart into a man.

I came back. Nobody in the library. The map of Modern Italy stared at me, and I stared at it—feeling as if I’d brought a hot pie to an empty table.

I tried the drawing-room. There lay his handkerchief on the floor—proof he had drifted in. The emptiness of the room—proof he had drifted out.

I tried the dining-room and came on Samuel, with a biscuit and a glass of sherry, standing as still as if he’d been set there for ornament, silently investigating the empty air. A minute before, Mr. Franklin had rung like fury for “a little light refreshment.” Samuel, nimble as a cook’s boy sent for herbs, had brought it up; and before the bell downstairs had done quivering with the violence of the pull, Mr. Franklin had vanished—leaving the sherry to breathe itself flat.

At last I found him in the morning-room, at the window, drawing hieroglyphics with his finger in the damp on the glass. The rain had filmed the pane; his restless hand made pale tracks through the moisture, as if he could write his troubles where nobody need answer.

“Your sherry is waiting for you, sir,” I said.

I might as well have addressed the wallpaper. He was sunk deep in his own thoughts, where no rope of mine could reach him.

“How do _you_ explain Rachel’s conduct, Betteredge?” says he, without turning.

Not being ready with the right reply, I produced _Robinson Crusoe_—firmly persuaded that if we searched long enough, somewhere among the goats and the gunpowder there would be a sensible explanation for a young lady’s perversity.

Mr. Franklin snapped the book shut as if it had offended his intellect, and—on the instant—tumbled into his German-English, that learned gabble of his which always made me feel as if my wits were being minced.

“Why not look into it?” he cries, as if I had personally objected to looking into it. “Why the devil lose your patience, Betteredge, when patience is all that’s wanted to arrive at the truth? Don’t interrupt me. Rachel’s conduct is perfectly intelligible, if you will only do her the common justice to take the Objective view first, and the Subjective view next, and the Objective-Subjective view to wind up with. What do we know? We know that the loss of the Moonstone, on Thursday morning last, threw her into a state of nervous excitement, from which she has not recovered yet. Do you mean to deny the Objective view, so far? Very well, then—don’t interrupt me. Now, being in a state of nervous excitement, how are we to expect that she should behave as she might otherwise have behaved to any of the people about her? Arguing in this way, from within-outwards, what do we reach? We reach the Subjective view. I defy you to controvert the Subjective view. Very well then—what follows? Good Heavens! the Objective-Subjective explanation follows, of course! Rachel, properly speaking, is _not_ Rachel, but Somebody Else. Do I mind being cruelly treated by Somebody Else? You are unreasonable enough, Betteredge; but you can hardly accuse me of that. Then how does it end? It ends, in spite of your confounded English narrowness and prejudice, in my being perfectly happy and comfortable. Where’s the sherry?”

My head was in such a state by then that I couldn’t have sworn whose head it was—mine or his. In that condition I managed, I believe, three Objective actions: I got him his sherry; I retreated to my own room; and I soothed myself with the most composing pipe of tobacco I ever smoked, drawing it in slow as a man draws in quiet, and listening to the house-sounds settle—the distant clink of glass, the faint creak of a door, the lowered voices in the passage. A house has its own music when it’s trying to recover itself; and when modern life doesn’t drown it with needless din, that music can heal you better than any lecture.

But I was not quit of Mr. Franklin so easily.

Drifting again out of the morning-room into the hall, he found his way down to the offices, smelt my tobacco, and was instantly reminded—like a man catching the scent of a dish he’s sworn off—that he had been simple enough to give up smoking for Miss Rachel’s sake. The next moment he burst in on me with his cigar-case and came out, in his neat, witty, unbelieving French way, upon the everlasting subject.

“Give me a light, Betteredge. Is it conceivable that a man can have smoked as long as I have without discovering that there is a complete system for the treatment of women at the bottom of his cigar-case? Follow me carefully, and I will prove it in two words. You choose a cigar, you try it, and it disappoints you. What do you do upon that? You throw it away and try another. Now observe the application! You choose a woman, you try her, and she breaks your heart. Fool! take a lesson from your cigar-case. Throw her away, and try another!”

I shook my head. Wonderfully clever, no doubt—like a sauce too sharp by half, meant to show off the cook rather than feed the eater. My own experience was dead against it.

“In the time of the late Mrs. Betteredge,” I said, “I felt pretty often inclined to try your philosophy, Mr. Franklin. But the law insists on your smoking your cigar, sir, when you have once chosen it.”

I pointed that with a wink. He laughed out, and for a moment we were as merry as crickets—till the next new side of him rose, as sure as another course following the first.

So it went on between my young master and me; and so, while Sergeant Cuff and the gardener wrangled over roses under glass, we spent the interval before the news came back from Frizinghall.

The pony-chaise returned a good half-hour before I expected it. The court-yard—washed clean by the rain—rang with the quick clop of hooves and the rattle of wheels; and in that sound, so homely and exact, I felt time itself coming back into step. My lady had decided to remain, for the present, at her sister’s house. The groom brought two letters from his mistress: one for Mr. Franklin, and one for me.

I sent Mr. Franklin’s to him in the library—where his driftings had now washed him up again. I took my own letter to my room.

A cheque dropped out when I opened it, and told me—before I had read a word—that Sergeant Cuff’s dismissal from the inquiry was settled. The sight of that paper, neat and cold and final, made my stomach turn as if I’d bitten on grit in good bread. I sent to the conservatory to say I wished to speak to the Sergeant directly.

He appeared with his mind still full of Begbie and the dog-rose, declaring that the equal of Mr. Begbie for obstinacy never had existed yet, and never would exist again.

I asked him to dismiss such wretched trifling and give his attention to a serious matter. He exerted himself enough, then, to notice the letter in my hand.

“Ah!” says he, in a weary way. “You have heard from her ladyship. Have I anything to do with it, Mr. Betteredge?”

“You shall judge for yourself, Sergeant.” And I read him the letter—with what emphasis and discretion I could command.

“My good Gabriel,—I request that you will inform Sergeant Cuff, that I have performed the promise I made to him; with this result, so far as Rosanna Spearman is concerned. Miss Verinder solemnly declares, that she has never spoken a word in private to Rosanna, since that unhappy woman first entered my house. They never met, even accidentally, on the night when the Diamond was lost; and no communication of any sort whatever took place between them, from the Thursday morning when the alarm was first raised in the house, to this present Saturday afternoon, when Miss Verinder left us. After telling my daughter suddenly, and in so many words, of Rosanna Spearman’s suicide—this is what has come of it.”

I looked up. “What do you think of that, Sergeant?”

“I should only offend you if I expressed _my_ opinion,” he answered. “Go on, Mr. Betteredge,” he says, with resignation enough to provoke a saint.

My tongue itched to “go on” in words of my own choosing. But my Christianity held firm, and I proceeded:

“Having appealed to Miss Verinder in the manner which the officer thought most desirable, I spoke to her next in the manner which I myself thought most likely to impress her. On two different occasions, before my daughter left my roof, I privately warned her that she was exposing herself to suspicion of the most unendurable and most degrading kind. I have now told her, in the plainest terms, that my apprehensions have been realised.

“Her answer to this, on her own solemn affirmation, is as plain as words can be. In the first place, she owes no money privately to any living creature. In the second place, the Diamond is not now, and never has been, in her possession, since she put it into her cabinet on Wednesday night.

“The confidence which my daughter has placed in me goes no further than this. She maintains an obstinate silence, when I ask her if she can explain the disappearance of the Diamond. She refuses, with tears, when I appeal to her to speak out for my sake. ‘The day will come when you will know why I am careless about being suspected, and why I am silent even to _you_. I have done much to make my mother pity me—nothing to make my mother blush for me.’ Those are my daughter’s own words.

“After what has passed between the officer and me, I think—stranger as he is—that he should be made acquainted with what Miss Verinder has said, as well as you. Read my letter to him, and then place in his hands the cheque which I enclose. In resigning all further claim on his services, I have only to say that I am convinced of his honesty and his intelligence; but I am more firmly persuaded than ever, that the circumstances, in this case, have fatally misled him.”

There the letter ended.

Before I handed over the cheque, I asked if he had any remark to make.

“It’s no part of my duty, Mr. Betteredge,” he says, “to make remarks on a case, when I have done with it.”

I tossed the cheque across the table to him. “Do you believe in _that_ part of her ladyship’s letter?” I asked, hotly.

He looked at the cheque and lifted his dismal eyebrows—acknowledging my lady’s liberality as if it were an unavoidable weather.

“This is such a generous estimate of the value of my time,” he said, “that I feel bound to make some return for it. I’ll bear in mind the amount in this cheque, Mr. Betteredge, when the occasion comes round for remembering it.”

“What do you mean?” I asked.

“Her ladyship has smoothed matters over for the present very cleverly,” says he. “But _this_ family scandal is of the sort that bursts up again when you least expect it. We shall have more detective-business on our hands, sir, before the Moonstone is many months older.”

If his words meant anything, and his tone meant anything, it came to this: that my mistress’s letter had convinced him Miss Rachel was hardened enough to resist the strongest appeal—and that she had deceived her own mother by lies.

I answered plain. “Sergeant Cuff, I consider that observation an insult to my lady and her daughter!”

“Mr. Betteredge, consider it as a warning to yourself, and you will be nearer the mark.”

Hot as I was, the infernal confidence of the man shut my mouth. I walked to the window to compose myself.

The rain had ceased. The air outside had that rinsed smell—stone cooled, earth newly opened. And who should I see waiting in the court-yard but Mr. Begbie, fixed there like a stake in the ground, ready to go on with the dog-rose war the instant the Sergeant showed his face.

“My compliments to the Sairgent,” says Begbie the moment he saw me. “If he’s minded to walk to the station, I’m agreeable to go with him.”

“What!” cries the Sergeant behind me, “are you not convinced yet?”

“The de’il a bit I’m convinced!” says Begbie.

“Then I’ll walk to the station!” says the Sergeant.

“Then I’ll meet you at the gate!” says Begbie.

I had been angry enough to bite iron; but no man’s anger can hold out long against such an interruption. The absurdity of it—this solemn officer and this stiff-necked gardener, hauling roses into court as if petals were evidence—worked on me like a sudden change of wind, cooling what had been too hot.

Sergeant Cuff saw the change and gave me a word in season.

“Come! come!” says he. “Why not treat my view of the case as her ladyship treats it? Why not say, the circumstances have fatally misled me?”

To take anything as her ladyship took it was a privilege worth having, even when it was offered by Sergeant Cuff. I cooled down to my customary level. I held any opinion of Miss Rachel, other than my lady’s opinion or mine, in lofty contempt.

The only thing I could not do was keep off the subject of the Moonstone.

My sense should have told me to let it rest. But sense, like good bread, takes time to learn; and the virtues the present generation boasts of weren’t baked in my oven.

He had hit me on the raw. The tender place still tingled. So I perversely led him back.

“I am quite satisfied myself,” I said. “But never mind that. Go on, as if I was still open to conviction. You think Miss Rachel is not to be believed on her word; and you say we shall hear of the Moonstone again. Back your opinion, Sergeant. Back your opinion.”

Instead of taking offence, he seized my hand and shook it till my fingers ached.

“I declare to heaven,” says he solemnly, “I would take to domestic service tomorrow, Mr. Betteredge, if I had a chance of being employed along with you! To say you are as transparent as a child, sir, is to pay the children a compliment which nine out of ten don’t deserve. There! there! we won’t begin to dispute again. You shall have it out of me on easier terms than that. I won’t say a word more about her ladyship, or about Miss Verinder—I’ll only turn prophet, for once in a way, and for your sake. I have warned you already that you haven’t done with the Moonstone yet. Very well. Now I’ll tell you, at parting, of three things which will happen in the future, and which, I believe, will force themselves on your attention, whether you like it or not.”

“Go on!” says I—airy as ever, though my stomach had begun, in spite of me, to tighten at the sound of the word “prophet.” Prophecies have a way of lodging in a house like a draught: you can’t see them, but you feel them in your bones.

“First,” says the Sergeant, “you will hear something from the Yollands—when the postman delivers Rosanna’s letter at Cobb’s Hole, on Monday next.”

If he had flung a bucket of cold water over me I don’t think it would have struck me more unpleasantly. In that instant, like a taste remembered too late, I felt what I had not faced: Miss Rachel’s solemn denial left Rosanna’s strange doings—the new nightgown, the hidden stained nightgown, all of it—hanging in the air without sense or savour. The question had been in my mouth for days, and I had never bitten down on it, not once, till Cuff forced it between my teeth.

“In the second place,” he went on, “you will hear of the three Indians again. You will hear of them in the neighbourhood, if Miss Rachel remains in the neighbourhood. You will hear of them in London, if Miss Rachel goes to London.”

I had lost all interest in the jugglers, and I had persuaded myself of my young lady’s innocence. This second prophecy I swallowed easily enough.

“So much for two of the three things,” says I. “Now for the third!”

“Third, and last,” says Sergeant Cuff, “you will, sooner or later, hear something of that money-lender in London, whom I have twice taken the liberty of mentioning already. Give me your pocket-book, and I’ll make a note for you of his name and address—so that there may be no mistake about it if the thing really happens.”

He wrote on a blank leaf, careful as any clerk weighing out measures:

“Mr. Septimus Luker, Middlesex-place, Lambeth, London.”

“There,” says he, pointing to it, “are the last words, on the subject of the Moonstone, which I shall trouble you with for the present. Time will show whether I am right or wrong. In the meanwhile, sir, I carry away with me a sincere personal liking for you, which I think does honour to both of us. If we don’t meet again before my professional retirement takes place, I hope you will come and see me in a little house near London, which I have got my eye on. There will be grass walks, Mr. Betteredge, I promise you, in _my_ garden. And as for the white moss rose—”

“The de’il a bit ye’ll get the white moss rose to grow, unless you bud him on the dogue-rose first!” cried a voice at the window.

We both turned. There was Mr. Begbie—eternal as a church clock—too eager for controversy to wait at the gate.

The Sergeant wrung my hand once more and darted out into the court-yard, hotter still.

“Ask him about the moss rose, when he comes back, and see if I have left him a leg to stand on!” he cried, hailing me through the window.

“Gentlemen, both!” I called after them, trying—once again—to moderate their noise as I had moderated it before. “In the matter of the moss rose there is a great deal to be said on both sides!”

I might as well have whistled to a milestone. Away they went together, battling over roses without asking or giving quarter. The last I saw, Mr. Begbie was shaking his obstinate head, and Sergeant Cuff had him by the arm like a prisoner in charge.

Ah, well—well. I own I couldn’t help liking the Sergeant, though I hated him all the time. Explain that, if you can. There’s more in a man’s nature than the words he has for it; and sometimes the truest things in us won’t fit the dominant language we’re forced to speak.

You will soon be rid of me and my contradictions. When I have reported Mr. Franklin’s departure, the history of the Saturday’s events will be finished at last. And when I have next described certain strange things that happened in the course of the new week, I shall have done my part of the story, and shall hand over the pen to the person appointed to follow my lead.

If you are as tired of reading this narrative as I am of writing it—Lord, how we shall enjoy ourselves on both sides a few pages further on!

I had kept the pony-chaise standing ready from the moment the notion of a night train had been spoken of—as you keep a kettle near the hob when storms are coming on and you distrust the temper of the fire. A household is much the same; you learn to lay your hand on the right thing before the need declares itself. So when the luggage showed first—those dumb, square-mouthed boxes that always look as if they are about to swallow up comfort—and Mr. Franklin followed it down the stairs, I knew he had at last done what he so seldom did: held fast to a resolution.

“So you have really made up your mind, sir?” I said, meeting him in the hall. “Why not wait a day or two longer, and give Miss Rachel another chance?”

The foreign polish was off him now. He did not answer in words. He put a letter into my hand—my lady’s letter—folded with that hard, careful neatness people use when they are trying to keep their feelings from running over.

Most of it was the same as what I had already had from her: the Diamond lost; the house turned inside out by a mystery that would not show its face; the police dismissed because their very name was poison to Miss Rachel. But at the end there was a piece about my young lady—one of those late sentences, slipped in like the last pinch of salt that changes the taste of the whole dish.

“You will wonder,” my lady wrote, “at my allowing my own daughter to keep me perfectly in the dark. A Diamond worth twenty thousand pounds has been lost—and I am left to infer that the mystery of its disappearance is no mystery to Rachel, and that some incomprehensible obligation of silence has been laid on her, by some person or persons utterly unknown to me, with some object in view at which I cannot even guess. Is it conceivable that I should allow myself to be trifled with in this way? It is quite conceivable, in Rachel’s present state. She is in a condition of nervous agitation pitiable to see. I dare not approach the subject of the Moonstone again until time has done something to quiet her. To help this end, I have not hesitated to dismiss the police-officer. The mystery which baffles us, baffles him too. This is not a matter in which any stranger can help us. He adds to what I have to suffer; and he maddens Rachel if she only hears his name.

“My plans for the future are as well settled as they can be. My present idea is to take Rachel to London—partly to relieve her mind by a complete change, partly to try what may be done by consulting the best medical advice. Can I ask you to meet us in town? My dear Franklin, you, in your way, must imitate my patience, and wait, as I do, for a fitter time. The valuable assistance which you rendered to the inquiry after the lost jewel is still an unpardoned offence, in the present dreadful state of Rachel’s mind. Moving blindfold in this matter, you have added to the burden of anxiety which she has had to bear, by innocently threatening her secret with discovery, through your exertions. It is impossible for me to excuse the perversity that holds you responsible for consequences which neither you nor I could imagine or foresee. She is not to be reasoned with—she can only be pitied. I am grieved to have to say it, but for the present, you and Rachel are better apart. The only advice I can offer you is, to give her time.”

I gave the letter back, and I was honestly sorry for him. There are pains that come with a noise—doors slammed, voices raised; and there are pains that come as quiet as a knife going in. This was one of the quiet ones.

“You know the proverb, sir,” I said, because it is a poor old servant’s trade to hunt up proverbs the way a cook hunts in her drawer for a warming-pan. “When things are at the worst, they’re sure to mend. Things can’t be much worse, Mr. Franklin, than they are now.”

He folded my lady’s letter as if it were a thing too hot to hold.

“When I came here from London with that horrible Diamond,” he said, “I don’t believe there was a happier household in England than this. Look at the household now! Scattered, disunited—the very air of the place poisoned with mystery and suspicion! Do you remember that morning at the Shivering Sand, when we talked about my uncle Herncastle, and his birthday gift? The Moonstone has served the Colonel’s vengeance, Betteredge, by means which the Colonel himself never dreamt of!”

Then he shook my hand and went out to the chaise.

I followed him down the steps. The evening lay long and bright on the old place, and yet it was as if some sound had been taken out of the air—the steady domestic hum that makes a house feel lived in. Penelope, poor girl, came crying to say good-bye. He kissed her. The other maids peeped and drew back again, as women do; for he was one of those men whom they all like, and that liking is a kind of weather in a servants’ hall—you can feel it shift without being told.

At the last moment I laid my hand on the chaise and stopped it.

“Just let us hear from you by letter, sir,” I said. “Tell us where you’re going to.”

He did not heed me at first. He was looking at the windows, the sweep of the gravel, the line of trees—the small familiar things a man takes in his eyes when his heart is not fit to speak. Then, all at once, he pulled his hat down over his face.

“Going?” he said, echoing me. “I am going to the devil!”

The pony started at the word, as if it had a Christian horror of it. I had only time to call after him, “God bless you, sir, go where you may!” before he was out of sight.

A sweet and pleasant gentleman—sweet, in spite of faults and follies. And he left a gap behind him, as a good fire leaves a chill when it goes out.

That Saturday night closed in dull and heavy. Summer can do that: hang a warm scent of mown grass and dust on the air, and yet make the heart feel cold. I kept myself from sinking by sticking to my pipe and my Robinson Crusoe. The women, except Penelope, kept the hours moving by talking of Rosanna’s suicide—turning it over and over as if it were a tough bit of meat that would not come right in the teeth.

They were obstinate: Rosanna had stolen the Moonstone; Rosanna had destroyed herself for terror of being found out. Penelope privately held to what she had said all along. But, oddly enough, her notion failed at the same point where my young lady’s assertion of Rosanna’s innocence failed: it left Rosanna’s secret journey to Frizinghall, and the business of the nightgown, like two ingredients in a recipe with no dish to belong to.

There was no use pressing that on Penelope. An objection slid off her mind like rain off a waterproof coat. My daughter, in that respect, inherits my superiority to reason—and has got ahead of her father.

Sunday brought back the close carriage from Mr. Ablewhite’s empty, with a message to me and written instructions for my lady’s maid and for Penelope.

My mistress had determined to take Miss Rachel to her house in London on Monday. The instructions set down what clothes were wanted, and directed the two maids to meet their mistresses in town at a given hour. Most of the servants were to follow. My lady had found Miss Rachel so unwilling to return to our house after what had happened in it that she would go to London direct from Frizinghall. I was to remain in the country, until further orders, to look after the house and grounds. The servants left with me were to be put upon board wages.

Reading that, I heard again Mr. Franklin’s words—“scattered, disunited”—as plainly as if they had been spoken in my ear. And thinking of him led me to uneasiness. His temper was not of the sort that sits down and cools like a stew left off the fire; it runs away like milk if you are not watching. By Sunday’s post I wrote to Mr. Jeffco, his father’s valet—an old acquaintance of mine—to beg he would let me know what Mr. Franklin had settled to do on arriving in London.

Sunday evening was duller, if possible, than Saturday. We ended our day of rest as hundreds of thousands end it: we anticipated bedtime and fell asleep in our chairs—letting the house noises die down one by one until even the clock seemed tired of striking.

What Monday did to the rest of the household I can’t say. It gave me a shake-up. The first of Sergeant Cuff’s prophecies—that I should hear from the Yollands—came true that day.

I had seen Penelope and my lady’s maid off by railway with the luggage for London, and was pottering about the grounds when I heard my name called. I turned, and there stood the fisherman’s daughter, Limping Lucy.

Take away the lame foot and the leanness—which, in my opinion, is a cruel drawback in a woman—and she had her merits: a dark, keen, clever face; a clear voice that cut through the outdoor hush like a gull’s cry; and a beautiful brown head of hair. A crutch was one of her misfortunes. A temper was her chief defect.

“Well, my dear,” I said, “what do you want with me?”

“Where’s the man you call Franklin Blake?” she asked, fixing me with a look that was like being pinned.

“That’s not a respectful way to speak of any gentleman,” I answered. “If you want my lady’s nephew, you will mention him as Mr. Franklin Blake.”

She limped nearer and looked as if she could have eaten me alive.

“Mr. Franklin Blake?” she repeated. “Murderer Franklin Blake would be a fitter name for him.”

My practice with the late Mrs. Betteredge served me. When a woman tries to put you out of temper, you put her out of temper instead. One word does it—and I said it pleasantly.

“Pooh!”

Her temper flamed up like dry thatch. She poised on her sound foot, lifted the crutch, and beat the ground three times so that the blows went up through the soles into my bones.

“He’s a murderer! he’s a murderer! he’s a murderer! He has been the death of Rosanna Spearman!”

She screamed it. One or two of the men working near us looked up, saw it was Limping Lucy, and looked away again, as people do when they know a squall is passing and there’s no use throwing yourself into it.

“He has been the death of Rosanna Spearman?” I said. “What makes you say that, Lucy?”

“What do you care? What does any man care? Oh, if she had only thought of the men as I think, she might have been living now!”

“She always thought kindly of me, poor soul,” I said, “and, to the best of my ability, I always tried to act kindly by her.”

I spoke gentle, because I saw the wretchedness under the insolence. Misery often talks impudent in humble life; it is the only way it can keep itself from breaking down.

My tone melted her. She bent her head and rested it on the top of her crutch, like a tired child.

“I loved her,” she said softly. “She had lived a miserable life, Mr. Betteredge—vile people had ill-treated her and led her wrong—and it hadn’t spoiled her sweet temper. She was an angel. She might have been happy with me. I had a plan for our going to London together like sisters, and living by our needles. That man came here, and spoilt it all. He bewitched her. Don’t tell me he didn’t mean it, and didn’t know it. He ought to have known it. He ought to have taken pity on her. ‘I can’t live without him—and, oh, Lucy, he never even looks at me.’ That’s what she said. Cruel, cruel, cruel.

“I said, ‘No man is worth fretting for in that way.’ And she said, ‘There are men worth dying for, Lucy, and he is one of them.’ I had saved up a little money. I had settled things with father and mother. I meant to take her away from the mortification she was suffering here. We should have had a little lodging in London, and lived together like sisters. She had a good education, sir, as you know, and she wrote a good hand. She was quick at her needle. I have a good education, and I write a good hand. I am not as quick at my needle as she was—but I could have done. We might have got our living nicely.

“And, oh, what happens this morning? what happens this morning? Her letter comes and tells me she has done with the burden of her life. Her letter comes, and bids me good-bye for ever.”

Then she lifted her head, and through tears the old flame sprang up again.

“Where is he? Where’s this gentleman I mustn’t speak of except with respect? Ha, Mr. Betteredge, the day is not far off when the poor will rise against the rich. I pray Heaven they may begin with him. I pray Heaven they may begin with him.”

Here was your average good Christian, and here was what comes of pushing average Christianity past endurance. The parson himself could hardly have done anything with her at that pitch. All I attempted was to keep her to the point, because the one thing worth anything in this world of ours is a fact.

“What do you want with Mr. Franklin Blake?” I asked.

“I want to see him.”

“For anything particular?”

“I have got a letter to give him.”

“From Rosanna Spearman?”

“Yes.”

“Sent to you in your own letter?”

“Yes.”

At that, I felt the air change about me. Sergeant Cuff had left his infection behind him. The detective-fever began to set in—like a sudden appetite, or a thirst, that makes you restless until it is satisfied.

“You can’t see Mr. Franklin,” I said.

“I must, and will, see him.”

“He went to London last night.”

She searched my face, and found the truth there. Without another word she turned instantly away toward Cobb’s Hole.

“Stop!” I said. “I expect news of Mr. Franklin Blake tomorrow. Give me your letter, and I’ll send it on to him by the post.”

She steadied herself on the crutch and looked back over her shoulder.

“I am to give it from my hands into his hands,” she said. “And I am to give it to him in no other way.”

“Shall I write and tell him what you have said?”

“Tell him I hate him. And you will tell him the truth.”

“Yes, yes. But about the letter—”

“If he wants the letter, he must come back here, and get it from me.”

With that she limped off.

All my dignity burnt away on the spot, and I followed her, trying to make her talk. It was all in vain. It is my misfortune to be a man, and Limping Lucy enjoyed disappointing me. Later I tried her mother. Good Mrs. Yolland could only cry and recommend a drop of comfort out of the Dutch bottle—like a woman who has nothing left to offer but a smell of gin and the sound of weeping. I found the fisherman on the beach; he said it was “a bad job,” and went on mending his net, the sea making its steady, unanswering noise behind him. Neither father nor mother knew more than I did.
