So there was nothing for it but to wait for morning and the chance of writing to Mr. Franklin.

You may imagine how I watched for the postman on Tuesday. He brought two letters.

One, from Penelope—which I had scarcely patience to read—announced that my lady and Miss Rachel were safely established in London.

The other, from Mr. Jeffco, informed me that his master’s son had left England already.

On reaching London, Mr. Franklin had gone straight to his father’s house. He arrived at an awkward time. Mr. Blake the elder was up to his eyes in House of Commons business, and amusing himself at home with that parliamentary plaything they call a private bill. Mr. Jeffco showed Mr. Franklin into his father’s study.

“My dear Franklin! why do you surprise me in this way? Anything wrong?”

“Yes; something wrong with Rachel; I am dreadfully distressed about it.”

“Grieved to hear it. But I can’t listen to you now.”

“When can you listen?”

“My dear boy! I won’t deceive you. I can listen at the end of the session, not a moment before. Good-night.”

“Thank you, sir. Good-night.”

That was the conversation inside. The conversation outside was shorter.

“Jeffco, see what time the tidal train starts tomorrow morning.”

“At six-forty, Mr. Franklin.”

“Have me called at five.”

“Going abroad, sir?”

“Going, Jeffco, wherever the railway chooses to take me.”

“Shall I tell your father, sir?”

“Yes; tell him at the end of the session.”

Next morning he started for foreign parts. Where, nobody could guess. Europe, Asia, Africa, America—Mr. Jeffco thought the chances as equally divided as might be, and I had no better reckoning.

That news shut the door on my one hope of bringing Limping Lucy and Mr. Franklin together. Penelope’s belief—that Rosanna had destroyed herself through love for Mr. Franklin Blake—stood confirmed, and that was all. Whether Rosanna’s letter contained a confession, as Mr. Franklin had suspected she wanted to make while living, nobody could say. It might be only a farewell and nothing more; or it might tell the whole truth about those strange proceedings Sergeant Cuff had detected, from the time the Moonstone was missed to the time she ran to her death at the Shivering Sand.

A sealed letter had been placed in Limping Lucy’s hand; a sealed letter it remained. We suspected she had been in Rosanna’s confidence; we tried to make her speak; we failed. Now one servant, now another—still clinging to the notion that Rosanna had stolen the Diamond and hidden it—went peering and poking about the rocks where she had been traced, and peering and poking in vain. The tide went out with its long sucking hush; the tide came in with its heavier, grinding return. Summer ran on; autumn came. The Quicksand that hid her body hid her secret too.

The news of Mr. Franklin’s departure on Sunday morning, and my lady’s arrival in London with Miss Rachel on Monday afternoon, had reached me by Tuesday’s post. Wednesday brought nothing. Thursday produced another budget from Penelope.

A great London doctor had been consulted and had earned his guinea by saying Miss Rachel had better be amused. Flower-shows, operas, balls—there was a whole round of gaieties before her; and, to her mother’s astonishment, Miss Rachel eagerly took to it all. Mr. Godfrey had called—sweet as ever on his cousin in spite of the birthday reception—and, to Penelope’s great regret, had been most graciously received, adding Miss Rachel’s name to one of his Ladies’ Charities on the spot. My mistress was out of spirits, and had held two long interviews with her lawyer. Penelope’s speculations followed, touching a poor relation of the family—Miss Clack, she who had sat next to Mr. Godfrey at the birthday dinner and had a pretty taste in champagne. Penelope was astonished Miss Clack had not called yet; she would surely not be long before she fastened herself on my lady, and so forth—women’s pin-pricks travelling as well by ink as by tongue. This would not be worth noting, except that you may be turned over to Miss Clack after parting with me; and if so, I beg you not to believe a word she says if she speaks of your humble servant.

Friday, nothing happened—except that one of the dogs showed signs of a breaking-out behind the ears. I gave him syrup of buckthorn and put him on pot-liquor and vegetables. Pass it over, if you please. The creature deserved proper physicking.

Saturday—the last day of the week—was the last day of my narrative.

The morning post brought a surprise: a London newspaper. The handwriting on the direction puzzled me until I compared it with the money-lender’s name and address in my pocket-book. It was Sergeant Cuff’s hand.

I looked through the paper and found an ink-mark drawn round one of the police reports. Here it is. Read it as I read it, and set what value you please on the Sergeant’s attention in sending me the news of the day:

“LAMBETH—Shortly before the closing of the court, Mr. Septimus Luker, the well-known dealer in ancient gems, carvings, intagli, &c., &c., applied to the sitting magistrate for advice. The applicant stated that he had been annoyed, at intervals throughout the day, by the proceedings of some of those strolling Indians who infest the streets. The persons complained of were three in number. After having been sent away by the police, they had returned again and again, and had attempted to enter the house on pretence of asking for charity. Warned off in the front, they had been discovered again at the back of the premises. Besides the annoyance complained of, Mr. Luker expressed himself as being under some apprehension that robbery might be contemplated. His collection contained many unique gems, both classical and Oriental, of the highest value. He had only the day before been compelled to dismiss a skilled workman in ivory carving from his employment (a native of India, as we understood), on suspicion of attempted theft; and he felt by no means sure that this man and the street jugglers of whom he complained, might not be acting in concert. It might be their object to collect a crowd, and create a disturbance in the street, and, in the confusion thus caused, to obtain access to the house. In reply to the magistrate, Mr. Luker admitted that he had no evidence to produce of any attempt at robbery being in contemplation. He could speak positively to the annoyance and interruption caused by the Indians, but not to anything else. The magistrate remarked that, if the annoyance were repeated, the applicant could summon the Indians to that court, where they might easily be dealt with under the Act. As to the valuables in Mr. Luker’s possession, Mr. Luker himself must take the best measures for their safe custody. He would do well perhaps to communicate with the police, and to adopt such additional precautions as their experience might suggest. The applicant thanked his worship, and withdrew.”

One of the wise ancients—on what occasion I forget—recommended us to look to the end. Looking to the end of these pages and wondering how I should bring them to it, I find the plain facts do it for me.

We have gone on, in this business of the Moonstone, from marvel to marvel; and here we end with the greatest marvel of all: Sergeant Cuff’s three predictions accomplished in less than a week from the time he made them.

My dear parents—now long beyond the reach of earthly disorder—left me, if they left me nothing else, a habit of putting things by.

In the first years of life, when the world was still manageable, my hair was brushed smooth before bed, and every ribbon and garment was folded and laid, always in the same sequence, upon the same chair, at the same place, at the foot of the same bed. Only after that came my little diary, with its dutiful lines written while the house settled into its night-quiet; and only after the diary came the “Evening Hymn,” repeated under the sheets, while the last sounds of the day—heels on paving-stones, a distant cart-wheel, a door-latch—thinned and died away into the mercy of sleep.

Later life has taken liberties. The Hymn has too often been displaced by meditations of a sharper flavour; and sleep—once a sweet, plain broth—has grown broken and thin, like a meal snatched beside a noisy street. But though care has made a clatter in my head, I have kept my diary, and I have kept my folding. One links me back to childhood, before papa was ruined; the other, intended only to discipline the fallen nature we inherit from Adam, has proved unexpectedly useful to my humble interests in another direction. It has enabled poor Me to serve the caprice of a wealthy member of the family into which my late uncle married. I am fortunate enough to be useful to Mr. Franklin Blake.

For some time past I have been cut off from all news of my relatives by marriage. When we are isolated and poor, we are not infrequently forgotten; and when we are serious besides, we are remembered chiefly as a nuisance. I am now living, for economy’s sake, in a little town in Brittany—inhabited by a select circle of sober English friends, and blessed with two comforts which go far with those who have learned to count: a Protestant clergyman and a cheap market.

The market is a small miracle of order amidst foreign tumult: baskets of onions with their papery skins whispering when touched; pale beans laid in neat rows; fish that still smell of the sea; and butter stamped firm as a seal. In the mornings the farmers come in with their carts, their wheels complaining softly on the stones; and at noon the town is briefly hushed, as if even the sparrows observed our English habit of resting the stomach in peace. I have often thought, standing with my modest purchases, how much of the soul depends on sound—the honest quiet of a place, the human scale of it, the absence of that metallic roar which modern life has begun to worship as “progress.” A city that never ceases shrieking, day and night, does more than weary the ear; it crowds out memory, and with memory goes language—those old words of home and field and hearth that no dictionary can restore once the last tongue that knew them has been silenced.

In this retirement—a Patmos amid the howling ocean of popery that surrounds us—a letter from England has reached me at last. My insignificant existence is suddenly remembered by Mr. Franklin Blake; and remembered, I must add, without even the decency of pretending affection. He wants something of me. The whim has seized him to stir up the deplorable scandal of the Moonstone; and I am to help him by writing the account of what I myself witnessed while visiting at Aunt Verinder’s house in London.

Pecuniary remuneration is offered—with that want of feeling peculiar to the rich. I am to re-open wounds that Time has scarcely closed; I am to take down from the shelf those bitter recollections, like jars of some strong pickle that sting the nose the moment the lid is lifted; and then I am to be compensated by a new laceration, in the shape of Mr. Blake’s cheque. My nature is weak. It cost me a hard struggle before Christian humility conquered sinful pride, and self-denial accepted the cheque.

Without my diary, I doubt—pray let me express it in the grossest terms!—if I could have honestly earned my money. With my diary, the poor labourer (who forgives Mr. Blake for insulting her) is worthy of her hire. Nothing escaped me at the time I was visiting dear Aunt Verinder. Everything was entered, thanks to my early training, day by day as it happened; and everything, down to the smallest particular, shall be told here. My sacred regard for truth is (thank God) far above my respect for persons. It will be easy for Mr. Blake to suppress what may not prove sufficiently flattering in these pages to the person chiefly concerned in them. He has purchased my time, but not even his wealth can purchase my conscience too.

My diary informs me that I was accidentally passing Aunt Verinder’s house in Montagu Square on Monday, the 3rd of July, 1848.

Seeing the shutters opened and the blinds drawn up, I felt it would be an act of polite attention to knock and make inquiries. The person who answered the door informed me that my aunt and her daughter (I really cannot call her my cousin!) had arrived from the country a week since, and meditated making some stay in London. I sent up a message at once, declining to disturb them, and only begging to know whether I could be of any use.

The person who answered the door took my message in insolent silence and left me standing in the hall. She is the daughter of a heathen old man named Betteredge—long, too long, tolerated in my aunt’s family. The hall, let me remark, had that stale-house smell which comes of servants who are familiar and floors that are spared; a faint reek of yesterday’s cooking lingered behind the stair-rail, and the very boards seemed to creak in a tone of impertinence. I sat down to wait for my answer. Having always a few tracts in my bag—as other women carry scented handkerchiefs or sweetmeats—I selected one which proved quite providentially applicable to the person who answered the door. The chair was hard; the air had the chill of stone and disuse; but the blessed consciousness of returning good for evil raised me above any trifling considerations of that kind. The tract was one of a series addressed to young women on the sinfulness of dress, written in a devoutly familiar style. Its title was: “A Word With You On Your Cap-Ribbons.”

“My lady is much obliged,” she said at last, “and begs you will come and lunch to-morrow at two.”

I passed over the manner in which she delivered this message, and the dreadful boldness of her look. I thanked this young castaway; and I said, in a tone of Christian interest, “Will you favour me by accepting a tract?”

She looked at the title as if it were a kitchen order badly written. “Is it written by a man or a woman, Miss? If it’s written by a woman, I had rather not read it on that account. If it’s written by a man, I beg to inform him he knows nothing about it.” She handed me back the tract and opened the door.

We must sow the good seed somehow. I waited till the door was shut on me and slipped the tract into the letter-box. When I had dropped another tract through the area railings—where it would, I hoped, meet the eye of some erring creature fetching coals or carrying up broth—I felt relieved, in some small degree, of a heavy responsibility towards others.

That evening there was a meeting of the Select Committee of the Mothers’-Small-Clothes-Conversion-Society. The object of this excellent charity is—as all serious people know—to rescue unredeemed fathers’ trousers from the pawnbroker, and to prevent their resumption, on the part of the irreclaimable parent, by abridging them immediately to suit the proportions of the innocent son. The work is very literal: the shears bite through worn cloth; the needle goes in and out with a quiet industry; there is a smell of old fustian, soap, and warm human bodies gathered close in a room; and one hears, under the women’s murmurs, the soft rasp of scissors like a patient insect. I was a member, at that time, of the select committee; and I mention the Society here because my precious and admirable friend, Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite, was associated with our work of moral and material usefulness.

I had expected to see him in the boardroom on that Monday evening, and had proposed to tell him, when we met, of dear Aunt Verinder’s arrival in London. To my great disappointment he never appeared. On my expressing surprise at his absence, my sisters of the Committee all looked up together from their trousers (we had a great pressure of business that night), and asked in amazement if I had not heard the news.

I acknowledged my ignorance, and was then told, for the first time, of an event which forms, so to speak, the starting-point of this narrative. On the previous Friday, two gentlemen—occupying widely different positions in society—had been the victims of an outrage which had startled all London. One of the gentlemen was Mr. Septimus Luker, of Lambeth. The other was Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite.

Living now in my present isolation, I have no means of introducing the newspaper-account of the outrage into my narrative; and I was also deprived, at the time, of the inestimable advantage of hearing the events related by the fervid eloquence of Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite. All I can do is to state the facts as they were stated, on that Monday evening, to me; proceeding on the plan I have been taught from infancy to adopt in folding up my clothes. Everything shall be put neatly, and everything shall be put in its place. These lines are written by a poor weak woman. From a poor weak woman who will be cruel enough to expect more?

The date—thanks to my dear parents, no dictionary that ever was written can be more particular than I am about dates—was Friday, June 30th, 1848.

Early on that memorable day, our gifted Mr. Godfrey happened to be cashing a cheque at a banking-house in Lombard Street. The name of the firm is accidentally blotted in my diary; and my sacred regard for truth forbids me to hazard a guess in a matter of this kind. Fortunately, the name of the firm doesn’t matter. What does matter is a circumstance that occurred when Mr. Godfrey had transacted his business.

On gaining the door, he encountered a gentleman—a perfect stranger to him—who was leaving the office exactly at the same time as himself. A momentary contest of politeness ensued as to who should first pass through the door of the bank. The stranger insisted on making Mr. Godfrey precede him; Mr. Godfrey said a few civil words; they bowed; and parted in the street.

Thoughtless and superficial people may say, Here is a very trumpery little incident related in an absurdly circumstantial manner. Oh, my young friends and fellow-sinners! beware of presuming to exercise your poor carnal reason. Oh, be morally tidy. Let your faith be as your stockings, and your stockings as your faith—both ever spotless, and both ready to put on at a moment’s notice!

I beg a thousand pardons. I have fallen insensibly into my Sunday-school style—most inappropriate in such a record as this. Let me try to be worldly. Let me say that trifles, in this case as in many others, led to terrible results. Merely premising that the polite stranger was Mr. Luker, of Lambeth, we will now follow Mr. Godfrey home to his residence at Kilburn.

He found waiting for him in the hall a poorly clad but delicate and interesting-looking little boy. The boy handed him a letter, merely mentioning that he had been entrusted with it by an old lady whom he did not know, and who had given him no instructions to wait for an answer. Such incidents were not uncommon in Mr. Godfrey’s large experience as a promoter of public charities. He let the boy go and opened the letter.

The handwriting was entirely unfamiliar. It requested his attendance, within an hour, at a house in Northumberland Street, Strand, which he had never had occasion to enter before. The object sought was to obtain from the worthy manager certain details on the subject of the Mothers’-Small-Clothes-Conversion-Society; the information was wanted by an elderly lady who proposed adding largely to the resources of the charity if her questions were met by satisfactory replies. She mentioned her name, and added that the shortness of her stay in London prevented her from giving any longer notice to the eminent philanthropist whom she addressed.

Ordinary people might have hesitated before setting aside their own engagements to suit the convenience of a stranger. The Christian hero never hesitates where good is to be done. Mr. Godfrey instantly turned back and proceeded to the house in Northumberland Street.

A most respectable though somewhat corpulent man answered the door; and on hearing Mr. Godfrey’s name immediately conducted him into an empty apartment at the back, on the drawing-room floor. He noticed two unusual things on entering the room. One was a faint odour of musk and camphor—like a sick-room disguised as a perfumer’s shop. The other was an ancient Oriental manuscript, richly illuminated with Indian figures and devices, lying open upon a table.

He was looking at the book—standing so that his back was turned towards the closed folding-doors communicating with the front room—when, without the slightest previous noise to warn him, he felt himself suddenly seized round the neck from behind. He had just time to notice that the arm round his throat was naked and of a tawny-brown colour before his eyes were bandaged, his mouth was gagged, and he was thrown helpless on the floor by (as he judged) two men. A third rifled his pockets and searched him—if, as a lady, I may venture to use such an expression—through and through to his skin.

Here I should greatly enjoy saying a few cheering words on the devout confidence which could alone have sustained Mr. Godfrey in an emergency so terrible as this. Perhaps, however, the position and appearance of my admirable friend at the culminating period of the outrage are hardly within the proper limits of female discussion. Let me pass over the next few moments, and return to him when the odious search had been completed.

The outrage had been perpetrated throughout in dead silence. At the end of it some words were exchanged among the invisible wretches in a language Mr. Godfrey did not understand, but in tones plainly expressive (to his cultivated ear) of disappointment and rage. He was suddenly lifted from the ground, placed in a chair, and bound there hand and foot. The next moment he felt air flowing in from an open door; listened; and concluded he was alone again in the room.

An interval elapsed. He heard a sound below like the rustling sound of a woman’s dress. It advanced up the stairs and stopped. A female scream rent the guilty air. A man’s voice below exclaimed, “Hullo!” A man’s feet ascended the stairs. Mr. Godfrey felt fingers unfastening his bandage and extracting his gag. He looked in amazement at two respectable strangers and faintly articulated, “What does it mean?” The two respectable strangers looked back and said, “Exactly the question we were going to ask you.”

The inevitable explanation followed. No—let me be scrupulously particular. Sal volatile and water followed, to compose dear Mr. Godfrey’s nerves. The explanation came next.

It appeared, from the statement of the landlord and landlady of the house (persons of good repute in the neighbourhood), that their first and second-floor apartments had been engaged on the previous day, for a week certain, by a most respectable-looking gentleman—the same who has been already described as answering the door to Mr. Godfrey’s knock. This gentleman had paid the week’s rent and all the week’s extras in advance, stating that the apartments were wanted for three Oriental noblemen, friends of his, visiting England for the first time.

Early on the morning of the outrage, two of the Oriental strangers, accompanied by their respectable English friend, took possession of the apartments. The third was expected to join them shortly; and the luggage (reported as very bulky) was announced to follow when it had passed through the Custom-house late in the afternoon. Not more than ten minutes previous to Mr. Godfrey’s visit, the third foreigner had arrived.

Nothing out of the common had happened, to the knowledge of the landlord and landlady downstairs, until within the last five minutes—when they had seen the three foreigners, accompanied by their respectable English friend, all leave the house together, walking quietly in the direction of the Strand. Remembering that a visitor had called, and not having seen the visitor also leave the house, the landlady had thought it strange that the gentleman should be left by himself upstairs. After a short discussion with her husband she had considered it advisable to ascertain whether anything was wrong. The result had followed as I have already attempted to describe it; and there the explanation ended.

An investigation was next made in the room. Dear Mr. Godfrey’s property was found scattered in all directions. When the articles were collected, however, nothing was missing. His watch, chain, purse, keys, pocket-handkerchief, note-book, and all his loose papers had been closely examined and then left unharmed. In the same way, not the smallest morsel of property belonging to the proprietors of the house had been abstracted. The Oriental noblemen had removed their own illuminated manuscript, and had removed nothing else.

What did it mean? Taking the worldly point of view, it appeared to mean that Mr. Godfrey had been the victim of some incomprehensible error committed by certain unknown men; that a dark conspiracy was on foot in the midst of us; and that our beloved and innocent friend had been entangled in its meshes. When the Christian hero of a hundred charitable victories plunges into a pitfall dug for him by mistake—oh, what a warning it is to the rest of us to be unceasingly on our guard! How soon may our own evil passions prove to be Oriental noblemen who pounce on us unawares!

I could write pages of affectionate warning on this one theme, but (alas!) I am not permitted to improve—I am condemned to narrate. My wealthy relative’s cheque—henceforth the incubus of my existence—warns me that I have not done with this record of violence yet. We must leave Mr. Godfrey to recover in Northumberland Street, and must follow the proceedings of Mr. Luker at a later period of the day.

After leaving the bank, Mr. Luker visited various parts of London on business errands. Returning to his own residence, he found a letter waiting for him, described as having been left a short time previously by a boy. In this case, as in Mr. Godfrey’s case, the handwriting was strange; but the name mentioned was the name of one of Mr. Luker’s customers. His correspondent announced—writing in the third person, apparently by the hand of a deputy—that he had been unexpectedly summoned to London. He had just established himself in lodgings in Alfred Place, Tottenham Court Road; and he desired to see Mr. Luker immediately on the subject of a purchase he contemplated making. The gentleman was an enthusiastic collector of Oriental antiquities and had been for many years a liberal patron of the establishment in Lambeth. Oh, when shall we wean ourselves from the worship of Mammon! Mr. Luker called a cab and drove off instantly to his liberal patron.

Exactly what had happened to Mr. Godfrey in Northumberland Street now happened to Mr. Luker in Alfred Place. Once more the respectable man answered the door and showed the visitor upstairs into the back drawing-room. There again lay the illuminated manuscript on a table. Mr. Luker’s attention was absorbed, as Mr. Godfrey’s attention had been absorbed, by that beautiful work of Indian art. He too was aroused from his studies by a tawny naked arm round his throat, by a bandage over his eyes, and by a gag in his mouth. He too was thrown prostrate and searched to the skin.

A longer interval elapsed than had passed in the experience of Mr. Godfrey; but it ended as before in the persons of the house suspecting something wrong and going upstairs to see what had happened. Precisely the same explanation which the landlord in Northumberland Street had given to Mr. Godfrey, the landlord in Alfred Place now gave to Mr. Luker. Both had been imposed upon in the same way by the plausible address and well-filled purse of the respectable stranger who introduced himself as acting for his foreign friends.

The one point of difference between the two cases occurred when the scattered contents of Mr. Luker’s pockets were being collected from the floor. His watch and purse were safe; but—less fortunate than Mr. Godfrey—one of the loose papers he carried about him had been taken away. The paper acknowledged the receipt of a valuable of great price which Mr. Luker had that day left in the care of his bankers.

This document would be useless for purposes of fraud, inasmuch as it provided that the valuable should only be given up on the personal application of the owner. As soon as he recovered himself, Mr. Luker hurried to the bank on the chance that the thieves who had robbed him might ignorantly present themselves with the receipt. Nothing had been seen of them when he arrived, and nothing was seen of them afterwards. Their respectable English friend had (in the opinion of the bankers) looked the receipt over before they attempted to make use of it, and had given them the necessary warning in good time.

Information of both outrages was communicated to the police, and the needful investigations were pursued, I believe, with great energy. The authorities held that a robbery had been planned on insufficient information received by the thieves. They had plainly not been sure whether Mr. Luker had, or had not, trusted the transmission of his precious gem to another person; and poor polite Mr. Godfrey had paid the penalty of having been seen accidentally speaking to him. Add to this that Mr. Godfrey’s absence from our Monday evening meeting had been occasioned by a consultation of the authorities at which he was requested to assist—and all the explanations required being now given, I may proceed with the simpler story of my own little personal experiences in Montagu Square.

I was punctual to the luncheon hour on Tuesday. Reference to my diary shows this to have been a chequered day—much in it to be devoutly regretted, much in it to be devoutly thankful for.

Dear Aunt Verinder received me with her usual grace and kindness. The house had its London smell—coal-dust, beeswax, and the faint sweetness of flowers that have been warmed too long in a close room; and from outside came that ceaseless urban din, the rolling and striking and rattling that never properly rests. Even at table, the city forced its voice upon one, like a vulgar man insisting on being heard.

After a little while I noticed something was wrong. Certain anxious looks escaped my aunt, all of which took the direction of her daughter. I never see Rachel myself without wondering how it can be that so insignificant-looking a person should be the child of such distinguished parents as Sir John and Lady Verinder. On this occasion, however, she not only disappointed me—she shocked me.

There was an absence of all ladylike restraint in her language and manner most painful to see. She was possessed by some feverish excitement which made her distressingly loud when she laughed, and sinfully wasteful and capricious in what she ate and drank at luncheon—tasting, rejecting, calling for one thing and then another, as if food were not God’s steady comfort but a toy to be flung aside. The very sounds she made—the quick scrape of knife on plate, the sudden clink of glass, that ungoverned burst of laughter—seemed to strike her poor mother like blows. I felt deeply for Lady Verinder even before the true state of the case had been confidentially made known to me.

Luncheon over, my aunt said, “Remember what the doctor told you, Rachel, about quieting yourself with a book after taking your meals.”

“I’ll go into the library, mamma,” she answered. “But if Godfrey calls, mind I am told of it. I am dying for more news of him, after his adventure in Northumberland Street.” She kissed her mother on the forehead, and looked my way. “Good-bye, Clack,” she said carelessly.

Her insolence roused no angry feeling in me. I only made a private memorandum to pray for her—praying, I may add, in my own words, not in those grand public phrases which sound well in the mouth of the prosperous, but often fail the poor when they try to lay their private griefs into the mould of a dominant tongue. There are feelings that will not take another person’s vocabulary; they slip through it like water through a sieve, leaving one with nothing but a wet hand and a shame at having tried.

When we were left by ourselves, my aunt told me the whole horrible story of the Indian Diamond—which, I am happy to know, it is not necessary to repeat here. She did not conceal from me that she would have preferred keeping silence on the subject. But when her own servants all knew of the loss of the Moonstone, and when some of the circumstances had actually found their way into the newspapers—when strangers were speculating whether there was any connection between what had happened at Lady Verinder’s country house and what had happened in Northumberland Street and Alfred Place—concealment was not to be thought of; and perfect frankness became a necessity as well as a virtue.

Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite entered the drawing-room as punctually as a dish arrives at table when the cook has been trained to respect the clock. Not too soon to throw the household into confusion; not too late to leave us standing with the door yawning open and the air of the corridor leaking in, colder and flatter than the room’s own temper. His whole manner had that finished completeness which, in domestic life, is sometimes mistaken for virtue because it never wrinkles the surface.

“Go to Miss Verinder,” my aunt said to the servant, “and tell her Mr. Ablewhite is here.”

We both asked after his health. We both spoke at once, as women do when they mean to be kind and cannot agree who shall be kind first. And he, with that practised gentleness which seems to hear two voices as neatly as a musician catches two notes, answered both of us in the same moment—my aunt receiving the words, I receiving the smile.

“What,” he cried, with infinite tenderness, “have I done to deserve all this sympathy? My dear aunt! my dear Miss Clack! I have merely been mistaken for somebody else. I have only been blindfolded; I have only been strangled; I have only been thrown flat on my back, on a very thin carpet, covering a particularly hard floor. Just think how much worse it might have been! I might have been murdered; I might have been robbed. What have I lost? Nothing but Nervous Force—which the law doesn’t recognise as property; so that, strictly speaking, I have lost nothing at all.

“If I could have had my own way, I would have kept my adventure to myself—I shrink from all this fuss and publicity. But Mr. Luker made his injuries public, and my injuries, as the necessary consequence, have been proclaimed in their turn. I have become the property of the newspapers, until the gentle reader gets sick of the subject. I am very sick indeed of it myself. May the gentle reader soon be like me!

“And how is dear Rachel? Still enjoying the gaieties of London? So glad to hear it! Miss Clack, I need all your indulgence. I am sadly behind-hand with my Committee Work and my dear Ladies. But I really do hope to look in at the Mothers’-Small-Clothes next week. Did you make cheering progress at Monday’s Committee? Was the Board hopeful about future prospects? And are we nicely off for Trousers?”

His voice—deep, rich, with a soft reverberation like a well-constructed room that gives back what it is given—made even the word Trousers sound like a benevolence. I was on the point of replying that we were, if anything, oppressed by the abundance of that article, when the door opened again, and in came the brisk disturbance of youth—Miss Verinder herself.

She crossed the room at an unladylike speed, as if she had run from one end of the house and brought the rush of it with her; her hair not arranged as it ought to have been arranged, her face flushed as if some inward heat had risen too suddenly—like broth that has been let boil too fast and will not clear.

“I am charmed to see you, Godfrey,” she said, in the off-hand manner of one young man greeting another. “I wish you had brought Mr. Luker with you. You and he—while our present excitement lasts—are the two most interesting men in all London. It’s morbid to say this; it’s unhealthy; it’s all that a well-regulated mind like Miss Clack’s most instinctively shudders at. Never mind that. Tell me the whole of the Northumberland Street story directly. I know the newspapers have left some of it out.”

I grieve to write it; yet truth, like salt, must be used though it stings. Mr. Godfrey—too human, alas—took her hand in both his, and laid it softly against his waistcoat, where one might suppose his heart to be. It was a direct encouragement of her recklessness, and of her insolent reference to me.

“Dearest Rachel,” he said, “the newspapers have told you everything—and they have told it much better than I can.”

“Godfrey thinks we all make too much of the matter,” my aunt remarked. “He has just been saying that he doesn’t care to speak of it.”

“Why?” Rachel asked, and in the single word there was a sharp lift—like a knife’s point catching the light.

He looked down at her with an indulgence which, to my mind, was as injudicious as it was undeserved. I felt called upon—gently—to interpose.

“Rachel, darling,” I said, “true greatness and true courage are ever modest.”

She never so much as glanced at me.

“You are a very good fellow in your way, Godfrey,” she returned, still speaking to him as if they were equals in some masculine club of frank speech. “But I am quite sure you are not great; I don’t believe you possess any extraordinary courage; and I am firmly persuaded—if you ever had any modesty—that your lady-worshippers relieved you of that virtue a good many years since. You have some private reason for not talking of your adventure in Northumberland Street; and I mean to know it.”

“My reason is the simplest imaginable,” he answered, still bearing it with patience. “I am tired of the subject.”

“You are tired of the subject? My dear Godfrey, I am going to make a remark.”

“What is it?”

“You live a great deal too much in the society of women. And you have contracted two very bad habits in consequence. You have learnt to talk nonsense seriously, and you have got into a way of telling fibs for the pleasure of telling them. You can’t go straight with your lady-worshippers. I mean to make you go straight with me. Come, and sit down. I am brimful of downright questions; and I expect you to be brimful of downright answers.”

She actually dragged him to a chair by the window, planting him where the light could fall full on his face, as if she meant to read him as one reads a page held near the glass. I looked to my aunt for intervention; but she sat unmoved, with a heaviness in her stillness that struck me as new—like a harvest field after reaping, when the bustle has gone and the stalks stand blunt and tired. Perhaps it was reaction after her late trials in the country. Perhaps it was something more. At her age, and with her autumnal exuberance of figure, it was not pleasant to observe.

Rachel began at once, taking no more notice of her mother or of me than if we were the furniture.

“Have the police done anything, Godfrey?”

“Nothing whatever.”

“It is certain, I suppose, that the three men who laid the trap for you were the same three men who afterwards laid the trap for Mr. Luker?”

“Humanly speaking, my dear Rachel, there can be no doubt of it.”

“And not a trace of them has been discovered?”

“Not a trace.”

“It is thought—is it not?—that these three men are the three Indians who came to our house in the country.”

“Some people think so.”

“Do you think so?”

“My dear Rachel, they blindfolded me before I could see their faces. I know nothing whatever of the matter. How can I offer an opinion on it?”

Even his angelic gentleness began to fray under such persistent tugging. He made as if to rise. She stopped him—took him by the shoulders and pushed him back into the chair. Do not say immodest; do not say guilty terror; we must not judge. Indeed, indeed, we must not judge.

“I want to know something about Mr. Luker, Godfrey.”

“I am again unfortunate, Rachel. No man knows less of Mr. Luker than I do.”

“You never saw him before you and he met accidentally at the bank?”

“Never.”

“You have seen him since?”

“Yes. We have been examined together, as well as separately, to assist the police.”

“Mr. Luker was robbed of a receipt which he had got from his banker’s—was he not? What was the receipt for?”

“For a valuable gem which he had placed in the safe keeping of the bank.”

“That’s what the newspapers say. It may be enough for the general reader; but it is not enough for me. The banker’s receipt must have mentioned what the gem was?”

“The banker’s receipt, Rachel—as I have heard it described—mentioned nothing of the kind. A valuable gem, belonging to Mr. Luker; deposited by Mr. Luker; sealed with Mr. Luker’s seal; and only to be given up on Mr. Luker’s personal application. That was the form, and that is all I know about it.”

She paused. She looked at her mother and sighed, as if the sigh were something she could not swallow. Then she looked again at Mr. Godfrey.

“Some of our private affairs, at home,” she said, “seem to have got into the newspapers?”

“I grieve to say, it is so.”

“And some idle people, perfect strangers to us, are trying to trace a connexion between what happened at our house in Yorkshire and what has happened since, here in London?”

“The public curiosity, in certain quarters, is, I fear, taking that turn.”

“The people who say that the three unknown men who ill-used you and Mr. Luker are the three Indians, also say that the valuable gem—”

She stopped. While she had been speaking she had been turning whiter and whiter, until the strong black of her hair made her face look ghastly by contrast, like flour on a dark board. We thought she would faint.

Mr. Godfrey again tried to rise. My aunt begged her to say no more. I followed my aunt’s appeal with a modest medicinal offering—a bottle of salts, ready to hand. None of it reached her. She held herself like a creature cornered.

“Godfrey, stay where you are. Mamma, there is not the least reason to be alarmed about me. Clack, you’re dying to hear the end of it—I won’t faint, expressly to oblige you.”

Those were her words—set down in my diary the moment I returned home. But do not judge.

She turned to him again, and forced the question through.

“Tell me plainly, Godfrey—do any of them say that Mr. Luker’s valuable gem is—the Moonstone?”

At the very name of the Indian Diamond, I saw a change pass over Mr. Godfrey. His complexion deepened; the easy suavity dropped away; and an indignation—noble, as I must call it—took him by the voice.

“They do say it,” he answered. “There are people who don’t hesitate to accuse Mr. Luker of telling a falsehood to serve some private interests of his own. He has over and over again solemnly declared that, until this scandal assailed him, he had never even heard of the Moonstone. And these vile people reply, without a shadow of proof to justify them: He has his reasons for concealment; we decline to believe him on his oath. Shameful! shameful!”

Rachel looked at him in a manner I cannot rightly express—strange, searching, as if she listened not only to his words but to the sound behind them. When he had done, she said:

“Considering that Mr. Luker is only a chance acquaintance of yours, you take up his cause, Godfrey, rather warmly.”

“I hope, Rachel,” he replied, “I take up the cause of all oppressed people rather warmly.”

It was spoken with such melting sincerity that it might have softened stone. Yet what is stone to the unregenerate heart? She sneered—she sneered at him to his face.

“Keep your noble sentiments for your Ladies’ Committees, Godfrey. I am certain that the scandal which has assailed Mr. Luker, has not spared you.”

Even my aunt’s torpor stirred at that.

“My dear Rachel,” she remonstrated, “you have really no right to say that!”

“I mean no harm, mamma—I mean good. Have a moment’s patience with me, and you will see.”

She turned back to Mr. Godfrey with something like pity, and—unladylike again—took him by the hand.

“I am certain,” she said, “that I have found out the true reason of your unwillingness to speak of this matter before my mother and before me. An unlucky accident has associated you in people’s minds with Mr. Luker. You have told me what scandal says of him. What does scandal say of you?”

“Don’t ask me!” he pleaded. “It’s better forgotten, Rachel—it is, indeed.”

“I will hear it!” she cried, fiercely, at the top of her voice.

“Tell her, Godfrey!” my aunt urged. “Nothing can do her such harm as your silence is doing now!”

His eyes filled with tears. He looked at Rachel as if asking leave to spare her—and then he said the words that fell like a dropped plate in a quiet room.

“If you will have it, Rachel—scandal says that the Moonstone is in pledge to Mr. Luker, and that I am the man who has pawned it.”

She sprang up with a scream, and stared from him to my aunt and from my aunt back to him, in such a frantic manner that I thought her reason gone.

“Don’t speak to me! Don’t touch me!” she exclaimed, shrinking from us all—like some hunted animal—into a corner. “This is my fault! I must set it right. I have sacrificed myself—I had a right to do that, if I liked. But to let an innocent man be ruined; to keep a secret which destroys his character for life—Oh, good God, it’s too horrible! I can’t bear it!”

My aunt half rose, then sat down again abruptly. She beckoned to me with a faintness that frightened me, and pointed into her work-box at a little phial.

“Quick!” she whispered. “Six drops, in water. Don’t let Rachel see.”

In any other moment I might have been struck by the secrecy; now there was no time for thought. I obeyed. Mr. Godfrey, unconsciously helping my concealment by drawing Rachel’s attention to him, spoke to her at the other end of the room in tones meant to soothe.

“Indeed, indeed, you exaggerate,” I heard him say. “My reputation stands too high to be destroyed by a miserable passing scandal like this. It will be all forgotten in another week. Let us never speak of it again.”

She was beyond him—beyond generosity itself.

“I must, and will, stop it,” she answered. “Mamma! hear what I say. Miss Clack! hear what I say. I know the hand that took the Moonstone. I know—” she stamped as she spoke, laying violent emphasis on the words—“I know that Godfrey Ablewhite is innocent! Take me to the magistrate, Godfrey! Take me to the magistrate, and I will swear it!”

My aunt caught my hand.

“Stand between us for a minute or two,” she whispered. “Don’t let Rachel see me.”

A bluish tinge had come over her face. I was startled. She read it and tried to quiet me.

“The drops will put me right in a minute or two,” she said; closed her eyes; waited.

While I stood as she bade me, I heard Mr. Godfrey remonstrating still.

“You must not appear publicly in such a thing as this,” he said. “Your reputation, dearest Rachel, is something too pure and too sacred to be trifled with.”

“My reputation!” She burst out laughing—a hard laugh, like a spoon struck against a glass. “Why, I am accused, Godfrey, as well as you. The best detective officer in England declares that I have stolen my own Diamond. Ask him what he thinks—and he will tell you that I have pledged the Moonstone to pay my private debts!”

She broke off, ran to her mother, and dropped on her knees.

“Oh mamma! mamma! mamma! I must be mad—mustn’t I?—not to own the truth now!”

Too vehement to notice my aunt’s condition, she was up again at once and back with Mr. Godfrey.

“I won’t let you—I won’t let any innocent man—be accused and disgraced through my fault. If you won’t take me before the magistrate, draw out a declaration of your innocence on paper, and I will sign it. Do as I tell you, Godfrey, or I’ll write it to the newspapers—I’ll go out, and cry it in the streets!”

We will call it hysterics, and not remorse. Mr. Godfrey pacified her by taking a sheet of paper and writing the declaration. She signed it with a feverish speed, as if her hand were being chased.

“Show it everywhere—don’t think of me,” she said, thrusting it to him. “I am afraid, Godfrey, I have not done you justice, hitherto, in my thoughts. You are more unselfish—you are a better man than I believed you to be. Come here when you can, and I will try and repair the wrong I have done you.”

She gave him her hand. Alas for our fallen nature; alas for Mr. Godfrey. He kissed it, and answered her in a tone so tender it was, in such circumstances, little better than a compromise with sin.

“I will come, dearest,” he said, “on condition that we don’t speak of this hateful subject again.”

Before anyone could speak further, a thundering knock at the street door startled us all. I looked through the window and saw the World, the Flesh, and the Devil waiting below—as typified in carriage and horses, a powdered footman, and three women dressed with such audacity that their silks and ornaments seemed to clatter even at a distance, as if fashion had become a kind of machinery.

Rachel steadied herself at once, as people do who have been crying in private and must go out into public brightness.

“They have come to take me to the flower-show,” she said, crossing to her mother. “One word, mamma, before I go. I have not distressed you, have I?”

Is such bluntness of moral feeling to be pitied or condemned? I incline to mercy; let us pity it.

The drops had done their work. My aunt’s complexion had returned to itself.

“No, no, my dear,” she said. “Go with our friends, and enjoy yourself.”

Rachel stooped and kissed her. As she came to the door, another change passed over her—she was in tears. I was moved by the momentary softening and, thinking to speak earnestly, allowed my compassion to show.

“What do you mean by pitying me?” she whispered bitterly as she passed. “Don’t you see how happy I am? I’m going to the flower-show, Clack; and I’ve got the prettiest bonnet in London.”

She blew me a kiss—hollow mockery—and was gone.

I cannot properly express the pity I felt for her; words, like money, are not plentiful with me. My heart bled for this misguided girl.

When I turned back, Mr. Godfrey was searching, softly and carefully, in different parts of the room. Before I could offer assistance, he had found what he wanted. He came to us with the declaration in one hand and a box of matches in the other.

“Dear aunt, a little conspiracy!” he said. “Dear Miss Clack, a pious fraud which even your high moral rectitude will excuse! Will you leave Rachel to suppose that I accept the generous self-sacrifice which has signed this paper? And will you kindly bear witness that I destroy it in your presence, before I leave the house?”

He struck a match; the sulphur scent rose sharp for an instant; and he set the paper alight, laying it to burn in a plate upon the table. The thin flame ate greedily through the inked lines, curling them into blackened lace.

“Any trifling inconvenience that I may suffer is as nothing,” he said, “compared with the importance of preserving that pure name from the contaminating contact of the world. There! We have reduced it to a little harmless heap of ashes; and our dear impulsive Rachel will never know what we have done! How do you feel? My precious friends, how do you feel? For my poor part, I am as light-hearted as a boy!”

He beamed on us, holding out a hand to my aunt and a hand to me. I was too affected to speak. In a kind of spiritual self-forgetfulness I lifted his hand to my lips. He murmured a soft remonstrance. For a moment I was lost—lifted, as it were, out of the room and out of myself.

When I opened my eyes again, it was like coming down from a high place to the ordinary floor. There was nobody but my aunt. Mr. Godfrey had gone.

I should like to stop here; I should like to let my narrative end at the ashes in the plate. But the unrelenting pressure of Mr. Franklin Blake’s cheque forces me onward; there was more to be disclosed in that Tuesday visit to Montagu Square.

Left alone with Lady Verinder, I turned naturally to the subject of her health—touching, as delicately as I could, on the strange anxiety she had shown to hide her indisposition and the remedy from her daughter’s observation.

Her reply surprised me.

“Drusilla,” she said—and if I have not mentioned that my Christian name is Drusilla, I mention it now—“you are touching—quite innocently, I know—on a very distressing subject.”

I rose at once; delicacy offered me only one course: apologies, and departure. She stopped me and insisted I sit again.

“You have surprised a secret,” she said, “which I had confided to my sister, Mrs. Ablewhite, and to my lawyer, Mr. Bruff, and to no one else. I can trust in their discretion; and I am sure, when I tell you the circumstances, I can trust in yours. Have you any pressing engagement, Drusilla? or is your time your own this afternoon?”

My time, of course, was wholly at my aunt’s disposal.

“Keep me company then,” she said, “for another hour. I have something to tell you which I believe you will be sorry to hear. And I shall have a service to ask of you afterwards, if you don’t object to assist me.”

So far from objecting, I was eager.

“You can wait here,” she went on, “till Mr. Bruff comes at five. And you can be one of the witnesses, Drusilla, when I sign my Will.”

Her Will. I thought of the little phial in the work-box; of the bluish tinge on her face; of the haste, the concealment, the drops measured out like a recipe whose bitterness must not be tasted by the young. A light—cold, steady, and not of this world—seemed to fall across my mind from an unmade grave.

My aunt’s secret was a secret no longer.

Consideration for my poor aunt forbade me to speak first. I sat close by her fireside, listening not only to the words she would presently trust me with, but to the room itself—the hush that lay between the ticks of the mantel-clock, the faint rasp of London wheels beyond the window, the soft domestic creak when she shifted in her chair. A household has its own language, made of small sounds; and when those sounds grow thin and careful, you know—before any doctor dares confess it—that sickness has been living there.

“I have been seriously ill, Drusilla, for some time past,” Lady Verinder said at last. “And, strange to say, without knowing it myself.”

“Yes, dear,” I answered, keeping my voice as steady as the clock.

“I brought Rachel to London, as you know, for medical advice. I thought it right to consult two doctors.” She spoke with that practical composure which wealthy houses cultivate, the kind that can order linen and consult physicians in the same tone.

“One of the two was a stranger to me,” she continued. “The other had been an old friend of my husband’s. After prescribing for Rachel, he asked to speak with me privately. I expected directions about my daughter. Instead, he took me by the hand, very gravely, and said, ‘You are, I am afraid, far more urgently in need of medical advice than your daughter.’ He questioned me. I answered lightly, at first—until I saw that my answers distressed him. He appointed to come the next day with another medical man, at an hour when Rachel would not be at home.”

She paused; and the quiet in the room thickened, like broth left too long to cool.

“The result,” she said, “was most kindly conveyed to me. They were satisfied that precious time had been lost which could never be regained, and that my case had passed beyond the reach of their art. For more than two years I have been suffering under an insidious form of heart disease. Without any symptoms to alarm me, it has, little by little, fatally broken me down. I may live for some months, or I may die before another day has passed. The doctors cannot—and dare not—speak more positively than this.”

The sentence fell among the familiar objects—curtains, books, the little table where tea might have stood—and made them all look like things seen for the last time.

“It would be vain to say,” she went on, “that I have not had miserable moments since the truth was made known to me. But I am more resigned now. I am doing my best to set my worldly affairs in order. My one great anxiety is that Rachel should be kept in ignorance. If she knew, she would at once attribute my broken health to anxiety about the Diamond, and would reproach herself bitterly—poor child—for what is in no sense her fault. Both doctors agree the mischief began two, if not three, years since. You will keep my secret, Drusilla—for I see sincere sorrow and sympathy for me in your face.”

She mistook what she saw. My sorrow was real enough; but beneath it, I felt a fierce, eager gratitude—an inward stir like a match taken to tinder. A door had opened on a labour I believed appointed to me. Here was a beloved creature standing, as it were, on the brink—unwarned, unfortified—and led, providentially led, to speak to me.

I put my arms around her. My tenderness, once released, insisted on touching and holding, as if faith itself could be made bodily by the pressure of an embrace.

“Oh!” I said. “The indescribable interest with which you inspire me! Oh, the good I mean to do you, dear, before we part!”

After a few earnest words of warning—gentle, but not to be mistaken—I offered her the choice of three precious friends, all at work in the neighbourhood, all ready to come at a word from me, all inexhaustible in exhortation as a kitchen range is inexhaustible in heat. But my aunt’s face clouded: she looked puzzled, even frightened, and met me with the one objection the world always keeps polished for use—she was not strong enough to face strangers.

I yielded—for the moment. My experience as Reader and Visitor (under not less, first and last, than fourteen beloved clerical friends) told me what was needed: preparation by books, a gradual seasoning of the mind, as you might temper a too-strong relish by slow additions, until it can be borne.

“You will read, dear, won’t you?” I asked, in my most winning way. “If I bring you my own precious books? Turned down at all the right places. Marked in pencil where you are to stop and ask yourself, ‘Does this apply to me?’”

Even that simple appeal startled her, so perishing is the influence of the world. “I will do what I can, Drusilla, to please you,” she said, with a look of surprise that was instructive—and terrible—to see.

Not a moment was to be lost. The mantel-clock told me I had just time to hurry home, select a first series of readings—a dozen, no more—and return by five o’clock to meet the lawyer and witness her will. I promised faithfully; and left the house on my errand of mercy.

When no interests but my own are involved, I take the omnibus with humility. But devotion has its extravagances. I committed the prodigality of taking a cab.

London received me with its usual harsh music: iron on stone; hooves striking sparks of sound; voices pitched to battle the roar of traffic. It is a cruelty we grow used to—this constant pounding that drives thought into corners and makes even prayer feel hurried. I endured it as I must, and reached my lodging, where my little library waited—those volumes I treasured as other women treasure spice-boxes and preserving pans, each one stored for its proper use, each one meant to keep what is most perishable from spoiling.

I chose and marked my first series of readings, slid them into a carpet-bag, and drove back to Montagu Square. I paid the cabman exactly his fare. He received it with an oath; upon which I instantly gave him a tract. If I had put a pistol to his head, he could hardly have shown greater consternation. He sprang up on his box, flung profane cries behind him, and drove off furiously. Quite useless, I am happy to say. I sowed the good seed in spite of him by throwing a second tract in at the window as the cab rattled away—its wheels battering the street like a drum that never tires.

At my aunt’s door the footman answered (not the person with the cap-ribbons, to my great relief). He informed me the doctor had called and was still shut up with Lady Verinder. Mr. Bruff, the lawyer, had arrived a minute since, and was waiting in the library. I was shown in there to wait too.

Mr. Bruff looked surprised to see me. We had met before under my aunt’s roof. He is the family solicitor: a man old and grizzled in the service of the world—prophet of Law and Mammon by day, and by leisure equally capable of reading a novel and tearing up a tract.

“Have you come to stay here, Miss Clack?” he asked, glancing at my carpet-bag.

To reveal the contents of that bag to him would have been to invite profanity. I lowered myself to his level and stated my business.

“My aunt has informed me that she is about to sign her will,” I said. “She has been so good as to ask me to be one of the witnesses.”

“Aye? aye? Well, Miss Clack, you will do. You are over twenty-one, and you have not the slightest pecuniary interest in Lady Verinder’s will.”

Not the slightest pecuniary interest. Oh, how thankful I was to hear it! If my aunt, possessed of thousands, had remembered poor me—if my name had appeared with a little legacy that would have made five pounds less of an object—my enemies might have doubted the motive that loaded me with my marked readings and drew upon my resources for the expense of a cab. Not the cruellest scoffer could doubt now. Much better so—surely, surely much better.

My meditative silence appeared to weigh on him. It forced him into talking, as if the quiet of the room, so unlike the street’s racket, made his worldly mind uneasy.

“Well, Miss Clack,” he said, “what’s the last news in the charitable circles? How is your friend Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite after the mauling he got from the rogues in Northumberland Street? Egad! they’re telling a pretty story about that charitable gentleman at my club!”

The manner in which he had spoken of my age and my lack of interest in the will I had passed over. But his tone about dear Mr. Godfrey was too much. After what had happened that afternoon, I felt bound to assert Mr. Ablewhite’s innocence whenever it was questioned—and, I own, felt equally bound to administer a sting to Mr. Bruff.

“I live very much out of the world,” I said; “and I don’t possess the advantage, sir, of belonging to a club. But I happen to know the story you allude to—and a viler falsehood was never told.”

“Yes, yes—you believe in your friend. Natural enough. Mr. Ablewhite won’t find the world in general quite so easy to convince as a committee of charitable ladies. Appearances are dead against him. He was in the house when the Diamond was lost. And he was the first person in the house to go to London afterwards. Ugly circumstances, ma’am—viewed by the light of later events.”

I ought to have stopped him there. I ought to have told him he spoke in ignorance of testimony offered by the only person competent to speak from positive knowledge. But the temptation to let him build his own downfall was too much. I asked what he meant by “later events,” with an air of innocence.

“Events in which the Indians are concerned,” he said, growing more confident the longer he talked. “What do they do the moment they’re let out of the prison at Frizinghall? They go straight to London and fix on Mr. Luker. What follows? Mr. Luker feels alarmed for the safety of ‘a valuable of great price’ he’s got in the house. He lodges it privately—under a general description—in his bankers’ strongroom. Clever of him; but the Indians are clever too. They suspect the ‘valuable’ is being shifted, and they hit on a bold way of clearing that suspicion up. Whom do they seize and search? Not Mr. Luker only—which would be intelligible—but Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite as well. Why? Mr. Ablewhite says they acted on blind suspicion after seeing him speak accidentally to Luker. Absurd. Half-a-dozen other people spoke to Luker that morning. Why weren’t they followed and trapped? No. The plain inference is that Mr. Ablewhite had his private interest in the ‘valuable’ as well as Luker, and that the Indians were uncertain which of the two had disposal of it—so they searched both. Public opinion says that. And public opinion, on this occasion, is not easily refuted.”

He ended with a look of such complacent wisdom that I could not resist drawing him a little farther before I struck.

“I don’t presume to argue with a clever lawyer like you,” I said. “But is it quite fair to Mr. Ablewhite to pass over the opinion of the famous London police officer who investigated this case? Not the shadow of a suspicion rested upon anybody but Miss Verinder, in the mind of Sergeant Cuff.”

“Do you mean to tell me you agree with the sergeant?”

“I judge nobody, sir, and I offer no opinion.”

“And I commit both those enormities,” he returned. “I judge the sergeant to have been utterly wrong; and I offer the opinion that if he had known Rachel’s character as I know it, he would have suspected everybody in the house but her. Secret, self-willed, odd, wild—unlike other girls. But true as steel, high-minded, generous to a fault. If the plainest evidence pointed one way, and nothing but Rachel’s word of honour pointed the other, I’d take her word before the evidence—lawyer as I am. Strong language, Miss Clack; but I mean it.”

“Would you object to illustrate your meaning, Mr. Bruff? Suppose you found Miss Verinder quite unaccountably interested in what has happened to Mr. Ablewhite and Mr. Luker? Suppose she asked the strangest questions about this dreadful scandal, and displayed the most ungovernable agitation when she found out the turn it was taking?”

“Suppose anything you please—it wouldn’t shake my belief in Rachel Verinder by a hair’s breadth.”

“She is so absolutely to be relied on as that?”

“So absolutely.”

“Then permit me to inform you,” I said, “that Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite was in this house not two hours since—and that his entire innocence of all concern in the disappearance of the Moonstone was proclaimed by Miss Verinder herself, in the strongest language I ever heard used by a young lady in my life.”

I enjoyed the triumph—an unholy triumph, I fear—of seeing him utterly confounded by a few plain words. He sprang up and stared, silent. I kept my seat and related the whole scene as it had occurred.

“And what do you say about Mr. Ablewhite now?” I asked, when I had done.

“If Rachel has testified to his innocence,” Mr. Bruff answered, “I believe in his innocence as firmly as you do. I’ve been misled by appearances like the rest of the world; and I’ll atone by publicly contradicting the scandal wherever I meet with it. In the meantime, allow me to congratulate you on the masterly manner in which you opened the full fire of your batteries on me at the moment I least expected it. You would have done great things in my profession, ma’am, if you had happened to be a man.”

He turned away and began walking irritably up and down the room. I could see the new light had not merely surprised him; it had disturbed him at the root. As he paced—boots tapping, then stopping, then tapping again in that restless rhythm of a mind at war—he let fall words that betrayed the abominable explanation he had previously accepted. He had suspected Mr. Godfrey of the infamy of stealing the Diamond, and had attributed Rachel’s conduct to a generous determination to conceal the crime. Now, on Rachel’s own authority—unassailable authority in his estimation—that whole view was shattered.

“What a case!” he muttered, stopping at the window and drumming on the glass. “It not only defies explanation—it’s even beyond conjecture.”

Nothing in those words required a reply—and yet I answered. It seems hardly credible that I could not leave him alone even then. But anything is credible when perversity gets the upper hand.

“Pardon me for intruding on your reflections,” I said. “But surely there is a conjecture to make which has not occurred to us yet.”

“Maybe. I own I don’t know what it is.”

“Before I was so fortunate as to convince you of Mr. Ablewhite’s innocence, you mentioned it as one reason for suspecting him—that he was in the house when the Diamond was lost. Permit me to remind you that Mr. Franklin Blake was also in the house when the Diamond was lost.”

He left the window, took a chair opposite mine, and looked at me steadily with a hard smile.

“You are not so good a lawyer, Miss Clack,” he said, “as I supposed. You don’t know how to let well alone.”

“I am afraid I fail to follow you,” I answered, modestly.

“It won’t do a second time. Franklin Blake is a prime favourite of mine, as you know. But never mind. I’ll adopt your view before you can turn on me. You’re quite right. I suspected Ablewhite on grounds which abstractedly justify suspecting Blake too. Very good—let’s suspect them together. It’s quite in his character, we will say, to be capable of stealing the Moonstone. The only question is whether it was his interest to do so.”

“Mr. Franklin Blake’s debts,” I remarked, “are matters of family notoriety.”

“And Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite’s debts haven’t arrived at that stage yet. True. But there are two difficulties in your theory. I manage Franklin Blake’s affairs, and the vast majority of his creditors—knowing his father to be rich—are content to charge interest and wait. That’s the first difficulty. The second is tougher still. I have it on Lady Verinder’s authority that Rachel was ready to marry Franklin Blake before that infernal Indian Diamond disappeared. She had drawn him on and put him off with the coquetry of a young girl; but she confessed to her mother that she loved him, and her mother trusted him with the secret. So there he was, with creditors willing to wait, and the certain prospect of marrying an heiress. Call him a scoundrel if you like—but tell me why he should steal the Moonstone.”

“The human heart is unsearchable,” I said gently. “Who is to fathom it?”

“In other words—though he hadn’t the shadow of a reason—he might have taken it through natural depravity. Very well. Say he did. Why the devil—”

“I beg your pardon, Mr. Bruff. If I hear the devil referred to in that manner, I must leave the room.”

“I beg your pardon—I’ll be more careful. All I mean is this: why—even supposing he took it—should Franklin Blake make himself the most prominent person in the house in trying to recover it? You’ll say he did that to divert suspicion. I answer he had no need—nobody suspected him. He first steals the Moonstone (without reason) through depravity; and then acts a part about the loss which is unnecessary, and which leads to his mortally offending the young lady who would otherwise have married him. That is what you are driven to assert if you associate the Diamond with Franklin Blake. No, no! After what has passed between us today, the dead-lock is complete. Rachel’s innocence is beyond doubt—as her mother knows, and as I know. Mr. Ablewhite’s innocence is equally certain—or Rachel would never have testified to it. And Franklin Blake’s innocence, as you have just seen, asserts itself. On one hand, we are morally certain of these things. On the other, we are equally sure somebody has brought the Moonstone to London, and that Mr. Luker, or his banker, is in private possession of it at this moment. What is the use of my experience—or anyone’s—in such a case? It baffles me; it baffles you; it baffles everybody.”

Not everybody, I thought. It had not baffled Sergeant Cuff. I was about to mention this—mildly, and with every protest against casting the smallest slur on Rachel—when the servant entered to say the doctor had gone and Lady Verinder was waiting to receive us.

That stopped the discussion. Mr. Bruff gathered his papers, looking a little worn by our talk. I lifted my carpet-bag of marked readings, feeling as if I could have gone on for hours. We proceeded in silence to my aunt’s room.

Before my narrative passes to what followed, I must explain why I have repeated my conversation with Mr. Bruff. I am ordered to include in my contribution to the shocking story of the Moonstone a plain disclosure—not only of the turn suspicion took, but of the names on which it rested at the time when the Indian Diamond was believed to be in London. A report of what passed in the library seemed exactly what was needed; and it has the moral advantage of requiring a sacrifice of sinful self-esteem. I have been obliged to confess that my fallen nature got the better of me. In making that humiliating confession, I get the better of my fallen nature. The moral balance is restored; the spiritual atmosphere feels clear once more. Dear friends—we may go on again.

The Will, which I had prepared myself to regard as a solemn banquet of last words and moral reflections, was dispatched like a plate whisked off a table before the blessing had been said. Mr. Bruff sent for Samuel to stand as second witness; ink was set out; the pen was put into my aunt’s hand; and the scratching of it on paper—thin as the rasp of a knife on a cheap dish—ended all, in less time than it takes a careful cook to taste a sauce.

I was moved—deeply moved—to offer a few suitable sentences on the occasion. But Mr. Bruff’s eye and manner were like the lid of a pot clapped down: they kept the steam of my feelings from rising. He folded the Will, and looked at me as if expecting me to withdraw and leave him alone with Lady Verinder.

My bag, with its precious stores, lay ready on my lap. My errand of mercy was not to be jostled out of the room by a lawyer’s glance. He might as well have tried to shove St. Paul’s Cathedral aside with his umbrella. To do him justice, he understood quickly. He muttered an expression which was not prayer, and retreated with the same sort of violence in which worldly men retreat from a truth they cannot bargain with.

As soon as we were alone, my poor aunt sank back upon the sofa, and the faintest colour rose in her face—as if the paper she had signed had cost her more blood than ink.

“I hope you won’t think yourself neglected, Drusilla,” she said. “I mean to give you your little legacy, my dear, with my own hand.”

Here was the very moment Providence bakes for us, like bread set to rise in secret warmth. I opened my bag at once, and drew forth the top volume—an early edition (only the twenty-fifth) of that searching book, believed to be by precious Miss Bellows, entitled *The Serpent at Home*. It lays the Evil One bare, not in far-off crimes, but in the homeliest daily habits; as if one were to find bitterness in the sugar-bowl, and corruption at the heart of a loaf.

The chapters most fitted for female meditation are, “Satan in the Hair Brush;” “Satan behind the Looking Glass;” “Satan under the Tea Table;” “Satan out of the Window”—and many more, setting snares in the very places where women most innocently move.

“Give your attention, dear aunt, to this precious book—and you will give me all I ask,” I said, and handed it to her open at a marked passage—one continued blaze of burning eloquence on the subject of Satan among the Sofa Cushions.

Lady Verinder—actually reclining, without thought, upon her own cushions—glanced at the page as one might glance at a strong spice when the palate is weak, and gave it back to me with confusion deepening.

“I’m afraid, Drusilla,” she said, “I must wait till I am a little better, before I can read that. The doctor—”

At the very word, I knew the flavour of what would follow: that cold, thin broth of unbelief which the medical profession ladles out for souls in danger, under the pretence that the body must not be disturbed. How often had these men—trained to measure pulse and temperature, and blind to everything that cannot be weighed—stood between me and the perishing, calling my books “exciting,” as if the prospect of salvation were a fever to be brought down!

“The doctor tells me,” my misguided relative went on, “that I am not so well today. He forbids me to see any strangers; and he orders me, if I read at all, only to read the lightest and the most amusing books. ‘Do nothing, Lady Verinder, to weary your head, or to quicken your pulse’—those were his last words, Drusilla, when he left me today.”

There was no use in openly setting my ministry above his. To do that would be to give him a handle; and such men are skilful at turning a patient’s weakness into a weapon. I yielded—for the moment only. There are many ways, as every prudent housekeeper knows, of introducing a wholesome ingredient into an unwilling mouth: if it will not go in as a draught, it may go in as a seasoning; if not at the table, then in the quiet hour when the appetite wakes of itself.

“You might feel stronger, dear, in an hour or two,” I said. “Or you might wake tomorrow morning with a sense of something wanting, and even this unpretending volume might be able to supply it. You will let me leave the book, aunt? The doctor can hardly object to that!”

Before she could refuse, I slipped it under the sofa cushions, half in and half out, close by her handkerchief and smelling-bottle. Each time her fingers went groping—each time her own small domestic necessities guided her hand—the book would be there. Sooner or later (who knows?) the book might touch her, as surely as yeast touches flour and makes it rise.

Having set my first seed, I judged it wise to withdraw.

“Let me leave you to repose, dear aunt; I will call again tomorrow.”

As I spoke, my eye fell towards the window. It was crowded with flowers—boxes and pots thick as a market-stall in summer. Lady Verinder loved these perishable treasures, and would rise, when she could, to smell them, as if scent could do what prayer had been forbidden to do.

A new thought—quick, practical, blessed—came to me.

“Oh! may I take a flower?” I said, and moved unsuspected to the window.

I did not take one away. I added one—another book from my bag—nestling it among geraniums and roses, where her hand, reaching for leaf and bloom, must find also the printed admonition. And as soon as I had done that, the thought followed, as naturally as a second course follows the first: Why not do the same in every room she enters? Why not season the whole house with truth, as an old countrywoman seasons a great pot—patiently, bit by bit, until the flavour is everywhere?

I said good-bye, and, crossing the hall, slipped into the library. Samuel came up to let me out; supposing I had already gone, he returned downstairs.

On the library table lay two of the “amusing books” recommended by that infidel doctor—light reading, meant to tickle rather than nourish. I covered them at once with two of my own publications, as one covers stale pastry with a clean cloth and sets wholesome bread in its place.

In the breakfast-room, my aunt’s favourite canary sang in his cage—thin, bright notes that made the air feel living. The poor little creature’s music was the sort that belongs to true domestic peace: not the coarse clang of street traffic, not the hammering and hooting of the modern city that drives thought into corners, but a small, clear sound like water dropping in a quiet garden. Lady Verinder always fed the bird herself. Groundsel lay strewn on a table beneath the cage. I put a book among the groundsel. When she came to feed the canary, the truth would lie there with the seed.

In the drawing-room, more opportunities waited. Her favourite musical pieces were on the piano; I slipped in two books among the sheets, so that any turning of pages would turn also to instruction. I placed another in the back drawing-room, beneath some unfinished embroidery that I knew to be hers—threads and patterns meant to carry on a tradition from hand to hand, like an old recipe passed down in a family’s own dialect, the words of it never quite translating into anything else.

A third little room opened from the back drawing-room, shut off by curtains instead of a door. On the chimney-piece lay my aunt’s plain old-fashioned fan.
