I opened my ninth book at a special passage and used the fan as a marker, keeping the place—so that when she lifted it, the words would lift with it.

Next came the question: should I go higher still, to the bedroom floor, at the risk of encountering the person with the cap-ribbons and receiving insult? But what is insult to one who has work to do? A poor Christian is the one who trembles at a raised voice.

I went upstairs.

All was quiet. Perhaps it was the servants’ tea-time; the house had that mid-afternoon hush, when cups clink faintly far away and footsteps are softened, and the whole place seems to listen to itself. My aunt’s room was in front. The miniature of my late dear uncle, Sir John, hung opposite the bed. It seemed to smile; it seemed to say, Deposit a book.

Tables stood on either side of the bed. Lady Verinder slept badly and wanted—or thought she wanted—many things at night. I put one book near the matches on one side, and one book under the box of chocolate drops on the other. Whether she reached for light, or reached for sweet comfort, there was a precious publication to meet her hand and say, without noise, without argument, Come—try me.

One book remained at the bottom of my bag. One room remained unexplored—the bath-room opening out of the bedroom. I peeped in; and the inward voice, which never deceives, whispered, You have met her everywhere else; meet her at the bath, and the work is done.

A dressing-gown lay across a chair. It had a pocket. Into that pocket I slipped my last book.

When I left the house, unsuspected by any of them, with my empty bag under my arm, my sense of duty done was exquisite. Worldly people chase Pleasure through the loud, jangling streets, through theatres and coffee-rooms and all those places where the air is thick with talk and the ear cannot rest; and they call themselves happy. How easy it is to be happy, if you will only be good.

That night, folding up my things and thinking of the true riches I had scattered from top to bottom of my wealthy aunt’s house, I felt as free from anxiety as if I had been a child again. I sang one verse of the Evening Hymn. I fell asleep before I could sing another.

Quite like a child again.

On rising next morning, how young I felt! I might add how young I looked, if I could dwell on the concerns of my own perishable body. But I cannot—and I add nothing.

Towards luncheon time—not for creature-comforts, but for the certainty of finding my dear aunt—I put on my bonnet to go to Montagu Square. As I was ready, the maid at my lodgings looked in.

“Lady Verinder’s servant, to see Miss Clack.”

I occupied the parlour-floor then: front parlour for my sitting-room—small, low-ceilinged, poor in furniture, but neat as a well-scoured pan. In the passage I saw Samuel, the young footman, civil and fresh-coloured, with a teachable look and an obliging manner. I had always felt a spiritual interest in Samuel, as one feels interest in a young kitchen-boy who might still be trained to take pride in honest work if only the right hand directs him.

I asked him in. He carried a large parcel under his arm. When he set it down, it seemed to alarm him.

“My lady’s love, Miss; and I was to say that you would find a letter inside.”

Having given the message, he looked as if he longed to run away. I detained him with a few inquiries.

Could I see my aunt if I called? No—she had gone out for a drive. Miss Rachel had gone with her. Mr. Ablewhite had taken a seat in the carriage too.

Knowing how sadly dear Mr. Godfrey’s charitable work was in arrear, I found it strange that he should be driving out like an idle man. I questioned Samuel again. Miss Rachel was going to a ball that night, and Mr. Ablewhite had arranged to come to coffee and go with her. There was a morning concert advertised for tomorrow, and Samuel was ordered to take places for a large party, including one for Mr. Ablewhite.

“All the tickets may be gone, Miss,” said the innocent youth, “if I don’t run and get them at once!”

He ran as he spoke—leaving me alone with thoughts that had no sweetness in them. That night we had a special meeting of the Mothers’-Small-Clothes-Conversion Society, summoned to obtain Mr. Godfrey’s advice and assistance. Instead of sustaining our sisterhood under an overwhelming flow of Trousers that quite prostrated our little community, he had arranged to take coffee in Montagu Square and go to a ball! The next afternoon had been chosen for the Festival of the British-Ladies’-Servants’-Sunday-Sweetheart-Supervision Society. Instead of appearing as the life and soul of that struggling institution, he was to be one of a party of worldlings at a morning concert!

What did it mean?

Alas! it meant our Christian Hero was about to reveal himself in a new character, and to become associated in my mind with one of the most awful backslidings of modern times.

But to return to the passing day. Left alone, I turned to the parcel which had intimidated Samuel. Had my aunt sent me my promised legacy, perhaps in cast-off clothes, worn-out silver spoons, unfashionable jewellery—any of those household remnants which the poor accept as they accept bones for broth, grateful for what there is?

I opened it—and what met my view?

The twelve precious publications I had scattered through the house, returned to me by the doctor’s orders.

Well might Samuel have shrunk from the parcel. Well might he have run.

My aunt’s letter was short, poor soul: she dared not disobey her medical man.

What was to be done now? With my training and principles, I had no more doubt than a good cook has about lighting the fire again when it has been damped. Once self-supported by conscience, once embarked on a manifest usefulness, the true Christian does not yield. Public or private influences do not move us once we have our mission. Taxation may follow; riots may follow; wars may follow; we go on.

We are above reason; we are beyond ridicule; we see with nobody’s eyes, hear with nobody’s ears, feel with nobody’s hearts but our own. Glorious privilege! And how is it earned? My friends, spare yourselves the inquiry. We are the only people who can earn it—for we are the only people who are always right.

In the case of my misguided aunt, the next form of perseverance revealed itself plainly. Preparation by clerical friends had failed, owing to her reluctance. Preparation by books had failed, owing to the doctor’s obstinacy. So be it.

What next?

Preparation by Little Notes.

If the books were driven out, their best passages could return disguised—copied out by different hands, addressed as letters to my aunt. Some sent by post; some distributed about the house on the same plan I had used before. As letters they would raise no suspicion; as letters they would be opened—and, once opened, might be read. Some I wrote myself:

“Dear aunt, may I ask your attention to a few lines—”  
“Dear aunt, I was reading last night, and I chanced on the following passage—”

Other letters were written by my valued fellow-workers of the sisterhood:

“Dear madam, pardon the interest taken in you by a true, though humble, friend—”  
“Dear madam, may a serious person surprise you by saying a few cheering words—”

Thus, under the respectable seal of correspondence, we reintroduced the precious words in a form not even the doctor’s watchful materialism would suspect. Before evening closed in, I had a dozen awakening letters instead of a dozen awakening books. Six I arranged to send by post at once; six I kept in my pocket for personal distribution next day.

Soon after two o’clock I was again in the field of pious conflict, making kind inquiries of Samuel at Lady Verinder’s door.

My aunt had had a bad night. She was again in the room where I had witnessed the Will, resting on the sofa, trying to sleep. I said I would wait in the library, on the chance of seeing her.

In the fervour of my zeal, it never occurred to me to inquire about Rachel. The house was quiet; it was past the hour at which the musical performance began. I assumed she and her pleasure-seeking party—Mr. Godfrey included—were all at the concert, and devoted myself to my work while time and opportunity were still mine.

My aunt’s correspondence of the morning—including the six awakening letters posted overnight—lay unopened on the library table. She had evidently not felt equal to grappling with so many; and if she entered later she might be daunted by the sight of the heap. I put one letter from my second set by itself on the chimney-piece, solitary, meant to tempt curiosity by its separation. A second I placed deliberately on the breakfast-room floor, so the first servant entering would think my aunt had dropped it and would be specially careful to restore it to her.

The basement floor thus sown, I ran upstairs to scatter my mercies next among the drawing-rooms.

As I entered the front room, a double knock sounded at the street-door—a soft, fluttering, considerate little knock, the sort that asks to be welcomed. Before I could slip back to the library where I was supposed to be waiting, Samuel was already in the hall answering.

It mattered little, I thought. In my aunt’s condition, visitors were not admitted.

To my horror and amazement, this visitor proved an exception. Samuel’s voice rose clearly after answering questions I did not hear:

“Upstairs, if you please, sir.”

The next moment I heard footsteps—a man’s footsteps—coming up toward the drawing-room floor. Who could this favoured male visitor be?

The answer sprang up: the doctor, of course.

In the case of any other person, I would have let myself be seen; there was nothing strange in my having tired of the library and come upstairs. But my self-respect would not permit me to meet the man who had insulted me by sending back my books. I slipped into the little third room beyond the back drawing-room and dropped the curtains that closed it off.

If I waited a minute or two, the usual thing would happen: the doctor would be conducted to his patient.

I waited. A minute. Two. More.

I heard the visitor walking restlessly to and fro. I heard him talking to himself. The voice stirred recognition in me like a familiar tune heard through a wall—heard, yet not quite grasped in the same shape, as if the very sound carried meanings that could not be translated into any other tongue of the heart.

Had I been mistaken? Was it not the doctor but someone else—Mr. Bruff, for instance?

No. An unerring instinct told me it was not Mr. Bruff.

Whoever he was, he still spoke to himself. I parted the heavy curtains the least morsel in the world and listened.

The words I heard were:

“I’ll do it today!”

And the voice that said them was Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite’s.

My hand fell from the curtain as if the cloth itself had scorched me. Yet it was not—pray understand it was not—the humiliation of my posture, crouched like a thief behind household drapery, that burned highest in my thoughts. It was the sentence I had heard, dropped by Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite with that dreadful steadiness which makes a resolution sound like a verdict:

He would do it—today.

Do what? I never once stopped to wonder why he was absent from the concert, though the violins below must at that moment have been drawing their bright, obedient threads through the air. I thought only of what new fall might be preparing for a man who had so lately stood—ah, how movingly he had stood!—like a tower among charitable causes, to be leaned on by Ladies’ Committees as wheat leans on a good season.

Would he turn renegade? Would he desert the Mothers’-Small-Clothes? Would the Sunday-Sweetheart-Supervision find itself suddenly without its favourite orator—without that angelic smile which seemed, like a well-glazed tart, to promise sweetness before one had even tasted? These were my terrors—pious terrors, if you please—when another voice cut in through the folds of my hiding-place.

A voice loud, bold, and lamentably deficient in every feminine modulation: Miss Rachel Verinder’s.

“Why have you come up here, Godfrey?” she said. “Why didn’t you go into the library?”

He answered with a low laugh—soft as the last fizz in a poured glass when the bubbles begin to die. “Miss Clack is in the library.”

“Clack in the library!” She sat down at once on the ottoman in the back drawing-room. “You are quite right, Godfrey. We had much better stop here.”

In the instant before, I had been fever-hot, uncertain what my next duty might be. I grew cold all over now, and certainty settled on me like a damp napkin laid to still an impatient dough. To show myself, after what I had already heard, was impossible. To retreat was equally impossible, unless I proposed to exit by the chimney. A martyrdom—plain, domestic martyrdom—was before me.

In justice to myself (for it is always in justice to myself that I must speak), I adjusted the curtains so that a slit of vision and a channel of sound remained. Then I met what was coming with the composure of a primitive Christian—one who is obliged, in suppressed hysterics, to hold on to something.

“Don’t sit on the ottoman,” Miss Rachel said. “Bring a chair, Godfrey. I like people to be opposite to me when I talk to them.”

He took the nearest seat, a low chair quite unequal to his tall frame. I had never seen his legs to such disadvantage; they looked, poor things, like long loaves forced into a shallow tin.

“Well?” she went on. “What did you say to them?”

“Just what you said, dear Rachel, to me.”

“That mamma was not at all well today? And that I didn’t quite like leaving her to go to the concert?”

“Those were the words. They were grieved to lose you at the concert, but they quite understood. All sent their love; and all expressed a cheering belief that Lady Verinder’s indisposition would soon pass away.”

“You don’t think it’s serious, do you, Godfrey?”

“Far from it! In a few days, I feel quite sure, all will be well again.”

“I think so, too,” she said. “I was a little frightened at first, but I think so too. It was very kind to go and make my excuses for me to people who are almost strangers to you. But why not have gone with them to the concert? It seems very hard that you should miss the music too.”

“Don’t say that, Rachel! If you only knew how much happier I am—here, with you!”

He clasped his hands and looked at her; and, in turning, he turned toward my curtain. My stomach sank in a clean, sick drop, as when a cook lifts a lid and finds the milk has boiled over—because there on his face was the very same pathetic expression with which he had charmed me while pleading at Exeter Hall. It was the expression that seems to say: I am suffering for the world; only, now, it was suffering for Rachel.

“It’s hard to get over one’s bad habits, Godfrey,” she answered. “But do try to get over the habit of paying compliments—do, to please me.”

“I never paid you a compliment, Rachel, in my life. Successful love may sometimes use the language of flattery, I admit. But hopeless love, dearest, always speaks the truth.”

He drew his chair close and took her hand when he said “hopeless love.” There was a silence—so still that I noticed, beyond the walls, the house’s own small music: a faint settling of boards, a far-off murmur of the concert like a sea in another weather, and—worse than either—the modern city’s distant grinding undertone, that eternal iron rasp of wheels and commerce. Even in a fine house, it seeps in; and once it does, it seems to make every tender word a little more desperate, as if affection must raise its voice to compete.

I understood then what his earlier words had meant. “I’ll do it today.” Alas, the most rigid propriety could scarcely fail to observe that he was doing it now.

“Have you forgotten what we agreed on, Godfrey, when you spoke to me in the country? We agreed that we were to be cousins, and nothing more.”

“I break the agreement, Rachel, every time I see you.”

“Then don’t see me.”

“Quite useless! I break the agreement every time I think of you. Oh, Rachel! how kindly you told me, only the other day, that my place in your estimation was a higher place than it had ever been yet! Am I mad to build the hopes I do on those dear words? Am I mad to dream of some future day when your heart may soften to me? Don’t tell me so, if I am! Leave me my delusion, dearest! I must have that to cherish, and to comfort me, if I have nothing else!”

His voice trembled. He put his white handkerchief to his eyes. Exeter Hall again! Nothing wanting but the cheers, the glass of water, and the practiced pause that invites applause. And yet—here there was only one listener; and I, behind my curtain, a second, hidden and unwilling.

Even Miss Rachel’s hard nature softened. She leaned a little nearer.

“Are you really sure, Godfrey, that you are so fond of me as that?”

“Sure! You know what I was, Rachel. Let me tell you what I am. I have lost every interest in life, but my interest in you. A transformation has come over me which I can’t account for, myself. Would you believe it? My charitable business is an unendurable nuisance to me; and when I see a Ladies’ Committee now, I wish myself at the uttermost ends of the earth!”

If apostasy ever had a taste, it was in that sentence: sharp as vinegar where one expects honey. I thought of the Mothers’-Small-Clothes—folded, counted, stored like provisions against moral famine. I thought of the Sunday-Sweetheart-Supervision and the other Societies too numerous to mention, all of them kneaded and risen on this man’s influence as bread rises on yeast. I thought of the poor struggling Female Boards who, figuratively speaking, drew the breath of their business-life through the nostrils of Mr. Godfrey—of that same Mr. Godfrey who now called our work a “nuisance,” and wished himself at the ends of the earth when he found himself in our company.

It tried my discipline before I could swallow my indignation dry and keep it down. Yet I did not lose a syllable.

“You have made your confession,” Miss Rachel said. “I wonder whether it would cure you of your unhappy attachment to me, if I made mine?”

He started. I started too. For one instant, both he and I thought we were about to hear the secret of the Moonstone.

“Would you think, to look at me,” she went on, “that I am the wretchedest girl living? It’s true, Godfrey. What greater wretchedness can there be than to live degraded in your own estimation? That is my life now.”

“My dear Rachel! it’s impossible you can have any reason to speak of yourself in that way!”

“How do you know I have no reason?”

“Can you ask me the question! I know it, because I know you. Your silence, dearest, has never lowered you in the estimation of your true friends. The disappearance of your precious birthday gift may seem strange; your unexplained connection with that event may seem stranger still—”

“Are you speaking of the Moonstone, Godfrey?”

“I certainly thought that you referred—”

“I referred to nothing of the sort. I can hear of the loss of the Moonstone, let who will speak of it, without feeling degraded in my own estimation. If the story of the Diamond ever comes to light, it will be known that I accepted a dreadful responsibility; it will be known that I involved myself in the keeping of a miserable secret—but it will be as clear as the sun at noon-day that I did nothing mean! You have misunderstood me, Godfrey. It’s my fault for not speaking more plainly. Cost me what it may, I will be plainer now.

“Suppose you were not in love with me? Suppose you were in love with some other woman?”

“Yes?”

“Suppose you discovered that woman to be utterly unworthy of you? Suppose you were quite convinced that it was a disgrace to you to waste another thought on her? Suppose the bare idea of ever marrying such a person made your face burn, only with thinking of it?”

“Yes?”

“And, suppose, in spite of all that—you couldn’t tear her from your heart? Suppose the feeling she had roused in you (in the time when you believed in her) was not a feeling to be hidden? Suppose the love this wretch had inspired in you—Oh, how can I find words to say it in! How can I make a man understand that a feeling which horrifies me at myself, can be a feeling that fascinates me at the same time? It’s the breath of my life, Godfrey, and it’s the poison that kills me—both in one!

“Go away! I must be out of my mind to talk as I am talking now. No! you mustn’t leave me—you mustn’t carry away a wrong impression. I must say what is to be said in my own defence. Mind this! He doesn’t know—he never will know, what I have told you. I will never see him—I don’t care what happens—I will never, never, never see him again! Don’t ask me his name! Don’t ask me any more!

“Let’s change the subject. Are you doctor enough, Godfrey, to tell me why I feel as if I was stifling for want of breath? Is there a form of hysterics that bursts into words instead of tears? I dare say! What does it matter? You will get over any trouble I have caused you, easily enough now. I have dropped to my right place in your estimation, haven’t I? Don’t notice me! Don’t pity me! For God’s sake, go away!”

She turned suddenly and beat her hands on the back of the ottoman—an ugly, desperate sound, like someone striking a loaf that refuses to rise. Her head dropped on the cushions; she burst into crying.

Before I had time to be properly shocked, Mr. Godfrey committed an act so entirely unforeseen that my very sense of propriety seemed to lose its language for naming it.

He fell on his knees at her feet—on both knees, I solemnly declare. Then he put his arms round her. And then—by two words only—he struck her as if with electricity.

“Noble creature!”

He said it with one of those practiced bursts that have made his fame. She sat still, either thunderstruck or fascinated—I do not know which—without even attempting to put his arms back where his arms ought to have been. As for me, I was so bewildered as to whether I ought first to close my eyes or stop my ears, that I did neither. I held the curtain, because in suppressed hysterics one must hold on to something.

“Yes,” he said, still in that evangelical cadence which turns common syllables into a kind of hymn, “you are a noble creature! A woman who can speak the truth, for the truth’s own sake—a woman who will sacrifice her pride, rather than sacrifice an honest man who loves her—is the most priceless of all treasures. When such a woman marries, if her husband only wins her esteem and regard, he wins enough to ennoble his whole life. You have spoken, dearest, of your place in my estimation. Judge what that place is—when I implore you on my knees, to let the cure of your poor wounded heart be my care. Rachel! will you honour me, will you bless me, by being my wife?”

By this time I should certainly have stopped my ears—if Miss Rachel had not answered him in the first sensible words I had ever heard from her.

“Godfrey,” she said, “you must be mad!”

“I never spoke more reasonably, dearest—in your interests, as well as in mine. Look for a moment to the future. Is your happiness to be sacrificed to a man who has never known how you feel towards him, and whom you are resolved never to see again? Is it not your duty to yourself to forget this ill-fated attachment? and is forgetfulness to be found in the life you are leading now? You have tried that life, and you are wearying of it already.

“Surround yourself with nobler interests than the wretched interests of the world. A heart that loves and honours you; a home whose peaceful claims and happy duties win gently on you day by day—try the consolation, Rachel, which is to be found there! I don’t ask for your love—I will be content with your affection and regard. Let the rest be left, confidently left, to your husband’s devotion, and to Time that heals even wounds as deep as yours.”

She began to yield—she, who had been raised (oh, what a bringing-up!) with so little understanding of the sacred rituals that keep a household’s soul in order: not merely how one speaks, but how one listens; not merely what one names, but what one dares not name; and how easily the dominant words of the world—wife, husband, duty—can be pressed like a seal over feelings that have no fitting language.

“Don’t tempt me, Godfrey,” she said. “I am wretched enough and reckless enough as it is. Don’t tempt me to be more wretched and more wreckless still!”

“One question, Rachel. Have you any personal objection to me?”

“I! I always liked you. After what you have just said to me, I should be insensible indeed if I didn’t respect and admire you as well.”

“Do you know many wives, my dear Rachel, who respect and admire their husbands? And yet they and their husbands get on very well. How many brides go to the altar with hearts that would bear inspection by the men who take them there? And yet it doesn’t end unhappily—somehow or other the nuptial establishment jogs on.

“The truth is, that women try marriage as a Refuge, far more numerously than they are willing to admit; and, what is more, they find that marriage has justified their confidence in it. Look at your own case once again. At your age, and with your attractions, is it possible for you to sentence yourself to a single life? Trust my knowledge of the world—nothing is less possible. It is merely a question of time. You may marry some other man, some years hence. Or you may marry the man, dearest, who is now at your feet, and who prizes your respect and admiration above the love of any other woman on the face of the earth.”

“Gently, Godfrey! you are putting something into my head which I never thought of before. You are tempting me with a new prospect, when all my other prospects are closed before me. I tell you again, I am miserable enough and desperate enough, if you say another word, to marry you on your own terms. Take the warning, and go!”

“I won’t even rise from my knees, till you have said yes!”

“If I say yes you will repent, and I shall repent, when it is too late!”

“We shall both bless the day, darling, when I pressed, and when you yielded.”

“Do you feel as confidently as you speak?”

“You shall judge for yourself. I speak from what I have seen in my own family. Tell me what you think of our household at Frizinghall. Do my father and mother live unhappily together?”

“Far from it—so far as I can see.”

“When my mother was a girl, Rachel (it is no secret in the family), she had loved as you love—she had given her heart to a man who was unworthy of her. She married my father, respecting him, admiring him, but nothing more. Your own eyes have seen the result. Is there no encouragement in it for you and for me?”

“You won’t hurry me, Godfrey?”

“My time shall be yours.”

“You won’t ask me for more than I can give?”

“My angel! I only ask you to give me yourself.”

“Take me!”

In those two words she accepted him.

He broke into a burst—an unholy rapture this time—drew her nearer and nearer until her face touched his; and then—No. I cannot prevail upon myself to write it plainly. Let me say only that I tried to close my eyes before it happened, and that I was one moment too late. I had calculated—fool that I was—on resistance. She submitted. To any right-feeling woman, that is enough said.

By then they understood each other so completely that I expected to see them walk off, arm in arm, to be married before the kettle could reboil. But Mr. Godfrey’s next words showed there remained one trifling formality.

He seated himself—unbidden this time—on the ottoman by her side.

“Shall I speak to your dear mother?” he asked. “Or will you?”

Miss Rachel refused both.

“Let my mother hear nothing from either of us, until she is better. I wish it to be kept a secret for the present, Godfrey. Go now, and come back this evening. We have been here alone together quite long enough.”

She rose, and in rising looked—at last—toward the little room where my martyrdom continued.

“Who has drawn those curtains?” she exclaimed. “The room is close enough, as it is, without keeping the air out of it in that way.”

She came to them. Her hand lifted—an inch from the fabric—and my discovery stood as certain as a spilled stain on a white cloth.

At that instant, the sound of hurried feet and a young man’s voice on the stairs tore through the house like a knife through paper. It was unmistakably the voice of alarm—raw, stripped of all servantly polish.

“Miss Rachel! where are you, Miss Rachel?”

She sprang back from the curtains and ran to the door. The fresh-coloured young footman blundered just inside; his ruddy complexion had drained away to a pale dough.

“Please to come downstairs, Miss! My lady has fainted, and we can’t bring her to again.”

In another moment I was alone—free to go down, and, by providence, still unobserved.

Mr. Godfrey passed me in the hall, hurrying out to fetch the doctor. “Go in, and help them!” he said, pointing.

I found Miss Rachel on her knees by the sofa, with Lady Verinder’s head on her bosom. One look at my aunt’s face—knowing what I knew—warned me of the dreadful truth; it was as if the usual living sounds around her had been stopped, and the room’s air had thickened into silence.

I kept my thoughts to myself until the doctor came. He arrived quickly enough, sent Miss Rachel out at once, and then told the rest of us—plainly—that Lady Verinder was no more.

Serious persons, in search of proofs of hardened scepticism, may be interested to hear that he showed no remorse whatever when he looked at me.

At a later hour I peeped into the breakfast-room and the library. My aunt had died without opening one of the letters I had addressed to her.

The shock of that discovery was so great that it never occurred to me—until some days afterwards—that she had also died without giving me my little legacy.

“Miss Clack presents her compliments to Mr. Franklin Blake; and, in forwarding him the fifth portion of her humble narrative, begs leave to confess herself unequal to enlarging—by any words of her own that are not poor and thin—on an event so dreadful, under such circumstances, as Lady Verinder’s death. The very name of it falls on the ear like a spoon dropped upon a stone floor in an otherwise orderly kitchen: a sharp sound that will not be forgotten, and that rings on after the hand has been stilled.

“She has therefore fastened to her manuscript certain copious Extracts from precious publications in her possession, all bearing upon this solemn subject; composed by persons whose language has the firmness of well-baked bread, and whose admonitions, if received, would be as wholesome as broth to the sick. And may those Extracts (Miss Clack fervently hopes) sound, not as the tinkling of vain society, but as the blast of a trumpet in the ears of her respected kinsman, Mr. Franklin Blake—cutting through the din and clamour of this modern world, where carts rattle, factories throb, and even grief is too often drowned beneath the coarse machinery of daily life.”

“Mr. Franklin Blake presents his compliments to Miss Clack, and begs to thank her for the fifth portion of her narrative. In returning the Extracts which accompanied it, he will refrain from specifying any personal objection which he may entertain to this species of literature, and will merely observe that the proposed additions are not necessary to the fulfilment of the purpose he has in view. The story required is the story witnessed; no more.”

“Miss Clack begs to acknowledge the return of her Extracts. She affectionately reminds Mr. Franklin Blake that she is a Christian, and that it is therefore quite impossible for him to offend her—no more than one may offend a patient old labourer by refusing him a second helping, when he has learned in the fields to endure hunger without complaint.

“Miss C. persists in feeling the deepest interest in Mr. Blake, and pledges herself, upon the first occasion when sickness may lay him low—when the house is hushed to the soft creak of boards and the careful pour of water into a glass—to offer him the use of her Extracts for the second time, as one offers a restorative cordial whose ingredients have been proved.

“In the meanwhile she would be glad to know, before beginning the final chapters of her narrative, whether she may be permitted to make her humble contribution complete by availing herself of the light which later discoveries have thrown on the mystery of the Moonstone; for there are truths which, like old family recipes spoken in a fading dialect, are lost if not set down while they can still be named.”

“Mr. Franklin Blake is sorry to disappoint Miss Clack. He can only repeat the instructions which he had the honour of giving her when she began her narrative. She is requested to limit herself to her own individual experience of persons and events, as recorded in her diary. Later discoveries she will be good enough to leave to the pens of those persons who can write in the capacity of actual witnesses.”

“Miss Clack is extremely sorry to trouble Mr. Franklin Blake with another letter. Her Extracts have been returned, and the expression of her matured views on the subject of the Moonstone has been forbidden. Miss Clack is painfully conscious that she ought (in the worldly phrase) to feel herself put down. But, no—Miss C. has learnt Perseverance in the School of Adversity, where hard fare teaches endurance, and where one learns, by the sound of one’s own footsteps in a narrow passage, that the loudest voices are not always the truest.

“Her object in writing is to know whether Mr. Blake (who prohibits everything else) prohibits the appearance of the present correspondence in Miss Clack’s narrative? Some explanation of the position in which Mr. Blake’s interference has placed her as an authoress seems due on the ground of common justice. And Miss Clack, on her side, is most anxious that her letters should be produced to speak for themselves—since words, once taken out of the mouth that shaped them, are so easily altered by the dominant tongues of the world, until the original flavour is gone.”

“Mr. Franklin Blake agrees to Miss Clack’s proposal, on the understanding that she will kindly consider this intimation of his consent as closing the correspondence between them.”

“Miss Clack feels it an act of Christian duty (before the correspondence closes) to inform Mr. Franklin Blake that his last letter—evidently intended to offend her—has not succeeded in accomplishing the object of the writer. She affectionately requests Mr. Blake to retire to the privacy of his own room, and to consider with himself whether the training which can thus elevate a poor weak woman above the reach of insult be not worthy of greater admiration than he is now disposed to feel for it; whether, in a time when every street is loud and every drawing-room louder, there is not something to be respected in the quiet discipline that keeps its own inward hearing clear.

“On being favoured with an intimation to that effect, Miss C. solemnly pledges herself to send back the complete series of her Extracts to Mr. Franklin Blake.”

To this letter no answer was received. Comment is needless.

(Signed) DRUSILLA CLACK.

The letters already placed on record will explain why I can do no more than announce—briefly, and without parade—that Lady Verinder died, and that the chapter of my narrative which ended with that fact must close over it like a stone. I am bound, from this point, to keep within my own knowledge and sight; and what I know next is this: that a whole month ran out after my aunt’s death before Rachel Verinder and I found ourselves again face to face.

That meeting brought me, for a few days, under the same roof with her; and during my stay there occurred a circumstance—touching her engagement to Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite—so necessary to the chain of events that I cannot pass it by.

Lady Verinder’s remains were removed from London and laid in the little cemetery by the church in her own park. I was invited, with the rest of the family, to attend the funeral. But it was impossible—after the shock—to rouse myself in a few short days; and I was told, besides, that the rector of Frizinghall was to read the service. I had myself once seen that clerical castaway seated at my aunt’s whist-table. Even had I been fit to travel, I question whether I should have felt justified in hearing him pronounce words of hope with a mouth that had so often been employed on other utterances.

The death left Rachel under the guardianship of her uncle by marriage, Mr. Ablewhite the elder, until she should marry or come of age. Under that new authority, Mr. Godfrey—no doubt—made known to his father the engagement which had been kept secret; and within ten days the secret was secret no longer among the family. The pressing problem with Mr. Ablewhite senior (another castaway, though he wore his coat with a prosperous smoothness) was how to make his power and his presence agreeable to the rich young niece who was to become his son’s wife.

Rachel, at the outset, resisted him—quietly, but resolutely—on the question of where she should live. Montagu Square was poisoned for her by her mother’s death. The Yorkshire house was haunted by the scandal of the lost Moonstone. Frizinghall itself might have been chosen, but her coming there, with grief on her like a black veil, would have chilled the entertainments of her cousins, the Miss Ablewhites; and Rachel, with that strange courtesy in her which could be firm without being loud, requested that her visit should be deferred.

So it ended—as such things do, in families where feelings are pushed from chair to chair like inconvenient furniture—in an aimless removal to Brighton. A furnished house was to be taken for Mrs. Ablewhite, an invalid daughter, and Rachel; and the guardian would join them later. Society would be shunned, in name; and Mr. Godfrey would be “at their disposal,” travelling backwards and forwards by the London train.

I mention this flitting from dwelling to dwelling—not for its own sake, but because it was the hinge on which my next knowledge turns. It was this Brighton house that brought Rachel and me together again.

My Aunt Ablewhite is a large, silent, fair woman, with one dominant trait: from her birth she has never been known to do anything for herself. She accepts help as naturally as she accepts air; she adopts opinions as she adopts cushions. If you placed the Grand Lama of Thibet at her sofa-side, she would listen to him as she listens to me, and would reflect his views with the same placid facility. With murderesses I have laboured and obtained results; with Aunt Ablewhite I have never advanced an inch—for there is nothing to seize upon, no resistance even, only a yielding that is not submission but vacancy.

She found the Brighton house as she found everything—by lying down. She stopped at a London hotel, composed herself upon a sofa, and sent for her son. The servants were discovered in like manner: she breakfasted in bed one morning, gave her maid a holiday, and told the girl she might “begin enjoying herself by fetching Miss Clack.”

I found my aunt at eleven o’clock, fanning herself in her dressing-gown amid the litter of a room that smelled of hotel linen aired too often and of over-sweet flowers standing in stale water. Outside, the street’s sound rose and fell—wheels, cries, the metallic clink of harness—over which the church-bells for a weekday service tried, with thin persistence, to lay their claim. The bells suggested an affectionate remonstrance.

“Oh, aunt!” I said sadly. “Is this worthy of a Christian Englishwoman? Is the passage from time to eternity to be made in this manner?”

“I’ll put on my gown, Drusilla,” she answered, “if you will be kind enough to help me.”

What could be said after that?

“Where is the list,” I asked, “of the servants whom you require?”

She shook her head; she had not even energy enough to keep the list. “Rachel has got it, dear,” she said, “in the next room.”

I went into the next room—and saw Rachel again for the first time since we had parted in Montagu Square.

She looked pitiably small and thin in the deep mourning that swallowed her like ink. Mourning-cloth has its own smell, close and harsh, as if it remembered the dye-vat and resented being brought into drawing-rooms; and on her it seemed not clothing but a weight. She rose when I entered, came forward, and held out her hand.

“I am glad to see you,” she said. “Drusilla, I have been in the habit of speaking very foolishly and very rudely to you, on former occasions. I beg your pardon. I hope you will forgive me.”

My face, I suppose, showed my astonishment. She coloured, then went on—steadier.

“In my poor mother’s lifetime, her friends were not always my friends, too. Now I have lost her, my heart turns for comfort to the people she liked. She liked you. Try to be friends with me, Drusilla, if you can.”

To any rightly constituted mind, the motive thus confessed was shocking. Comfort sought, not where it should be sought, but among human attachments; repentance offered, not from conviction and duty, but from sentiment and impulse! And yet—deplorable as it was—it suggested a point at which one might begin. There could be no harm, I thought, in ascertaining how far the loss had changed her; and I resolved to try her, carefully, upon the subject of her engagement to Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite.

I met her advance with cordiality, and at her own request sat by her on the sofa. We spoke of family affairs, of arrangements, of Brighton—always stopping short, by her tacit firmness, of that one arrangement which ended in marriage. I tried, by gentle approaches, to turn the talk toward it. She eluded me—not with rudeness, but with a patience that was almost more provoking. Any open reference at that early moment might have jarred upon our new truce; besides, I had learned enough. The reckless defiance I had witnessed in Montagu Square was gone. She sat quieter, spoke lower, looked sometimes as if she listened to something not in the room—an inward sea perhaps, louder than the outward one.

I felt my duty of interference revive in me with fervour. I believed in rapidity of proceeding. And as a practical beginning, I turned at once to the servants.

“Where is the list, dear?”

She gave it to me.

“Cook, kitchen-maid, housemaid, and footman,” I read. “My dear Rachel, these servants are only wanted for a term—the term during which your guardian has taken the house. We shall have difficulty in finding persons of character to accept so temporary an engagement, if we try in London. Has the house in Brighton been found yet?”

“Yes. Godfrey has taken it; and persons in the house wanted him to hire them as servants. He thought they would hardly do for us, and came back having settled nothing.”

“And you have no experience in these matters, Rachel?”

“None whatever.”

“And Aunt Ablewhite won’t exert herself?”

“No, poor dear. Don’t blame her, Drusilla. I think she is the only really happy woman I have ever met with.”

“There are degrees in happiness, darling. We must talk some day on that subject. In the meantime I will undertake the difficulty about the servants. Your aunt will write a letter to the people of the house—”

“She will sign a letter, if I write it for her, which comes to the same thing.”

“Quite the same thing. I shall get the letter, and I will go to Brighton tomorrow.”

“How extremely kind of you! We will join you as soon as you are ready for us. And you will stay, I hope, as my guest. Brighton is so lively; you are sure to enjoy it.”

Thus the invitation was given—and with it opened before me the prospect of useful interference.

It was the middle of the week. By Saturday afternoon the house was ready. In that short interval I had sifted—not the characters only, but the religious views as well—of all disengaged servants who applied, and had made a selection which my conscience approved. A kitchen is a pulpit of its own kind; if a cook’s tongue is loose with irreverence, it seasons every dish; if a housemaid’s ear is trained only to giggling gossip, she carries it like crumbs from room to room. I examined them as carefully as if I had been choosing yeast for bread: what rises unseen can sour the whole loaf.

I found, too, and called on two serious friends in the town, to whom I could confide the pious object that had brought me to Brighton. One—a clerical friend—secured sittings for us in the church where he ministered. The other, a single lady like myself, placed her library at my disposal—a store of precious publications. I borrowed half-a-dozen works, chosen with a direct view to Rachel; and when I had distributed them in the rooms she would most likely use—here on a table, there by a chair, there again where the hand must almost touch them in passing—I considered my preparations complete.

Sound doctrine in the servants who waited on her; sound doctrine in the minister who preached to her; sound doctrine in the books that lay in her way—such was the treble welcome my zeal had prepared for the motherless girl.

On that Saturday afternoon I sat at the window and waited. Brighton’s life moved below in a continual tide: voices, footsteps, laughter that snapped and broke like thin twigs; the distant, deeper wash of the sea; and always the undertone of wheels, the hard modern grinding that never seems to rest. The giddy throng passed and repassed. How many of them felt, as I did, the exquisite relief of duty done? An awful question. Let it rest.

Between six and seven the travellers arrived. To my indescribable surprise they were escorted, not by Mr. Godfrey, but by the lawyer, Mr. Bruff.

“How do you do, Miss Clack?” he said. “I mean to stay this time.”

His words recalled—plainly enough—the occasion at Montagu Square on which I had obliged him to postpone his business to mine. It was evident the old worldling had come with an object of his own. I had prepared a little Paradise for Rachel—and here was the Serpent already at the gate.

“Godfrey was very much vexed, Drusilla, not to be able to come with us,” said my Aunt Ablewhite. “There was something in the way which kept him in town. Mr. Bruff volunteered to take his place, and make a holiday of it till Monday morning. By-the-bye, Mr. Bruff, I’m ordered to take exercise, and I don’t like it. That”—she pointed to an invalid going by in a wheeled chair, drawn by a man—“is my idea of exercise. If it’s air you want, you get it in your chair. And if it’s fatigue you want, I am sure it’s fatigue enough to look at the man.”

Rachel stood apart at a window, her face turned toward the sea as if she were listening rather than looking. The sea has its own speech—heavy syllables of surf, long breaths of wind. Here, even through the house’s hush, you could hear it like a great creature shifting in sleep.

“Tired, love?” I asked.

“No. Only a little out of spirits,” she answered. “I have often seen the sea, on our Yorkshire coast, with that light on it. And I was thinking, Drusilla, of the days that can never come again.”

Mr. Bruff remained to dinner and stayed through the evening. The more I observed him, the more certain I became that he had come for a purpose—and that Rachel was concerned in it. He talked, hour after hour, with his air of ease; the same godless gossip, the same well-fed composure; and yet more than once I caught him letting his hard, cunning eyes rest on Rachel with a peculiar attention, as if measuring what he had done or meant to do.

When he left, he said nothing remarkable. He invited himself to luncheon the next day and went away to his hotel.

Sunday morning made it impossible to get Aunt Ablewhite out of her dressing-gown in time for church. Her invalid daughter—suffering, in my opinion, from nothing but laziness inherited and cultivated—announced she meant to remain in bed all day. Rachel and I went alone.

My clerical friend preached magnificently on the sinfulness of little sins and the heathen indifference of the world to them. For more than an hour his glorious voice thundered through the building. The congregation sat as if beaten upon by sound. When we came out, I asked Rachel:

“Has it found its way to your heart, dear?”

“No,” she answered. “It has only made my head ache.”

This might have discouraged some people. Nothing discourages me.

We found Aunt Ablewhite and Mr. Bruff at luncheon. Rachel declined eating anything and said she had a headache. Instantly the lawyer seized his opportunity.

“There is only one remedy for a headache,” he said. “A walk, Miss Rachel, is the thing. I am entirely at your service, if you will honour me by accepting my arm.”

“With the greatest pleasure. A walk is the very thing I was longing for.”

“It’s past two,” I suggested gently. “And the afternoon service begins at three.”

“How can you expect me to go to church again,” she asked petulantly, “with such a headache as mine?”

Mr. Bruff opened the door for her. In another minute they were both out of the house together.

I do not know when I have felt the solemn duty of interfering so strongly as I felt it at that moment. But what was to be done? Nothing—except to wait, and interfere at the first opportunity later.

On my return from the afternoon service, I found they had just come back. One look told me the lawyer had said what he had come to say. Rachel was silent and thoughtful—thoughtful with a weight I had not seen in her before. Mr. Bruff paid her devoted attention and looked at her with marked respect. He took an early leave, pleading a dinner engagement, intending to return to London by the first train next morning.

At the door he said to her, low but distinct:

“Are you sure of your own resolution?”

“Quite sure,” she answered.

The instant his back was turned, Rachel withdrew to her room. She did not appear at dinner. Her maid—the person with the cap-ribbons—came down to say her headache had returned. I ran up and offered all sorts of sisterly services through the door. It was locked; she kept it locked. Plenty of obstructive material here! I felt cheered and stimulated by that locked door.

When her tea went up next morning, I followed it. I sat by her bedside and spoke earnestly. She listened with languid civility, her attention fixed on folding and refolding the frilling of her nightgown. My friend’s precious publications lay huddled together on a table in the corner, like good counsel pushed out of the way.

Had she looked into them? Yes—and they had not interested her.

Would she allow me to read certain passages of the deepest interest, which might have escaped her? No, not now—she had other things to think of.

It was plainly necessary to touch her through worldly concerns.

“Do you know, love,” I said, “I had an odd fancy yesterday about Mr. Bruff? I thought, when I saw you after your walk, that he had been telling you some bad news.”

Her fingers dropped; her black eyes flashed at me.

“Quite the contrary!” she said. “It was news I was interested in hearing—and I am deeply indebted to Mr. Bruff for telling me of it.”

“Yes?” I said, gently.

Her fingers returned to the frilling; she turned her head away, sullenly.

I had met such resistance, in the course of plying the good work, hundreds of times; it merely urged me to try again. In my dauntless zeal, I ran the risk and alluded openly to her engagement.

“News you were interested in hearing?” I repeated. “I suppose, my dear Rachel, that must be news of Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite?”

She started up in bed and turned deadly pale. It was on the very edge of her tongue to answer me with the insolence of former days. She checked herself—lay back—considered—and then said, with a steadiness that startled me more than anger would have done:

“I shall never marry Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite.”

My turn to start.

“What can you possibly mean?” I cried. “The marriage is considered by the whole family as a settled thing!”

“Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite is expected here today,” she said doggedly. “Wait till he comes—and you will see.”

“But my dear Rachel—”

She rang the bell.

“Penelope! my bath.”

In the state of my feelings, I do sincerely believe she had hit on the only possible way of forcing me to leave.

A mere worldly mind would have found my position difficult. I had counted on warning her against a hasty marriage, and so leading her onward; and now, if she was to be believed, there was to be no marriage at all. But a working Christian of experience takes broader views. If she broke off an engagement on which the Ablewhites counted, it could only end—in the event of her firmness—in hard words and bitter accusations. And after the storm? A salutary moral depression; pride exhausted, stubbornness exhausted; and then she would turn for sympathy to the nearest person offering it. I was that nearest person—brimful of comfort, charged to overflowing with words in season. Never had the evangelising prospect looked brighter to my eyes.

She came down to breakfast, ate nothing, and spoke scarcely at all. After breakfast she wandered from room to room, like one who has mislaid something and cannot even name it. Then, suddenly, she opened the piano and began to play.

The music she chose was scandalously profane—stage-music that carries with it, like a smell of stale gas and paint, memories of places where applause drowns conscience and where art is bought and sold like trinkets. The very notes, flung out into the quiet house, seemed to quarrel with the sea’s grave cadence outside, as if modern noise had marched in to trample older harmonies. It would have been premature to interfere then. I privately ascertained the hour at which Mr. Godfrey was expected, and escaped the sound by leaving the house.

Being alone, I called on my two resident friends. It was an indescribable luxury to find myself again among serious persons, in rooms where the quiet was not empty but attentive—where the ear could rest without being assaulted by Brighton’s perpetual clatter, and where speech did not turn upon fashion and “liveliness” but upon realities. Encouraged and refreshed, I returned in excellent time to await the expected visitor.

I entered the dining-room—empty at that hour—and found myself face to face with Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite.

He did not attempt to avoid me. On the contrary, he advanced with eagerness.

“Dear Miss Clack, I have been only waiting to see you! Chance set me free of my London engagements sooner than I had expected, and I have got here earlier than my appointed time.”

Not the faintest embarrassment troubled him—though this was our first meeting since Montagu Square. He did not know I had witnessed that scene; but he must have known that my labours among the charitable societies, my intercourse with friends in other good works, must have informed me of his shameful neglect of his Ladies and his Poor. And yet there he stood, all charming voice and irresistible smile, as if such derelictions were but crumbs brushed from his coat.

“Have you seen Rachel yet?” I asked.

He sighed gently—and took me by the hand.

I should certainly have snatched my hand away, if his answer had not paralysed me with astonishment.

“I have seen Rachel,” he said, in perfect tranquillity. “You are aware, dear friend, that she was engaged to me? Well, she has taken a sudden resolution to break the engagement. Reflection has convinced her that she will best consult her welfare and mine by retracting a rash promise, and leaving me free to make some happier choice elsewhere. That is the only reason she will give, and the only answer she will make to every question that I can ask.”

“What have you done on your side?” I inquired. “Have you submitted?”

“Yes,” he said, with the most unruffled composure, “I have submitted.”

His conduct was so utterly inconceivable that I stood bewildered, my hand still in his. It is rude to stare; it is indelicate to stare at a gentleman. I committed both improprieties.

“What does it mean?” I said, as if in a dream.

“Permit me to tell you,” he replied. “And suppose we sit down?”

He led me to a chair. I have an indistinct remembrance that he was very affectionate. I do not think he put his arm round my waist to support me—but I am not sure. I was quite helpless; and his ways with ladies were very endearing. At any rate, we sat down. I can answer for that, if I can answer for nothing more.

“I have lost a beautiful girl, an excellent social position, and a handsome income,” Mr. Godfrey began; “and I have submitted to it without a struggle. What can be the motive for such extraordinary conduct as that? My precious friend, there is no motive.”

“No motive?” I repeated.

“Let me appeal, my dear Miss Clack, to your experience of children,” he went on. “A child pursues a certain course of conduct. You are greatly struck by it, and you attempt to get at the motive. The dear little thing is incapable of telling you its motive. You might as well ask the grass why it grows, or the birds why they sing. Well! in this matter, I am like the dear little thing—like the grass—like the birds. I don’t know why I made a proposal of marriage to Miss Verinder. I don’t know why I have shamefully neglected my dear Ladies. I don’t know why I have apostatised from the Mothers’ Small-Clothes. You say to the child, Why have you been naughty? And the little angel puts its finger into its mouth, and doesn’t know. My case exactly, Miss Clack! I couldn’t confess it to anybody else. I feel impelled to confess it to you!”

He poured it out with that easy abundance of words which is the dangerous gift of a fluent nature—words that can soothe like warm broth when they choose, and scald like poured sugar when vanity has been boiling underneath. I listened, as I always did, with my mind alert for the hidden seasoning: the pinch of truth, the excess of self-love, the accidental confession that slips out as naturally as steam from a kettle.

“Best of friends, exert your intellect, and help me,” he proceeded. “Tell me—why does a time come when these matrimonial proceedings of mine begin to look like something done in a dream? Why does it suddenly occur to me that my true happiness is in helping my dear Ladies, in going my modest round of useful work, in saying my few earnest words when called on by my Chairman? What do I want with a position? I have got a position! What do I want with an income? I can pay for my bread and cheese, and my nice little lodging, and my two coats a year. What do I want with Miss Verinder? She has told me with her own lips (this, dear lady, is between ourselves) that she loves another man, and that her only idea in marrying me is to try and put that other man out of her head. What a horrid union is this! Oh, dear me, what a horrid union is this! Such are my reflections, Miss Clack, on my way to Brighton. I approach Rachel with the feeling of a criminal who is going to receive his sentence. When I find that she has changed her mind too—when I hear her propose to break the engagement—I experience (there is no sort of doubt about it) a most overpowering sense of relief. A month ago I was pressing her rapturously to my bosom. An hour ago, the happiness of knowing that I shall never press her again, intoxicates me like strong liquor. The thing seems impossible—the thing can’t be. And yet there are the facts, as I had the honour of stating them when we first sat down together in these two chairs. I have lost a beautiful girl, an excellent social position, and a handsome income; and I have submitted to it without a struggle. Can you account for it, dear friend? It’s quite beyond me.”

As he finished, the house itself seemed to answer him with its own little noises—the domestic rustle of comfort that goes on, indifferent to a man’s moral convulsions. Somewhere below, a chair shifted. Somewhere outside, wheels ground faintly on the road. Brighton had its ceaseless seaside breath, and the rooms had that hushed, carpeted stillness which makes every human voice sound like an accusation.

His magnificent head sank on his breast, as if the weight of his own eloquence had bowed him down. He abandoned his riddle in despair.

To me the case was plain. I saw it as distinctly as I ever saw a recipe written in a clear hand: exalted ability humbled; the pride of public good works bruised into real humility; a salutary fall permitted by Providence, that the soul may remember it is not self-sustaining. The fine nature in him—so long overlaid by worldly glitter—was showing through again in his horror of a marriage built on another woman’s divided heart, and in his eagerness to return to the useful, plain, daily round among his Ladies and his Poor.

I put this view before him in a few simple and sisterly words. His joy was beautiful to see. He compared himself, as I went on, to a lost man emerging from the darkness into the light. When I answered for a loving reception of him at the Mothers’ Small-Clothes, the grateful heart of our Christian Hero overflowed. He pressed my hands alternately to his lips. Overwhelmed by the exquisite triumph of having got him back among us, I let him do what he liked with my hands. I closed my eyes. I felt my head, in an ecstasy of spiritual self-forgetfulness, sinking on his shoulder.

In a moment more I should certainly have swooned away in his arms, but for an interruption from the outer world which brought me to myself again—a harsh, vulgar, metallic clatter outside the door, as if the household had flung its knives and forks at my very nerves. The footman came in to lay the table for luncheon. The silver’s sharp ring on china, the scrape of plate, the brisk, professional breathing of a servant intent on order—those sounds, so innocent in themselves, fell on my ear like the jangling of a factory-bell in a place dedicated to prayer.

Mr. Godfrey started up, and looked at the clock.

“How time flies with you!” he exclaimed. “I shall barely catch the train.”

I ventured to ask why he was in such hurry to get back to town. His answer had the flavour of future trouble in it; I tasted family discord as surely as I tasted vinegar in an over-soured sauce.

“I have heard from my father,” he said. “Business obliges him to leave Frizinghall for London today, and he proposes coming on here, either this evening or tomorrow. I must tell him what has happened between Rachel and me. His heart is set on our marriage—there will be great difficulty, I fear, in reconciling him to the breaking-off of the engagement. I must stop him, for all our sakes, from coming here till he is reconciled. Best and dearest of friends, we shall meet again!”

With those words he hurried out. In equal haste I ran upstairs, to compose myself in my own room before meeting Aunt Ablewhite and Rachel at the luncheon-table—before I had to sit, like a watchful historian of human appetites, and observe how the mouth takes its food when the mind is starving.

I am well aware—to dwell for a moment yet on the subject of Mr. Godfrey—that the all-profaning opinion of the world has charged him with having his own private reasons for releasing Rachel from her engagement at the first opportunity she gave him. It has also reached my ears that his anxiety to recover his place in my estimation has been attributed, in certain quarters, to a mercenary eagerness to make his peace (through me) with a venerable committee-woman at the Mothers’ Small-Clothes, abundantly blessed with the goods of this world, and a beloved and intimate friend of my own. I notice these odious slanders only to declare that they never had a moment’s influence on my mind. I have exhibited the fluctuations in my opinion of our Christian Hero exactly as I find them recorded in my diary. In justice to myself, let me add that, once reinstated in his place in my estimation, my gifted friend never lost that place again. I write with the tears in my eyes, burning to say more. But no—I am cruelly limited to my actual experience of persons and things. In less than a month from the time of which I am now writing, events in the money-market (which diminished even my miserable little income) forced me into foreign exile, and left me with nothing but a loving remembrance of Mr. Godfrey which the slander of the world has assailed, and assailed in vain.

Let me dry my eyes, and return to my narrative.

I went downstairs to luncheon, naturally anxious to see how Rachel was affected by her release from her marriage engagement.

It appeared to me—but I own I am a poor authority in such matters—that the recovery of her freedom had set her thinking again of that other man whom she loved, and that she was furious with herself for not being able to control a revulsion of feeling of which she was secretly ashamed. Who was the man? I had my suspicions—but it was needless to waste time in idle speculation. When I had converted her, she would, as a matter of course, have no concealments from me. I should hear all about the man; I should hear all about the Moonstone. If I had had no higher object in stirring her up to a sense of spiritual things, the motive of relieving her mind of its guilty secrets would have been enough of itself to encourage me to go on.

The meal passed with that strained propriety which is sometimes more exhausting than open quarrel. The knives did their obedient work; the forks rose and fell like little shining machines; and, over all, the room held the seaside hush pierced by small sounds—glass touched by ring, spoon tapped on saucer—each trifling noise magnified by what could not be spoken. A house may be as well-managed as a kitchen, and yet, if the hearts in it are in disorder, every clink of the table becomes an omen.

Aunt Ablewhite took her exercise in the afternoon in an invalid chair. Rachel accompanied her.

“I wish I could drag the chair,” she broke out, recklessly. “I wish I could fatigue myself till I was ready to drop.”

She was in the same humour in the evening. I discovered in one of my friend’s precious publications—the Life, Letters, and Labours of Miss Jane Ann Stamper, forty-fourth edition—passages which bore with a marvellous appropriateness on Rachel’s present position. Upon my proposing to read them, she went to the piano. Conceive how little she must have known of serious people, if she supposed that my patience was to be exhausted in that way! The piano’s bright little tyranny of sound—the fashionable prattle of notes—was to her what a street-organ is to an earnest reader: a way of drowning meaning. I kept Miss Jane Ann Stamper by me, and waited for events with the most unfaltering trust in the future.

Old Mr. Ablewhite never made his appearance that night. But I knew the importance which his worldly greed attached to his son’s marriage with Miss Verinder—and I felt a positive conviction (do what Mr. Godfrey might to prevent it) that we should see him the next day. With his interference, the storm on which I had counted would certainly come, and the salutary exhaustion of Rachel’s resisting powers would as certainly follow. I am not ignorant that old Mr. Ablewhite has the reputation generally (especially among his inferiors) of being a remarkably good-natured man. According to my observation of him, he deserves his reputation as long as he has his own way, and not a moment longer.

The next day, exactly as I had foreseen, Aunt Ablewhite was as near to being astonished as her nature would permit, by the sudden appearance of her husband. He had barely been a minute in the house before he was followed, to my astonishment this time, by an unexpected complication in the shape of Mr. Bruff.

I never remember feeling the presence of the lawyer to be more unwelcome than I felt it at that moment. He looked ready for anything in the way of an obstructive proceeding—capable even of keeping the peace with Rachel for one of the combatants! He had the quiet, well-oiled manner of men who enter a room as if they had always belonged to it; and yet the very smoothness of him set my nerves on edge, like the perpetual hum of modern contrivances—respectable noise, authorized noise, that still disturbs the inward ear.

“This is a pleasant surprise, sir,” said Mr. Ablewhite, addressing himself with his deceptive cordiality to Mr. Bruff. “When I left your office yesterday, I didn’t expect to have the honour of seeing you at Brighton today.”

“I turned over our conversation in my mind, after you had gone,” replied Mr. Bruff. “And it occurred to me that I might perhaps be of some use on this occasion. I was just in time to catch the train, and I had no opportunity of discovering the carriage in which you were travelling.”

Having given that explanation, he seated himself by Rachel. I retired modestly to a corner—with Miss Jane Ann Stamper on my lap, in case of emergency. My aunt sat at the window, placidly fanning herself as usual. Mr. Ablewhite stood up in the middle of the room with his bald head much pinker than I had ever seen it yet, and addressed himself in the most affectionate manner to his niece.

“Rachel, my dear,” he said, “I have heard some very extraordinary news from Godfrey. And I am here to inquire about it. You have a sitting-room of your own in this house. Will you honour me by showing me the way to it?”

Rachel never moved. Whether she was determined to bring matters to a crisis, or whether she was prompted by some private sign from Mr. Bruff, is more than I can tell. She declined doing old Mr. Ablewhite the honour of conducting him into her sitting-room.

“Whatever you wish to say to me,” she answered, “can be said here—in the presence of my relatives, and in the presence” (she looked at Mr. Bruff) “of my mother’s trusted old friend.”

“Just as you please, my dear,” said the amiable Mr. Ablewhite. He took a chair. The rest of them looked at his face—as if they expected it, after seventy years of worldly training, to speak the truth. I looked at the top of his bald head; having noticed on other occasions that the temper which was really in him had a habit of registering itself there.

“Some weeks ago,” pursued the old gentleman, “my son informed me that Miss Verinder had done him the honour to engage herself to marry him. Is it possible, Rachel, that he can have misinterpreted—or presumed upon—what you really said to him?”

“Certainly not,” she replied. “I did engage myself to marry him.”

“Very frankly answered!” said Mr. Ablewhite. “And most satisfactory, my dear, so far. In respect to what happened some weeks since, Godfrey has made no mistake. The error is evidently in what he told me yesterday. I begin to see it now. You and he have had a lovers’ quarrel—and my foolish son has interpreted it seriously. Ah! I should have known better than that at his age.”

The fallen nature in Rachel—the mother Eve, so to speak—began to chafe at this.

“Pray let us understand each other, Mr. Ablewhite,” she said. “Nothing in the least like a quarrel took place yesterday between your son and me. If he told you that I proposed breaking off our marriage engagement, and that he agreed on his side—he told you the truth.”

The little thermometer at the top of Mr. Ablewhite’s head began to indicate a rise of temper. His face grew more amiable than ever; but the pink deepened, as if the heat inside him were trying to find an outlet where it could.

“Come, come, my dear!” he said, in his most soothing manner, “now don’t be angry, and don’t be hard on poor Godfrey! He has evidently said some unfortunate thing. He was always clumsy from a child—but he means well, Rachel, he means well!”

“Mr. Ablewhite, I have either expressed myself very badly, or you are purposely mistaking me. Once for all, it is a settled thing between your son and myself that we remain, for the rest of our lives, cousins and nothing more. Is that plain enough?”

The tone made it impossible, even for him, to pretend he misunderstood her. The pink went up another shade; and the voice that answered ceased to be the voice that belongs to a notoriously good-natured man.

“I am to understand, then,” he said, “that your marriage engagement is broken off?”

“You are to understand that, Mr. Ablewhite, if you please.”

“I am also to take it as a matter of fact that the proposal to withdraw from the engagement came, in the first instance, from you?”

“It came, in the first instance, from me. And it met, as I have told you, with your son’s consent and approval.”

The thermometer rose to the top of the register. The colour changed suddenly from pink to scarlet.

“My son is a mean-spirited hound!” cried this furious old worldling. “In justice to myself as his father—not in justice to him—I beg to ask you, Miss Verinder, what complaint you have to make of Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite?”

Here Mr. Bruff interfered for the first time.

“You are not bound to answer that question,” he said to Rachel.

Old Mr. Ablewhite fastened on him instantly.

“Don’t forget, sir,” he said, “that you are a self-invited guest here. Your interference would have come with a better grace if you had waited until it was asked for.”

Mr. Bruff took no notice. The smooth varnish on his wicked old face never cracked. Rachel thanked him for the advice he had given to her, and then turned to old Mr. Ablewhite—preserving her composure in a manner which (having regard to her age and her sex) was simply awful to see.

“Your son put the same question to me which you have just asked,” she said. “I had only one answer for him, and I have only one answer for you. I proposed that we should release each other, because reflection had convinced me that I should best consult his welfare and mine by retracting a rash promise, and leaving him free to make his choice elsewhere.”

“What has my son done?” persisted Mr. Ablewhite. “I have a right to know that. What has my son done?”

She persisted just as obstinately on her side.

“You have had the only explanation which I think it necessary to give to you, or to him,” she answered.

“In plain English, it’s your sovereign will and pleasure, Miss Verinder, to jilt my son?”

Rachel was silent for a moment. Sitting close behind her, I heard her sigh—an audible lowering of the spirit, like a flame starved for air. Mr. Bruff took her hand, and gave it a little squeeze. She recovered herself, and answered as boldly as ever.

“I have exposed myself to worse misconstruction than that,” she said. “And I have borne it patiently. The time has gone by, when you could mortify me by calling me a jilt.”

She spoke with a bitterness which satisfied me that the scandal of the Moonstone had been recalled to her mind in some way. The very name of that dreadful affair was like a foreign word that cannot be made innocent again once it has been shouted in a public place—its sound carries its stain, no matter how you try to translate it into something milder.

“I have no more to say,” she added wearily, not addressing the words to anyone in particular, and looking away from us all, out of the window nearest to her.

Mr. Ablewhite got upon his feet and pushed away his chair so violently that it toppled over on the floor. The crash was ugly in that quiet room—like the sudden brute note of machinery heard in a field.

“I have something more to say on my side,” he announced, bringing down the flat of his hand on the table with a bang. “I have to say that if my son doesn’t feel this insult, I do!”

Rachel started and looked at him in sudden surprise.

“Insult?” she repeated. “What do you mean?”

“Insult!” reiterated Mr. Ablewhite. “I know your motive, Miss Verinder, for breaking your promise to my son! I know it as certainly as if you had confessed it in so many words. Your cursed family pride is insulting Godfrey, as it insulted me when I married your aunt. Her family—her beggarly family—turned their backs on her for marrying an honest man, who had made his own place and won his own fortune. I had no ancestors. I wasn’t descended from a set of cut-throat scoundrels who lived by robbery and murder. I couldn’t point to the time when the Ablewhites hadn’t a shirt to their backs, and couldn’t sign their own names. Ha! ha! I wasn’t good enough for the Herncastles, when I married. And now it comes to the pinch, my son isn’t good enough for you. I suspected it, all along. You have got the Herncastle blood in you, my young lady! I suspected it all along.”

“A very unworthy suspicion,” remarked Mr. Bruff. “I am astonished that you have the courage to acknowledge it.”

Before Mr. Ablewhite could find words to answer in, Rachel spoke in a tone of the most exasperating contempt.

“Surely,” she said to the lawyer, “this is beneath notice. If he can think in that way, let us leave him to think as he pleases.”

From scarlet, Mr. Ablewhite was now becoming purple. He gasped for breath; he looked backwards and forwards from Rachel to Mr. Bruff in such a frenzy of rage with both of them that he didn’t know which to attack first. His wife, who had sat impenetrably fanning herself up to this time, began to be alarmed, and attempted, quite uselessly, to quiet him.

I had, throughout this distressing interview, felt more than one inward call to interfere with a few earnest words, and had controlled myself under a dread of the possible results—very unworthy of a Christian Englishwoman who looks, not to what is meanly prudent, but to what is morally right. At the point at which matters had now arrived, I rose superior to all considerations of mere expediency. If I had contemplated interposing any remonstrance of my own humble devising, I might possibly have still hesitated. But the domestic emergency which confronted me had been most marvellously and beautifully provided for in the Correspondence of Miss Jane Ann Stamper—Letter one thousand and one, on “Peace in Families.” I rose in my modest corner, and I opened my precious book.

“Dear Mr. Ablewhite,” I said, “one word!”

When I first attracted the attention of the company by rising, I could see that he was on the point of saying something rude to me. My sisterly form of address checked him. He stared at me in heathen astonishment.

“As an affectionate well-wisher and friend,” I proceeded, “and as one long accustomed to arouse, convince, prepare, enlighten, and fortify others, permit me to take the most pardonable of all liberties—the liberty of composing your mind.”

He began to recover himself; he was on the point of breaking out—he would have broken out with anybody else. But my voice (habitually gentle) possesses a high note or so in emergencies. In this emergency, I felt imperatively called upon to have the highest voice of the two.

I held up my precious book before him; I rapped the open page impressively with my forefinger.

“Not my words!” I exclaimed, in a burst of fervent interruption. “Oh, don’t suppose that I claim attention for my humble words! Manna in the wilderness, Mr. Ablewhite! Dew on the parched earth! Words of comfort, words of wisdom, words of love—the blessed, blessed, blessed words of Miss Jane Ann Stamper!”

I was stopped there by a momentary impediment of breath. Before I could recover myself, this monster in human form shouted out furiously:

“Miss Jane Ann Stamper be ——!”

It is impossible for me to write the awful word which is here represented by a blank. I shrieked as it passed his lips; I flew to my little bag on the side table; I shook out all my tracts; I seized the one particular tract on profane swearing, entitled Hush, for Heaven’s Sake!; I handed it to him with an expression of agonised entreaty. He tore it in two, and threw it back at me across the table. The rest of them rose in alarm, not knowing what might happen next. I instantly sat down again in my corner.
