There had once been an occasion, under somewhat similar circumstances, when Miss Jane Ann Stamper had been taken by the two shoulders and turned out of a room. I waited, inspired by her spirit, for a repetition of her martyrdom.

But no—it was not to be. His wife was the next person whom he addressed.

“Who—who—who,” he said, stammering with rage, “who asked this impudent fanatic into the house? Did you?”

Before Aunt Ablewhite could say a word, Rachel answered for her.

“Miss Clack is here,” she said, “as my guest.”

Those words had a singular effect on Mr. Ablewhite. They changed him from a man in a state of red-hot anger to a man in a state of icy-cold contempt. It was plain to everybody that Rachel had said something—short and plain as her answer had been—which gave him the upper hand of her at last.

“Oh?” he said. “Miss Clack is here as your guest—in my house?”

It was Rachel’s turn to lose her temper. Her colour rose, and her eyes brightened fiercely. She turned to the lawyer, and, pointing to Mr. Ablewhite, asked haughtily, “What does he mean?”

Mr. Bruff interfered for the third time.

“You appear to forget,” he said, addressing Mr. Ablewhite, “that you took this house as Miss Verinder’s guardian, for Miss Verinder’s use.”

“Not quite so fast,” interposed Mr. Ablewhite. “I have a last word to say, which I should have said some time since, if this——” He looked my way, pondering what abominable name he should call me—“if this Rampant Spinster had not interrupted us. I beg to inform you, sir, that, if my son is not good enough to be Miss Verinder’s husband, I cannot presume to consider his father good enough to be Miss Verinder’s guardian. Understand, if you please, that I refuse to accept the position which is offered to me by Lady Verinder’s will. In your legal phrase, I decline to act. This house has necessarily been hired in my name. I take the entire responsibility of it on my shoulders. It is my house. I can keep it, or let it, just as I please. I have no wish to hurry Miss Verinder. On the contrary, I beg her to remove her guest and her luggage, at her own entire convenience.”

He made a low bow, and walked out of the room.

That was Mr. Ablewhite’s revenge on Rachel, for refusing to marry his son.

The instant the door closed, Aunt Ablewhite exhibited a phenomenon which silenced us all. She became endowed with energy enough to cross the room.

Miss Clack has laid down her pen; and, in the order of these depositions, it falls to me to take it up.

There are two plain reasons why my hand is wanted next. First, because certain matters have been hinted at, and left to ferment in the reader’s mind without being properly brought to the light. Miss Verinder had her private cause for breaking her engagement—and I was the man who forced the truth to the surface. Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite had his private cause for withdrawing his claim—and I was the man who found the telltale seam in it.

Second, because (for good or ill, I am still uncertain which) my own life at this period touched the mystery of the Indian Diamond. I received, at my office, an Oriental stranger of distinguished manners, whom I take to have been—unquestionably—the chief of the three Indians; and I met, on the following day, the traveller Murthwaite, and spoke with him of the Moonstone in a way that bears upon later events. But these things are not yet. The first link in the chain—chronologically, and therefore narratively—is the broken engagement; and the first scene, oddly enough, opens not in a drawing-room, nor at a railway-station, but at the bedside of my late client and friend, Sir John Verinder.

There is a sort of hush that belongs to sickrooms of the old English kind: thick curtains that swallow street-noise; a coal-fire that breathes and settles; the faint, domestic ticking of a clock whose small industry seems indecently confident in the presence of mortality. In such a hush, you hear the very scratch of a pen as if it were a saw. Sir John, who had always been a kindly man with a stubborn distaste for troublesome duties, had put off making his will as he had put off other unpleasantnesses—until illness, like a bailiff, had taken him by the shoulder and said, “Now.”

Lady Verinder had pressed him; I had pressed him. He admitted we were right—then smiled, changed the subject, and escaped into health again. It was only when the sentence was pronounced in his body that the message came to me at last, and I went to take his instructions.

He was dozing when I entered. He roused at the sound of my step on the carpet, looked at me with the mild impatience of a man interrupted in comfort, and said:

“How do you do, Mr. Bruff? I sha’n’t be very long about this. And then I’ll go to sleep again.”

I took out my papers and pen with the practised economy of a man who has done the thing a hundred times and has no wish to do it a hundred and one. The inkstand gave off that cold metallic smell which, in certain houses, clings to the very idea of legal business—like sealing-wax and dust.

“Are you ready?” he asked.

I dipped my pen.

“I leave everything to my wife,” said Sir John. “That’s all.” And he rolled, with the satisfied air of a child who has said his lesson, and composed himself for sleep.

I was obliged, in my office as his solicitor, to be cruel.

“Am I to understand,” I said, “that you leave the whole of the property, of every sort and description of which you die possessed, absolutely to Lady Verinder?”

“Yes,” he answered, with a faint frown. “Only, I put it shorter. Why can’t you put it shorter, and let me go to sleep again? Everything to my wife. That’s my Will.”

In ten minutes, the simplest will I ever drew was written and executed. Sir John’s estate—land and money—was entirely within his disposal; and if, in other cases, such a sweeping gift might have made me uneasy, I knew Lady Verinder to be not only worthy of trust (as all good wives are), but capable of administering it—a rarer gift, in my experience, than people like to admit. When I left, the old gentleman was finishing his interrupted nap, and the fire made that soft collapsing sound as a coal gives in.

Lady Verinder justified her husband’s confidence without delay. In the first days of widowhood—those days when the very house seems to mourn with a peculiar quietness, and the simplest domestic actions sound louder than they ought—she sent for me and made her own will. I had not to coax her into prudence. Her mind took the measure of her position with a clearness that was almost bracing. My responsibility was merely to give legal body to her instructions. Before Sir John had been a fortnight in his grave, the future of their daughter had been provided for with wisdom and tenderness.

That will lay for years in its fireproof box at my office—years that, for my own comfort, I do not count too closely. It was not until the summer of 1848 that I had occasion to look at it again, and then under circumstances which no lawyer ever welcomes—circumstances in which parchment and ink are forced to stand as the last servants of a life that is slipping away.

At the date I name, the doctors pronounced upon Lady Verinder what was, in plain terms, a sentence of death. She told me first; and she asked to go over her will with me again.

Rachel’s provisions could not be improved: there was nothing to add, nothing to take away. But some minor legacies—those small bequests by which family feeling expresses itself in shillings and guineas—had altered with time; and three or four codicils were needed. After securing her wishes immediately, for fear of accident, I asked permission to embody the new instructions in a second will, clean and coherent, so that repetitions and confusions should not disfigure the instrument. I own, frankly, that such disfigurements offend my professional sense as sour wine offends a careful palate.

The execution of this second will Miss Clack has already described, she having been obliging enough to witness it. As far as Rachel’s pecuniary interest went, the second will was word for word the counterpart of the first. The only changes related to the appointment of a guardian and certain provisions attendant on that appointment, made under my advice. After Lady Verinder’s death the will went, in the usual way, into the hands of my proctor, to be proved.

In about three weeks—so far as my memory serves—the first faint sound reached me of something moving beneath the surface: the sort of sound you catch, at night, in a quiet country house, when a shutter has come loose and is tapping softly, softly, as if trying not to be heard.

I happened to be looking in at my proctor’s office, and saw, at once, by his manner that he had something more than routine to tell.

“I have some news for you,” he said. “What do you think I heard at Doctors’ Commons this morning? Lady Verinder’s will has been asked for, and examined, already!”

The words fell oddly in my ear. There was nothing contestable in the will, and no person, to my knowledge, with the slightest interest in examining it. (For the benefit of those who do not know the practice: any will may be examined at Doctors’ Commons by anyone who applies, on payment of a shilling.)

“Did you hear who asked for it?” I said.

“Yes. The clerk told me readily enough. Mr. Smalley—of Skipp and Smalley. The will has not been copied into the Folio Registers yet; so they had to let him see the original. He went over it carefully, and made a note in his pocket-book. Have you any idea what he wanted with it?”

“None,” I answered. “But I shall find out before I am a day older.”

I returned at once to my office. If another firm had been concerned, discovery might have cost me more trouble. But I possessed an easy handle in this case: my common-law clerk—an excellent man—was Mr. Smalley’s brother. Moreover, through this indirect connection, Skipp and Smalley had, for years, enjoyed something of my professional patronage: cases that fell, like crumbs, from my table when I declined them. It is surprising how clearly a man hears his duty when his bread is in another’s hand. I intended, if necessary, to remind them of that.

I spoke to my clerk immediately, told him what had happened, and sent him to his brother’s office with my compliments and a plain question: why had Messrs. Skipp and Smalley found it necessary to examine Lady Verinder’s will?

The message brought Mr. Smalley back to me, accompanied by his brother. He admitted, at once, that he had acted under instructions received from a client. Then he asked me whether it would not be a breach of professional confidence to say more.

We had a smart discussion. He was right; I was wrong. I was angry and suspicious; and I insisted. Worse than that, I refused to receive any further information as a secret in my keeping. I claimed perfect freedom to use my discretion. Worst of all, I took an advantage of my position.

“Choose, sir,” I said, “between the risk of losing your client’s business and the risk of losing mine.”

It was indefensible—a tyranny, pure and simple. Like other tyrants, I carried my point. Mr. Smalley smiled with resigned politeness and gave me the name.

Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite.

That name was enough. I wanted to know no more.

And now, so far as Lady Verinder’s will concerns this matter, the reader must stand, from this point, on the same level of knowledge as myself.

Rachel Verinder had nothing but a life-interest in the property. Her mother’s excellent sense, joined to my experience, had guarded her against the danger—always possible to a young woman of fortune—of being made the victim of a needy and unscrupulous man. Neither Rachel, nor any husband she might take, could raise a sixpence on the land or on the money. They would have the London house and the Yorkshire house to live in; they would have the handsome income—and that was all.

When I considered what I had discovered, I was perplexed as to my next step.

Hardly a week had passed since I had heard, with surprise and distress, of Miss Verinder’s intended marriage. I admired her sincerely, and was attached to her as I had been attached to her mother. I had been inexpressibly grieved to hear she was about to throw herself away on Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite. And now the man—whom I had always believed to be a smooth-tongued impostor—plainly revealed the mercenary object on his side. Many marriages are made on motives no better, I know. But if the girl were your sister, you would not shrug your shoulders quite so easily.

One practical question presented itself at once. Would Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite hold to his engagement after his solicitor had discovered the truth?

That depended on his pecuniary position, of which I knew nothing. If he was not desperately pressed, Rachel’s income alone might be worth the bargain to him. But if he needed a large sum, and needed it by a fixed time, then Lady Verinder’s will met the case precisely and saved Rachel—quietly, almost mechanically—from falling into a scoundrel’s hands.

In the latter event, there would be no need for me to distress Miss Rachel in the first days of mourning by an immediate disclosure. In the former, if I kept silent, I should be conniving at a marriage that would make her miserable for life.

My doubts ended in action. I called at the London hotel where Mrs. Ablewhite and Miss Verinder were staying. They told me they were going to Brighton the next day, and that an unexpected obstacle prevented Mr. Godfrey from accompanying them. I proposed, on the spot, to take his place. While I reasoned at a distance, I could hesitate. When I saw Rachel, the hesitation ended. Whatever the cost, I would tell her the truth.

I found my opportunity the day after my arrival at Brighton, when we were out walking together.

“May I speak to you,” I asked, “about your marriage engagement?”

“Yes,” she said indifferently, “if you have nothing more interesting to talk about.”

“Will you forgive an old friend and servant of your family, Miss Rachel, if I venture to ask whether your heart is set on this marriage?”

“I am marrying in despair, Mr. Bruff—on the chance of dropping into some sort of stagnant happiness which may reconcile me to my life.”

The words were startling; they suggested a story below the surface—some secret romance, some wound not named. But my purpose was limited, and I declined (as we say in my profession) to pursue the question into its side issues.

“Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite can hardly be of your way of thinking,” I said. “His heart must be set on the marriage, at any rate?”

“He says so, and I suppose I ought to believe him. He would hardly marry me, after what I have owned to him, unless he was fond of me.”

It struck me then, sharply, how little the most obvious danger presents itself to a generous mind. The notion of a man marrying her for selfish ends had never entered her head. The task I had taken on began to look harder.

“It sounds strangely,” I went on, “in my old-fashioned ears—”

“What sounds strangely?” she asked.

“To hear you speak of your future husband as if you were not quite sure of the sincerity of his attachment. Are you conscious of any reason for doubting him?”

Her quickness of perception—quick as a hand to a hot dish before the mind has named the heat—caught something in my voice, or my manner. She stopped, withdrew her arm from mine, and looked at me straight.

“Mr. Bruff,” she said, “you have something to tell me about Godfrey Ablewhite. Tell it.”

I knew her well enough to obey her. I told her what I had discovered: that his solicitor had examined Lady Verinder’s will; what that implied; and what the will, in fact, allowed and forbade.

She put her arm into mine again and walked on slowly. Her hand tightened, not consciously, but as if her nerves had taken hold of my sleeve. Her face grew paler as I spoke. She did not interrupt me once. When I had finished, she still said nothing. Her head drooped a little; she walked on beside me as if she had forgotten where she was, or that I existed—lost in her own thoughts.

I did not disturb her. I have learned, through long observation, that Rachel Verinder’s nature did not run to questions and confidences as other girls’ natures do. Many young women, told of anything that moves them, question you at once, then fly to a friend to talk it over. Rachel’s instinct was to shut herself up within her own mind and think. Such self-dependence is praised as a virtue in men. In women it has the drawback of morally separating them from their sex, and so exposing them to misconstruction. I dare say I share the world’s prejudice in that matter—except where Rachel is concerned. In her, that solitary strength had always seemed to me one of the virtues of her character; and it had bearing, too, on my view of her connexion—whatever it might be—with the loss of the Moonstone. Bad as appearances looked; shocking as it was to find her associated in any way with an undiscovered theft; I was still satisfied that she had done nothing unworthy of herself, because I was satisfied she did nothing—good or ill—without that inward retirement and consideration first.

We had walked, I should say, nearly a mile before she roused. She looked up at me, and on her face there came a faint reflection of the smile I had seen there in happier times—the most irresistible smile I have ever known in a woman.

“I owe much already to your kindness,” she said. “And I feel more deeply indebted to it now than ever. If you hear any rumours of my marriage when you get back to London, contradict them at once, on my authority.”

“Have you resolved to break your engagement?” I asked.

“Can you doubt it?” she returned, with a proud quietness, “after what you have told me!”

“My dear Miss Rachel,” I said, “you are very young—and you may find more difficulty in withdrawing than you anticipate. Have you no one—a lady, of course—whom you can consult?”

“No one,” she answered.

It distressed me to hear it. She was young; she was lonely; and she bore it as if loneliness were merely another piece of furniture in her room, to be dusted and endured. The impulse to help her overcame any sense I might have had of my own unfitness, and I offered, as well as I could, such advice as occurred to me on the moment. I have advised countless clients through awkward troubles, but I had never before advised a young lady how to extricate herself from a marriage engagement.

In brief, I recommended this: that she should tell Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite, at a private interview, that he had betrayed to her certain knowledge the mercenary motive on his side; that marriage after that discovery was impossible; and that he must choose—either to fall in with her wish and secure her silence, or to force her, by opposition, to let the world know why she withdrew. If he denied or defended himself, she was to refer him to me.

She listened attentively, thanked me with that graceful courtesy which sat so naturally upon her, and then said quietly that she could not follow my advice.

“May I ask,” I said, “what objection you see to it?”

She hesitated; then met me with a question.

“Suppose you were asked to express your opinion of Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite’s conduct,” she began.

“Yes?”

“What would you call it?”

“I should call it the conduct of a meanly deceitful man.”

“Mr. Bruff! I have believed in that man. I have promised to marry that man. How can I tell him he is mean, how can I tell him he has deceived me, how can I disgrace him in the eyes of the world after that? I have degraded myself by ever thinking of him as my husband. If I say what you tell me to say to him—I am owning that I have degraded myself to his face. I can’t do that. After what has passed between us, I can’t do that! The shame of it would be nothing to him. But the shame of it would be unendurable to me.”

There, without reserve, was one of the peculiarities of her nature: a sensitive horror of contact with anything base, so intense that it blinded her to what she owed to herself and placed her, by her own nobleness, in danger of a false position. Until then I had been a little uncertain whether my advice had been proper. After those words I felt no doubt it was the best that could be given, and I pressed it again.

She shook her head.

“He has been intimate enough with me to ask me to be his wife. He has stood high enough in my estimation to obtain my consent. I can’t tell him to his face that he is the most contemptible of living creatures, after that!”

“But, my dear Miss Rachel,” I remonstrated, “it’s equally impossible for you to withdraw without giving some reason.”

“I shall say that I have thought it over, and that I am satisfied it will be best for both of us if we part.”

“No more than that?”

“No more.”

“Have you thought what he may say?”

“He may say what he pleases.”

I could not but admire her delicacy and her resolution; and I could not but see she was putting herself in the wrong. I entreated her to consider her position. I reminded her she would expose herself to odious misconstruction.

“You can’t brave public opinion,” I said, “at the command of private feeling.”

“I can,” she answered. “I have done it already.”

“What do you mean?”

“You have forgotten the Moonstone, Mr. Bruff. Have I not braved public opinion there, with my own private reasons for it?”

That answer silenced me. It set me trying—vainly—to trace, out of that strange avowal, the explanation of her conduct at the time of the Diamond’s disappearance. Perhaps I might have managed it when I was younger. I could not manage it now.

I made a last remonstrance before we returned to the house. She was immovable. When I left her that day my feelings were in conflict. She was obstinate; she was wrong. She was interesting; she was admirable; she was deeply to be pitied. I made her promise to write to me the moment she had any news, and I returned to my business in London with a mind ill at ease.

On the evening of my return—before her promised letter could possibly reach me—I was surprised by a visit from Mr. Ablewhite the elder. He informed me that Mr. Godfrey had received his dismissal—and had accepted it—that very day.

With the view I already took, the fact stated in those words made the motive plain. He needed a large sum of money, and needed it by a given time. Rachel’s income might have helped him to live; it could not help him to raise capital. Lady Verinder’s will had saved Rachel from him by the mere legal impossibility of his profiting as he intended; and Rachel had released herself without encountering serious opposition.

Any exultation I might have felt at this lucky turn was checked by what passed at my interview with old Mr. Ablewhite.

He came, of course, to ask whether I could explain Miss Verinder’s extraordinary conduct. I could not. The annoyance of my refusal, added to irritation produced by some recent interview with his son, threw him off his guard. His looks and language convinced me that Miss Verinder would find him a merciless man to deal with when he joined the ladies at Brighton the next day.

The next duty before me is to set down what little I know—additional, but not unimportant—touching the Moonstone; or, to speak with greater accuracy, touching the Indian design for recovering it. The thing itself, like a spice that seems insignificant between the fingers and yet dominates the whole dish, was small in appearance and strong in consequence; and it bore, as I shall hereafter have cause to show, very remarkably on events still to come.

About a week or ten days after Miss Verinder had left us, one of my clerks came into my private room at the office with a card upon his open palm, as if he were offering some delicate morsel which it would be a pity to drop.

“A gentleman is below, sir. He asks to see you.”

I took the card. A foreign name was written on it—one of those combinations of letters which the eye tastes and then forgets. But the line beneath, in plain English, struck like the crack of a spoon on a copper pan:

“Recommended by Mr. Septimus Luker.”

The audacity of a person in Mr. Luker’s position presuming to recommend anybody to me so discomposed my mind that, for an instant, I could do nothing but stare at the words, as though they were a misprint.

My clerk, observing my silence, ventured his own relish of the matter.

“He is rather a remarkable-looking man, sir—so dark that we have all taken him for an Indian, or something of that sort.”

There are times when curiosity is as sudden and irresistible as a scent from a kitchen door in a cold street. The clerk’s impression, combined with that line from Luker, brought the Moonstone instantly into my thoughts. I decided—very much to the young man’s surprise—to see the stranger at once.

If this appears unprofessional, let anyone who reads me remember that no living man in England—at any rate no living man outside of India—can claim to have been so intimately mixed up with the romance of that Diamond as I have been. I was trusted with the secret of Colonel Herncastle’s plan for escaping assassination. I received his letters, written from time to time to report himself still alive. I drew his Will leaving the Moonstone to Miss Verinder. I persuaded his executor to act upon that Will, on the chance that the jewel might prove a valuable acquisition to the family. And lastly, I combated Mr. Franklin Blake’s scruples and induced him to be the means of conveying the Diamond to Lady Verinder’s house. If there is any prescriptive right of interest in the Moonstone, and in everything connected with it, I may surely claim it.

The moment my mysterious client was shown in, I felt an inward conviction that I stood in the presence of one of the three Indians—probably their chief.

He was dressed with care in European clothes; the cut and the cloth were as correct as any tailor in the Strand could desire. But the deep swarthiness of his skin, his long, supple figure, and a gravity of carriage—polite, composed, and strangely elegant—betrayed the Oriental origin of him to any intelligent eye. There was, too, something in his stillness, like a man who keeps his own counsel the way a good cook keeps a fire: steady, unseen, and always ready.

I pointed to a chair and requested him to state his business.

He began by apologising—in excellent English, chosen with that exactness which makes the language itself feel like borrowed property—for the liberty of troubling me. Then, without flourish, he produced a small parcel. The outer covering was cloth of gold. He removed it; beneath was a second wrapping of silk. From these he took a little box and placed it on my table.

The casket was beautiful—ebony for the ground, and jewels set into it with a richness that had the look of something consecrated rather than merely costly. It lay there like a relic, and yet he handled it as a man handles his own knife—confidently, without reverence, as if it served a purpose.

“I have come, sir,” he said, “to ask you to lend me some money. And I leave this as an assurance to you that my debt will be paid back.”

I touched the card with my finger.

“And you apply to me,” I said, “at Mr. Luker’s recommendation?”

He bowed.

“May I ask,” I continued, “how it is that Mr. Luker himself did not advance the money you require?”

“Mr. Luker informed me, sir, that he had no money to lend.”

“And so he recommended you to come to me?”

The Indian, in his turn, indicated the card.

“It is written there,” he said.

Brief; exact; and perfectly to the purpose. If the Moonstone had been in my keeping, I have no doubt this man would have murdered me without a moment’s hesitation. Short of that, I am bound to record that he was the model of a client. He might not have respected my life; but he did what none of my own countrymen ever do, in all my experience—he respected my time.

“I am sorry,” I said, “that you should have had the trouble of coming. Mr. Luker is mistaken in sending you here. I am entrusted, like other men of my profession, with money to lend. But I never lend it to strangers, and I never lend it upon such a security as you have produced.”

Most men, when refused, stir the air with argument, like a bad cook whisking when he should let the sauce settle. This man did nothing of the sort. He made me another bow, wrapped the casket again in its silk and its cloth of gold with hands that wasted no movement, and rose at once to go.

“Will your condescension towards a stranger excuse my asking one question,” he said, “before I take my leave?”

I bowed in return. Only one question at parting! The average, in my experience, was fifty.

“Supposing, sir, it had been possible (and customary) for you to lend me the money,” he proceeded, “in what space of time would it have been possible (and customary) for me to pay it back?”

“According to the usual course pursued in this country,” I answered, “you would have been entitled to pay the money back, if you liked, in one year from the date at which it was first advanced.”

He made a last bow—lower than all the others—and went out.

It was done in a moment: soft, sudden, and noiseless; he slipped away with a supple, cat-like ease that startled me, I own. In the office below there were the ordinary sounds—the faint rasp of pen on paper, the intermittent shuffling of feet, a door somewhere closing with that hard modern smack which reverberates through a building and seems to quarrel with every quiet thought. Yet he made no sound belonging to himself. It was as if he had walked on silence, leaving only the common noises behind him, and taking his own with him.

When I recovered my composure and began to reflect, I came to one clear conclusion about this otherwise incomprehensible visitor. His face, his voice, his manner—while he was in my room—were under such control that scrutiny slid off him as water slides off polished stone. But he had given me one chance, for all that, of seeing beneath his smooth surface.

He had shown no sign whatever of fixing anything I said in his mind—until I came to the customary time allowed for repayment. At that point, for the first time, he looked me straight in the face while I spoke. The inference was plain: he had asked the question with a special purpose, and he had a special interest in the answer.

The more I considered the matter, the more strongly I suspected that the entire production of the casket and the request for a loan had been mere formalities—like a ceremonial washing of hands before a meal whose true flavour is in the one last mouthful—designed to lead naturally to that inquiry at parting.

I had satisfied myself of this, and was attempting to go a step further and penetrate his motive, when a letter was brought in, bearing no less a signature than Mr. Septimus Luker’s.

He begged my pardon in terms of sickening servility, and assured me that he could explain matters to my satisfaction if I would consent to see him. Again I made an unprofessional sacrifice to curiosity, and appointed him to call at my office the next day.

Mr. Luker was, in every respect, so inferior a creature to the Indian—so vulgar, so ugly, so cringing, so tediously prosy—that he is not worthy of being reported at length. The substance of what he told me was this.

On the day before I received the Indian’s visit, Mr. Luker had himself been favoured with a call from that same accomplished gentleman. In spite of the European dress, Luker had instantly identified him as the chief of the three Indians who had formerly annoyed him by loitering about his house, and who had left him no alternative but to consult a magistrate. From that discovery he had rushed at once—naturally enough—to the conclusion that he must now be in the power of one of the three men who had blindfolded him, gagged him, and robbed him of his banker’s receipt. He became, he said, paralysed with terror and believed his last hour had arrived.

The Indian, on his side, preserved the character of a perfect stranger. He produced the little casket and made the very same application for a loan that he afterwards made to me. As the speediest way of getting rid of him, Mr. Luker declared instantly that he had no money to lend.

Thereupon the Indian asked to be informed of the best and safest person to apply to for the loan he wanted. Mr. Luker answered that the best and safest person, in such cases, was usually a respectable solicitor. Pressed to name one, he mentioned me—for no better reason, as he confessed with a candour born of cowardice, than that, in the extremity of his fear, my name was the first that occurred to him.

“The perspiration was pouring off me like rain, sir,” the wretched creature concluded. “I didn’t know what I was talking about. And I hope you’ll look over it, Mr. Bruff, sir, in consideration of my having been really and truly frightened out of my wits.”

I excused him graciously enough; it was the readiest method of releasing myself from his presence. Before he left, however, I detained him with one inquiry.

Had the Indian said anything noticeable at the moment of quitting Mr. Luker’s house?

Yes. He had asked precisely the same question at parting which he had asked of me; and he had received, of course, the same answer.

What did it mean?

Mr. Luker’s explanation gave me no help. Left to myself, my ingenuity proved quite unequal to grapple with the difficulty. I had a dinner engagement that evening, and went upstairs to dress in no very genial frame of mind—little suspecting that, on this particular occasion, the way to my dressing-room and the way to discovery meant one and the same thing.

The one man at our host’s table who carried with him, as naturally as another gentleman carries his watch-chain, the sense of distant places and hidden dangers, was Mr. Murthwaite. London had made a show of him on his return—as it always makes a show of any traveller who has escaped alive from countries where the map turns vague and the air turns murderous. He had announced, with that calm improvidence which society calls courage, that he meant to go back again, and press farther in—into regions left, by common consent, to the silence of their own mountains.

It is not an everyday thing to dine with a celebrated personage and to have, at the back of one’s mind, the perfectly reasonable expectation that the next public mention of him may be in the form of an obituary, or a rumour of violent death. The idea did him no injustice; it was only the tribute which civilised life pays to a man who repeatedly stakes it against the unknown.

When the ladies withdrew, and the servants had closed the door, the room altered at once—as rooms do. The talk fell, by that English inevitability which needs no more prompting than a decanter, into politics. The voices grew broader; the opinions grew louder; and the very knives and glasses seemed to take on a sharper ring, as if they too were enlisted under party colours.

To me, politics has always been the thinnest soup on the table: much heat, little nourishment. I glanced at Murthwaite when the wine had made its first circuit. He was performing, with admirable consideration for our host, the difficult feat of listening without hearing. His eyelids had the look of shutters half-drawn against a dreary street-noise; he was settling himself, inch by inch, into that genial doze which good claret encourages and bad discussion compels.

It occurred to me that there was one subject in the world which might rouse him: a subject in which the marvellous and the practical had been mixed together, like spice in ordinary bread, until no man could separate them. I leaned towards him.

“If I am not mistaken, Mr. Murthwaite, you knew the late Lady Verinder, and took some interest in the strange succession of events which ended in the loss of the Moonstone?”

He woke as if the words had been a bell sounded close to his ear.

“Who are you, sir?” he asked.

I told him—my name, my profession, and my past connection with the Herncastle family; not forgetting the odd position I had once held in relation to the Colonel and his Diamond. At that, Murthwaite shifted his chair a little, so that the Conservatives and Liberals—equally monotonous at the moment—were left behind his shoulder, and he could give his whole attention to me.

“Have you heard anything lately,” he said, “of the Indians?”

“I have every reason to believe,” I answered, “that one of them had an interview with me, in my office, yesterday.”

I had thought him hard to astonish. He stared at me now as if, for once, the improbable had forced its way into his experience. I related, exactly, what had happened to Mr. Luker, and what had happened to myself.

“It is clear the Indian’s parting inquiry had an object,” I concluded. “Why should he be so anxious to know the time at which a borrower of money is usually privileged to pay the money back?”

“Is it possible you don’t see his motive, Mr. Bruff?”

“I am ashamed of my stupidity, Mr. Murthwaite—but I certainly don’t see it.”

He looked at me with that quiet relish for clearness which belongs to men who have lived among dangers where a mistake means a grave. Then, very patiently, he began to probe my dulness to the bottom.

“Let me ask you one question. In what position does the conspiracy to seize the Moonstone now stand?”

“I can’t say,” I answered. “The Indian plot is a mystery to me.”

“The Indian plot can only be a mystery to you,” he said, “because you have never seriously examined it. Shall we run it over together—from the time when you drew Colonel Herncastle’s Will, to the time when the Indian called at your office? In your position it may be of very serious importance to Miss Verinder’s interests, that you should take a clear view of this matter in case of need. Tell me—will you penetrate the Indian’s motive for yourself? or do you wish me to save you the trouble?”

There was no mistaking the practical kindness under his tone. I saw his purpose at once, and chose the first alternative.

“Very good,” he said. “We will begin with the ages of the three Indians. I can testify they all look much about the same age—and you can judge whether the man you saw was, or was not, in the prime of life. Not forty, you think? My idea too. We will say not forty.

“Now look back to the time when Colonel Herncastle came to England, and when you were concerned in the plan he adopted to preserve his life. I don’t want you to count the years. It is clear that these present Indians, at their age, must be the successors of three other Indians—high-caste Brahmins, all of them, when they left their own country—who followed the Colonel to these shores.

“These present men have succeeded to the men who were here before them. If they had only done that, the matter would not be worth inquiry. But they have done more: they have succeeded to the organisation their predecessors established in this country. Don’t start. It is a trumpery affair, according to our notions. Reckon it as including command of money; the services, when needed, of that shady sort of Englishman who lives in the byways of foreign life in London; and, lastly, the secret sympathy of such few men of their own country—and formerly, at least, of their own religion—as happen to be employed in ministering to some of the multitudinous wants of this great city. Nothing formidable. But worth notice at starting; we may have occasion to refer to it.

“Now I ask you a question, and I expect your experience to answer it. What event gave the Indians their first chance of seizing the Diamond?”

“The first chance,” I said, “was clearly offered to them by Colonel Herncastle’s death. They would be aware of his death, I suppose?”

“As a matter of course. And his death gave them their first chance. Up to that time the Moonstone was safe in the strongroom of the bank. You drew the Colonel’s Will leaving the jewel to his niece; and the Will was proved. As a lawyer, you know what course the Indians would take—under English advice—after that.”

“They would procure a copy of the Will from Doctors’ Commons.”

“Exactly. One of those English intermediaries would get them the copy. It would inform them that the Moonstone was bequeathed to Lady Verinder’s daughter, and that Mr. Blake the elder, or a person appointed by him, was to place it in her hands. Information about Lady Verinder and Mr. Blake would be easy to obtain.

“The only difficulty for the Indians would be whether to attempt the Diamond in its removal from the bank, or wait until it was taken down to Yorkshire to Lady Verinder’s house. The second way is the safer way—and there is your explanation of the Indians at Frizinghall, disguised as jugglers, waiting their time.

“In London, they had their organisation to keep them informed. Two men would do it: one to follow anybody who went from Mr. Blake’s house to the bank; and one to treat the lower servants with beer and hear the house-news. Those precautions would tell them Mr. Franklin Blake had been to the bank, and that Mr. Franklin Blake was the only person going to Lady Verinder. What followed on that discovery you remember.”

I did remember. Franklin Blake had detected one of the spies; he had hastened his journey northward; and, by old Betteredge’s sensible counsel, had placed the Diamond in the bank at Frizinghall before the Indians were ready even to suspect him in the neighbourhood.

But one question had always troubled me. The Indians, ignorant of those precautions, must have supposed the Diamond was in the house—why did they make no attempt on Lady Verinder’s home during the whole interval before Miss Verinder’s birthday? I put the difficulty plainly, adding that I knew of the boy and the drop of ink and all the rest, and that any explanation founded on clairvoyance would carry no weight with me.

“Nor with me,” Murthwaite said. “The clairvoyance here is merely the romantic side of the Indian character. It refreshes and encourages those men—quite inconceivable, I grant you, to the English mind—to surround their perilous errand with a halo of the marvellous. Their boy is unquestionably sensitive to mesmeric influence; and, under it, he has reflected what was already in the mind of the person mesmerising him. I have tested clairvoyance and never found it go beyond that. The Indians do not investigate so; they believe their boy a Seer. In that marvel they find a new interest in the purpose that unites them.

“But we have nothing to do with mesmerism or anything hard of belief. My object is to trace results, by rational means, to natural causes. Have I satisfied you so far?”

“Not a doubt of it. But I am waiting, with some anxiety, for the rational explanation of my difficulty.”

He smiled, as if the thing I feared most was the easiest for him to lift.

“It is the easiest difficulty of all. Admit your statement: the Indians were not aware of what Mr. Franklin Blake had done with the Diamond—for we find them making their first mistake on the first night of his arrival.”

“Their first mistake?”

“The mistake of letting themselves be surprised, lurking on the terrace at night, by Gabriel Betteredge. However, they had the merit of seeing they had taken a false step—for, with plenty of time, they never came near the house for weeks afterwards.”

“Why? That is what I want to know.”

“Because no Indian ever runs an unnecessary risk. The clause you drew in the Colonel’s Will informed them that the Moonstone was to pass absolutely into Miss Verinder’s possession on her birthday. Now tell me—what was the safest course? To attempt the Diamond while it was under the control of Mr. Franklin Blake, who had already shown he could suspect and outwit them? Or to wait till the Diamond was at the disposal of a young girl, who would innocently delight in wearing a magnificent jewel at every opportunity?

“Want proof? Take their own conduct. They appeared after waiting all those weeks on Miss Verinder’s birthday, and were rewarded for their patience by seeing the Moonstone in the bosom of her dress. When I heard the story later that evening, I felt sure what risk Mr. Franklin Blake had run—they would certainly have attacked him had he not ridden back with other people—and I was convinced an even worse risk was in store for Miss Verinder. I recommended following the Colonel’s plan, destroying the identity of the gem by cutting it into separate stones. How its extraordinary disappearance that night made my advice useless, defeated the Hindoo plot, and how all further action by the Indians was paralysed next day by their confinement as rogues and vagabonds—you know as well as I do. The first act in the conspiracy closes there. Have I met your difficulty?”

I could not deny it. His knowledge of Indian character, and his having no pile of other wills clouding his memory, gave him an advantage I was obliged to admit.

“So far, so good,” he resumed. “The first chance was lost when they were committed to prison at Frizinghall. When did the second chance offer itself? While they were still in confinement—as I can prove.”

He drew out his pocket-book and opened it at a certain page.

“I was staying with friends at Frizinghall at the time. A day or two before the Indians were set free—on a Monday, I think—the governor came to me with a letter. It had been left for the Indians by Mrs. Macann, from whom they had hired their lodging; and it had arrived by ordinary post the morning before. The prison authorities noticed the postmark ‘Lambeth,’ and that the direction, though in correct English, was oddly unlike the customary method. On opening it they found a foreign language, which they guessed—rightly—was Hindustani. They came to me to translate it. I copied the original and the translation into my pocket-book.”

He handed it to me. The address had been copied first, all in one unpunctuated paragraph:

“To the three Indian men living with the lady called Macann at Frizinghall in Yorkshire.”

Below were the Hindoo characters. At the end came his English rendering, brief and uncanny:

“In the name of the Regent of the Night, whose seat is on the Antelope, whose arms embrace the four corners of the earth.

“Brothers, turn your faces to the south, and come to me in the street of many noises, which leads down to the muddy river.

“The reason is this.

“My own eyes have seen it.”

There was neither date nor signature.

I handed back the book, confessing that the letter puzzled me.

“I can explain the first sentence,” he said, “and the conduct of the Indians explains the rest. The moon-god in Hindoo mythology is a four-armed deity seated on an antelope; one of his titles is Regent of the Night. That looks suspiciously like an indirect reference to the Moonstone.

“Now, what did the Indians do after they were allowed to receive the letter? On the very day they were set free they went straight to the station and took the first train to London. We thought, at Frizinghall, it was a pity their proceedings were not watched; but after Lady Verinder dismissed the police-officer and stopped inquiry, no one could presume to stir. They were free to go—and they went. What was the next news of them?”

“They were annoying Mr. Luker,” I said, “by loitering about his house at Lambeth.”

“Did you read the report of his application to the magistrate?”

“Yes.”

“He referred, if you remember, to a foreign workman in his employment whom he had dismissed on suspicion of attempted theft, and whom he suspected of collusion with the Indians. The inference is plain as to who wrote this letter—and as to which of Mr. Luker’s Oriental treasures the workman attempted to steal.”

The inference was indeed too plain. I had never doubted that the Moonstone had found its way into Luker’s hands at that time. The only question had been how the Indians discovered the circumstance; and now, like the rest, the answer lay before me in daylight.

I told Murthwaite I felt as if I could trust him to lead me blindfold through any remaining turns of this maze. He accepted the compliment with quiet good humour.

“You shall give me information in your turn before we go on,” he said. “Somebody must have taken the Moonstone from Yorkshire to London. Somebody must have raised money on it, or it would never have been in Luker’s possession. Has there been any discovery of who that person was?”

“None that I know of.”

“There was a story—was there not?—about Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite. I am told he is an eminent philanthropist—which is decidedly against him, to begin with.”

I agreed, heartily, on that general principle; but I felt bound—without mentioning Miss Verinder’s name—to inform him that Ablewhite had been cleared on evidence I could answer for as entirely beyond dispute.

“Very well,” Murthwaite said. “Let time clear it up. Meanwhile we must get back to the Indians, on your account.

“Their journey to London ended in another defeat. The loss of their second chance is mainly attributable to Luker’s cunning and foresight—he doesn’t stand at the top of the ancient profession of usury for nothing. By dismissing the man in his employment, he deprived the Indians of their confederate’s help in getting into the house. By promptly transporting the Moonstone to his banker’s, he took them by surprise before they could frame a new plan. How they suspected what he had done, and how they contrived to possess themselves of his banker’s receipt, are too recent to dwell on. Let it be enough that they know the Moonstone is once more out of their reach, deposited—under the general description of ‘a valuable of great price’—in a banker’s strong room.

“Now, Mr. Bruff—what is their third chance of seizing the Diamond? And when will it come?”

As he asked the question, the meaning of the Indian’s visit to my office sprang into view at last, as if a shutter had been flung back and the whole room flooded with light.

“I see it!” I cried. “They take it for granted, as we do, that the Moonstone has been pledged; and they want to be certainly informed of the earliest period at which the pledge can be redeemed—because that will be the earliest period at which the Diamond can be removed from the bank!”

“I told you,” he said, “you would find it out for yourself if I gave you a fair chance. In a year from the time when the Moonstone was pledged, the Indians will be on the watch for their third chance. Luker’s own lips have told them how long they must wait; and your respectable authority has satisfied them he spoke the truth. When do we suppose—roughly—that the Diamond found its way into the money-lender’s hands?”

“Towards the end of last June,” I answered, “as well as I can reckon it.”

“And we are now in the year ’forty-eight. Very good. If the unknown person who has pledged the Moonstone can redeem it in a year, the jewel will be in that person’s possession again at the end of June, ’forty-nine. I shall be thousands of miles from England and English news then. But it may be worth your while to take a note of it, and arrange to be in London at the time.”

“You think something serious will happen?” I asked.

“I think,” he answered, “I shall be safer among the fiercest fanatics of Central Asia than I should be if I crossed the door of the bank with the Moonstone in my pocket. The Indians have been defeated twice running. It’s my firm belief they won’t be defeated a third time.”

That was the last he said. Coffee came in; chairs moved; the talk broke up and dispersed, like smoke drifting into corners; and we went upstairs to the ladies.

I made my note at once, and I close my narrative by repeating it here:

June, ’forty-nine. Expect news of the Indians, towards the end of the month.

And having done so, I lay down the pen, which I have no further claim to use, and leave it to the writer who follows me next.

In the spring of eighteen hundred and forty-nine I was wandering in the East, living as travellers do—by altered plans, by improvised camps, by the daily arithmetic of water-skins and baggage, and by that thin domestic comfort which a well-ordered tent can still contrive in a wilderness. My route had shifted since the last letters I had caused to be sent to me; and the change obliged me to despatch one of my servants to the English consul in a city which my revised scheme no longer touched. He was to fetch my correspondence and remittances, and to rejoin me at a named place and time.

An accident, for which the man was in no way answerable, detained him on his errand. For a week we waited on the desert’s edge—camped where the sand began, and the last stubborn scrub clung to its stony ground. The days had a hard, bright silence to them; the nights filled, not with music, but with the uneasy little sounds of a temporary household: canvas snapping, a kettle’s lid ticking as it cooled, the dry scrape of sand against tent-pegs, and—beyond all—an immense hush that made each human movement feel like an intrusion. Even the men spoke lower than usual, as if the land demanded it. I remember how the clink of a spoon on a metal cup travelled; how a servant’s cough seemed to stain the air; how the nearest camel’s slow chewing sounded like machinery in a cathedral.

At the end of that week the missing man appeared at the tent-door with my money and my letters.

“I am afraid I bring you bad news, sir,” he said; and, among the packets, he pointed to one with a mourning border, addressed in Mr. Bruff’s hand.

There is nothing, in calamities of this sort, so unendurable as suspense. I broke the seal of the black-edged letter first.

It told me my father was dead, and that I was heir to his great fortune. The wealth came with its weight of obligation. Mr. Bruff entreated me to return at once. By daybreak the next morning I was on my way home.

If my old friend Betteredge has painted me, at the time of my departure from England, a shade too fantastically—if, in his honest quaintness, he has credited one of my cousin’s satirical allusions with serious truth, and persuaded himself he actually saw French, German, and Italian “sides” of me that existed nowhere but in his own imagination—I can forgive the over-colouring. In one point he did not exceed the truth. I had been wounded to the heart by Rachel’s treatment of me, and I had left England in the rawest keenness of the bitterest disappointment of my life.

I went abroad resolved to forget her, if change and absence could serve me. Those who deny that they help do not read human nature with any accuracy. They force the mind to turn outward; they compel a man, by sheer multiplicity of new objects, new faces, new voices, to look away from the sore place in himself. I never forgot Rachel. But the remembrance, in time, lost its sharpest venom. Little by little, distance interposed; novelty laid its cool hand on my fever; other landscapes, other habits, other tongues made a screen between her and me.

And yet, with the first act of turning homeward, the screen began to thin. The nearer I drew to England—the nearer to the streets where English speech ran quick and careless in the mouth, and the old familiar noises returned (wheels on paving-stones, the iron cough of an engine, the shout of porters, the very advertising cries that cheapened everything they touched)—the more irresistibly her influence recovered its hold. When I left, her name was the last I would have suffered to pass my lips. When I returned, it was the first I asked after when Mr. Bruff and I met again.

I was told, of course, of all that had happened in my absence: in other words, of all that has been related since Betteredge’s narrative began—one circumstance only excepted. Mr. Bruff did not then feel himself at liberty to tell me the private motives which had influenced Rachel and Godfrey Ablewhite, on either side, to recall their promise of marriage. I spared him embarrassing questions. It was relief enough, after the jealous misery of hearing that she had ever contemplated being Godfrey’s wife, to know that she had thought better of it, and had released herself from the engagement.

Having heard the past, my questions moved—naturally, inevitably—to the present. Under whose care had Rachel been placed after leaving Mr. Bruff’s house? Where was she living now?

She was living with Mrs. Merridew, a widowed sister of the late Sir John Verinder, whom Lady Verinder’s executors had requested to act as guardian, and who had accepted the charge. They were reported to be getting on admirably, and to be established for the season in Mrs. Merridew’s house in Portland Place.

Half an hour after receiving this information I was on my way to Portland Place—without having had the courage to confess it to Mr. Bruff.

The servant who opened the door was not sure whether Miss Verinder was at home. I sent him up with my card as the shortest way of settling the question. He returned with an impenetrable face and informed me that Miss Verinder was out.

I might have suspected other people of denying themselves to me by contrivance. It was impossible to suspect Rachel. I left word that I would call again at six that evening.

At six o’clock I was told, for the second time, that Miss Verinder was not at home.

Had any message been left for me? No.

Had Miss Verinder received my card? The servant begged my pardon—Miss Verinder had received it.

The inference lay too plain to be escaped. Rachel declined to see me.

On my side, I declined to accept such treatment without at least seeking the reason. I sent up my name to Mrs. Merridew, and requested the favour of a personal interview at whatever hour she pleased to name. Mrs. Merridew made no difficulty; she received me at once.

I was shown into a comfortable little sitting-room and found a comfortable little elderly lady. The room had the air of a household that valued quiet order—the kind of place where the domestic sounds are softened on purpose: a thick carpet that drinks footsteps, curtains that blunt the street-noise, a coal-fire tended to glow rather than blaze. Even the tea-things, set ready on a table, seemed arranged with a kind of mild discipline. Outside, Portland Place sent up its constant hum—carriage-wheels and harness, door-knockers, the commerce of voices—but within there was a careful hush, as if the house were a shell shut against the coarser world.

Mrs. Merridew was very good. She expressed regret and surprise—entirely on my account. But she declared, with polite patience which nothing could tire, that she was not in a position to offer me any explanation; and that she could not press Rachel on a matter which appeared to relate only to private feeling.

Over and over again, in different forms of the same gentleness, she said it. Over and over again, in different forms of the same hopelessness, I heard it. And that was all I gained.

My last chance was to write to Rachel.

The next day my servant took my letter, with strict orders to wait for an answer. The answer came back—literally one sentence:

“Miss Verinder begs to decline entering into any correspondence with Mr. Franklin Blake.”

Fond as I was of her, I felt the insult of it. The few words had the chill of a door shut in the face—language used as a weapon because it is so neat, so unmistakable, so final. There are affronts that shout; this one whispered, and yet cut deeper for its restraint.

Mr. Bruff came in to speak to me on business before I had recovered my self-command. I dismissed the business at once and laid the whole case before him. He proved as incapable of enlightening me as Mrs. Merridew.

Had any slander been spoken of me in Rachel’s hearing? Mr. Bruff knew of none.

Had she referred to me in any way while she stayed under his roof? Never.

Had she, during all my long absence, asked so much as whether I was living or dead? No such question had ever passed her lips.

I took from my pocket-book the letter Lady Verinder had written to me at Frizinghall on the day I left Yorkshire. I pointed out two sentences to Mr. Bruff:

“The valuable assistance which you rendered to the inquiry after the lost jewel is still an unpardoned offence, in the present dreadful state of Rachel’s mind. Moving blindfold in this matter, you have added to the burden of anxiety which she has had to bear, by innocently threatening her secret with discovery through your exertions.”

“Is it possible,” I asked, “that the feeling towards me described there is as bitter as ever against me now?”

Mr. Bruff looked honestly distressed.

“If you insist on an answer,” he said, “I own I can place no other interpretation on her conduct than that.”

I rang the bell and ordered my servant to pack my portmanteau and send out for a railway guide.

Mr. Bruff stared at me in astonishment. “May I ask what you are going to do?”

“I am going to Yorkshire,” I answered, “by the next train.”

“For what purpose?”

“Mr. Bruff, the assistance I innocently rendered to the inquiry after the Diamond was an unpardoned offence in Rachel’s mind nearly a year since; and it remains an unpardoned offence still. I won’t accept that position. I am determined to find out the secret of her silence towards her mother, and her enmity towards me. If time, pains, and money can do it, I will lay my hand on the thief who took the Moonstone.”

The worthy old gentleman attempted to remonstrate—to call me back to reason—to do his duty by me in short. I was deaf to everything. No earthly consideration could have shaken the resolution in me then.

“I shall take up the inquiry again,” I went on, “at the point where I dropped it; I shall follow it onward, step by step, till I come to the present time. There are missing links in the evidence as I left it which Gabriel Betteredge can supply; and to Gabriel Betteredge I go.”

Towards sunset that evening I stood once more on the well-remembered terrace and looked at the peaceful old country house. It lay as it had lain in my memory, quiet in the long light, with the sense of settled habit clinging to it like the smell of stored linen and old wood. The very air had a different voice here. Instead of London’s ceaseless clangour there were rural layers of sound—the small click of insects, a distant cart on a softer road, the wind worrying the leaves, a bird calling as if it had always called and would always call. The place seemed to speak in its own dialect of stillness: not the dead silence of the desert, but the living hush of land that has been listened to for generations.

The gardener was the first person I met in the empty grounds. He told me Betteredge had left him an hour since, sunning himself in the customary corner of the back yard.

I knew the corner well, and I said I would go to him myself.

I walked round by the familiar paths and passages and looked in at the open gate of the yard. There he was—the dear old friend of days that were never to come again—there he was in the old corner, on the old beehive chair, with his pipe in his mouth and his Robinson Crusoe on his lap, and the two dogs—his constant table-companions, as it were—dozing on either side of him. The yard held its homely smells: earth warmed all day by the sun; straw; a faint tarry breath from the woodwork; and, underneath, that indefinable savour of a place where people have lived and worked long enough for their very habits to become part of the air.

As I stood where I was, the last slanting rays threw my shadow before me. Either the dogs saw it, or their quick scent warned them; they started up with a growl.

Betteredge started too, quieted them with a word, shaded his failing eyes with his hand, and looked inquiringly at the figure in the gateway.

My own eyes filled. I was obliged to wait a moment before I could trust myself to speak to him.

“Betteredge,” I said, touching the book on his knee, “has Robinson Crusoe been prophesying again to-night? Has he told you to expect Franklin Blake?”

“By the Lord Harry, Mr. Franklin!” the old man cried. “That’s exactly what he’s done!”

With my hand under his elbow I helped him up from the beehive chair; and for an instant he stood wavering between his amazement at me and his awe of the volume, like a man whose faith has been rewarded too abruptly. The verdict went, as it always went with Betteredge, in favour of print. He held the book open in both hands and stared at it with a hungry, superstitious delight—as if the paper itself might steam and crackle, and the castaway step out smelling of salt and ship’s biscuit.

“Here’s the bit, Mr. Franklin!” he broke out, finding his tongue again. “As I live by bread, sir—here’s the very line I had under my eye the moment before you came in. Page one hundred and fifty-six: ‘I stood like one Thunderstruck, or as if I had seen an Apparition.’ If that isn’t plain English for ‘Expect Mr. Franklin Blake popping up of a sudden’—then there’s no meaning in the language!” He shut the book with a clap that sounded through the deserted hall, and at last gave me the hand I offered.

I was ready for questions. Betteredge had none—not at first. The old domestic instinct sprang up in him, prompt as the striking of a match in a dark kitchen: feed the visitor; make him warm; set the house in order round him, if only for an hour.

“Walk in, Mr. Franklin,” he said, with his quaint bow, holding the door behind him. “I’ll ask what brings you afterwards. I must make you comfortable first. There have been sad changes since you went away. The house is shut up, the servants are gone. Never mind! I’ll cook your dinner; and the gardener’s wife will make your bed—and if there’s a bottle of our famous Latour claret left in the cellar, down your throat, sir, that bottle shall go. Welcome! Heartily welcome!”

It cut me to the quick to balk him. But this was Rachel’s house now. After London—after what had been said and left unsaid there—I could not take meat under her roof, nor sleep between her walls, and keep the smallest self-respect alive. I took Betteredge by the arm and led him out into the garden, where the evening air smelt of damp earth and box and the day’s last warmth lingering in the stone.

There, with the long hush of the grounds round us—the distant rooks settling, the soft stir of leaves, and, beyond that, the faintest edge of road-noise like an ill-mannered whisper—I told him the truth.

Between his loyalty to Rachel and his affection for me, the old man was torn, and his distress showed itself in the very way he set his jaw. When he spoke, he spoke as he always did—plain, homely, and seasoned with that stubborn philosophy which had been brewed in kitchens and pantries rather than in books.

“Miss Rachel has her faults—I’ve never denied it,” he began. “And riding the high horse, now and then, is one of them. She’s been trying to ride over you—and you’ve put up with it. Lord, Mr. Franklin, don’t you know women by this time better than that? You’ve heard me talk of the late Mrs. Betteredge?”

I had heard of the late Mrs. Betteredge often enough—produced, like a sour ingredient, to explain the whole cookery of the sex. He brought her out now with the same certainty, as if her memory lived in the kitchen spice-box.

“Different women have different ways of riding,” he went on. “The late Mrs. Betteredge took her exercise on that animal whenever I denied her anything she had set her heart on. So sure as I came home from my work on those occasions, so sure was my wife to call to me up the kitchen stairs—and say, after my brutal treatment of her, she hadn’t the heart to cook me my dinner.

“I put up with it for a time—just as you’re putting up with it now from Miss Rachel. At last my patience wore out. I went down, and I took Mrs. Betteredge—affectionately, mind—up in my arms, and carried her, holus-bolus, into the best parlour where she received her company. ‘That’s the right place for you, my dear,’ says I; and back I goes to the kitchen. I locked myself in, took off my coat, turned up my shirt-sleeves, and cooked my own dinner. When it was done, I served it up in my best manner—and enjoyed it most heartily. I had my pipe and my drop of grog afterwards; and then I cleared the table, washed the crockery, cleaned the knives and forks, put the things away, and swept up the hearth. When it was as bright and clean again as bright and clean could be, I opened the door and let Mrs. Betteredge in. ‘I’ve had my dinner, my dear,’ says I; ‘and I hope you’ll find I’ve left the kitchen all that your fondest wishes can desire.’ For the rest of that woman’s life, Mr. Franklin, I never had to cook my dinner again!

“Moral: you’ve put up with Miss Rachel in London; don’t put up with her in Yorkshire. Come back to the house!”

It was unanswerable—in its own iron way. I could only tell him that even he, with all his honest tyranny of affection, could not persuade me.

“It’s a lovely evening,” I said. “I shall walk to Frizinghall and stay at the hotel. Come to me to-morrow morning and breakfast with me. I have something to say to you.”

He shook his head slowly.

“I am heartily sorry,” he answered. “I had hoped to hear things were smooth and pleasant again between you and Miss Rachel. If you must have your own way, sir—there’s no need to go to Frizinghall for a bed. It’s to be had nearer than that. There’s Hotherstone’s Farm—barely two miles. You can hardly object to that on Miss Rachel’s account,” he added, with a sly lift at the corner of his mouth. “Hotherstone lives on his own freehold.”

I remembered it directly: a farm-house tucked into a sheltered valley, with a bright stream running near it—the sort of place where the air seems to come strained and sweet from grass and water. The farmer let a spare parlour and bedroom now and again to anglers and sketchers and wanderers. It was, in truth, exactly the refuge I wanted—near enough to the house for business, far enough for pride.

“Are the rooms to let?” I asked.

“Mrs. Hotherstone herself begged my good word for them yesterday,” he said.

“Then I’ll take them with the greatest pleasure.”

We went back into the yard for my travelling-bag. Betteredge put a stick through its handle and swung it over his shoulder; and with the burden came back his first bewilderment. He looked at the house—as if expecting it to speak—then wheeled and looked at me more incredulously still.

“I’ve lived a goodish long time in the world,” said the best old servant breathing, “but the like of this I never did expect to see. There stands the house, and here stands Mr. Franklin Blake—and, Damme, if one of them isn’t turning his back on the other, and going to sleep in a lodging!”

He started off, wagging his head and growling as if he were scolding the very evening for permitting such a thing.

“There’s only one more miracle that can happen,” he flung back at me over his shoulder. “The next thing you’ll do, Mr. Franklin, will be to pay me back that seven-and-sixpence you borrowed when you were a boy.”

The sarcasm comforted him; it put the world straight in his mouth, if nowhere else. We passed through the lodge gates. Once we were outside the grounds, hospitality—by Betteredge’s code—ended, and curiosity began. He dropped back till we walked level, and spoke as if we had met by accident on the road.

“Fine evening for a walk, Mr. Franklin. Supposing you had gone to the hotel at Frizinghall, sir?”

“Yes?”

“I should have had the honour of breakfasting with you to-morrow morning.”

“Then come and breakfast with me at Hotherstone’s Farm instead.”

“Much obliged to you, sir. But it wasn’t exactly breakfast I was driving at.” He gave up his winding approach and came straight out. “If it’s no secret, I’m burning to know what’s brought you down here in this sudden way.”

“What brought me here before?” I asked.

“The Moonstone, Mr. Franklin. But what brings you now, sir?”

“The Moonstone again, Betteredge.”

He stopped dead in the grey thickening twilight and stared as if the words had been shouted wrong by my lips.

“If that’s a joke, sir,” he said slowly, “I’m afraid I’m getting a little dull. I don’t take it.”

“It’s no joke. I have come to take up the inquiry that was dropped when I left England. I have come to do what nobody has done yet—to find out who took the Diamond.”

“Let the Diamond be!” he burst out. “Take my advice and let it be. That cursed Indian jewel has misguided everybody that’s come near it. Don’t waste your money and your temper—in the fine spring-time of your life—by meddling with the Moonstone. How can you hope to succeed, saving your presence, when Sergeant Cuff himself made a mess of it? Sergeant Cuff!” He lifted a stern forefinger, as if the name were a scripture. “The greatest policeman in England!”

“My mind is made up,” I said. “Even Sergeant Cuff doesn’t daunt me. By-the-by, I may want to speak with him sooner or later. Have you heard anything of him?”

“The Sergeant won’t help you, Mr. Franklin.”

“Why not?”

“There’s been an event in police-circles since you went away. The great Cuff has retired. He’s got a little cottage at Dorking; and he’s up to his eyes in the growing of roses. I have it in his own handwriting. He has grown the white moss rose without budding it on the dog-rose first. And Mr. Begbie the gardener is to go to Dorking and own the Sergeant has beaten him at last.”

“It doesn’t much matter,” I answered. “I must do without Sergeant Cuff. And I must trust to you, at starting.”

I spoke, perhaps, too carelessly. Betteredge’s mouth tightened; and there was a small disturbance in his manner—as if something in him had shifted and settled again, like a dish moved in a dark cupboard.

“You might trust to worse than me,” he retorted, a little sharp.

It struck me then that he had something in reserve and was fighting with himself about giving it up.

“I expect you to help me,” I said, “in picking up the fragments of evidence Sergeant Cuff has left behind. I know you can do that. Can you do no more?”

“What more can you expect from me?” he asked, putting on his humblest face as if it were an apron he could tie at will.

“I expect more—from what you said just now.”

“Mere boasting,” he returned stubbornly. “Some people are born boasters, and never get over it. I’m one.”

There was only one way to reach him. I went to the heart—Rachel.

“Betteredge, would you be glad to hear that Rachel and I were good friends again?”

“I’ve served your family to mighty little purpose if you doubt it!”

“Do you remember how Rachel treated me before I left England?”

“As well as if it was yesterday. My lady herself wrote you a letter about it; and you were so good as to show it to me. It said Miss Rachel was mortally offended with you for the part you had taken in trying to recover her jewel. And neither my lady, nor you, nor anybody else could guess why.”

“True. And I come back and find her mortally offended still. I knew the Diamond was at the bottom of it last year, and I know it is at the bottom of it now. I have tried to see her—she won’t see me. I have tried to write—she won’t answer. How am I to clear it up? The chance of searching into the loss of the Moonstone is the one chance of inquiry that Rachel herself has left me.”

He listened; and the quiet around us deepened—the stream-sound, far off, like someone gently stirring broth; the last birds calling themselves to rest. The world had room for plain words there.

“There is no ill-feeling in this, Mr. Franklin, on your side—is there?” he asked at last.

“There was some anger when I left London,” I said. “But it’s worn out. I want Rachel to come to an understanding with me—and I want nothing more.”

He hesitated, then spoke with jealous tenderness for his young mistress, as if even her name needed guarding.

“You don’t feel any fear, sir—supposing you make discoveries—in regard to what you may find out about Miss Rachel?”

“I am as certain of her as you are,” I answered. “The fullest disclosure of her secret will reveal nothing that can alter her place in your estimation—or in mine.”

That swept away what was left of his scruple. He threw up his hands, as if surrendering to fate and affection together.

“If I am doing wrong to help you,” he said, “all I can say is—I’m as innocent of seeing it as the babe unborn! I can put you on the road to discovery, if you can only go on by yourself. You remember that poor girl of ours—Rosanna Spearman?”

“Of course.”

“You always thought she had some sort of confession about this matter of the Moonstone that she wanted to make to you?”

“I couldn’t account for her strange conduct in any other way.”

“You may set that doubt at rest whenever you please.”

It was my turn to stop. The twilight had thickened; his face was lost to me, but I could hear, in the set of his voice, that he meant every syllable.

“What do you mean?” I asked, more impatiently than I intended.

“Steady, sir,” he said. “I mean what I say. Rosanna Spearman left a sealed letter behind her—a letter addressed to you.”

“Where is it?”

“In the possession of a friend of hers at Cobb’s Hole. You must have heard tell of Limping Lucy—a lame girl with a crutch.”

“The fisherman’s daughter?”

“The same.”

“Why wasn’t the letter forwarded to me?”

“Limping Lucy has a will of her own. She wouldn’t give it into any hands but yours. And you had left England before I could write.”

“Then let’s go back and get it at once.”

“Too late to-night. They’re great savers of candles along our coast; and they go to bed early at Cobb’s Hole.”

“Nonsense! We might get there in half an hour.”

“You might, sir. And when you did, you’d find the door locked.”

He pointed down the slope. A small light glimmered below us; and in the same moment the bubbling of a stream rose clearer in the still evening air—pure country sound, undrowned by wheels or engines, as if the valley kept its own old language of water and stone while the louder world forgot how to listen.

“There’s the Farm, Mr. Franklin. Make yourself comfortable for to-night, and come to me to-morrow morning—if you’ll be so kind?”

“You will go with me to the fisherman’s cottage?”

“Yes, sir.”

“Early?”

“As early as you like.”

We went down the path that led to Hotherstone’s Farm.

My remembrance of what passed at Hotherstone’s Farm is no better than a taste lingering after a meal—strong in one instant, gone the next, leaving only impressions that the senses can swear to, while the mind cannot marshal the order of them.

I recall a welcome as hearty as a kitchen fire newly stirred; a supper of such prodigality that it might have victualled a parish—cold roast and hot, bread with a crust that spoke of honest flour, butter with the clean sweet smell of churn-work, and the sharp comfort of pickled things that bite the tongue awake. I recall, too, the farmhouse quiet—boards that creaked with age instead of machinery, a draught sighing under the door, and, underneath all, the living sound of the place: distant cattle shifting in their stalls, a bucket set down, a hinge complaining once and then being still. They were country noises—each one with its proper interval of silence—and they rested the ear like a familiar dialect rests the heart.

In my bedroom there was cleanliness enough to shame London lodging-houses. Only one article in it awakened my indignation: a feather-bed. That soft, smothering contrivance—made, I suppose, in the days when our forefathers mistook suffocation for comfort—kept me waking, striking match after match and nursing the little candle as if it were a witness I dared not lose. When daylight came, and with it the first bird-notes outside—small, clear syllables in the morning air—I felt a bodily relief at the mere prospect of rising, as if my lungs had been given back to me.

Over-night I had arranged with Betteredge that I should call for him, on our way to Cobb’s Hole, as early as I chose. Interpreted by my impatience to possess Rosanna Spearman’s letter, “as early as I chose” meant as early as I could.
