I would not wait for breakfast at the Farm; I took a crust in my hand—dry, with the faint nutty scent of yesterday’s baking—and set out, half fearing I might surprise the excellent Betteredge still among his blankets.

To my relief he was ready before me, and waiting with his stick.

“How are you this morning, Betteredge?”

“Very poorly, sir.”

“Sorry to hear it. What do you complain of?”

“I complain of a new disease, Mr. Franklin, of my own inventing. I don’t want to alarm you, but you’re certain to catch it before the morning is out.”

“The devil I am!”

“Do you feel an uncomfortable heat at the pit of your stomach, sir? and a nasty thumping at the top of your head? Ah! not yet? It will lay hold of you at Cobb’s Hole, Mr. Franklin. I call it the detective-fever; and I first caught it in the company of Sergeant Cuff.”

“Aye, aye! and the cure in this instance is to open Rosanna Spearman’s letter, I suppose? Come along, and let’s get it.”

Even at that early hour we found the fisherman’s wife astir in her kitchen. There were the domestic smells of a life that begins with the hands: damp wood warming near the hearth; yesterday’s fish-boards rinsed clean; tea-leaves newly scalded; and, under all, the salt breath that crept in with every opening of the door, bringing the sea into the house as surely as if it were a member of the family.

On Betteredge presenting me, good Mrs. Yolland performed a ceremony I learned afterwards to be reserved for strangers of distinction. She set a bottle of Dutch gin and a couple of clean pipes upon the table, and began, with an amplitude of inquiry that might have covered the whole kingdom:

“What news from London, sir?”

Before I could find an answer to so comprehensive a question, something moved—no, not something: an apparition advanced out of a dark corner of the kitchen.

A wan, wild, haggard girl, with remarkably beautiful hair and a fierce keenness in her eyes, came limping on a crutch to the table. She fixed her gaze on me with an intensity that made me feel less a man than an object set down for inspection—an object that roused in her at once curiosity and disgust, and fascinated her in spite of both.

“Mr. Betteredge,” she said, never taking her eyes from my face, “mention his name again, if you please.”

“This gentleman’s name,” answered Betteredge—laying a stress on the word gentleman as if it were a weapon—“is Mr. Franklin Blake.”

She turned her back on me and left the room, as suddenly as she had come.

Mrs. Yolland, I believe, offered apologies for her daughter’s odd behaviour; Betteredge, no doubt, translated them into the politeness of his own vocabulary. I cannot answer for either; my attention had been caught by the sound of the crutch, as a listener’s ear is caught by a wrong note in a familiar tune.

Thump—thump, up the wooden stairs. Thump—thump, across the room above our heads. Thump—thump, down again. And then the girl stood in the open doorway with a letter in her hand, beckoning me out.

I left more apologies in the middle of their course than I care to remember, and followed her—limping on before me faster and faster—down the slope of the beach. The village behind us made its small morning music: a gull’s harsh cry, a net being shaken, a child calling, the low talk of fishermen. But she led me behind some boats, out of sight and hearing, to where the shore lay bare and private, and there she stopped and faced me for the first time.

“Stand there,” she said. “I want to look at you.”

There was no mistaking her expression. She regarded me with abhorrence so plain that it needed no interpreter. It is a hard thing for any man to be examined as if he were foul; harder still to endure it in silence.

To shift the subject—like a child trying to escape a scolding—I ventured:

“I think you have got a letter to give me. Is it the letter there, in your hand?”

“Say that again,” was the only answer.

I repeated my words.

“No,” she said to herself, rather than to me, still holding me in the merciless net of her eyes. “I can’t find out what she saw in his face. I can’t guess what she heard in his voice.”

Then, as if a weight had pressed her down, she looked away and rested her head wearily on the top of her crutch.

“Oh, my poor dear,” she said—softly now, in the first gentle tones I had heard from her. “Oh, my lost darling! what could you see in this man?”

In an instant she flung up her head again and looked at me with returning fierceness.

“Can you eat and drink?” she asked.

I did my best to keep my gravity. “Yes.”

“Can you sleep?”

“Yes.”

“When you see a poor girl in service, do you feel no remorse?”

“Certainly not. Why should I?”

She thrust the letter into my face, as if it were something that burned her fingers.

“Take it!” she cried. “I never set eyes on you before. God Almighty forbid I should ever set eyes on you again.”

With that she limped away at the top of her speed, leaving me alone with the sea-wind and my bewilderment.

The interpretation that rose first in my mind will have risen, no doubt, in every other. I could only suppose her to be mad. And having arrived at that convenient conclusion, I turned to the more interesting object in my hand.

The address ran:

“For Franklin Blake, Esq. To be given into his own hands (and not to be trusted to anyone else), by Lucy Yolland.”

I broke the seal. The envelope contained a letter, and the letter contained a slip of paper. I read the letter first:

“Sir,—If you are curious to know the meaning of my behaviour to you, whilst you were staying in the house of my mistress, Lady Verinder, do what you are told to do in the memorandum enclosed with this—and do it without any person being present to overlook you.

“Your humble servant,
“ROSANNA SPEARMAN.”

I turned to the slip of paper. It was headed “Memorandum,” and I copy it word for word:

“To go to the Shivering Sand at the turn of the tide. To walk out on the South Spit, until I get the South Spit Beacon, and the flagstaff at the Coast-guard station above Cobb’s Hole in a line together. To lay down on the rocks, a stick, or any straight thing to guide my hand, exactly in the line of the beacon and the flagstaff. To take care, in doing this, that one end of the stick shall be at the edge of the rocks, on the side of them which overlooks the quicksand. To feel along the stick, among the seaweed (beginning from the end of the stick which points towards the beacon), for the Chain. To run my hand along the Chain, when found, until I come to the part of it which stretches over the edge of the rocks, down into the quicksand. And then, to pull the chain.”

The last words were underlined in the original.

As I finished them, Betteredge’s voice rose behind me—breathless with the very disease he had named.

“I can’t stand it any longer, Mr. Franklin. What does her letter say? For mercy’s sake, sir, tell us, what does her letter say?”

I handed him the letter and memorandum. He read the letter with little show of interest; but the memorandum worked on him at once, as a scent works on a hound.

“The Sergeant said it!” he cried. “From first to last, sir, the Sergeant said she had got a memorandum of the hiding-place. And here it is! Lord save us, Mr. Franklin, here is the secret that puzzled everybody, from the great Cuff downwards, ready and waiting, as one may say, to show itself to you! It’s the ebb now, sir, as anybody may see for themselves. How long will it be till the turn of the tide?”

He looked up and hailed a lad at some distance mending a net.

“Tammie Bright!”

“I hear you!”

“When’s the turn of the tide?”

“In an hour’s time.”

We both looked at our watches.

“We can go round by the coast, Mr. Franklin,” said Betteredge, “and get to the quicksand in that way with plenty of time to spare. What do you say, sir?”

“Come along!”

As we walked, I made Betteredge revive my memory of the circumstances connected with Rosanna Spearman at the time of Sergeant Cuff’s inquiry. The sequence came back, linked like the very chain we were going to seek: Rosanna’s journey to Frizinghall, when the whole household believed her ill in her room; her locked door at night, her candle kept burning till morning; her suspicious purchase from Mrs. Yolland of the japanned tin case and the two dog’s chains; the Sergeant’s conviction that she had hidden something at the Shivering Sand, and his complete ignorance of what that something might be.

The whole story, with its domestic facts and its secret hours, returned as plainly as if it had been spoken in my ear, when we reached the place and walked out together on the low ledge of rocks called the South Spit.

With Betteredge’s help I got myself into the right position to see the beacon and the coast-guard flagstaff exactly in a line. We laid my stick on the uneven rock, as neatly as we could, following the memorandum’s direction.

Then we looked at our watches again. There were nearly twenty minutes yet before the turn of the tide.

I proposed we should wait on the beach rather than on wet rocks slick with weed. When we reached the dry sand, I prepared to sit down—and, to my surprise, Betteredge prepared to leave me.

“What are you going away for?” I asked.

“Look at the letter again, sir, and you will see.”

A glance reminded me I was charged to make the discovery alone.

“It’s hard enough for me to leave you, at such a time as this,” said Betteredge. “But she died a dreadful death, poor soul—and I feel a kind of call on me, Mr. Franklin, to humour that fancy of hers. Besides,” he added, lowering his voice, “there’s nothing in the letter against your letting out the secret afterwards. I’ll hang about in the fir-plantation, and wait till you pick me up. Don’t be longer than you can help, sir. The detective-fever isn’t an easy disease to deal with, under these circumstances.”

He went.

The waiting—short by the clock—lengthened by the mind, until each minute felt like a separate trial. The only comfort I had, and it was no small one, was the habit of smoking. I lit a cigar and sat on the slope of the beach.

The morning had the clean brilliance of a well-scoured pan. Sunlight poured over sea and sand; the air, sharp with salt and weed, made the mere act of breathing feel like nourishment. The bay, lonely though it was, carried its own order of sounds: the small lick of water at stones, the wind threading the rigging of distant boats, the gulls’ cries—wild, untaught phrases belonging to no human grammar. There was no machinery here to grind one note without mercy; each sound appeared, spoke its piece, and fell silent again, leaving space for thought.

Even the quicksand, bare at low water, glittered with a false golden brightness, as if it could smile. And yet, as I watched, it betrayed itself.

The turn of the tide came before my cigar was finished. I saw first the preliminary heaving, and then the awful shiver that ran over the surface—like a living thing struck by fear. It was as if some spirit moved below, and the sand could not keep still over it. I threw away my cigar and went back to the rocks.

I followed Rosanna’s directions. Beginning at the end of the stick nearest the beacon, I felt along its line among the weed, touching rock after rock. More than half-way, nothing. Then, in a narrow fissure within reach of my forefinger, I felt metal—cold, harsh, unmistakable. The chain.

Trying to follow it by touch towards the quicksand, I found my hand stopped by a thick growth of seaweed fastened into the crevice—no doubt tightened in the time since Rosanna chose her hiding-place. I could neither pull up the weed nor force my hand through it.

Marking the spot where the stick’s end lay nearest the sand, I changed my plan. I would “sound” immediately under the rock, on the chance of striking the chain where it dipped down into the quicksand.

I took up the stick and knelt at the brink.

In that position my face was within a few feet of the quicksand’s surface. Nearness made it more hateful. Now and again the shivering fit ran through it, and the sight of the thing so close shook my nerves. A fancy seized me—horrible and absurd—that the dead woman might appear here to aid my search; that she might rise through the heaving sand and point. The notion chilled me in the warm sunlight. I own I shut my eyes at the moment the stick’s point entered the quicksand.

The next instant, before the stick could have sunk more than inches, terror fell away—and excitement took me all at once, like a hand tightening on the throat.

At my first blind attempt I had sounded right. The stick struck the chain.

Gripping the roots of the seaweed with my left hand, I stretched myself over the brink and felt with my right under the overhanging rock. My fingers found the chain. I drew it up without difficulty, and there, fastened to the end, was the japanned tin case.

The sea had rusted the chain so thoroughly that I could not unfasten it from the hasp. I braced the case between my knees and, with all my strength, forced off the cover.

A white substance filled the interior. I put in my hand: linen. As I drew it out, a crumpled letter came with it. I looked at the direction, saw my name, and put it at once in my pocket.

Then I pulled out the linen completely. It came in a thick roll, moulded to the shape of the case and preserved by its close confinement. I carried it to the dry sand and unrolled it, smoothing it out with my hands.

It was an article of dress. A nightgown.

On the upper side there were only folds and creases. I turned it. And there—instantly—was the smear of paint from the door of Rachel’s boudoir.

My eyes held to that stain as if it were a word in a language I had once spoken and forgotten; and my mind leapt back, from the brightness of the beach to that dark time of suspicion and inquiry. Sergeant Cuff’s very voice came to me, repeating his conclusion as though he stood at my shoulder.

“Find out whether there is any article of dress in this house with the stain of paint on it. Find out who that dress belongs to. Find out how the person can account for having been in the room, and smeared the paint between midnight and three in the morning. If the person can’t satisfy you, you haven’t far to look for the hand that took the Diamond.”

The words travelled over my memory again and again with a mechanical persistence, like a refrain one cannot stop hearing.

A voice broke in upon it, calling to me. I looked up. Betteredge, his patience conquered, was visible between the sandhills returning to the beach.

The sight of him brought me back sharply to the present and to the one remaining question. I had found the stain. To whom did the nightgown belong?

My first impulse was to take the letter from my pocket—the letter from the case. But a shorter way occurred to me. The nightgown itself would tell its owner, if it were marked.

I lifted it and searched for the mark.

I found it.

And I read—MY OWN NAME.

There were the familiar letters; there was the sun; there were the waters glittering; there was Betteredge coming nearer, step by step. I looked down again.

My own name—plainly confronting me. I had left London saying, “If time, pains, and money can do it, I will lay my hand on the thief who took the Moonstone.” I had penetrated the secret the quicksand had kept from every other living creature. And now, on the unanswerable evidence of the paint-stain, I had discovered myself as the thief.

I can set down nothing of what I felt. The shock did its work too completely: it stopped my mind as one stops a clock.

Betteredge told me afterwards that when he found me I laughed—laughed in his face—then thrust the nightgown into his hands and bade him read the riddle for himself. Of our talk on the beach I retain not so much as a shadow. The first clear picture that returns is of the fir plantation: Betteredge and I moving homeward between the dark, resinous stems; the sea-wind combing the needles overhead with its steady whisper; Betteredge speaking as if he had got hold of my sleeve and would not let me slip away again.

“You’ll face it, Mr. Franklin,” he said. “And I’ll face it too—after a glass of grog.”

So the scene shifts—like a lantern turned—to his little sitting-room. My proud resolve never to set foot again in Rachel’s house goes down before the coolness of the shaded place, the homely stillness, the sheltering smell of old books and clean linen, the quiet domestic order of it. Betteredge mixed the grog—spirits and water, nothing more; but he drew the water icy from the well, and the cold made a faint mist on the glass. He stirred it with a practised hand as if it were broth for a sick man, and the faint, sharp breath of the liquor rose to my nostrils and seemed to wake my nerves instead of drowning them. At any other time, at such an hour, it would have stupefied me. As it was, it tightened me—set me, so to speak, back upon my feet.

Betteredge, on his side, grew firmer with every mouthful—like one of those old country trees that straightens when the wind slackens.

There is something grotesque, when I look at myself now, in the first refuge I took. Instead of rushing to London, instead of casting myself into solitude, instead of straining my brain upon the hideous impossibility that confronted me, I sat at ten o’clock in the morning drinking spirits and water with an old servant. Yet the comfort of Betteredge’s familiar face—its honest wrinkles, its faithful anxiety—was real comfort; and his grog, coarse as it was, served me then as no delicate draught could have served. Let those who never waver from dignity and logic in any extremity, from cradle to grave, judge me.

“Now, Mr. Franklin, there’s one thing certain, at any rate,” Betteredge said, and flung the nightgown down upon the table between us, pointing at it as if it could hear. “He’s a liar, to begin with.”

That was not how it presented itself to my mind.

“I am as innocent of taking the Diamond as you are,” I answered. “But the witness stands against me. The paint is a fact. The name is a fact.”

He lifted my glass and pressed it back into my hand with the gentlest obstinacy.

“Facts?” he repeated. “Take a drop more grog, Mr. Franklin, and you’ll get over the weakness of believing in facts! Foul play, sir!” He lowered his voice, as if the very boards might betray us with a creak. “That’s how I read the riddle. Foul play somewhere—and you and I must find it out. Was there nothing else in the tin case when you put your hand in?”

His question struck, and with it came the recollection of the letter in my pocket. I drew it out and opened it. Page upon page, close-written. I turned impatiently to the end for the name.

“Rosanna Spearman.”

At the sight of it, a remembrance flashed up, and with it a suspicion—mean, quick, and hateful.

“Stop!” I cried. “Rosanna Spearman came to my aunt from a reformatory? Rosanna Spearman had once been a thief?”

“There’s no denying that, Mr. Franklin. What of it now, if you please?”

“What of it? How do we know she may not have stolen the Diamond after all? How do we know she may not have smeared my nightgown on purpose with the paint?”

Betteredge put his hand on my arm—stayed me before I could go further.

“You will be cleared of this, Mr. Franklin, beyond all doubt. But I hope you won’t be cleared in that way. See what the letter says, sir. In justice to the girl’s memory, see what it says.”

The earnestness of him rebuked me like kindness. “You shall judge her by her own words,” I said. “I’ll read it out.”

I began. The first lines were these:

“Sir—I have something to own to you. A confession which means much misery, may sometimes be made in very few words. This confession can be made in three words. I love you.”

The paper slipped from my fingers. I stared at Betteredge.

“In the name of Heaven,” I said, “what does it mean?”

He shrank from answering, as if a sound in the house—some distant closing of a door, some faint domestic stir—might overhear what ought to be spoken only to the dead.

“You and Limping Lucy were alone together this morning, sir,” he said at last. “Did she say nothing about Rosanna Spearman?”

“She never even mentioned Rosanna Spearman’s name.”

“Please to go back to the letter, Mr. Franklin. I tell you plainly, I can’t find it in my heart to distress you, after what you have had to bear already. Let her speak for herself, sir. And get on with your grog. For your own sake, get on with your grog.”

So I took up the letter again and went on, Betteredge listening in silence, the room holding itself quiet around us as if it, too, had manners and would not interrupt.

“It would be very disgraceful to me to tell you this, if I was a living woman when you read it. I shall be dead and gone, sir, when you find my letter. It is that which makes me bold. Not even my grave will be left to tell of me. I may own the truth—with the quicksand waiting to hide me when the words are written.

“Besides, you will find your nightgown in my hiding-place, with the smear of the paint on it; and you will want to know how it came to be hidden by me? and why I said nothing to you about it in my life-time? I have only one reason to give. I did these strange things, because I loved you.

“I won’t trouble you with much about myself, or my life, before you came to my lady’s house. Lady Verinder took me out of a reformatory. I had gone to the reformatory from the prison. I was put in the prison, because I was a thief. I was a thief, because my mother went on the streets when I was quite a little girl. My mother went on the streets, because the gentleman who was my father deserted her. There is no need to tell such a common story as this, at any length. It is told quite often enough in the newspapers.

“Lady Verinder was very kind to me, and Mr. Betteredge was very kind to me. Those two, and the matron at the reformatory, are the only good people I have ever met with in all my life. I might have got on in my place—not happily—but I might have got on, if you had not come visiting. I don’t blame you, sir. It’s my fault—all my fault.

“Do you remember when you came out on us from among the sandhills, that morning, looking for Mr. Betteredge? You were like a prince in a fairy-story. You were like a lover in a dream. You were the most adorable human creature I had ever seen. Something that felt like the happy life I had never led yet, leapt up in me at the instant I set eyes on you. Don’t laugh at this if you can help it. Oh, if I could only make you feel how serious it is to me!

“I went back to the house, and wrote your name and mine in my work-box, and drew a true lovers’ knot under them. Then, some devil—no, I ought to say some good angel—whispered to me, ‘Go and look in the glass.’ The glass told me—never mind what. I was too foolish to take the warning. I went on getting fonder and fonder of you, just as if I was a lady in your own rank of life, and the most beautiful creature your eyes ever rested on. I tried—oh, dear, how I tried—to get you to look at me. If you had known how I used to cry at night with the misery and the mortification of your never taking any notice of me, you would have pitied me perhaps, and have given me a look now and then to live on.

“It would have been no very kind look, perhaps, if you had known how I hated Miss Rachel. I believe I found out you were in love with her, before you knew it yourself. She used to give you roses to wear in your button-hole. Ah, Mr. Franklin, you wore my roses oftener than either you or she thought! The only comfort I had at that time, was putting my rose secretly in your glass of water, in place of hers—and then throwing her rose away.

“If she had been really as pretty as you thought her, I might have borne it better. No; I believe I should have been more spiteful against her still. Suppose you put Miss Rachel into a servant’s dress, and took her ornaments off? I don’t know what is the use of my writing in this way. It can’t be denied that she had a bad figure; she was too thin. But who can tell what the men like? And young ladies may behave in a manner which would cost a servant her place. It’s no business of mine. I can’t expect you to read my letter, if I write it in this way. But it does stir one up to hear Miss Rachel called pretty, when one knows all the time that it’s her dress does it, and her confidence in herself.

“Try not to lose patience with me, sir. I will get on as fast as I can to the time which is sure to interest you—the time when the Diamond was lost.

“But there is one thing which I have got it on my mind to tell you first.

“My life was not a very hard life to bear, while I was a thief. It was only when they had taught me at the reformatory to feel my own degradation, and to try for better things, that the days grew long and weary. Thoughts of the future forced themselves on me now. I felt the dreadful reproach that honest people—even the kindest of honest people—were to me in themselves. A heart-breaking sensation of loneliness kept with me, go where I might, and do what I might, and see what persons I might. It was my duty, I know, to try and get on with my fellow-servants in my new place. Somehow, I couldn’t make friends with them. They looked (or I thought they looked) as if they suspected what I had been. I don’t regret, far from it, having been roused to make the effort to be a reformed woman—but, indeed, indeed it was a weary life. You had come across it like a beam of sunshine at first—and then you too failed me. I was mad enough to love you; and I couldn’t even attract your notice. There was great misery—there really was great misery in that.

“Now I am coming to what I wanted to tell you. In those days of bitterness, I went two or three times, when it was my turn to go out, to my favourite place—the beach above the Shivering Sand. And I said to myself, ‘I think it will end here. When I can bear it no longer, I think it will end here.’ You will understand, sir, that the place had laid a kind of spell on me before you came. I had always had a notion that something would happen to me at the quicksand. But I had never looked at it, with the thought of its being the means of my making away with myself, till the time came of which I am now writing. Then I did think that here was a place which would end all my troubles for me in a moment or two—and hide me for ever afterwards.

“This is all I have to say about myself, reckoning from the morning when I first saw you, to the morning when the alarm was raised in the house that the Diamond was lost.

“I was so aggravated by the foolish talk among the women servants, all wondering who was to be suspected first; and I was so angry with you (knowing no better at that time) for the pains you took in hunting for the jewel, and sending for the police, that I kept as much as possible away by myself, until later in the day, when the officer from Frizinghall came to the house.

“Mr. Seegrave began, as you may remember, by setting a guard on the women’s bedrooms; and the women all followed him upstairs in a rage, to know what he meant by the insult he had put on them. I went with the rest, because if I had done anything different from the rest, Mr. Seegrave was the sort of man who would have suspected me directly. We found him in Miss Rachel’s room. He told us he wouldn’t have a lot of women there; and he pointed to the smear on the painted door, and said some of our petticoats had done the mischief, and sent us all downstairs again.

“After leaving Miss Rachel’s room, I stopped a moment on one of the landings, by myself, to see if I had got the paint-stain by any chance on my gown. Penelope Betteredge (the only one of the women with whom I was on friendly terms) passed, and noticed what I was about.

“‘You needn’t trouble yourself, Rosanna,’ she said. ‘The paint on Miss Rachel’s door has been dry for hours. If Mr. Seegrave hadn’t set a watch on our bedrooms, I might have told him as much. I don’t know what you think—I was never so insulted before in my life!’

“Penelope was a hot-tempered girl. I quieted her, and brought her back to what she had said about the paint on the door having been dry for hours.

“‘How do you know that?’ I asked.

“‘I was with Miss Rachel, and Mr. Franklin, all yesterday morning,’ Penelope said, ‘mixing the colours, while they finished the door. I heard Miss Rachel ask whether the door would be dry that evening, in time for the birthday company to see it. And Mr. Franklin shook his head, and said it wouldn’t be dry in less than twelve hours. It was long past luncheon-time—it was three o’clock before they had done. What does your arithmetic say, Rosanna? Mine says the door was dry by three this morning.’

“‘Did some of the ladies go upstairs yesterday evening to see it?’ I asked. ‘I thought I heard Miss Rachel warning them to keep clear of the door.’

“‘None of the ladies made the smear,’ Penelope answered. ‘I left Miss Rachel in bed at twelve last night. And I noticed the door, and there was nothing wrong with it then.’

“‘Oughtn’t you to mention this to Mr. Seegrave, Penelope?’

“‘I wouldn’t say a word to help Mr. Seegrave for anything that could be offered to me!’

“She went to her work, and I went to mine.

“My work, sir, was to make your bed, and to put your room tidy. It was the happiest hour I had in the whole day. I used to kiss the pillow on which your head had rested all night. No matter who has done it since, you have never had your clothes folded as nicely as I folded them for you. Of all the little knick-knacks in your dressing-case, there wasn’t one that had so much as a speck on it. You never noticed it, any more than you noticed me. I beg your pardon; I am forgetting myself. I will make haste, and go on again.

“Well, I went in that morning to do my work in your room. There was your nightgown tossed across the bed, just as you had thrown it off. I took it up to fold it—and I saw the stain of the paint from Miss Rachel’s door!

“I was so startled by the discovery that I ran out with the nightgown in my hand, and made for the back stairs, and locked myself into my own room, to look at it in a place where nobody could intrude and interrupt me.

“As soon as I got my breath again, I called to mind my talk with Penelope, and I said to myself, ‘Here’s the proof that he was in Miss Rachel’s sitting-room between twelve last night, and three this morning!’

“I shall not tell you in plain words what was the first suspicion that crossed my mind, when I had made that discovery. You would only be angry—and, if you were angry, you might tear my letter up and read no more of it.

“Let it be enough, if you please, to say only this. After thinking it over to the best of my ability, I made it out that the thing wasn’t likely, for a reason that I will tell you. If you had been in Miss Rachel’s sitting-room, at that time of night, with Miss Rachel’s knowledge (and if you had been foolish enough to forget to take care of the wet door) she would have reminded you—she would never have let you carry away such a witness against her, as the witness I was looking at now! At the same time, I own I was not completely certain in my own mind that I had proved my own suspicion to be wrong. You will not have forgotten that I have owned to hating Miss Rachel. Try to think, if you can, that there was a little of that hatred in all this. It ended in my determining to keep the nightgown, and to wait, and watch, and see what use I might make of it. At that time, please to remember, not the ghost of an idea entered my head that you had stolen the Diamond.”

There I stopped for the second time. I had listened to the dead woman’s voice—heard in it, against my will, a truth of suffering; and I can honestly say I was distressed. I had already repented of the suspicion I had cast on her memory before reading a line. But when I reached the passage about the nightgown, bitterness rose in me as if the very words had a sting.

“Read the rest for yourself,” I said, and passed the letter across the table. “If there is anything in it that I must look at, tell me as you go on.”

“I understand you, Mr. Franklin,” Betteredge answered. “It’s natural, sir, in you. And, God help us all!” he added under his breath, “it’s no less natural in her.”

This is what followed in the letter:

“Having determined to keep the nightgown, and to see what use my love, or my revenge (I hardly know which) could turn it to in the future, the next thing to discover was how to keep it without the risk of being found out.

“There was only one way—to make another nightgown exactly like it, before Saturday came, and brought the laundry-woman and her inventory to the house.

“I was afraid to put it off till next day (the Friday); being in doubt lest some accident might happen in the interval. I determined to make the new nightgown on that same day (the Thursday), while I could count, if I played my cards properly, on having my time to myself. The first thing to do (after locking up your nightgown in my drawer) was to go back to your bedroom—not so much to put it to rights (Penelope would have done that for me, if I had asked her) as to find out whether you had smeared off any of the paint-stain from your nightgown, on the bed, or on any piece of furniture in the room.

“I examined everything narrowly, and at last, I found a few streaks of the paint on the inside of your dressing-gown—not the linen dressing-gown you usually wore in that summer season, but a flannel dressing-gown which you had with you also. I suppose you felt chilly after walking to and fro in nothing but your nightdress, and put on the warmest thing you could find. At any rate, there were the stains, just visible, on the inside of the dressing-gown. I easily got rid of these by scraping away the stuff of the flannel. This done, the only proof left against you was the proof locked up in my drawer.

“I had just finished your room when I was sent for to be questioned by Mr. Seegrave, along with the rest of the servants. Next came the examination of all our boxes. And then followed the most extraordinary event of the day—to me—since I had found the paint on your nightgown. This event came out of the second questioning of Penelope Betteredge by Superintendent Seegrave.

“Penelope returned to us quite beside herself with rage at the manner in which Mr. Seegrave had treated her. He had hinted, beyond the possibility of mistaking him, that he suspected her of being the thief. We were all equally astonished at hearing this, and we all asked, Why?

“‘Because the Diamond was in Miss Rachel’s sitting-room,’ Penelope answered. ‘And because I was the last person in the sitting-room at night!’

“Almost before the words had left her lips, I remembered that another person had been in the sitting-room later than Penelope. That person was yourself. My head whirled round, and my thoughts were in dreadful confusion. In the midst of it all, something in my mind whispered to me that the smear on your nightgown might have a meaning entirely different to the meaning which I had given to it up to that time. ‘If the last person who was in the room is the person to be suspected,’ I thought to myself, ‘the thief is not Penelope, but Mr. Franklin Blake!’

“In the case of any other gentleman, I believe I should have been ashamed of suspecting him of theft, almost as soon as the suspicion had passed through my mind.

“But the bare thought that you had let yourself down to my level, and that I, in possessing myself of your nightgown, had also possessed myself of the means of shielding you from being discovered, and disgraced for life—I say, sir, the bare thought of this seemed to open such a chance before me of winning your good will, that I passed blindfold, as one may say, from suspecting to believing. I made up my mind, on the spot, that you had shown yourself the busiest of anybody in fetching the police, as a blind to deceive us all; and that the hand which had taken Miss Rachel’s jewel could by no possibility be any other hand than yours.

“The excitement of this new discovery of mine must, I think, have turned my head for a while. I felt such a devouring eagerness to see you—to try you with a word or two about the Diamond, and to make you look at me, and speak to me, in that way—that I put my hair tidy, and made myself as nice as I could, and went to you boldly in the library where I knew you were writing.

“You had left one of your rings upstairs, which made as good an excuse for my intrusion as I could have desired. But, oh, sir! if you have ever loved, you will understand how it was that all my courage cooled, when I walked into the room, and found myself in your presence. And then, you looked up at me so coldly, and you thanked me for finding your ring in such an indifferent manner, that my knees trembled under me, and I felt as if I should drop on the floor at your feet. When you had thanked me, you looked back, if you remember, at your writing. I was so mortified at being treated in this way, that I plucked up spirit enough to speak. I said, ‘This is a strange thing about the Diamond, sir.’ And you looked up again, and said, ‘Yes, it is!’ You spoke civilly (I can’t deny that); but still you kept a distance—a cruel distance between us. Believing, as I did, that you had got the lost Diamond hidden about you, while you were speaking, your coolness so provoked me that I got bold enough, in the heat of the moment, to give you a hint. I said, ‘They will never find the Diamond, sir, will they? No! nor the person who took it—I’ll answer for that.’ I nodded, and smiled at you, as much as to say, ‘I know!’ This time, you looked up at me with something like interest in your eyes; and I felt that a few more words on your side and mine might bring out the truth. Just at that moment, Mr. Betteredge spoilt it all by coming to the door. I knew his footstep, and I also knew that it was against his rules for me to be in the library at that time of day—let alone being there along with you. I had only just time to get out of my own accord, before he could come in and tell me to go. I was angry and disappointed; but I was not entirely without hope for all that. The ice, you see, was broken between us—and I thought I would take care, on the next occasion, that Mr. Betteredge was out of the way.

“When I got back to the servants’ hall, the bell was going for our dinner. Afternoon already! and the materials for making the new nightgown were still to be got! There was but one chance of getting them. I shammed ill at dinner; and so secured the whole of the interval from then till tea-time to my own use.”

After Betteredge had given me the name of Mr. Candy’s assistant, he shut the subject up as a housekeeper shuts a drawer that has been opened too long, and went back—grave, methodical, and a little offended at interruption—to the remainder of Rosanna Spearman’s letter.

I took my place at the window, where the air came in with the faint salt of the coast upon it, and with all the soft little noises of the grounds—leaf rubbing leaf in the shrubbery, a distant latch clicked by a servant, the far-off wash of the sea. Those domestic sounds, so harmless and so steady, had a strange power of pressing Ezra Jennings’s face and voice out of my mind, as if the house itself would allow no new presence to settle there. Little by little, the unaccountable impression he had made on me ebbed away; and the old current returned—my own case, my own danger, my own need.

My plan, such as I could hold to in that fevered state, came back like a refrain: to go to London that day; to put everything before Mr. Bruff; and, above all, to obtain—at any cost—a personal interview with Rachel. There was still more than an hour before the train. And there was a bare chance—no more—that Betteredge might find, in Rosanna’s unread pages, something serviceable to me before I left the very roof under which the Diamond had vanished.

Betteredge read on.

The letter ended thus:

“You have no need to be angry, Mr. Franklin, even if I did feel some little triumph at knowing that I held all your prospects in life in my own hands. Anxieties and fears soon came back to me. With the view Sergeant Cuff took of the loss of the Diamond, he would be sure to end in examining our linen and our dresses. There was no place in my room—there was no place in the house—which I could feel satisfied would be safe from him. How to hide the nightgown so that not even the Sergeant could find it? and how to do that without losing one moment of precious time?—these were not easy questions to answer. My uncertainties ended in my taking a way that may make you laugh. I undressed, and put the nightgown on me. You had worn it—and I had another little moment of pleasure in wearing it after you.

“The next news that reached us in the servants’ hall showed that I had not made sure of the nightgown a moment too soon. Sergeant Cuff wanted to see the washing-book.

“I found it, and took it to him in my lady’s sitting-room. The Sergeant and I had come across each other more than once in former days. I was certain he would know me again—and I was not certain of what he might do when he found me employed as servant in a house in which a valuable jewel had been lost. In this suspense, I felt it would be a relief to me to get the meeting between us over, and to know the worst of it at once.

“He looked at me as if I was a stranger, when I handed him the washing-book; and he was very specially polite in thanking me for bringing it. I thought those were both bad signs. There was no knowing what he might say of me behind my back; there was no knowing how soon I might not find myself taken in custody on suspicion, and searched. It was then time for your return from seeing Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite off by the railway; and I went to your favourite walk in the shrubbery, to try for another chance of speaking to you—the last chance, for all I knew to the contrary, that I might have.

“You never appeared; and, what was worse still, Mr. Betteredge and Sergeant Cuff passed by the place where I was hiding—and the Sergeant saw me.

“I had no choice, after that, but to return to my proper place and my proper work, before more disasters happened to me. Just as I was going to step across the path, you came back from the railway. You were making straight for the shrubbery, when you saw me—I am certain, sir, you saw me—and you turned away as if I had got the plague, and went into the house.”

(The writer is entirely mistaken. I never noticed her. I meant to turn into the shrubbery; but remembered, at the same moment, that my aunt might wish to see me after my return from the railway, and went in.)

“I made the best of my way indoors again, returning by the servants’ entrance. There was nobody in the laundry-room at that time; and I sat down there alone. I have told you already of the thoughts which the Shivering Sand put into my head. Those thoughts came back to me now. I wondered in myself which it would be harder to do, if things went on in this manner—to bear Mr. Franklin Blake’s indifference to me, or to jump into the quicksand and end it for ever in that way?

“It’s useless to ask me to account for my own conduct, at this time. I try—and I can’t understand it myself.

“Why didn’t I stop you, when you avoided me in that cruel manner? Why didn’t I call out, ‘Mr. Franklin, I have got something to say to you; it concerns yourself, and you must, and shall, hear it?’ You were at my mercy—I had got the whip-hand of you, as they say. And better than that, I had the means (if I could only make you trust me) of being useful to you in the future. Of course, I never supposed that you—a gentleman—had stolen the Diamond for the mere pleasure of stealing it. No. Penelope had heard Miss Rachel, and I had heard Mr. Betteredge, talk about your extravagance and your debts. It was plain enough to me that you had taken the Diamond to sell it, or pledge it, and so to get the money of which you stood in need. Well! I could have told you of a man in London who would have advanced a good large sum on the jewel, and who would have asked no awkward questions about it either.

“Why didn’t I speak to you! why didn’t I speak to you!

“I wonder whether the risks and difficulties of keeping the nightgown were as much as I could manage, without having other risks and difficulties added to them? This might have been the case with some women—but how could it be the case with me? In the days when I was a thief, I had run fifty times greater risks, and found my way out of difficulties to which this difficulty was mere child’s play. I had been apprenticed, as you may say, to frauds and deceptions—some of them on such a grand scale, and managed so cleverly, that they became famous, and appeared in the newspapers. Was such a little thing as the keeping of the nightgown likely to weigh on my spirits, and to set my heart sinking within me, at the time when I ought to have spoken to you? What nonsense to ask the question! The thing couldn’t be.

“Where is the use of my dwelling in this way on my own folly? The plain truth is plain enough, surely? Behind your back, I loved you with all my heart and soul. Before your face—there’s no denying it—I was frightened of you; frightened of making you angry with me; frightened of what you might say to me (though you had taken the Diamond) if I presumed to tell you that I had found it out. I had gone as near to it as I dared when I spoke to you in the library. You had not turned your back on me then. You had not started away from me as if I had got the plague. I tried to provoke myself into feeling angry with you, and to rouse up my courage in that way. No! I couldn’t feel anything but the misery and the mortification of it. ‘You’re a plain girl; you have got a crooked shoulder; you’re only a housemaid—what do you mean by attempting to speak to Me?’ You never uttered a word of that, Mr. Franklin; but you said it all to me, nevertheless! Is such madness as this to be accounted for? No. There is nothing to be done but to confess it, and let it be.

“I ask your pardon, once more, for this wandering of my pen. There is no fear of its happening again. I am close at the end now.

“The first person who disturbed me by coming into the empty room was Penelope. She had found out my secret long since, and she had done her best to bring me to my senses—and done it kindly too.

“‘Ah!’ she said, ‘I know why you’re sitting here, and fretting, all by yourself. The best thing that can happen for your advantage, Rosanna, will be for Mr. Franklin’s visit here to come to an end. It’s my belief that he won’t be long now before he leaves the house.’

“In all my thoughts of you I had never thought of your going away. I couldn’t speak to Penelope. I could only look at her.

“‘I’ve just left Miss Rachel,’ Penelope went on. ‘And a hard matter I have had of it to put up with her temper. She says the house is unbearable to her with the police in it; and she’s determined to speak to my lady this evening, and to go to her Aunt Ablewhite tomorrow. If she does that, Mr. Franklin will be the next to find a reason for going away, you may depend on it!’

“I recovered the use of my tongue at that. ‘Do you mean to say Mr. Franklin will go with her?’ I asked.

“‘Only too gladly, if she would let him; but she won’t. He has been made to feel her temper; he is in her black books too—and that after having done all he can to help her, poor fellow! No! no! If they don’t make it up before tomorrow, you will see Miss Rachel go one way, and Mr. Franklin another. Where he may betake himself to I can’t say. But he will never stay here, Rosanna, after Miss Rachel has left us.’

“I managed to master the despair I felt at the prospect of your going away. To own the truth, I saw a little glimpse of hope for myself if there was really a serious disagreement between Miss Rachel and you. ‘Do you know,’ I asked, ‘what the quarrel is between them?’

“‘It is all on Miss Rachel’s side,’ Penelope said. ‘And, for anything I know to the contrary, it’s all Miss Rachel’s temper, and nothing else. I am loth to distress you, Rosanna; but don’t run away with the notion that Mr. Franklin is ever likely to quarrel with her. He’s a great deal too fond of her for that!’

“She had only just spoken those cruel words when there came a call to us from Mr. Betteredge. All the indoor servants were to assemble in the hall. And then we were to go in, one by one, and be questioned in Mr. Betteredge’s room by Sergeant Cuff.

“It came to my turn to go in, after her ladyship’s maid and the upper housemaid had been questioned first. Sergeant Cuff’s inquiries—though he wrapped them up very cunningly—soon showed me that those two women (the bitterest enemies I had in the house) had made their discoveries outside my door, on the Tuesday afternoon, and again on the Thursday night. They had told the Sergeant enough to open his eyes to some part of the truth. He rightly believed me to have made a new nightgown secretly, but he wrongly believed the paint-stained nightgown to be mine. I felt satisfied of another thing, from what he said, which it puzzled me to understand. He suspected me, of course, of being concerned in the disappearance of the Diamond. But, at the same time, he let me see—purposely, as I thought—that he did not consider me as the person chiefly answerable for the loss of the jewel. He appeared to think that I had been acting under the direction of somebody else. Who that person might be, I couldn’t guess then, and can’t guess now.

“In this uncertainty, one thing was plain—that Sergeant Cuff was miles away from knowing the whole truth. You were safe as long as the nightgown was safe—and not a moment longer.

“I quite despair of making you understand the distress and terror which pressed upon me now. It was impossible for me to risk wearing your nightgown any longer. I might find myself taken off, at a moment’s notice, to the police court at Frizinghall, to be charged on suspicion, and searched accordingly. While Sergeant Cuff still left me free, I had to choose—and at once—between destroying the nightgown, or hiding it in some safe place, at some safe distance from the house.

“If I had only been a little less fond of you, I think I should have destroyed it. But oh! how could I destroy the only thing I had which proved that I had saved you from discovery? If we did come to an explanation together, and if you suspected me of having some bad motive, and denied it all, how could I win upon you to trust me, unless I had the nightgown to produce? Was it wronging you to believe, as I did and do still, that you might hesitate to let a poor girl like me be the sharer of your secret, and your accomplice in the theft which your money-troubles had tempted you to commit? Think of your cold behaviour to me, sir, and you will hardly wonder at my unwillingness to destroy the only claim on your confidence and your gratitude which it was my fortune to possess.

“I determined to hide it; and the place I fixed on was the place I knew best—the Shivering Sand.

“As soon as the questioning was over, I made the first excuse that came into my head, and got leave to go out for a breath of fresh air. I went straight to Cobb’s Hole, to Mr. Yolland’s cottage. His wife and daughter were the best friends I had. Don’t suppose I trusted them with your secret—I have trusted nobody. All I wanted was to write this letter to you, and to have a safe opportunity of taking the nightgown off me. Suspected as I was, I could do neither of those things with any sort of security, at the house.

“And now I have nearly got through my long letter, writing it alone in Lucy Yolland’s bedroom. When it is done, I shall go downstairs with the nightgown rolled up, and hidden under my cloak. I shall find the means I want for keeping it safe and dry in its hiding-place, among the litter of old things in Mrs. Yolland’s kitchen. And then I shall go to the Shivering Sand—don’t be afraid of my letting my footmarks betray me!—and hide the nightgown down in the sand, where no living creature can find it without being first let into the secret by myself.

“And, when that’s done, what then?

“Then, Mr. Franklin, I shall have two reasons for making another attempt to say the words to you which I have not said yet. If you leave the house, as Penelope believes you will leave it, and if I haven’t spoken to you before that, I shall lose my opportunity forever. That is one reason. Then, again, there is the comforting knowledge—if my speaking does make you angry—that I have got the nightgown ready to plead my cause for me as nothing else can. That is my other reason. If these two together don’t harden my heart against the coldness which has hitherto frozen it up (I mean the coldness of your treatment of me), there will be the end of my efforts—and the end of my life.

“Yes. If I miss my next opportunity—if you are as cruel as ever, and if I feel it again as I have felt it already—good-bye to the world which has grudged me the happiness that it gives to others. Good-bye to life, which nothing but a little kindness from you can ever make pleasurable to me again. Don’t blame yourself, sir, if it ends in this way. But try—do try—to feel some forgiving sorrow for me! I shall take care that you find out what I have done for you, when I am past telling you of it myself. Will you say something kind of me then—in the same gentle way that you have when you speak to Miss Rachel? If you do that, and if there are such things as ghosts, I believe my ghost will hear it, and tremble with the pleasure of it.

“It’s time I left off. I am making myself cry. How am I to see my way to the hiding-place if I let these useless tears come and blind me?

“Besides, why should I look at the gloomy side? Why not believe, while I can, that it will end well after all? I may find you in a good humour tonight—or, if not, I may succeed better tomorrow morning. I sha’n’t improve my plain face by fretting—shall I? Who knows but I may have filled all these weary long pages of paper for nothing? They will go, for safety’s sake (never mind now for what other reason) into the hiding-place along with the nightgown. It has been hard, hard work writing my letter. Oh! if we only end in understanding each other, how I shall enjoy tearing it up!

“I beg to remain, sir, your true lover and humble servant,

“ROSANNA SPEARMAN.”

Betteredge finished without a word. He put the letter back into its envelope with the care of a man laying away something that burns the fingers, and sat with his head bowed, eyes fixed on the carpet, as if he were listening—not to the room, not to me, but to the long after-sound of misery that the poor girl’s handwriting left behind it.

“Betteredge,” I said, “is there any hint to guide me at the end of the letter?”

He looked up slowly, and sighed, as one might sigh over a kettle that will not boil though the fire is good.

“There is nothing to guide you, Mr. Franklin,” he answered. “If you take my advice you will keep the letter in the cover till these present anxieties of yours have come to an end. It will sorely distress you, whenever you read it. Don’t read it now.”

I put the letter away in my pocket-book.

There was, in truth, a reason for sparing myself at a time when my fortitude had already been tried like overworked linen—rubbed and wrung again and again, till the very fibres cried out. Twice, that unhappy creature had made her last attempt to speak to me. Twice, by a fatality of accident and good intention, I had repulsed her.

On the Friday night, as Betteredge has already related elsewhere, she found me alone at the billiard-table. Her manner and her words would have suggested to any man, in my situation, that she was about to confess guilty knowledge of the Diamond’s disappearance. For her own sake, I showed no eager interest; for her own sake, I kept my eyes on the balls instead of on her face. And what came of it? I sent her away—wounded where she lived.

On the Saturday, again—on the day when she must have foreseen, from Penelope’s warning, that my departure was near—the same cruel mischance pursued us. She tried once more to meet me in the shrubbery walk, and found me there with Betteredge and Sergeant Cuff. In her hearing, the Sergeant—working some underhand purpose of his own—appealed to my interest in Rosanna Spearman. And again, for her own sake as I believed, I met him with a flat denial, declaring, loudly enough that she might hear me, that I felt “no interest whatever in Rosanna Spearman.” At those words—meant only to warn her off, and keep her from forcing a private confidence upon me—she turned away and left us: cautioned of danger, as I then thought; self-doomed to destruction, as I know now.

From that point the chain had already been traced, link by link, to the astounding discovery at the quicksand. The circle of retrospect closed on itself. I could leave the miserable story of Rosanna Spearman—so wretched that even now it stings me to touch it—to speak, by its own silence and horror, what I have no heart to say. And I might pass on from the suicide at the Shivering Sand, with its strange and terrible influence on my present position and future prospects, to the claims of the living, and to events already at work—slow, patient, and remorseless as tide and time—preparing my weary journey out of darkness into light.

I went down to the railway station with Gabriel Betteredge at my side; and I carried, like two contraband articles against my own peace, Rosanna Spearman’s letter in my pocket and the nightgown—folded tight, as if linen could be made to hold its tongue—in a little bag for Mr. Bruff’s inspection before I slept.

We left the house in a silence so absolute that it seemed to have weight. For the first time, in all my knowledge of him, Betteredge walked with me and offered no homely proverb, no comfortable commonplace—nothing. The only sound was the country morning itself: damp leaves whispering where the hedge met the road, a lark turning its small music over and over as if it were kneading it into the air, and—further off—the faint, mechanical insistence of wheels and iron that told of the line. I noticed it the more because the human voice was absent; and I thought, with a bitterness I could not dismiss, how easily a man’s inward life is altered when the world around him changes its tune.

I had questions that burned my mouth. Once we were clear of the lodge gates I forced speech between us.

“Before I go to London,” I said, “I have two questions to ask you. They relate to myself—and I believe they will surprise you.”

“If they’ll only put that poor creature’s letter out of my head, Mr. Franklin,” he answered, “they may surprise me till kingdom come. Begin, sir.”

“My first question is this. Was I drunk on the night of Rachel’s birthday?”

“You drunk!” Betteredge stopped short, as if I had accused the sun of rising in the west. “Why, it’s the great defect of your character, Mr. Franklin, that you only drink with your dinner, and never touch a drop afterwards!”

“But the birthday was a special occasion. I might have abandoned my habits.”

He considered, searching his memory with that slow, honest precision of his—like a cook who tastes again, for certainty, what he has already judged.

“You did go out of your habits,” he said at last. “And I’ll tell you how. You looked wretchedly ill. We persuaded you to take a drop of brandy-and-water to cheer you.”

“I am not used to it. It is quite possible—”

“Wait a bit. I knew you weren’t used too. I poured you out half a wineglass of our fifty-year-old Cognac—real noble liquor, sir—and (more shame to me!) I drowned it in near a tumbler-full of cold water. A child couldn’t have got drunk on it—let alone a grown man.”

He spoke of that Cognac as people speak of old harvests, as if time had been stored in it and could be wasted by careless dilution; and his very indignation helped me. I could trust his palate and his memory. If Betteredge said no man could be made drunk on it, then drunkenness was not my refuge.

I tried the second question, the one I had kept back like the sharpest knife.

“Before I was sent abroad, Betteredge, you saw a great deal of me when I was a boy. Tell me plainly—do you remember anything strange of me after I had gone to bed? Did you ever find me walking in my sleep?”

He stopped again, looked at me steadily, nodded once as if he had caught the scent of my thought, and resumed his walk.

“I see your drift now,” he said. “You’re trying to account for how you got the paint on your nightgown without knowing it. It won’t do, sir. You’re miles away from the truth. Walk in your sleep? You never did such a thing in your life.”

I knew, even as he spoke, that he must be right. My life had never been solitary enough for such a habit to escape notice. At home, abroad—always there had been eyes enough, voices enough, rooms shared, servants passing, friends close at hand. Yet still, with a kind of miserable obstinacy that was not reason but mere self-defence, I clung to those two explanations—drunkenness or sleep-walking—because they were the only ones that allowed me to remain, in my own sight, the man I believed myself to be.

Betteredge, who could read a man’s stubbornness in his shoulders, broke both my props with a bluntness that was almost mercy.

“Let’s try it another way, sir. Keep your own opinion and see where it takes you. If we’re to believe the nightgown—which I don’t for one—you not only smeared the paint off the door without knowing it, but you also took the Diamond without knowing it. Is that right so far?”

“Quite right. Go on.”

“Very good. Say you were drunk, or asleep on your feet, when you took the jewel. That accounts for that night and the next morning. But how does it account for what’s happened since? The Diamond’s been taken to London since then. The Diamond’s been pledged to Mr. Luker since then. Did you do those things without knowing it too? Were you drunk when I saw you off in the pony-chaise that Saturday evening? Did you walk in your sleep to Mr. Luker’s when the train had brought you up? Excuse me saying it, Mr. Franklin, but this business has upset you. You’re not fit yet to judge for yourself. The sooner you lay your head alongside Mr. Bruff’s head, the sooner you’ll see your way out of the dead-lock you’ve got into.”

We reached the station with barely time. I gave him my London address, told him to write if needful, and promised to send him any news I could.

Just as I turned to bid him goodbye, my eye caught the book-and-newspaper stall. There, like an odd figure repeated by a dream that will not be shaken off, stood Mr. Candy’s assistant again—the man with the piebald hair. He was speaking to the stall-keeper. He looked up; our eyes met in the same instant. Ezra Jennings lifted his hat to me. I returned the salute and got into a carriage as the train began to move.

It was a relief—ridiculous as it was—to fasten on anything that seemed, personally, of no importance. The rattle of the wheels, the iron singing under us, the chorus of stations swallowed and flung behind, all made a kind of travelling music; and in that music my mind kept catching, again and again, on the absurdity that I should see the man twice in one day. Yet even that thought had its use: it diverted me from the more dreadful repetition—Rachel’s face, Rachel’s silence, and the white linen with the smear of paint that looked, to my fancy, like a stain that had soaked through to the bone.

I reached London too late to find Mr. Bruff in chambers. I drove to his private house at Hampstead and disturbed him dozing in his dining-room—his favourite pug on his lap, a bottle of wine at his elbow, the air warm with that comfortable domestic odour of a well-kept room: polished wood, cloth, old paper, and the faint, civilised sharpness of wine waiting to be poured.

I told my story. When I had finished, he showed me what he thought, not by words at first, but by what he did. He ordered lights and strong tea into his study; and he sent a message to the ladies of his family that we were not to be disturbed on any pretence.

Then he examined the nightgown, as if it were an exhibit laid out on a table in court; and after that he read Rosanna Spearman’s letter through from beginning to end. Only when the paper was folded up again did he speak.

“Franklin Blake,” he said, “this is a very serious matter—in more respects than one. In my opinion it concerns Rachel quite as nearly as it concerns you. Her extraordinary conduct is no mystery now. She believes you have stolen the Diamond.”

The words, cool from his lips, struck like a hand on a bruise. I had shrunk from arriving at that conclusion by my own reasoning; but the conclusion had been waiting for me all the same. My resolve to see Rachel rested, in truth, on that ground.

“The first step in this investigation,” Mr. Bruff went on, “is to appeal to Rachel. She has been silent from motives which I, who know her character, can readily understand. But it is impossible to submit to that silence any longer. She must be persuaded to tell us—or she must be forced to tell us—on what grounds she bases her belief that you took the Moonstone. The chances are that the whole case, serious as it seems now, will tumble to pieces if we can only break through Rachel’s inveterate reserve and make her speak.”

“That is comforting for me,” I said; and the bitterness broke through. “I should like to know—”

“You would like to know how I justify it,” he interposed. “I can tell you in two minutes. Understand, in the first place, I look at this from a lawyer’s point of view. It is a question of evidence. Very well. The evidence breaks down at the outset on one important point.”

“On what point?”

“You shall hear. I admit the name-mark proves the nightgown to be yours. I admit the paint-mark proves the nightgown made the smear on Rachel’s door. But what evidence is there to prove that you are the person who wore it on the night when the Diamond was lost?”

The objection—simple, plain, deadly—fell exactly where my own mind had felt the weakness, without daring to seize it. Mr. Bruff took up Rosanna’s letter again.

“I can understand,” he continued, “that this confession is distressing to you. I can understand you may hesitate to analyse it impartially. I am not in your position. I can bring my professional experience to bear on it as on any document.

“Without alluding to the woman’s career as a thief, I will merely remark that her own letter proves her an adept at deception; and I argue from that that I am justified in suspecting she has not told the whole truth. I won’t start any theory yet; I will only say this. If Rachel has suspected you on the evidence of the nightgown only, the chances are ninety-nine to a hundred that Rosanna Spearman showed it to her. In that case, we have Rosanna’s own admission that she was jealous of Rachel; her own confession that she changed the roses; her own acknowledgment that she saw a glimpse of hope for herself in the prospect of a quarrel between Rachel and you.

“I don’t stop to ask who took the Moonstone—as a means to her end, Rosanna Spearman would have taken fifty Moonstones. I only say that the disappearance of the jewel gave this reclaimed thief, who was in love with you, the opportunity of setting you and Rachel at variance for the rest of your lives. She had not decided on destroying herself then, remember; and having the opportunity, I distinctly assert that it was in her character, and in her position, to take it. What do you say to that?”

“Some such suspicion crossed my mind,” I admitted, “as soon as I opened the letter.”

“Exactly. And then you pitied the poor creature, and couldn’t find it in your heart to suspect her. Does you credit, my dear sir—does you credit!”

“But suppose it turns out I did wear the nightgown? What then?”

“I don’t see how the fact can be proved,” he answered. “But assuming the proof possible, the vindication of your innocence would be no easy matter. We won’t go into that now. Let us wait and see whether Rachel hasn’t suspected you on the evidence of the nightgown only.”

“Good God—how coolly you talk of Rachel suspecting me!” I burst out. “What right has she to suspect me, on any evidence, of being a thief?”

“A very sensible question,” said Mr. Bruff. “Rather hotly put—but worth considering. What puzzles you puzzles me. Search your memory and tell me this. Did anything happen while you were staying at the house—not to shake Rachel’s belief in your honour, of course—but to shake her belief, no matter with how little reason, in your principles generally?”

The question opened, like a trap-door, on something I had forgotten in the worse confusion that followed. I started to my feet, the blood rushing to my face.

There had been an incident—wretched enough at the time, and now more wretched for the uses it might have been put to. I told him.

I had been foolish enough—straitened for money, as I so often was—to accept a loan in Paris from the keeper of a small restaurant, a man who knew my tastes and my face as well as he knew his own kitchen. I could remember the place as one remembers a dish: the steam of stock in the back, the sharp scent of vinegar and chopped herbs, the crust of bread always warm as if it had just been broken, the talk in a quick dialect that made even quarrelling sound like singing. A time had been fixed for repayment; and when the time came I found myself, like thousands of honest men, unable to keep my engagement. I sent him a bill. My name was unfortunately too well known on such papers; he failed to negotiate it. His affairs fell into disorder; bankruptcy stared him in the face; and a relative of his—a French lawyer—came to England to find me and insist on payment.

He was violent; and he took the wrong way with me. We quarrelled loudly. My aunt and Rachel were in the next room. Lady Verinder came in and insisted on knowing what it meant. The Frenchman produced his credentials and declared me responsible for the ruin of a poor man who had trusted to my honour. My aunt paid him on the spot and sent him away. She knew me too well to take his view, but she was shocked at my carelessness—and justly angry that I had placed myself in a position which, but for her, might have become disgraceful.

Whether Rachel heard, or her mother told her, I could not say. Rachel took her own romantic, high-flown view. I was “heartless,” “dishonourable,” without “principle”—there was “no knowing what I might do next.” The breach lasted the whole of the following day. The day after, I made my peace and thought no more of it.

Had she remembered it, at the critical moment when her faith in me was assailed again, and far more terribly?

Mr. Bruff answered at once, gravely.

“It would have its effect on her mind. And I wish, for your sake, it had not happened. Still, we have discovered there was a predisposing influence against you. That clears one uncertainty out of the way. I see nothing more we can do now. Our next step must be the step that takes us to Rachel.”

He rose and walked thoughtfully up and down the room, the carpet taking his footfall into itself, the kettle in the next room beginning to murmur as if the house, too, had a voice to contribute. Twice I was on the point of telling him I had resolved to see Rachel myself; twice I held back, unwilling to thrust my own impatience upon his age and his habits of caution.

“The grand difficulty,” he resumed, “is how to make her show her whole mind without reserve. Have you any suggestions?”

“I have made up my mind,” I said, “to speak to Rachel myself.”

“You!” He stopped and stared as if I had proposed to conduct the case by throwing dice. “You, of all the people in the world!”

He checked himself, took another turn, and then his face altered with that quick professional honesty which can change its plan when a new chance appears.

“Wait a little,” he said. “In cases of this extraordinary kind, the rash way is sometimes the best way.”

He weighed it for a minute. Then he decided.

“Nothing venture, nothing have. You have a chance in your favour which I don’t possess—and you shall be the first to try the experiment.”

“A chance in my favour?” I repeated.

For the first time since my arrival, his face softened into a smile.

“This is how it stands,” he said. “I tell you fairly I don’t trust your discretion, and I don’t trust your temper. But I do trust in Rachel still preserving, in some remote little corner of her heart, a certain perverse weakness for you. Touch that—and trust the consequences for the fullest disclosures that can flow from a woman’s lips. The question is—how are you to see her?”

“She has been a guest of yours,” I answered. “May I suggest—if nothing is said about me beforehand—that I might see her here?”

“Cool!” said Mr. Bruff; and with that single comment he began pacing again.

“In plain English,” he resumed, “my house is to be turned into a trap to catch Rachel, with a bait to tempt her, in the shape of an invitation from my wife and daughters. If you were anybody else but Franklin Blake, and if this matter was one atom less serious than it is, I should refuse point-blank. As things are, I firmly believe Rachel will live to thank me for turning traitor to her in my old age. Consider me your accomplice. Rachel shall be asked to spend the day here; and you shall receive due notice.”

“When? Tomorrow?”

“Tomorrow won’t give time to get her answer. Say the day after.”

“How shall I hear from you?”

“Stay at home all the morning and expect me to call.”

I thanked him with all the gratitude I felt—gratitude made fierce by fear. I declined his kind invitation to sleep at Hampstead and returned to my lodgings.

Of the next day I can only say it was the longest day of my life. Innocent as I knew myself, certain as I was that the vile imputation must sooner or later be cleared away, there was still in me a sense of self-abasement which made me shrink from every friend’s face. We hear, commonly from superficial observers, that guilt can look like innocence. I believe the truer axiom is that innocence can look like guilt. I caused myself to be denied to every caller, and only ventured out when night had muffled the streets and softened their harsh, incessant London sound—the clatter and shout and rumble that seemed, in those hours, to grind every private thought into the same public dust.

The next morning Mr. Bruff surprised me at the breakfast-table. He placed a large key in my hand and announced, with the dry candour of a man who dislikes his own stratagems even when they serve justice, that he felt ashamed of himself for the first time in his life.

“Is she coming?” I asked.

“She is coming today—to lunch, and to spend the afternoon with my wife and my girls.”

“Are Mrs. Bruff and your daughters in the secret?”

“Inevitably. But women, as you may have observed, have no principles. My family don’t feel my pangs of conscience. The end being to bring you and Rachel together again, my wife and daughters pass over the means employed as composedly as if they were Jesuits.”

“I am infinitely obliged to them. What is this key?”

“The key of the gate in my back-garden wall. Be there at three this afternoon. Let yourself in; make your way through by the conservatory door. Cross the small drawing-room, and open the door in front of you into the music-room. There you will find Rachel—and find her alone.”

“How can I thank you?”

“I’ll tell you how. Don’t blame me for what happens afterwards.”

With that he left me.

The hours that remained were as long as my vigil the day before. To occupy myself I opened my letters. One was from Betteredge. I tore it open, hungry for any word from home; and found, to my disappointment, an apology warning me to expect no news of importance.

In the next sentence the everlasting Ezra Jennings appeared again—like a refrain returning in spite of the singer. He had stopped Betteredge on the way out of the station and asked who I was. On being told, he mentioned having seen me to his master, Mr. Candy. Mr. Candy, hearing it, had driven over himself to Betteredge to express regret at our missing each other. He had a reason, he said, for particularly wishing to speak with me; and when I was next near Frizinghall, he begged I would let him know.

Apart from Betteredge’s affectionate philosophy, that was the substance. The faithful old man confessed, in his own way, that he had written “mainly for the pleasure of writing” to me.

I crumpled the letter and thrust it into my pocket. Even the odd recurrence of Ezra Jennings could not hold my mind then. Everything in me flowed towards the coming interview with Rachel as surely as all kitchen smells find their way, sooner or later, to the one open door.

As Hampstead church struck three, I fitted Mr. Bruff’s key into the lock of the gate in the garden wall.

When I first stepped into the garden, and turned to secure the door behind me, I felt—shameful as it was—a guilty doubtfulness about what might happen next. I looked furtively to either side, suspecting an unseen witness, some lurking eye, some ear eager for scandal.

But there was no one. The walks lay empty. The only observers were the birds shifting in the shrubs and the bees busy in their small commerce with the flowers. Their humming made a soft, steady undertone, as if the place had its own harmless industry and could not understand the harsher traffic of human motives.

I crossed the garden, entered the conservatory, and passed through the small drawing-room. As my hand went to the door opposite I heard a few plaintive chords on the piano within—idly struck, tentative, like a voice trying its own note before it speaks. She had often touched the instrument so when I stayed at her mother’s—half music, half thought.

I was obliged to wait, to steady myself. Past and present rose together, side by side, and the contrast shook me as no accusation had done. After a minute I forced my courage into place, like a man setting a dish before guests though his hands tremble.

I opened the door.

At the instant I showed myself in the doorway, Rachel rose from the piano as if the last chord she had struck had snapped the thread that held her sitting. I shut the door behind me; the click went through the room like a spoon rapped against a crystal glass—small, sharp, and somehow loud enough to change the air.

We faced each other with the long drawing-room between us, and silence between us longer still. She looked at me with all of her—every faculty gathered up into sight alone—so that even her breathing seemed to wait for what her eyes should decide.

A misgiving struck me that I had come too suddenly. I stepped forward, and kept my voice low, as one speaks in a house where someone sleeps.

“Rachel.”

That single sound brought her back to life: colour warmed in her cheeks as if heat had been turned under a pan, and her limbs obeyed her again. She came towards me without a word, slowly—drawn forward as though some influence outside her will pulled her along. With every step her eyes brightened, quick and waking. I forgot why I was there. I forgot the foul suspicion hanging on my name. I saw only the woman I loved, coming nearer and nearer, trembling, wavering—until I could bear no more restraint. I caught her to me and covered her face with kisses.

For a moment—only a moment—I believed she returned them; believed she too had been carried away from everything except the old tenderness. Then memory seized her like a hand at the throat.
