With a cry that was horror itself, and with a strength that might have flung a heavier man than I, she thrust me back. Her eyes hardened into merciless anger; her mouth took on merciless contempt. She looked me over as if I had been some stranger who had dared to touch her.

“You coward!” she said. “You mean, miserable, heartless coward!”

Those were her first words.

“I remember a time, Rachel,” I answered, “when you could have told me I had offended you in a worthier way than that. I beg your pardon.”

Bitterness crept into my voice in spite of me. Her eyes, which had turned away, came back unwillingly, and she answered low, with a sullen submission I had never heard from her.

“Perhaps there is some excuse for me,” she said. “After what you have done, is it a manly action to find your way to me as you have found it today? It seems a cowardly experiment—to try an experiment on my weakness for you. It seems a cowardly surprise—to surprise me into letting you kiss me. But that is only a woman’s view. I ought to have known it couldn’t be your view. I should have done better if I had controlled myself, and said nothing.”

The apology humiliated me worse than the insult.

“If my honour was not in your hands,” I said, “I would leave you this instant, and never see you again. You speak of what I have done. What have I done?”

“What have you done! You ask that question of me?”

“I ask it.”

“I have kept your infamy a secret,” she said, “and I have suffered the consequences of concealing it. Have I no claim to be spared the insult of your asking me what you have done? Is all sense of gratitude dead in you? You were once a gentleman. You were once dear to my mother, and dearer still to me—”

Her voice broke. She dropped into a chair, turned her back on me, and covered her face.

I waited until I could trust myself. In that pause I scarcely knew which pricked me more—the sting of her contempt, or the proud resolve that shut me out from all fellowship with her distress.

“If you will not speak first,” I said, “I must. I have come here with something serious to say. Will you do me the common justice of listening while I say it?”

She neither moved nor answered. I did not appeal again; I did not step one inch nearer to her chair. Pride met pride. I told her, from beginning to end, what I had discovered at the Shivering Sand, and all that had led to it. It took time; she never looked round; she never spoke.

I held my temper fast, because my whole future depended on it. The time had come to try Mr. Bruff’s theory. In the breathless interest of that last experiment, I moved so as to stand in front of her.

“I have a question to ask you,” I said. “It obliges me to refer again to a painful subject. Did Rosanna Spearman show you the nightgown? Yes, or no?”

She sprang up and came close of her own accord, searching my face as if to read there some line she had missed before.

“Are you mad?” she asked.

I kept my voice quiet. “Rachel, will you answer my question?”

She went on, heedless of it.

“Have you some object to gain that I don’t understand? Some mean fear about the future, in which I am concerned? They say your father’s death has made you a rich man. Have you come here to compensate me for the loss of my Diamond? And have you heart enough left to feel ashamed of your errand? Is that the secret of your pretence of innocence, and your story about Rosanna Spearman? Is there a motive of shame at the bottom of all the falsehood, this time?”

I stopped her. My control snapped.

“You have done me an infamous wrong!” I cried. “You suspect me of stealing your Diamond. I have a right to know—and I will know—the reason why!”

“Suspect you!” she flung back, anger rising to meet mine. “You villain, I saw you take the Diamond with my own eyes!”

In those words my whole position fell in ruins. The very ground Mr. Bruff had made me stand upon gave way. Innocent as I was, I stood mute; and to her eyes—to any eyes—I must have looked like guilt itself, struck dumb.

She drew back from my silence as if it frightened her.

“I spared you, at the time,” she said. “I would have spared you now, if you had not forced me to speak.” She moved as if to go, then hesitated at the door. “Why did you come here to humiliate yourself?” she asked. “Why did you come here to humiliate me?” She took a few steps, stopped again, and burst out, “For God’s sake, say something! If you have any mercy left, don’t let me degrade myself in this way! Say something—and drive me out of the room!”

I went towards her, scarcely knowing what I did—perhaps meaning to detain her until she had told me more. From the moment I understood she condemned me on the evidence of her own sight, nothing—not even my certainty of my own innocence—was clear. I took her hand and tried to speak with steadiness. All that came was:

“Rachel, you once loved me.”

She shuddered and turned her face away. Her hand lay powerless and trembling in mine.

“Let go of it,” she whispered.

Yet my touch had the same effect my voice had had at the first: after “coward,” after “thief,” while her hand remained in mine, I felt her yield—in spite of herself—as if some deeper habit of feeling still answered to me before her pride could interpose. I drew her back gently into the middle of the room, seated her by me, and spoke as plainly as I could.

“Rachel, I can’t explain the contradiction in what I am going to tell you. I can only speak the truth as you have spoken it. You saw me—before God, you saw me take the Diamond. Before God who hears us, I declare I now know I took it for the first time! Do you doubt me still?”

She neither heeded nor heard.

“Let go of my hand,” she repeated faintly.

Her head sank on my shoulder, and her hand, even as she begged release, closed on mine as if it could not help itself.

I did not press that question again. But I could not stop there. My only chance—my one hope—was that in the chain of what she had seen, some small overlooked trifle might exist, some crumb that careful examination could follow to the truth. I kept her hand. I summoned back what I could of old confidence and old tenderness.

“I want to ask you something,” I said. “Tell me everything that happened from the time we wished each other good-night, to the time you saw me take the Diamond.”

She lifted her head and made another effort to free herself.

“Oh, why go back to it!” she said. “Why go back to it!”

“I will tell you why. You are the victim, and I am the victim, of some monstrous delusion that wears the mask of truth. If we look at what happened on the night of your birthday together, we may end in understanding each other yet.”

Her head fell back on my shoulder. Tears gathered and slid slowly down her cheeks.

“Oh!” she said. “Have I never had that hope? Have I not tried to see it, as you are trying now?”

“You have tried by yourself,” I answered. “You have not tried with me.”

That seemed to stir hope in her—hope answering hope, as one voice finds another in a quiet room. She became docile; more than that, she exerted herself, opened her mind, and helped me.

“Let us begin,” I said, “after we had wished each other good-night. Did you go to bed, or sit up?”

“I went to bed.”

“Did you notice the time?”

“Not very late. About twelve, I think.”

“Did you fall asleep?”

“No. I couldn’t sleep that night.”

“You were restless?”

“I was thinking of you.”

The tone of it—more than the words—went through me. I waited before I could go on.

“Had you any light in your room?”

“None—until I got up again and lit my candle.”

“How long after you went to bed?”

“About an hour, I think. About one o’clock.”

“Did you leave your bedroom?”

“I was going to. I had put on my dressing-gown, and I was going into my sitting-room to get a book—”

“Had you opened your bedroom door?”

“I had just opened it.”

“But you had not gone into the sitting-room?”

“No—I was stopped.”

“What stopped you?”

“I saw a light under the door; and I heard footsteps coming towards it.”

“Were you frightened?”

“Not then. I knew my poor mother was a bad sleeper. And I remembered she had tried hard that evening to persuade me to let her take charge of my Diamond. She was anxious about it—unreasonably, as I thought. I fancied she was coming to see if I was in bed, and to speak of it again if she found me up.”

“What did you do?”

“I blew out my candle, so that she might think I was in bed. I was unreasonable too—I was determined to keep my Diamond where I had put it.”

“After you blew out the candle, did you go back to bed?”

“I had no time. At the moment I blew it out, the sitting-room door opened, and I saw—”

“You saw?”

“You.”

“Dressed as usual?”

“No.”

“In my nightgown?”

“In your nightgown—with your bedroom candle in your hand.”

“Alone?”

“Alone.”

“Could you see my face?”

“Yes.”

“Plainly?”

“Quite plainly. The candle showed it to me.”

“Were my eyes open?”

“Yes.”

“Did you notice anything strange? Anything fixed or vacant in them?”

“Nothing. Your eyes were bright—brighter than usual. You looked about as if you knew you were where you ought not to be, and were afraid of being found out.”

“Did you observe how I walked?”

“You walked as you always do. You came in as far as the middle of the room—and stopped and looked about you.”

“What did you do when you first saw me?”

“I could do nothing. I was petrified. I couldn’t speak, I couldn’t call out, I couldn’t even move to shut my door.”

“Could I see you, where you stood?”

“You might certainly have seen me. But you never looked towards me. It’s useless to ask. I am sure you never saw me.”

“How are you sure?”

“Would you have taken the Diamond? Would you have acted as you did afterwards? Would you be here now—if you had seen I was awake and looking at you? Don’t make me talk of that part! I want to answer quietly. Help me keep as calm as I can. Go on to something else.”

She was right; I went on.

“What did I do after I stopped in the middle of the room?”

“You turned away and went straight to the corner near the window—where my Indian cabinet stands.”

“When I was at the cabinet my back must have been to you. How did you see what I did?”

“When you moved, I moved.”

“So as to see my hands?”

“There are three glasses in my sitting-room. As you stood there, I saw all you did reflected in one of them.”

“What did you see?”

“You put your candle on the top of the cabinet. You opened and shut one drawer after another, until you came to the drawer I had put my Diamond in. You looked at the open drawer for a moment. Then you put your hand in and took the Diamond out.”

“How do you know I took it out?”

“I saw your hand go into the drawer. I saw the gleam of the stone between your finger and thumb when you drew your hand out.”

“Did my hand go back to the drawer again—to close it, for instance?”

“No. You had the Diamond in your right hand; you took the candle from the cabinet with your left.”

“Did I look about again after that?”

“No.”

“Did I leave the room immediately?”

“No. You stood quite still for what seemed a long time. I saw your face sideways in the glass. You looked like a man thinking—and dissatisfied with his own thoughts.”

“What happened next?”

“You roused yourself suddenly and went straight out.”

“Did I close the door after me?”

“No. You passed quickly into the passage and left the door open.”

“And then?”

“Then your light disappeared, and the sound of your steps died away, and I was left alone in the dark.”

“Did nothing happen after that—until the whole house knew the Diamond was lost?”

“Nothing.”

“Are you sure? Might you not have slept for part of the time?”

“I never slept. I never went back to my bed. Nothing happened until Penelope came in at the usual time in the morning.”

I let go her hand, rose, and walked once round the room. Every question I could frame had been answered; every detail I could crave lay before me. I had returned—once more—to the notions of sleep-walking and intoxication, and once more they collapsed; the witness who had seen me had, by what she described, destroyed them. What was left to say? What to do? The Theft—only the Theft—stood there like a thing one could touch: the one solid object in the middle of a darkness that had no edges. Not a glimmer from the Shivering Sand; not a glimmer now.

This time she spoke first.

“Well?” she said. “You have asked, and I have answered. You made me hope something from it, because you hoped something. What have you to say now?”

There was warning in her tone: my influence over her was gone again, as if a door had closed.

“We were to look at what happened on my birthday night together,” she went on, “and we were then to understand each other. Have we done that?”

She waited, pitiless. I answered badly; I made the fatal mistake. The helplessness of my situation exasperated me, and I reproached her for the silence that had kept me in ignorance until now.

“If you had spoken when you ought to have spoken,” I began, “if you had done me the common justice to explain yourself—”

Her fury broke out at once, like flame finding air.

“Explain myself!” she repeated. “Is there another man like this in the world? I spare him when my heart is breaking; I screen him when my own character is at stake; and he—of all human beings, he—turns on me now and tells me I ought to have explained myself! After believing in him as I did, after loving him as I did, after thinking of him by day and dreaming of him by night—he wonders I didn’t charge him with his disgrace the first time we met. ‘My heart’s darling, you are a thief! My hero whom I love and honour, you have crept into my room under cover of night and stolen my Diamond!’ That is what I ought to have said. You villain, you mean, mean, mean villain, I would have lost fifty diamonds rather than see your face lying to me as I see it lying now!”

I took up my hat. In mercy to her—yes, in mercy to her—I turned away without a word and opened the door by which I had entered.

She followed, snatched the door from my hand, shut it, and pointed me back.

“No!” she said. “Not yet! It seems I owe a justification of my conduct to you. You shall stay and hear it. Or you shall stoop to the lowest infamy of all, and force your way out.”

It wrung my heart to see her and to hear her. I could only make a sign that I submitted.

As I went back and took my chair, the crimson of her anger began to fade. She waited, steadied herself, and when she spoke again there was but one sign of feeling left: she did not look at me. Her hands were locked together in her lap; her eyes fixed on the floor.

“I ought to have done you the common justice to explain myself,” she said, repeating my words. “You shall see whether I tried, or not. I told you just now I never slept, and never returned to my bed after you left my sitting-room. It’s useless to trouble you by dwelling on what I thought—you would not understand my thoughts. I will only tell you what I did, when time enough had passed to let me recover myself. I refrained from alarming the house, and telling everybody what had happened—as I ought to have done. In spite of what I had seen, I was fond enough of you to believe—no matter what!—any impossibility, rather than admit to myself you were deliberately a thief. I thought and thought—and I ended in writing to you.”

“I never received the letter,” I said.

“I know you never received it. Wait, and you shall hear why. My letter would have told you nothing openly; it would not have ruined you for life if it had fallen into other hands. It would only have said—in a manner you could not possibly have mistaken—that I had reason to know you were in debt, and that it was in my experience and in my mother’s experience of you that you were not very discreet, or very scrupulous, about how you got money when you wanted it. You would have remembered the visit of the French lawyer, and you would have known what I referred to. If you read on after that, you would have come to an offer I had to make—the offer, privately (not a word, mind, openly between us!), of the loan of as large a sum of money as I could get—and I would have got it!” Her colour rose as she lifted her eyes to me. “I would have pledged the Diamond myself, if I could have got the money in no other way! In those words I wrote to you. Wait! I did more than that. I arranged with Penelope to give you the letter when nobody was near. I planned to shut myself into my bedroom, and to have the sitting-room left open and empty all the morning. And I hoped—with all my heart and soul I hoped—that you would take the opportunity and put the Diamond back secretly in the drawer.”

I tried to speak. She lifted her hand and stopped me, impatient; the quick alternation of her temper had brought anger rising again. She stood up and came nearer.

“I know what you are going to say,” she went on. “You are going to remind me again that you never received my letter. I can tell you why. I tore it up.”

“For what reason?” I asked.

“For the best of reasons. I preferred tearing it up to throwing it away upon such a man as you! What was the first news that reached me in the morning? Just as my little plan was complete, what did I hear? I heard that you—you—were the foremost person in the house in fetching the police. You were the active man; you were the leader; you were working harder than any of them to recover the jewel! You even carried your audacity far enough to ask to speak to me about the loss of the Diamond—the Diamond you yourself had stolen; the Diamond that was all the time in your own hands! After that proof of your horrible falseness and cunning, I tore up my letter. But even then—even when I was maddened by the searching and questioning of the policeman whom you had sent in—even then there was some infatuation in my mind which wouldn’t let me give you up. I said to myself, ‘He has played his vile farce before everybody else in the house. Let me try if he can play it before me.’ Somebody told me you were on the terrace. I went down to the terrace. I forced myself to look at you; I forced myself to speak to you. Have you forgotten what I said?”

I might have told her I remembered every word. But of what use was it then?

How could I make her understand that what she had said had astonished and distressed me; that it had made me fear for her in the tremor of her nerves; that it had even cast, for one fleeting moment, a doubt across my mind whether the loss of the jewel was as mysterious to her as to us all—but that it had not given me the faintest glimpse of the truth? Without one shadow of proof to set beside my bare denial, how could I persuade her that I knew no more than any stranger of what was in her thoughts when she spoke to me on the terrace?

Late that evening, when the city had quieted to the thinner music of wheels and distant calls—when even my lodging-house seemed to listen, as a kitchen listens between courses—I was surprised by Mr. Bruff.

There was a change in him that struck me like a familiar dish made wrongly. His usual brisk assurance was gone; the man looked as if some sourness had got into his blood. He took my hand—he had never done that in his life before—and for a moment said nothing.

“Are you going back to Hampstead?” I asked, merely to break the silence.

“I have just left Hampstead,” he answered. “I know, Mr. Franklin, that you have got at the truth at last. But I tell you plainly, if I could have foreseen the price that was to be paid for it, I should have preferred leaving you in the dark.”

“You have seen Rachel?”

“I have come here after taking her back to Portland Place; it was impossible to let her return in the carriage by herself. I can hardly hold you responsible—considering that you saw her in my house and by my permission—for the shock that this unlucky interview has inflicted on her. All I can do is to provide against a repetition of the mischief. She is young—she has a resolute spirit—she will get over this, with time and rest to help her. I want to be assured that you will do nothing to hinder her recovery. May I depend on your making no second attempt to see her—except with my sanction and approval?”

The words were calm; but there was that underlying sharpness in them which is the rasp of a knife on a board when you would rather hear the gentle hiss of fat meeting heat.

“After what she has suffered, and after what I have suffered,” I said, “you may rely on me.”

“I have your promise?”

“You have my promise.”

He visibly eased, as a man does when a pot at last comes to the boil and proves it will not boil over. He set down his hat and drew his chair nearer.

“That’s settled!” he said. “Now, about the future—your future, I mean. To my mind, the result of the extraordinary turn which the matter has now taken is briefly this. In the first place, we are sure that Rachel has told you the whole truth, as plainly as words can tell it. In the second place—though we know that there must be some dreadful mistake somewhere—we can hardly blame her for believing you to be guilty, on the evidence of her own senses; backed, as that evidence has been, by circumstances which appear, on the face of them, to tell dead against you.”

“I don’t blame Rachel,” I put in. “I only regret that she could not prevail on herself to speak more plainly to me at the time.”

“You might as well regret that Rachel is not somebody else,” he returned. “And even then, I doubt if a girl of any delicacy, whose heart had been set on marrying you, could have brought herself to charge you to your face with being a thief. Anyhow, it was not in Rachel’s nature to do it. In a very different matter to this matter of yours—which placed her, however, in a position not altogether unlike her position towards you—I happen to know that she was influenced by a similar motive to the motive which actuated her conduct in your case. Besides, as she told me herself, on our way to town this evening, if she had spoken plainly, she would no more have believed your denial then than she believes it now. What answer can you make to that? There is no answer to be made to it. Come, come, Mr. Franklin! my view of the case has been proved to be all wrong, I admit—but, as things are now, my advice may be worth having for all that. I tell you plainly, we shall be wasting our time, and cudgelling our brains to no purpose, if we attempt to try back, and unravel this frightful complication from the beginning. Let us close our minds resolutely to all that happened last year at Lady Verinder’s country house; and let us look to what we can discover in the future, instead of to what we can not discover in the past.”

“Surely you forget,” I said, “that the whole thing is essentially a matter of the past—so far as I am concerned?”

He leaned forward, and his voice took that practical ring which lawyers carry like a set of keys.

“Answer me this. Is the Moonstone at the bottom of all the mischief—or is it not?”

“It is—of course.”

“Very good. What do we believe was done with the Moonstone, when it was taken to London?”

“It was pledged to Mr. Luker.”

“We know that you are not the person who pledged it. Do we know who did?”

“No.”

“Where do we believe the Moonstone to be now?”

“Deposited in the keeping of Mr. Luker’s bankers.”

“Exactly. Now observe. We are already in the month of June. Towards the end of the month (I can’t be particular to a day) a year will have elapsed from the time when we believe the jewel to have been pledged. There is a chance—to say the least—that the person who pawned it, may be prepared to redeem it when the year’s time has expired. If he redeems it, Mr. Luker must himself—according to the terms of his own arrangement—take the Diamond out of his banker’s hands. Under these circumstances, I propose setting a watch at the bank, as the present month draws to an end, and discovering who the person is to whom Mr. Luker restores the Moonstone. Do you see it now?”

I did see it; and yet I disliked it. It was like being told to watch a loaf rise, knowing hunger, knowing that you cannot touch it until the yeast has finished its quiet work.

I admitted—unwillingly—that the idea was new.

“It’s Mr. Murthwaite’s idea quite as much as mine,” said Bruff. “It might have never entered my head, but for a conversation we had together some time since. If Mr. Murthwaite is right, the Indians are likely to be on the lookout at the bank, towards the end of the month too—and something serious may come of it. What comes of it doesn’t matter to you and me except as it may help us to lay our hands on the mysterious Somebody who pawned the Diamond. That person, you may rely on it, is responsible (I don’t pretend to know how) for the position in which you stand at this moment; and that person alone can set you right in Rachel’s estimation.”

“I can’t deny,” I said, “that the plan you propose meets the difficulty in a way that is very daring, and very ingenious, and very new. But—”

“But you have an objection to make?”

“Yes. My objection is, that your proposal obliges us to wait.”

“Granted. As I reckon the time, it requires you to wait about a fortnight—more or less. Is that so very long?”

“It’s a life-time, Mr. Bruff, in such a situation as mine. My existence will be simply unendurable to me, unless I do something towards clearing my character at once.”

“Well, well, I understand that. Have you thought yet of what you can do?”

“I have thought of consulting Sergeant Cuff.”

“He has retired from the police. It’s useless to expect the Sergeant to help you.”

“I know where to find him; and I can but try.”

Bruff considered, as if weighing a dose.

“Try,” he said. “The case has assumed such an extraordinary aspect since Sergeant Cuff’s time, that you may revive his interest in the inquiry. Try, and let me hear the result. In the meanwhile,” he added, rising, “if you make no discoveries between this, and the end of the month, am I free to try, on my side, what can be done by keeping a lookout at the bank?”

“Certainly,” I answered—“unless I relieve you of all necessity for trying the experiment in the interval.”

He smiled, took up his hat, and paused with his hand on it, as if locking away the last of his counsel.

“Tell Sergeant Cuff,” he said, “that I say the discovery of the truth depends on the discovery of the person who pawned the Diamond. And let me hear what the Sergeant’s experience says to that.”

So we parted.

Early the next morning I set out for Dorking—the small town where Betteredge had told me Cuff had chosen to end his days. The journey had its own thin concert: rails clicking with mechanical regularity, stations clanging with porters’ cries, London’s harsher roar falling behind like a tide that withdraws but never quite leaves the ears. I caught myself longing for the remembered quiet of Lady Verinder’s house—quiet, now, made bitter by what it held.

At the hotel I asked my questions and got my directions. The cottage lay by a by-road a little out of the town, snug in its own plot of garden ground, protected by a good brick wall behind and at the sides, and by a high quickset hedge in front. The gate, with smartly painted trellis-work at the top, was locked. I rang, and peered through the lattice.

There were roses everywhere: the great man’s chosen preoccupation blooming like a private language. They climbed and clustered; they looked in at the windows; they overran the very doorway, as if they meant to bar out the old work of listening to lies and footsteps. The famous thief-taker, withdrawn from the city’s din, seemed to have given his remaining senses to scent and colour, letting the moral stink of London wash away in petal-soft air.

An elderly woman opened the gate, and with the first words out of her mouth broke the hope I had brought so carefully with me.

He had started—only the day before—on a journey to Ireland.

“Has he gone there on business?” I asked.

She smiled, as if at a child who thinks a man can carry two lives at once.

“He has only one business now, sir,” she said; “and that’s roses. Some great man’s gardener in Ireland has found out something new in the growing of roses—and Mr. Cuff’s away to inquire into it.”

“Do you know when he will be back?”

“It’s quite uncertain, sir. Mr. Cuff said he should come back directly, or be away some time, just according as he found the new discovery worth nothing, or worth looking into. If you have any message to leave for him, I’ll take care, sir, that he gets it.”

I gave her my card, and wrote on it in pencil: “I have something to say about the Moonstone. Let me hear from you as soon as you get back.” Then there was nothing left but to submit to circumstances and return to London.

In the irritable state of my mind, that failure acted like grit between the teeth. The noise of the streets—carts, calls, iron on stone—seemed louder for having been denied the garden’s hush. That day, on my return, I resolved that the next morning should find me on a new attempt—some attempt—at forcing my way through all obstacles from darkness to light.

What form was the next experiment to take?

If Betteredge had been with me, and if I had been foolish enough to let him overhear the disorderly kitchen of my thoughts, he would have said the German side of me was uppermost. In truth, I sat for most of the night smoking and building theories as a cook spoils a broth by adding one flavour after another until nothing can be tasted clearly. Each conjecture was more elaborate and more improbable than the last. When I slept, my fancies pursued me into dreams. I rose with Objective-Subjective and Subjective-Objective so knotted together in my head that I began the day by doubting—on purely philosophical grounds—whether anything, the Diamond included, existed at all.

How long I might have wandered in that fog, I cannot tell. Accident saved me, and I bless it for the plain, domestic way it did so. I wore that morning the same coat I had worn on the day of my interview with Rachel. Hunting in one pocket for something else, my fingers met a crumpled paper. I drew it out—and there was Betteredge’s forgotten letter.

It seemed hard on the good old man to leave him unanswered. I went to the table and read again.

A letter that has nothing of the least importance in it is not always easy to answer. Betteredge’s came into that troublesome class. Mr. Candy’s assistant—Ezra Jennings—had told his master he had seen me; and Mr. Candy, in his turn, wished to see me and say something when next I was near Frizinghall. What could I reply that would be worth the ink? I sat idly, and on the very sheet I had promised to give Betteredge, began drawing likenesses from memory of Mr. Candy’s assistant—that odd man with the piebald hair, whose look and manner had lodged in me like a taste you cannot name but cannot forget. Then, as if the paper itself had provoked me, it suddenly struck me: here was Ezra Jennings again, intruding between me and my object.

I tore up a dozen portraits at least—every one with that same remarkable hair—and flung them into the waste-basket; and then wrote Betteredge a perfectly commonplace answer. It had one excellent effect. A few plain sentences in plain English cleared my mind as a draught of cool water clears the palate.

Now, returning to the puzzle with a steadier appetite for facts, I tried to deal with it practically. The events of the memorable night were still unintelligible; so I went back farther, searching the earlier hours of the birthday for any incident that might prove a clue.

Had anything happened while Rachel and I were finishing the painted door? later, when I rode over to Frizinghall? afterwards, when I went back with Godfrey Ablewhite and his sisters? later again, when I put the Moonstone into Rachel’s hands? later still, when the company came and we all assembled at dinner?

My memory answered readily enough until it reached the dinner-table—then it stopped dead, like a knife that meets bone. I could not even be sure of the number of guests who had sat with me.

That blank, and the suspicion it awakened, came together. When we ourselves become the subject of inquiry, we grow distrustful of what we cannot recall. I resolved to get the names of those who had been present, to set down what each could remember of the evening, and to compare that harvest of recollection with what had happened after the company had left. Unlikely as it seemed, I had groped—without knowing it—toward the root of the matter. I wanted only a hint to start me right; and before another day had passed, one of the guests would give it.

First, I needed the complete list. Betteredge could supply it. I determined to go back to Yorkshire that day and begin the investigation the next morning.

But I had missed the train that left London before noon. There was nothing for it but to wait nearly three hours for the next departure. Could I use the interval?

My thoughts returned—obstinate as hunger—to the birthday dinner. Though I could not marshal the guests in my mind, I remembered that most were from Frizinghall or its neighbourhood. But not all. I myself had been one of the few from outside; Mr. Murthwaite another; Godfrey Ablewhite a third. Mr. Bruff—no, business had kept him away. Had any ladies been there whose usual home was London? Only Miss Clack came back to me. Here were three people, at least, whom I ought to see before leaving town. I drove at once to Mr. Bruff’s office, not knowing their addresses and hoping he could direct me.

Bruff was too busy to spare me more than a minute; but in that minute he contrived to discourage every question I put. My newly conceived method of inquiry he dismissed as fanciful. Mr. Murthwaite, he said, was already on his way back to the scene of his past adventures. Miss Clack had suffered losses and, for economy, had settled in France. Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite might or might not be discoverable in London. Suppose I tried his club? And suppose I excused Mr. Bruff while he returned to his business and wished me good morning?

Thus narrowed, my London work became one thing: find Godfrey. I took the hint and drove to his club.

In the hall I met a member—an old friend of my cousin’s, and an acquaintance of my own—who gave me Godfrey’s address and, besides that, two items of recent news.

Godfrey, it seemed, had not been discouraged by Rachel’s withdrawal. Soon afterward he had offered himself to another young lady, reputed a great heiress. The match had been considered settled, when it too had suddenly broken off—owing, people said, to a serious difference between the bridegroom and the lady’s father on the question of settlements.

As compensation for this second disaster, Godfrey had been remembered in money by one of his admirers: a rich old lady, highly respected at the Mothers’ Small-Clothes-Conversion-Society, and a great friend of Miss Clack’s (to whom she left nothing but a mourning ring), had bequeathed Godfrey a legacy of five thousand pounds. Having secured this addition to his resources, he had been heard to remark that he required respite from charitable labours, and that his doctor prescribed “a run on the Continent” as likely to benefit his health. If I wished to see him, I had better lose no time.

I went at once.

The same unlucky exactness that had made me a day too late at Sergeant Cuff’s cottage made me again a day too late. Godfrey had left London the previous morning by the tidal train for Dover; he was to cross to Ostend; his servant believed he was going on to Brussels. His return was uncertain; but he would be away at least three months.

I went back to my lodgings depressed. Three guests from the birthday dinner—three exceptionally intelligent people—were out of reach at the precise moment when I needed them. My last hopes rested on Betteredge and on such of Lady Verinder’s friends as might still be found near Rachel’s country house.

This time I travelled straight to Frizinghall, making it the centre of my inquiry. I arrived too late in the evening to see Betteredge. Next morning I sent a messenger in a fly with a letter, asking him to join me at the hotel as soon as he could.

During the interval—less than two hours, if no delay occurred—I resolved to begin with those I could reach. My relatives, the Ablewhites, and Mr. Candy. The doctor had expressed a special wish to see me, and lived in the next street. I went to him first.

After what Betteredge had told me, I expected to see illness in the doctor’s face. I was not prepared for the ruin that met me when he entered and shook my hand. His eyes were dim; his hair had turned quite grey; his face was wizen; his figure had shrunk. In memory he had been a lively, rattling little man, full of jokes and social indiscretions; now nothing seemed left of him but the old vulgar smartness in his dress. The man was a wreck; and in cruel contrast his clothes and jewellery were as gay and gaudy as ever, like bright sugar on spoiled fruit.

“I have often thought of you, Mr. Blake,” he said, “and I am heartily glad to see you again at last. If there is anything I can do for you, pray command my services, sir—pray command my services!”

He hurried the commonplace words with needless eagerness, and stared at me with a curiosity about my business in Yorkshire that he was childishly unable to hide.

I had foreseen that I must offer some personal explanation, if I meant to enlist the memories of Lady Verinder’s friends. I had arranged my phrases on the road, and now tried them on Mr. Candy.

“I was in Yorkshire the other day, and I am in Yorkshire again now, on rather a romantic errand,” I said. “It is a matter, Mr. Candy, in which the late Lady Verinder’s friends all took some interest. You remember the mysterious loss of the Indian Diamond, now nearly a year since? Circumstances have lately happened which lead to the hope that it may yet be found—and I am interesting myself, as one of the family, in recovering it. Among the obstacles in my way, there is the necessity of collecting again all the evidence which was discovered at the time, and more if possible. There are peculiarities in this case which make it desirable to revive my recollection of everything that happened in the house, on the evening of Miss Verinder’s birthday. And I venture to appeal to her late mother’s friends who were present on that occasion, to lend me the assistance of their memories—”

I got no farther. As plainly as if the change had been audible, I saw in his face that my attempt had failed. He sat restlessly picking at the points of his fingers while I spoke, his dim watery eyes fixed on me with a vacant, wistful inquiry that was painful to witness. Whatever his mind was doing, it was not following my words. The only way to recall him seemed to be to change the subject sharply.

“So much,” I said, forcing a lighter tone, “for what brings me to Frizinghall! Now, Mr. Candy, it’s your turn. You sent me a message by Gabriel Betteredge—”

He stopped picking at his fingers and suddenly brightened up.

The doctor’s neat little house had a morning hush about it—the kind that belongs to respectable streets before carts begin to clatter and boys begin to shout. Mr. Candy’s young housemaid stood at the open door with the light pouring in behind her, bright as skimmed milk in a white basin. When I turned in the threshold, that light fell full upon the assistant’s face.

If Betteredge had meant that Ezra Jennings looked—by the common measure—like a man who ought not to be trusted, I could not deny it. His skin had the dark, unsettled tint of a gipsy cast; his cheeks were hollow; the bones of his face stood out as if the flesh had been spared them. His eyes—dreamy, steady, too old for their youth and too young for their sadness—made you feel he was listening to something that wasn’t said aloud. His hair was the strangest part of him: coloured as if two lives had laid claim to it, one dark and one fair, and neither had wholly prevailed. Even his figure carried a contradiction—gaunt and worn, yet with a spring in it that belonged to a younger man.

And yet, with all of that against him, there was an appeal in him that went past judgment and straight to feeling—something human that asked, without asking, to be allowed a place among ordinary men. My sense told me to answer his question about the doctor, and go. My sympathy—inscrutable as a scent that catches you before you’ve found its source—held me.

“Are you walking my way, Mr. Jennings?” I said, seeing his hat in his hand. “I am going to call on my aunt, Mrs. Ablewhite.”

He answered that he had a patient to visit, and that his road lay the same way as mine.

We went out together. The housemaid, who had been all pleasantness to me—cheerful as warm bread—changed at once when Ezra spoke to her. He said something quietly about the hour he might return. She received it with pursed lips, her eyes busy with the door-frame, the step, the street—anywhere but his face. It was a small thing; but small things, repeated every day, are how a house teaches a man whether he belongs. Betteredge had said he was unpopular. I believed it.

What a life, to be endured by a man who did not complain.

Down the steps and into the clean morning, Ezra Jennings let the subject of Mr. Candy lie between us like an untasted dish, waiting for me to break it. His silence had meaning in it: It’s your turn now. I had my own reasons for wanting to speak; so I began.

“Judging by the change I see in him,” I said, “Mr. Candy’s illness must have been far more serious than I had supposed.”

“It is almost a miracle,” he replied, “that he lived through it.”

“Is his memory never any better than I have found it today? He has been trying to speak to me—”

“Of something which happened before he was taken ill?” he asked, taking up my broken phrase as neatly as if he had been accustomed to stitch scattered words together.

“Yes.”

“His memory of events at that time is hopelessly enfeebled,” said Ezra Jennings. “It is almost to be deplored—poor fellow—that even the wreck of it remains. He remembers, dimly, that he formed plans—said things—meant to do this or that. But he cannot recall what the plans were, or what the thing was. And he knows it. He is painfully conscious of his deficiency, and painfully anxious to hide it. If he could only have recovered in complete oblivion, he would have been happier. Perhaps we should all be happier”—and here a sad little smile touched him and was gone—“if we could but completely forget.”

“There are some events,” I said, “the memory of which a man would be unwilling entirely to lose.”

“That is, I hope, true of most men, Mr. Blake. I am afraid it cannot truly be said of all. Have you any reason to suppose that the lost remembrance he tried to recover—while you were with him—was important to you?”

He had come, without any urging, upon the very matter that had been pulling me like a hook ever since I left the sick-room. I had stayed, at first, because he interested me; I held back my purpose until I knew whether I could trust him. What he had shown already—without emphasis, without display—was a quiet self-possession that did not seem learned, but native. A gentleman, not because he claimed it, but because he could not help being it. Whatever his motive in questioning me, I felt safe, so far, in answering plainly.

“I believe I have a strong interest,” I said, “in tracing that lost remembrance. Can you suggest any method by which I might assist his memory?”

A sudden quickness lit his brown eyes—as if the word memory had struck a string that was tuned already.

“Mr. Candy’s memory is beyond the reach of assistance,” he said. “I have tried often enough since his recovery to speak positively.”

I could not hide my disappointment. I owned it at once.

“I confess you led me to hope for a less discouraging answer.”

He smiled—not with mockery, but as a man smiles who has a second thought behind his first.

“It may not be final,” he said. “It may be possible to trace Mr. Candy’s lost recollection without appealing to Mr. Candy himself.”

“Indeed? Is it an indiscretion to ask how?”

“By no means. My only difficulty is explaining myself. May I trust to your patience, if I refer once more to the illness—and speak this time without sparing you certain professional details?”

“Pray go on. You have interested me already.”

My eagerness seemed to please him. We had now left the last houses behind and were in that open country where the road begins to breathe. There were hedges at either side, with damp leaves that held the sun in drops; there were birds busy with the morning; and from far off came the slow, comfortable sound of a cart somewhere—wood and iron meeting the road at steady intervals. It was a kinder sound than the town’s jangling; it belonged to labour, not to hurry. Ezra Jennings paused and, with a gentleness that surprised me in so worn a man, picked a few wild flowers from the hedge—little pale things with a clean green smell on their stems.

“How beautiful they are!” he said, simply, showing me the nosegay. “And how few people in England seem to admire them as they deserve.”

“You have not always been in England?” I asked.

“No. I was born, and partly brought up, in one of our colonies. My father was an Englishman; but my mother—We are straying away from our subject, Mr. Blake; and it is my fault. The truth is, I have associations with these modest little hedgeside flowers—It doesn’t matter. We were speaking of Mr. Candy. To Mr. Candy let us return.”

The reluctance with which he had let those personal words escape him, joined to the melancholy he carried like a shadow, satisfied me that I had read truly in his face at least thus much: he had suffered deeply, and foreign blood ran in him.

“You have heard, I dare say, of the original cause of Mr. Candy’s illness?” he resumed. “The night of Lady Verinder’s dinner-party was a night of heavy rain. My employer drove home through it in his gig, and reached the house wetted to the skin. He found an urgent message from a patient waiting; and he most unfortunately went at once to visit the sick person, without stopping to change his clothes. I was detained that night by a case at some distance from Frizinghall. When I returned the next morning, I found his groom in alarm, waiting to take me to his room. By that time the mischief was done; the illness had set in.”

“It has only been described to me, in general terms, as a fever,” I said.

“I can add nothing that will make the description more accurate,” he answered. “From first to last it assumed no specific form. I sent at once to two of Mr. Candy’s medical friends in the town—both physicians—to give me their opinion. They agreed with me that it looked serious; but they dissented strongly from the view I took of the treatment. We differed entirely in what we drew from the pulse. They argued from the rapidity of the beat: ‘lowering treatment,’ they said, was the only treatment. I admitted the rapidity; but I pointed to its feebleness as indicating exhaustion and a plain necessity for stimulants. They were for gruel, lemonade, barley-water, and so on. I was for champagne—or brandy—ammonia and quinine. A serious difference, as you see: two physicians of established local repute against a stranger who was only an assistant in the house.

“For the first few days I had no choice but to give way; and the patient steadily sank. I appealed again to the evidence of the pulse—its rapidity unchecked, its feebleness increased. The two doctors took offence. They said, ‘Mr. Jennings, either we manage this case, or you manage it. Which is it to be?’ I said, ‘Gentlemen, give me five minutes to consider, and that plain question shall have a plain reply.’

“When the time expired, I said, ‘You positively refuse to try the stimulant treatment?’ They refused in so many words. ‘Then I mean to try it at once, gentlemen.’—‘Try it, Mr. Jennings, and we withdraw from the case.’ I sent down to the cellar for a bottle of champagne and administered half a tumbler-full with my own hand. The two physicians took up their hats, and left the house.”

“You assumed a serious responsibility,” I said. “In your place, I am afraid I should have shrunk from it.”

“In my place,” he answered, “you would have remembered that Mr. Candy had taken you into his employment under circumstances which made you his debtor for life. You would have watched him sinking hour by hour, and you would have risked anything rather than let the one man on earth who had befriended you die before your eyes.

“Don’t suppose I had no sense of the position I placed myself in. There were moments when I felt all the misery of my friendlessness, all the peril of my responsibility. If I had been a happy man—if I had led a prosperous life—I believe I should have sunk under the task. But I had no happy time to look back on; no past peace of mind to force itself into contrast with my present anxiety—and I held firm.

“I took an interval in the middle of the day, when his condition was at its best, for the repose I needed. For the rest of the four-and-twenty hours, as long as his life was in danger, I never left his bedside. Towards sunset the delirium came on, as usual; it lasted more or less through the night, and then intermitted at that terrible time in the early morning—from two o’clock to five—when the vital energies, even of the healthiest, are at their lowest. It is then Death gathers most abundantly. It was then that Death and I fought our fight over the bed.

“I never hesitated. When wine failed, I tried brandy. When stimulants lost influence, I doubled the dose. After an interval of suspense—the like of which I hope to God I shall never feel again—there came a day when the rapidity of the pulse diminished slightly, but appreciably; and better still, the beat changed to steadiness and strength. Then I knew I had saved him; and then I broke down. I laid his wasted hand back on the bed and burst out crying. An hysterical relief—nothing more. Physiology says, truly, that some men are born with female constitutions—and I am one of them.”

He made that bitter little apology without self-pity, speaking as quietly as he had spoken throughout, as if he feared—almost morbidly—being turned into an object of sentiment. Even the countryside seemed to keep a respectful quiet around his confession: the low wash of wind on the moor, the distant creak of wheels, the birds’ thin calls—sounds that belonged to life going on, indifferent and constant, while one man watched another man’s breath as if it were the only sound in the world.

“You may ask why I have wearied you with all these details,” he continued. “It is the only way I can properly introduce what I have to say next. Now that you know my position at the time of his illness, you will understand the need I had—at intervals—of lightening the burden on my mind.

“I have had the presumption, for some years past, to occupy my leisure in writing a book for my profession—on the brain and the nervous system. It will probably never be finished; and it will certainly never be published. But it has been the friend of many lonely hours; and it helped me to while away the anxious time at Mr. Candy’s bedside. I told you he was delirious; and I mentioned the time at which his delirium came on?

“Well, I had reached a section of my book touching that question of delirium. I will not trouble you with my theory at length; I will confine myself to what your interest requires. It has often occurred to me, in practice, to doubt whether we can justifiably infer—in cases of delirium—that the loss of the faculty of speaking connectedly implies, of necessity, the loss of the faculty of thinking connectedly. Mr. Candy’s illness gave me an opportunity of putting that doubt to the test.

“I understand shorthand; and I took down the patient’s ‘wanderings’ exactly as they fell from his lips. Do you see what I am coming to, Mr. Blake?”

I saw it as clearly as if a lamp had been suddenly lit in a dark room, and I waited, hardly daring to breathe.

“At odds and ends of time,” he went on, “I copied my shorthand notes into ordinary writing—leaving large spaces between the broken phrases, even between single words, as they fell disconnectedly. Then I treated the result on something like the principle of a child’s puzzle. At first it is confusion; but it may be brought into shape, if you can find the right way. Acting on this plan, I filled each blank with what the words on either side suggested as meaning—altering again and again, until my additions followed naturally upon what came before, and fitted naturally into what came after.

“The result was that I not only occupied many vacant and anxious hours, but arrived at something which seemed to confirm my view: after putting the broken sentences together I found the superior faculty of thinking going on, more or less connectedly, while the inferior faculty of expression was in almost complete confusion.”

“One word,” I broke in. “Did my name occur in any of his wanderings?”

“You shall hear. Among my written experiments there is one in which your name occurs. For nearly the whole of one night his mind was occupied with something between himself and you. I have the broken words, as they dropped from his lips, on one sheet of paper; and the links of my own discovering which connect them, on another. The product is an intelligible statement—first, of something actually done in the past; secondly, of something he contemplated doing in the future, if his illness had not stopped him. The question is whether this does, or does not, represent the lost recollection he tried to find when you called on him this morning.”

“Not a doubt of it!” I cried. “Let us go back directly and look at the papers!”

“Quite impossible.”

“Why?”

He turned his face a little toward me, and his voice had a grave steadiness in it that admitted no heat.

“Put yourself in my position. Would you disclose to another what had dropped unconsciously from the lips of your suffering patient and your helpless friend, without first knowing there was a necessity to justify you in opening your lips?”

He was unanswerable; but I argued still.

“My conduct,” I said, “would depend greatly on whether the disclosure compromised my friend or not.”

“I disposed of that side of the question long since,” he replied. “Wherever my notes included anything Mr. Candy might have wished to keep secret, those notes were destroyed. My manuscript experiments include nothing now which he would have hesitated to communicate if he had recovered his memory. In your case, I have every reason to suppose my notes contain something he actually wished to say to you.”

“And yet you hesitate?”

“And yet I hesitate. Remember the circumstances under which I obtained what I possess. Harmless as it is, I cannot prevail on myself to give it up unless you first satisfy me there is a reason. He was so miserably ill—and so helplessly dependent on me. Is it too much to ask that you only hint what your interest is in his lost recollection—or what you believe that recollection to be?”

To answer him with the frankness he deserved was to name aloud the degrading thing I could scarcely bear even to think: that I stood suspected of stealing the Diamond. Strong as my interest in him had grown, it did not conquer that reluctance. I took refuge—miserably—in the vague explanations I had prepared for strangers.

This time I could not accuse my listener of impatience. Ezra Jennings heard me out patiently—more than patiently, with an anxious attention that showed he was trying to do me justice even while I withheld the truth.

When I finished, he shook his head.

“I am sorry to have raised your expectations only to disappoint them,” he said. “Throughout the whole period of Mr. Candy’s illness, from first to last, not one word about the Diamond escaped his lips. The matter with which I heard him connect your name has, I assure you, no discoverable relation whatever with the loss or recovery of Miss Verinder’s jewel.”

As he said it, we came to a place where the highway forked into two roads. One led toward Mr. Ablewhite’s house. The other ran out to a moorland village two or three miles away. Ezra Jennings stopped at the turning that led to the village.

“My way lies in this direction,” he said. “I am really and truly sorry that I can be of no use to you.”

His voice carried sincerity; his eyes rested on me for a moment with a melancholy interest that was almost a kindness. He bowed and, without another word, set off along the village road.

I stood and watched him go, feeling as if he carried away, step by step, the very clue I needed—carried it away into that wide, open land where sound thins out and a man’s thoughts grow loud. He walked a little distance, then turned and looked back. Seeing me still at the parting, he stopped as if doubting whether I wished to call him.

There was no time to argue with myself—to remind myself that I was throwing away, out of pride, what might be the turning-point of my life. There was only time to act first and think afterwards. I called him back—and as I did it, I said to myself, Now there is no help for it. I must tell him the truth.

He returned at once. I went a few steps toward him along the road.

“Mr. Jennings,” I said, “I have not treated you quite fairly. My interest in tracing Mr. Candy’s lost recollection is not the interest of recovering the Moonstone. A serious personal matter is at the bottom of my visit to Yorkshire. I have but one excuse for not having dealt frankly with you: it is more painful to me than I can say to mention to anybody what my position really is.”

For the first time I saw embarrassment cross his face.

“I have no right, Mr. Blake, and no wish,” he said, “to intrude myself into your private affairs. Allow me to ask your pardon, on my side, for having—most innocently—put you to a painful test.”

“You have a perfect right,” I answered, “to fix the terms on which you feel justified in revealing what you heard at Mr. Candy’s bedside. I understand and respect your delicacy. How can I expect to be taken into your confidence if I decline to admit you into mine? You ought to know—and you shall know—why I am interested in discovering what Mr. Candy wanted to say to me. If I turn out mistaken—if you prove unable to help me when you know what I want—I shall trust to your honour to keep my secret. And something tells me I shall not trust in vain.”

“Stop, Mr. Blake. I have a word to say, which must be said before you go any farther.”

I stared at him. Some powerful emotion seemed to seize him, as sudden as a squall that changes the whole weather of a day. His gipsy complexion turned to a livid grey; his eyes, a moment ago so controlled, grew wild and glittering; and his voice dropped into a low tone—stern, resolute—which I had not yet heard from him. Whatever was stored in him, for good or ill, rose up and showed itself like lightning in a dark sky.

“Before you place any confidence in me,” he said, “you ought to know—and you must know—under what circumstances I have been received into Mr. Candy’s house. It will not take long. I don’t profess, sir, to tell my story to any man. My story will die with me. All I ask is to be permitted to tell you what I have told Mr. Candy. If you are still of the same mind when you have heard that, you will command my attention and command my services. Shall we walk on?”

The misery he held down, and which had shaken loose for an instant, silenced me. I answered only by a sign, and we went on together.

After a few hundred yards he stopped at a gap in the rough stone wall where the moor opened out—brown grass and scattered heath running to the horizon, the wind moving over it with a long, low voice like distant surf.

“Do you mind resting a little, Mr. Blake?” he asked. “I am not what I was—and some things shake me.”

The two hours Ezra Jennings had condemned me to wait might have sat lightly enough on another man. On me they worked like a bodily fever. I could not keep still; I could not speak—could not even endure the honest face of Betteredge—until I had wrung from Jennings all he knew, and made his knowledge mine.

So I broke off, without ceremony, the visit I had meant to pay Mrs. Ablewhite. I even shrank from meeting my own companion at the inn.

Back at Frizinghall, I wrote Betteredge a line—cold, brief, and miserably unlike myself—telling him I had been called away for a few hours; that I should return towards three; that he was to order dinner at the usual time, and divert himself as he pleased. He had friends enough in that little town; and he would fill the interval more easily than I could.

Then I escaped again into the country.

The moorland round Frizinghall lay wide and spare under a thin sky, the wind combing the heather with a dry, whispering sound—one of those honest sounds which do not lie to the ear. Every step took me farther from the town’s clatter: no wheels grinding, no iron striking iron, no shop-bell’s bright little scream. Only the hush of turf, the scattered call of birds, and (far away) a dog’s bark softened by distance, as if the land itself were teaching the voice to be gentle. The quiet steadied me; it did not comfort me. My mind went round and round the same circle, like a hand grinding spice on a stone—heat, friction, and no release—until my watch told me, at last, that the time had come.

I returned to Mr. Candy’s house, and found Ezra Jennings ready.

He was waiting in a small, bare room that opened by a glazed door into the surgery. The walls were the colour of old buff leather and the decoration upon them was a horror—bright diagrams, garish with disease, as if suffering had been made into a sort of vulgar shop-sign. A skull sat above a row of dingy medical books where another man might have placed a bust; a big deal table was blotched and splashed with ink; the chairs were the plain wooden kind you find in kitchens where broth is stirred and bread is cut; a worn drugget lay like a tired rag in the middle of the boards. In the wall was a sink, with basin and waste-pipe rudely fitted—an innocent thing, in any other house, for washing hands or rinsing a cup; here, it carried the cold suggestion of blood and instruments. On the shelf, the mahogany case of an instrument lay with the quiet weight of concealed purpose; near it, a huge roll of lint—white, patient, and dreadful—kept its own place.

Outside the window, a few pots of flowers stood in the light; bees moved among them with that soft, industrious hum that belongs to summer and gardens and the ordinary sweetness of life. Birds were singing. And now and then, from some neighbouring house, there came the faint, intermittent jingle of a piano—tuneless, careless, played by fingers that had no notion what sort of room their little noise was trespassing upon. In any other place those sounds would have been friendly. Here they came like intruders, as if the world of dinners and household talk, of teacups and children’s feet, had put its ear to a door behind which nothing spoke but pain.

“I make no apology, Mr. Blake, for receiving you here,” Jennings said. “It is the only room where we can be sure of not being interrupted. My papers are ready. And here are two books which we may need to consult. Bring your chair to the table.”

I sat. He handed me two folio leaves—one sparsely covered; the other thick with writing in red and black ink, packed from top to bottom. In the irritation of my suspense, the second page looked like a wall.

“Have some mercy on me,” I said. “Tell me what I am to expect before I try to read this.”

“Willingly. But first—one or two questions more.”

“Ask what you please.”

He looked at me with that sad sweetness of his—no affectation, no professional hardness—only a kind attention that seemed to carry its own fatigue.

“You have told me that you have never, to your knowledge, tasted opium.”

“To my knowledge,” I repeated, and felt the ominous emphasis of his words.

“You will see presently why I keep that reservation. Last year, at this time, you suffered from nervous irritation. You slept badly at night—except on the birthday night, when you slept soundly. Is that true?”

“Quite true.”

“Can you assign any cause for the nervous suffering and the sleeplessness?”

“I can assign none. Betteredge guessed at one—but—”

“Anything is worth mentioning here. What did Betteredge say?”

“That I had left off smoking.”

“Had you been a habitual smoker?”

“Yes.”

“Did you give it up suddenly?”

“Yes.”

He nodded as if I had supplied a missing link.

“Then Betteredge was right. A man must have no common constitution who can throw off a habit of that kind at once without some temporary disorder of the nerves. Your sleepless nights are accounted for, to my mind.” He paused a moment, and then asked quietly: “Do you remember any dispute with Mr. Candy at the birthday dinner, or after it, about his profession?”

The question stirred a remembrance I had left to sleep. I recalled the substance, though not the foolish detail: my rash talk at table, my obstinate attack on medicine, Mr. Candy’s irritation, Lady Verinder’s interference, and our reconciliation before we parted.

“One thing more,” Jennings said. “It is important. Had you any reason for special anxiety about the Diamond at that time?”

“The strongest reasons. I knew it was the object of a conspiracy. I had been warned to protect Miss Verinder as the possessor of the stone.”

“Was the Diamond spoken of immediately before you went to bed?”

“It was the subject of talk between Lady Verinder and her daughter.”

“In your hearing?”

“Yes.”

Jennings took up his notes and placed them in my hands as if he were putting a verdict into them.

“Mr. Blake, read now by the light of what you have told me, and you will make two discoveries about yourself. First: you entered Miss Verinder’s sitting-room and took the Diamond in a trance produced by opium. Second: the opium was given to you by Mr. Candy—without your knowledge—as a practical refutation of what you had said to him at dinner.”

For a moment the room, with all its ghastly objects and its stolen garden-sounds, swam before me. I sat with the papers as if they had turned to stone.

“Try to forgive poor Mr. Candy,” Jennings said gently. “He has done dreadful mischief—but innocently. But for his illness, he would have returned to Lady Verinder’s the next morning and owned the trick. Miss Verinder would have heard. Miss Verinder would have questioned him. And the truth, which has lain hidden for a year, would have been found in a day.”

Anger came back first—anger, and a kind of sick humiliation.

“Mr. Candy is beyond my resentment,” I said. “But treachery is treachery. I may forgive it—I shall not forget it.”

“Every medical man commits that treachery, as you call it, sometimes in practice,” Jennings answered. “The ignorant distrust of opium is not confined to the poor. Doctors are driven to deceive patients. I do not defend the folly of playing you such a trick under such circumstances. I only ask you to judge motives more mercifully.”

“How was it done?” I asked. “Who gave me laudanum without my knowing it?”

“I cannot tell you. Mr. Candy dropped nothing on that part of the matter during his illness. Perhaps your own memory—”

“No.”

“Then it is useless to pursue it. The laudanum was secretly given in some way. Leave that. There are nearer necessities.” His voice sharpened, not with cruelty, but with purpose. “Read my notes. Familiarise yourself with what happened. I have something bold—startling—to propose, that concerns the future.”

Those words pricked me into attention as a knife pricks a numb limb.

I took the sheets in the order he had placed them. The top one held only scattered fragments—broken utterances, as if a mind in delirium had dropped bits of sentence like crumbs on a floor. I read:

“... Mr. Franklin Blake ... clever and agreeable ... down a peg ... medicine ... confesses ... sleep at night ... tell him ... out of order ... medicine ... groping in the dark mean one and the same thing ... all the company at the dinner-table ... I say ... groping after sleep ... nothing but medicine ... he says ... leading the blind ... know what it means ... witty ... a night’s rest in spite of his teeth ... wants sleep ... Lady Verinder’s medicine chest ... five-and-twenty minims ... without his knowing it ... tomorrow morning ... Well, Mr. Blake ... medicine today ... never ... without it ... out, Mr. Candy ... excellent ... without it ... down on him ... truth ... something besides ... excellent ... dose of laudanum, sir ... bed ... what ... medicine now.”

I handed it back.

“That is what you heard at his bedside?”

“Literally and exactly—except for repetitions,” Jennings said. “He repeated certain words and phrases over and over, according as the idea mattered to him. That helped me to piece the fragments together.” He pointed to the second sheet. “And don’t suppose I pretend I give you Mr. Candy’s connected expressions. I give you the connected thought that lay underneath. Judge for yourself.”

Now I turned to the other page, and understood at once what I was looking at: the black ink held Mr. Candy’s broken phrases; between them, in red ink, Jennings had stitched the sense—like a careful cook taking scraps and bones and, with patience and skill, drawing from them a strong clear broth. It was not magic; it was method. Out of ravelled delirium, he had made a plain narrative that any man could follow.

“Mr. Franklin Blake is clever and agreeable, but he wants taking down a peg when he talks of medicine. He confesses that he has been suffering from want of sleep at night. I tell him that his nerves are out of order, and that he ought to take medicine. He tells me that taking medicine and groping in the dark mean one and the same thing. This before all the company at the dinner-table. I say to him, you are groping after sleep, and nothing but medicine can help you to find it. He says to me, I have heard of the blind leading the blind, and now I know what it means. Witty—but I can give him a night’s rest in spite of his teeth. He really wants sleep; and Lady Verinder’s medicine chest is at my disposal. Give him five-and-twenty minims of laudanum tonight, without his knowing it; and then call tomorrow morning. ‘Well, Mr. Blake, will you try a little medicine today? You will never sleep without it.’—‘There you are out, Mr. Candy: I have had an excellent night’s rest without it.’ Then, come down on him with the truth! ‘You have had something besides an excellent night’s rest; you had a dose of laudanum, sir, before you went to bed. What do you say to the art of medicine, now?’”

The shock of it was not merely the accusation against my own conduct. It was the petty vanity of the motive—the doctor’s desire to triumph at breakfast with a neat little sting of “I told you so”—set against the enormous consequence. The mind sickened at such disproportion.

In spite of myself, the first clear feeling that rose was admiration of Jennings’s ingenuity—the way he had brought order out of chaos. I began to speak of it; he stopped me with a modest movement.

“Do you draw the same conclusion I draw?” he asked. “That you were acting under the influence of laudanum in what you did that night?”

“I am too ignorant,” I said. “I can only accept your opinion, and I believe you are right.”

“Very well. Then comes the great question. You are convinced; I am convinced. How do we carry our conviction into other minds?”

I pointed to the pages between us.

“Useless,” he said, and shook his head. “Useless for three reasons. First: they were taken under circumstances outside common experience—men distrust what lies beyond their own habits. Second: they represent a medical and metaphysical theory—men distrust theory. Third: they are of my making—nothing but my word guarantees they are not fabricated. Remember what I told you on the moor, and ask yourself what my word is worth. No, Mr. Blake. The only value of these notes, to the world, is that they show us how to prove you innocent. We must put our conviction to the test—and you are the man to prove it.”

“How?” I said.

He leaned forward, his eyes intent, his voice low.

“Are you willing to try a bold experiment?”

“I will do anything to clear myself.”

“Will you submit to personal inconvenience for a time?”

“To any.”

“Will you be guided implicitly by my advice? You may be laughed at by fools. You may be opposed by friends whose opinions you respect.”

“Tell me what to do,” I burst out. “Come what may, I’ll do it.”

He answered, very steadily:

“You shall steal the Diamond unconsciously for the second time—in the presence of witnesses whose testimony is beyond dispute.”

I sprang up as if the words had struck me. My throat tightened. I tried to speak and could not; I could only stare at him across the stained deal table, with the skull looking down between us.

“I believe it can be done,” he went on. “And it shall be done—if you will help me. Compose yourself. Sit down and hear me.” He waited until I obeyed him—mechanically, like a man dragged back from a precipice. Then he said: “You have resumed smoking. How long?”

“For nearly a year.”

“More or less than before?”

“More.”

“Will you give it up again? Suddenly—as you gave it up then.”

I began to see the outline of his design—like the first smell of yeast in a kitchen tells you bread is being prepared.

“I will give it up from this moment,” I said.

“If the same consequences follow as followed last June,” he continued—“if you suffer again from sleepless nights—we gain our first step. We bring you back towards your nervous condition of the birthday night. If we can then revive—nearly revive—the domestic circumstances that surrounded you; if we can occupy your mind again with the Diamond, as it occupied you then; we may replace you, as nearly as possible, physically and morally, in the same position in which opium found you last year. In that case, a repetition of the dose may lead, in some degree, to a repetition of the result. That is my proposal, in brief. And now you shall see I have authority for it.”

He opened one of the books beside him, at a place marked by a slip of paper.

“I am not going to weary you with a lecture,” he said. “I only mean to prove I am not asking you to do this on a fancy of my own. Science sanctions what I propose.” He handed me the slip. “Here is the principle, stated by Dr. Carpenter. Read it.”

I read:

“There seems much ground for the belief, that every sensory impression which has once been recognised by the perceptive consciousness, is registered (so to speak) in the brain, and may be reproduced at some subsequent time, although there may be no consciousness of its existence in the mind during the whole intermediate period.”

“Plain?” he asked.

“Perfectly.”

He pushed the open book towards me and pointed to a passage marked in pencil.

“Now read this case. The book is Dr. Elliotson’s Human Physiology. The case rests on Mr. Combe’s authority.”

I read:

“Dr. Abel informed me of an Irish porter to a warehouse, who forgot, when sober, what he had done when drunk; but, being drunk, again recollected the transactions of his former state of intoxication. On one occasion, being drunk, he had lost a parcel of some value, and in his sober moments could give no account of it. Next time he was intoxicated, he recollected that he had left the parcel at a certain house, and there being no address on it, it had remained there safely, and was got on his calling for it.”

“Plain again?” he asked.

“As plain as need be.”

He replaced the slip and closed the book.

“Are you satisfied I have not spoken without authority?” he said. “If not, I can point you to more.”

“I am satisfied,” I answered. “Without another word.”

“Then let us be just on both sides. Something can be said against the experiment as well as for it. If we could exactly reproduce last year’s conditions, the result would certainly be the same. But that is impossible. We can only approximate. If we don’t get you near enough, we fail. If we do get near enough—and I hope we shall—you may so far repeat your proceedings as to satisfy any reasonable person that, morally speaking, you are guiltless of the theft. I have stated the question fairly, I think. If anything is unclear, ask me.”

“There is one point,” I said. “I don’t understand the effect of laudanum. I can’t reconcile it with my walking downstairs, along corridors, opening drawers, returning to my room. Those are active acts. I thought opium stupefied a man, and then sent him to sleep.”

“The common error,” Jennings replied. “At this moment, I am exerting my intelligence—such as it is—in your service, under a dose of laudanum ten times larger than Mr. Candy gave you. But don’t take my word even for that. I have brought independent testimony—testimony that will carry weight with you and with your friends.”

He took up the second book and held it out.

“There are the Confessions of an English Opium-Eater. Take it away and read the passage I have marked. You will find that when De Quincey had what he calls ‘a debauch of opium,’ he went to the Opera to enjoy the music—or wandered the London markets on Saturday night, watching the poor bargain and scheme for their Sunday dinners. So much for a man’s capacity to move and act under opium.”

“I am answered so far,” I said, turning the book over in my hands. “But I am not answered yet as to the effect produced by the opium on myself.”

Few words are needed from me to bring to an end what Ezra Jennings has already set down.

On the morning of the twenty-sixth I woke, as a man wakes from chloroform—alive, sane, and utterly dispossessed of memory. From the first moment when opium took me by the hand, to the instant I opened my eyes in Rachel’s sitting-room, all was blank.

What followed my waking I will not relate in detail. The result is enough. Rachel and I understood each other—before either of us had spoken one word of explanation. I decline to account for the swiftness of that reconciliation; Rachel declines also. Those who have ever had their hearts taken by storm will know what passed between us when Ezra Jennings closed the door and left us alone.

We should, however, have been discovered—certainly discovered—if Rachel’s quick sense had not been quicker than Mrs. Merridew’s footsteps. Rachel heard the soft rustle of the old lady’s gown along the corridor: the particular sound of stiff silk that whispers, even when it means well. In a moment Rachel was out and in her way.

“What is the matter?” I heard Mrs. Merridew ask.

“The explosion!” Rachel answered, with a steadiness that might have carried off any domestic emergency from a scorched pudding to a broken heart. She took Mrs. Merridew by the arm and led her down into the garden “out of the way of the impending shock,” as if Ezra Jennings’s scientific contrivance had been a cannon to be fired off.

When they returned, Mrs. Merridew met me in the hall and spoke with the benign superiority of a lady who has been at school when “science” was a new word.

“Explosions, Mr. Blake, are infinitely milder than they were. I assure you I barely heard Mr. Jennings’s explosion from the garden. And no smell afterwards, that I can detect, now we have come back to the house! I must really apologise to your medical friend. It is only due to him to say that he has managed it beautifully!”

So—after vanquishing Betteredge and Mr. Bruff, Ezra Jennings vanquished Mrs. Merridew too. There is more liberal feeling in the world than we give it credit for; and it is sometimes won by nothing grander than the absence of an after-smell.

At breakfast Mr. Bruff made no secret of his reason for wanting me in London by the morning train.
